The Hermit, the Devil, and the Star

by thetalee

Summary

Buck is back at the 118, and Eddie's healed from the sniper, but that doesn't mean everything's rainbows and unicorns. He knows there's something wrong with Maddie, no matter how many times she and Chimney say everything's fine. He knows there's something wrong with Eddie, even if his boyfriend insists otherwise. At least everything seems to be okay with Daniel, this time.

Chapter 1

Recovering from a severe injury, Buck knew, was an arduous and painful process. It was not something easily done alone, without the support of those who cared enough to push you forward, who cared enough to stay when you were at your worse. And God, Buck knew he’d been at his worse when it was him struggling along. It was amazing anyone had stuck around to put up with it.

Back in the Spring, Eddie was shot by a former LAPD sniper with a grudge against firefighters and paramedics. Buck was intimately aware of the fact that it was not Eddie’s first time being shot, having traced the scars many a day and night. Buck also knew that Eddie didn’t have the support then to push him through recovery that he did now, the support that Buck had with his leg. It was an unspoken knowledge, gleamed from what he’d seen of Ramon and Helena when he lived in El Paso.

That was why, this time, Buck was making sure Eddie was getting the support he needed, the support Buck himself got. Whether he was happy about it or not.

“You’re enjoying this too much,” Eddie grumbled. He was wearing a loose tank top and shorts, clothes that were easy to move around in.

“What? Making sure my boyfriend can still use his arm?” Buck rolled his eyes as he moved a chair to make room. “Shock, horror.”

“Now you know how I felt when I had mono,” Danny commented from the dining room. “He forced me to drink gallons of water and then refused to let me up to use the bathroom.”

They were at Eddie’s house. Technically speaking, Buck was almost always at Eddie’s house, now, whenever he wasn’t working. He’d already spent much of his off time here, it had just increased exponentially since the shooting. There were a lot of things that Eddie needed help with while he was down a hand, things Carla couldn’t always be there for, and Buck stepped into the role before Eddie even had the chance to stubbornly refuse.

He’d more or less moved out of the apartment and into the Diaz house, but neither Eddie nor Danny had said anything about it.

“Way to sound appreciative of my care, guys,” was Buck’s dry response. “Really, it warms my heart.”

“I appreciate it!” Chris called out.

“Suck up,” Danny stage-whispered.

Chris giggled.

He, along with Danny and Austin, were seated around the small dining table. They were playing a card game and Buck had thought it was Go Fish, but the piles of pretzels in front of them suggested something else entirely. Not that Buck was gonna bring it to Eddie’s attention.

“You’re officially my favorite, Superman,” Buck told the kid.

“Does that mean I get to decide what’s for dinner?”

“Sure thing. As long as it’s something spicy.”

“Oh, screw you,” Danny sniped.

“Tough it out, gringo,” Eddie told him. “What am I doing?”

Buck picked up his clipboard, with the list of exercises that Eddie’s doctor had given them during the last appointment. She’d given Eddie the all clear to start working on his shoulder’s range of motion, with an emphasis on not rushing anything. Having gone out of his way to see what a regular bullet did to the inside of a person, having seen what a large caliber round had done to Eddie, Buck understood perfectly well why they couldn’t jump into anything too strenuous even now.

“Codman’s exercises,” he read off.

“Oh, the arm pendulum thing.” Eddie nodded to himself as he peered at the paper as well and then bent over at the waist. He propped himself up on the arm of the chair with his good arm, letting his right one hang down loose. “How many?”

Buck was too busy staring at the view his boyfriend was providing to even notice he was being asked anything. At least until his brother’s obnoxiousness broke him out of it.

“Oh, puke!” Danny crowed. “There are children in here!”

Danny had reached across the table to cover Chris’ eyes, the kid giggling as he tried to dodge his hand. Buck blew a raspberry at him, and then refocused on his smirking boyfriend’s face.

“Sorry, uh. What was the question?”

“How many times?” Eddie repeated.

“Oh.” Buck looked at the clipboard. “Ten times one way, ten times the other.”

Eddie nodded and slowly started to swing his arm in a clockwise motion, grimacing as the movement tugged at the still healing muscles of his shoulders. When he was first discharged, he’d been put on the same heavy duty painkillers as Buck had been after his leg. But now he was basically on extra strength Tylenol, good enough to dull the edges of the pain, but not enough to smother it entirely.

After watching him for a bit, Buck looked over to the trio at the table.

“Have you heard from Maddie recently?” he asked.

Danny made a face.

“Yeah, but not really,” was his answer. “It was the same as the last time I spoke to her, all, ‘no, Dan, I’m fine, don’t worry about it.’ I dropped in on them after work the other day, or tried to. Chimney said she was taking a nap.”

Around the time that Eddie was shot, Maddie suddenly quit her job at dispatch. There’d been no warning, at least not any that Buck had seen at the time. She had yet to give an actual reason, no matter how many times he and Danny probed her about it. And looking back, she’d been growing quieter and more distant for a while.

Buck chewed on his lip as he fretted. He knew Chimney, he trusted Chimney. But still.

“You don’t think…?”

“Don’t think what?” Danny glanced up at him and then his lips drew into a flat line. “Oh, no. I mean, she killed the last one. Chimney knows she killed the last one. He’s not that dumb.”

“High praise,” Austin dryly commented. “He hasn’t said anything to Hen or Cap that I’ve heard. I’m not even sure they know she quit her job.”

“They haven’t said anything to me.” Buck frowned as he thought. “They mostly ask about how we’re doing.”

He gestured between himself and Eddie.

“Aw, they care,” Eddie quipped. The strain in his voice took away some of the humor. “I’ve told them we’re fine, I don’t know why they keep asking you.”

“’Cause you might be lying.”

It was a comment that earned Buck a glare.

“Straight flush!” Chris suddenly shouted. He had his cards splayed out on the table in front of him. “I win!”

“Straight- Hold on.” Eddie straightened up. “Danny, are you teaching my son how to play poker?”

“Valuable life skills, Diaz,” Danny drawled as he tossed his own cards onto the table and pushed some pretzels towards Chris’ grabby little hands. Buck vividly remembered the Christmas party at the station, when his brother used the same excuse before hustling a bunch of orphans at a game of pool. “’Sides, I’m not the one teaching him.”

“Yeah! Uncle Austin is!”

The expression that crossed Danny’s face at the phrase, the expression that he wore as he looked at Austin, was so soft that it made Buck feel like a voyeur. It was not an unfamiliar expression, Buck had seen it on his brother’s face often enough. He could say something, get some revenge for all the times Danny had embarrassed him in the past, but it just felt wrong.

He’d do it later, when it was just the two of them.

“Dan can’t teach anyone,” Austin said. He gathered up the cards and started shuffling them. “He doesn’t know half the rules.”

Danny stuck his nose in the air with a haughty sniff.

“The eye candy doesn’t need to know the rules,” he said.

“C’mon.” Buck tapped Eddie’s (uninjured) shoulder. “You’ve got a couple more exercises, then I’ll get you more meds and an ice pack.”

His boyfriend gave him his best hangdog look, but went back to work all the same.

Then

“That’s the exact same thing the last lady said!” Daniel snarled.

He was having, perhaps, one of the worst nights of his life. Mere hours ago, he’d been at Station 133 and unable to do anything but watch as his baby brother was hurting. Buck had been miles away from him, then, held hostage by some fucked up kid with a grudge and a predisposition towards explosives. Buck had been miles away, pinned by the leg beneath a god damn ladder truck.

There was nothing Daniel could do but watch the spectacle with everyone else through the television screen as the little bastard shouted demands and Nash answered them. There was nothing he could do but stand there while Eddie and the others ran to Buck’s side and surrounded him, blocking him from view. There was nothing Daniel could do but swallow back the bile that crept up his throat as he watched – as he heard, the sound managing to reach the cameras hovering around the scene – his brother scream in pain while they tried to free him from the truck.

His crew behind him hadn’t said a damn thing the entire time, even as he carelessly blocked the TV from view with his body. There was only utter silence in that room, unbroken except by the ringing of Daniel’s phone – Maddie’s ringtone, again and again – and a news anchor’s constant commentary. Not that Daniel heard a single word that they said.

It wasn’t until Buck was free, it wasn’t until the entire crowd of on-lookers did what Daniel could not, and he was being carried off to an ambulance that Daniel tore himself away from the television. He stormed through the room, his coworkers parting like a sea before him, and headed for his car. Mehta didn’t say anything, and that was good. Daniel wasn’t certain he would have wasted the time killing the man if he tried to keep him from getting to Buck.

His phone rang again as he fumbled for his keys. It wasn’t Maddie’s ringtone, and it wasn’t Maddie’s voice when he answered.

“They’re taking him to Byrne Memorial,” Austin told him. Not even a greeting, just straight to the point.

“He’s allergic to naproxen,” Daniel said as he finally got the car door unlocked. He yanked it open and flung himself into the driver’s seat. “Eddie knows. Make sure he tells them.”

“I will.”

The journey from station to hospital flew by, in that Daniel sped through the streets of Los Angeles and dared any pig in a uniform to even try pulling him over. When he arrived, he burst into the building without a single care for anyone else. There wasn’t a single thought beyond the fact that his baby brother was hurting and he hadn’t been able to be there.

He wasn’t very kind as he asked after Buck, snapping at the person behind the desk and then nearly biting Eddie’s head off. He paced, agitated and impatient, as he was stuck waiting to hear something, anything. Eventually, everyone but Austin – who’d arrived after he did, because he had to take the engine back to their station – was giving him a wide berth. Even complete strangers were side-eying him from across the room, not that he noticed or cared.

Which brings us to-

“That’s the exact same thing the last lady said!” Daniel snarled.

“I’m sorry, sir.” The nurse held a clipboard to his chest like it would shield him from Daniel’s anger. His voice was grating, and his scrubs were an offensive shade of green. Or maybe that was just Daniel’s bad mood coloring everything. “But that’s all I have to tell you.”

“You didn’t tell me anything! What was the god damn point of-”

A hand to his shoulder, accompanied by a quiet, “Dan,” had him twisting his head around. Austin was right there beside him, as he had been almost from the moment he joined Daniel and them at the hospital. The only person who was willing to be near Daniel at the moment.

So of course Daniel snapped at him.

“What!?”

“There’s a couple vending machines down the hall there,” Austin said, jerking his head towards the doors across the room. “I’m gonna hit them up. Want anything?”

Daniel blinked at him. Out of the corner of his eye, he saw the nurse scurrying away.

“Uh, yeah, sure. Whatever.”

Austin simply nodded, gave his shoulder a squeeze, and then strode off in the direction of the vending machines. Daniel watched him go, thrown slightly by the discordance of that interaction. Then he shook his head and started pacing again to work out the agitated energy thrumming through him.

Or he intended to, anyway, except Maddie set down the styrofoam cup of bad hospital coffee that Chimney had brought her and got up from the uncomfortable looking chair she’d been camped in. She approached him slowly, cautiously, the way one might approach a stray dog that they didn’t trust not to bite.

“What did this one have to say?” she asked. “Before you ran him off, too.”

Daniel felt his lip curl.

“The same thing as the last one,” he bit out. “Fuckin’ nothing.”

“Try to think of it as no news is good news,” she suggested, and rudely refused to burst into flames when he glared at her. “It means nothing’s gone wrong.”

“Everything’s gone wrong, Maddie! We- We don’t even know if they’re chopping his leg off right now!”

“They’d come out and ask permission if it got that far.”

She said it so matter-of-factly that it raised Daniel’s hackles. He opened his mouth, something cruel climbing its way up his throat, only to be waylaid by his phone ringing. There was a brief glimmer of relief on Maddie’s face when he dug it out, already knowing who it was.

“Alma, I don’t-”

“I’ll be there around two, maybe three,” she stated. Something was being zipped up in the background. “Unless there’s a delay.”

“You- What?” Daniel ignored his sister’s unspoken questions as he scowled in confusion. “What the hell does that mean?”

“Exactly what it sounded like. Iggy! ¿Dónde está mi cargador?”

Iggy – Ignacio – was the newest boyfriend. Daniel was hearing about him even before Alma started dating him. Daniel’s opinion of the man was yet to be decided, but Alma’s smile was audible when she talked about Iggy and her daughters liked him, so he was already leagues ahead of the previous deadbeats she’d dated in the past.

Hopefully he wouldn’t need to take a bat to the knee of this one.

“¡Aquí!” was Iggy’s distant answer. He sounded like a dweeb.

“Okay.” Daniel wasn’t sure why he was surprised by Alma’s announcement. She might’ve been only three years older than him, but she’d bullied her way into the role of honorary aunt for both him and Buck almost the moment she laid eyes on them. It helped that she’d seemed so much older than him then. “I’ll, uh. Text me when you land and I’ll be there.”

Maybe he’d even have actual news about Buck by then.

When Alma hung up, in a hurry to get everything packed and then get out the door, Daniel slid his phone back into his pocket. He intended to start pacing again, no more capable of standing still in that moment than Buck was his entire life. But the look on Maddie’s face, vaguely anxious, vaguely annoyed, made him pause.

“What is it?” he hesitantly asked.

“I called Mom-”

“Jesus fuck, Maddie!”

Several people twisted their heads around to glare at Daniel. He just ignored them all.

“She didn’t answer,” she said. “But I left a message.”

“Of course she didn’t answer, she puts it on Do Not Disturb at night.” Daniel shook his head at his sister, doing his best to keep his temper in check. “Why the hell did you call her?”

“It’s Mom. Why shouldn’t I have?”

“Because fuck her, that’s why.”

Maddie scowled at him and then stomped away, back to her seat and her bad coffee. Daniel glared at her back the entire time, up until Austin announced his return with a,

“Dan.”

“What?” Daniel snapped, turning around.

“Here.”

Austin, entirely unperturbed about being snapped at, was holding out a couple packets of Skittles. There was nothing in his other hand, no sign he got himself anything. Daniel stared at him as something unfurled in his chest that he couldn’t quite describe. Something with wings that spread out and filled up the empty spaces of his rib cage. He took the candy with a half-whispered thanks, mind too busy tumbling ass over tit while a single phrase repeated itself over and over and over again:

Oh, shit.

Now

After Eddie got shot, after he took some time to help Eddie and Chris settle in, Buck transferred back to the 118 as cover. Bobby was similarly out, having been shot by the same sniper that shot Eddie, except through turnouts and with a much smaller caliber, so there were several weeks in a row where he could enjoy working with his old friends again without that worry.

But Bobby wasn’t gone forever. He came back, on light duty but still capable of captaining, and things… Were fine, actually. Awkward at first, certainly, especially that first day. An awkwardness that the entire crew felt. But he and Bobby had talked, repeatedly, about where the mistakes were made, what the mistakes were.

So it was fine, actually. Better than his shifts before he transferred. Even when Eddie healed and came back, Buck still didn’t leave. Something that Danny, and Jon, and Flowers, and Nola, and all the rest continued to give him shit for. But his spot at the 133 didn’t stay empty for very long.

“So.” Buck popped a fry into his mouth. “How’s it going?”

Albert, sitting across from him, let out a groan and let his head fall onto the table top. He just barely avoided his own tray of food.

“Was that a good groan or a bad groan? I couldn’t tell.”

He’d invited the youngest Han out to eat while Chris was at school and Eddie helped Pepa with something. It had been a while since they actually hung out, between Albert’s accident and Eddie’s injury and everything in between. The last time was when Albert graduated the academy to be a firefighter, but that had been more of a group thing.

“A bad groan,” Albert said into the table before sitting up. “I’ve been there for weeks, now, and I still haven’t been out on a single call.”

Buck winced.

“Not a single one?”

“No. All I do is roll hoses, make coffee, clean. That isn’t being a firefighter.”

“It kinda is, actually.” Buck shrugged at Albert’s scowl. “We don’t have janitors, Al, and someone’s gotta make sure the station’s not a pigsty.”

“But does it always have to be me?”

Buck didn’t really have an answer to that. He did remember, however, how the 133’s last probie quit in their second month. He wondered if he’d received similar treatment to Albert.

“Okay, but what about Danny?” he asked. “He’s not being a dick to you, is he?”

Buck didn’t think he would be, but still.

“No. He’s actually teaching me stuff,” Albert said. “Everyone else is weird about him, though.”

“Weird?” Buck blinked and jerked his head back. “Weird how?”

“The first day, one of the paramedics warned me to be careful around him.”

“What!?”

“They all think he’s a psychopath.”

Buck’s first instinct was to immediately call up the 133’s paramedics – he had all of their numbers, at least the ones on Danny and Albert’s shift – and start yelling at them. Except he also remembered how Danny stormed into the station to yell at Bobby. He remembered how Danny threw the St. Paul fire, how he threw Bobby’s children, in Bobby’s face and didn’t seem to feel an ounce of guilt about it.

Someone was still getting yelled at, but he could see where they were coming from.

“One of them said he made their last probie cry,” Albert continued. “And they got this weird look on their face when I called him Danny? But they also relaxed, so I don’t know. Everyone’s weird there.”

“You’re not wrong,” Buck commented as he grabbed another fry. And then, as a thought struck him, he felt a smile spread across his face. “Hey, Al? How’d you feel about doing me a favor that’ll also annoy Danny?”

Albert, for his part, seemed intrigued.

Chapter 2

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Returning to the 118 was weird in that it wasn’t weird. Not beyond those first couple shifts after Bobby came back from leave. Buck basically slotted back into his spot, so easily that it was almost as if he had never left. There were a couple new faces – for example, there was Ravi, the probie that jinxed everyone a few months ago – and some different equipment, but things were mostly the same. Hell, he even had his old locker again. The biggest, most noticeable difference was that his tag on his gear cage now read E. Buckley, just like his one at the 133 and just like what was on his turnout coat ever since he switched stations the first time.

Nola and the others from the 133 were giving him shit about leaving them almost constantly. In good fun, of course. They had a couple group chats, one for work related stuff and one that was far less professional and conspicuously lacking in captains, and Buck was still in the latter. Often now, he’d ask for context about something and get a variation of “you’d know if you hadn’t abandoned us!” before getting an answer. And he’d dragged Eddie along with him a couple times when they invited him out with everyone else.

That they simply didn’t drop Buck the moment he transferred back was a nice feeling. Especially in comparison to how it’d been after his leg and the tsunami, when it felt like a struggle to keep from being left behind by everyone but Eddie and Danny and Maddie. If only everything else was as nice and easy.

“What’s his problem today?” Hen asked him near the start of one shift.

Buck glanced over to Eddie, who was lining up a shot on the pool table. That morning was looking to be a slow one, and they were playing a game of pool. Buck’s boyfriend wasn’t quite scowling at the cue ball, but he was much the same as he’d been all day: wearing an expression just this side of stormy, more reticent and taciturn than he normally was.

Honestly, Buck was more surprised that it took this long for someone to break and ask.

“His parents called again last night,” he answered.

Hen grimaced in sympathy.

When Eddie had kissed Buck at his shield ceremony at the end of his probationary year, in front of all their coworkers and friends and family, Ramon and Helena Diaz had not been best pleased. Buck didn’t quite remember the words that they shouted, busy as he was trying to taste Eddie’s tonsils at the time, but he knew they weren’t friendly. He was told, afterwards, that Danny and Hen dragged a raging Ramon out of the station with Helena and her daughters not far behind.

It was the last time Eddie spoke to either of them for two whole years, and it was only marginally better when it came to Adriana and Sophia. Buck knew how much it wore away at his boyfriend, even as neither of them were at all surprised that it turned out the way it did. There’d been a reason, after all, that Eddie clung to the closet for so very long, a reason why Buck had never tried dragging him out.

But at some point shortly after the shooting, a couple days after Eddie came home, either Pepa or Isabel finally told them what happened. Or so Buck assumed, because that was when Eddie’s phone rang with Helena’s number. They’d both stared at it, and then Eddie – groggy with the pain killers – slowly reached out and answered it.

“How is that going?” Bobby asked, sitting at the table right behind Buck and Hen.

“It’s…” Buck’s sigh was overshadowed by the clacking of the pool balls as they ricocheted of each other. “I don’t know. What I hear sounds fine, but I’m not exactly racing to talk to them, so I can’t really say.”

“They’re fine until I remind them that Buck exists.” Eddie silently cursed as he flubbed his next shot and straightened up. “I’m right here, you know. Even if Buck was capable of being qu-"

"Don't," Bobby interrupted.

Eddie rolled his eyes.

"Not loud," he said. "I’d still be able to hear you talking about me.”

“Hey!” Buck glowered. “I can be not loud.”

The blatant disbelief from the three of them was simply rude and uncalled for.

“No you can't,” Chimney stated as he finally joined them. Buck frowned at him, wondering why he got in later than everyone else. “What are we talking about?”

“My parents,” Eddie answered. He stepped out of the way so Hen could take her turn. “They act like nothing’s changed since they cut me off. But then I mention Buck, and suddenly…”

“They only ever tolerated me,” Buck told the others, and then laughed when Hen’s shot failed to sink any of the balls. “Ha! Loser.”

“Don’t make me hit you,” Hen threatened with a smile, jabbing at his stomach with her pool stick.

“I’m sure they’ll come around,” Bobby said.

“Yeah, sure.” Eddie scoffed and handed the captain the pool stick. “I just have to get shot again.”

“My mother wasn’t exactly the most… Accepting,” Hen stated as they watched Bobby take and fail his own shot. “She didn’t even show up on our wedding day. But now she’s watching her grandson and eating dinner with my wife.”

Hen’s mother, Toni, had shown up earlier in the year. Buck didn’t have all the details, since he was at the 133 at the time, but what he had gathered – from what Austin overheard, and what Buck and Eddie got from Hen herself – Toni had simply shown up one day. Hen went from annoyed about it to moving her mother into her home in about the span of a week.

So pretty much how Chimney reacted to Albert’s unexpected arrival in his life.

“If we’re taking a vote, I’m voting that you don’t get shot again.” Buck bent over to line up his own shot. He might have dipped a bit farther than he truly needed to, and smirked when he caught his boyfriend looking. “We’ve been there, we’ve done that, and I don’t really want to- Hey!”

He shot up and rubbed at his stinging butt cheek, which had just been smacked by a pool stick.

“What was that for?” he asked with a pout.

“You know damn well what,” Hen told him. “Now take your shot like a normal person and not a model for Playgirl.”

“This is homophobic,” Buck complained.

Her scoff expressed perfectly well what she thought about that.

Bobby’s phone began to buzz where he had it sitting on one of the small high tops that surrounded the pool table. He handed the pool stick back to Eddie and went to pick it up, frowning at whatever he saw.

“Earthquake warning,” he said, and then looked to them. “You guys feel anything?”

Everyone shook their heads. Buck’s phone began to buzz and, when he pulled it out, showed a-

“M- Mine’s a tsunami warning,” he read aloud, the words trembling a little.

Then it was Eddie’s turn, who checked his own phone and found-

“Mudslide warning,” he said.

“It’s like our greatest hits,” Chimney quipped.

“So why isn’t the bell going off?” Buck asked.

All these warnings, the bell should have been going crazy. Hell, even the smallest of earthquakes sent the city into a frenzy. Yet, there was nothing but silence.

Silence and Hen frowning down at her own phone.

“Don’t tell us,” Chimney said. “The bombs are landing in five minutes.”

“No.” Hen shook her head and then held her phone out so they could all see her screen. “Evidently, I’m dialing Nine-One-One.”

Oh. That wasn’t good.

“I’m not understanding,” Danny admitted as he lounged in a chair and stared at the television. The news was reporting on a call that the 118 went on earlier, their last one before getting off shift. “They all just kept going? Even though something was clearly wrong?”

“Yup.” Buck flopped onto the couch beside Eddie, having done his part in Chris’ bedtime routine. “Just a whole line of lemmings.”

It turned out that it wasn’t just the cell phones going bonkers. He and Eddie had to rescue a woman from her car when she followed her GPS’ directions off the road and into the water. They got there just in time for another car, part of a long line of cars, to go careening off the road as well and slam into hers.

It was a mess of angry drivers shouting and honking their horns, apparently incapable of thinking for themselves. Worse, Buck and Eddie had to deal with all that while soaked through. Hell was wet socks in waterlogged boots while a soccer mom berated them for not magically clearing the way for her.

“Y’all made fun of me for not trusting that shit,” Eddie pointed out, turning and rearranging himself where he sat so he was leaning against Buck. “And now Skynet’s trying to drown everyone.”

“Your technophobia is very cute,” Buck told him. He pecked a kiss on Eddie’s temple and got a beaming smile in return. “How bad was it over there?”

“Not bad, actually.” Danny shrugged. “Since half the city was being directed into being your guys’ problem. Heard dispatch was a mess, though.”

“I don’t even want to imagine,” Eddie said. “Hen’s phone kept dialing Nine-One-One. She ended up just turning it off.”

“Yeah, her and the other half of the city. Josh said it was constant. Something about the cellphone providers.”

Buck scrunched up his face as he glanced back to the news on the TV, still covering the navigation mishap. The company behind that particular GPS, GoGo Anywhere – a name that made Buck think of boots every time he heard it – was blaming a bug in their new software update. But it was weird that it happened on the same day their phones went insane, right?

“Josh had lunch with Maddie again today,” Danny said.

Buck nearly sent Eddie toppling off the couch as he sat up. Ever since Maddie up and quit her job at Metro Dispatch, Buck and Danny had both been worried about her. She insisted, time and time again, that she was fine, but neither of them were convinced. Especially since she always looked and sounded so tired whenever they managed to talk to her.

“What did he say?” Buck asked while ignored his boyfriend’s grumpy scowl.

“Same thing as always.” Danny shrugged. “Looked tired, still. Wouldn’t tell him much, just kept saying everything was okay when he asked.”

“Chimney was late this morning. He didn’t say why.”

“Not much we can do if she doesn’t tell us anything.”

Danny’s words were shadowed by what was no doubt years of experience with this very thing. He’d known about Doug’s true nature long before Maddie came running to Los Angeles, Buck knew. He’d tried to step in, tried to get Maddie out, but was thoroughly rebuffed and was stuck with the awareness that their sister needed help and the inability to provide it.

Buck wondered if how he felt now was anything like how Danny felt then.

“I should be getting back to the apartment,” Danny said.

He heaved himself out of his chair with a sigh, reluctance dragging each and every movement, and went to where he’d kicked his shoes off by the door. Buck reached out, an aborted attempted to hold him back.

“Or- Or you could-”

He cut himself off. This wasn’t his house, even if it had been weeks since he last spent the night in his bed at the apartment. He couldn’t just invite someone to sleep over, even if that person was Danny.

“None of us have to work tomorrow,” Eddie said, picking up the thread. “You can just stay the night.”

Danny wanted to, Buck could tell. He just needed a push.

“Chris badgered me into making him pancakes in the morning,” he lied. “You can have actual breakfast, not just cheap cereal.”

His brother’s eyes darted between him and Eddie. Danny didn’t believe his words one bit, but it also gave him the excuse to do what Buck knew he already wanted to do. It was evident in the way, after a moment, he nodded a bit too eagerly and said,

“Yeah, okay, squirt. Sure.”

Danny went back to the chair and sank down into, more relaxed than he had been minutes prior. When Buck and Eddie finally went to bed, because they needed to be up to take Chris to school, they got the spare bedding out and set up the couch for Danny, along with a spare pair of sweats to sleep in. When they turned out the lights in the living room, Danny was stretched out with his bare feet hanging off the end of the couch.

“You’re worried about him, too,” Eddie stated simply once the bedroom door was closed behind them.

“Yeah,” Buck admitted with a shrug. “I don’t think he does well alone. And I’ve left him alone a lot.”

“We can start doing surprise sleep overs at the apartment,” his boyfriend said. “Chris won’t complain about it.”

“I bet we could convince him to watch Chris overnight,” Buck joked with a sly smile and wagging eyebrows.

Eddie just laughed and shoved him towards the bed.

Then

“What are you doin’ tonight?” Mendoza suddenly asked, a couple hours before their shift ended.

It was the middle of Daniel’s third month with the 133, the fourth since he and Evan moved to Los Angeles, and the sixth since he flew down to Peru to see his brother when said brother didn’t come home for two fucking years. There’d been an adjustment period, both with the new coworkers and the new environment. Los Angeles was nothing like El Paso, the very atmosphere was different. Everything from their neighbors at their new apartment building – the elevator hadn’t broken down even once in their time living there, which was a longer period of functionality than the elevator at their old building ever managed – to the calls that Daniel was being sent out on.

Much like when they left Hershey, Daniel didn’t really keep in touch with anyone he left behind. Alma and her daughters were the sole exception; not even Daniel’s old coworkers made the cut. That was fine, though, because El Paso – and certain people who lived in El Paso – was best left in the rear view mirror.

Evan was thriving, or at least he seemed to be thriving. He was spending a lot of his days at the beach, and was working nights tending at some bar. He’d also seemed to have turned into a slut during that Neverending Gap Year Road Trip, not that Daniel could – or even would – judge him for it.

He was just happy to be coming home to dirty dishes in the sink, wet towels on the bathroom floor, and obnoxious snoring.

“What am I doing tonight?” Daniel repeated.

He was caught by such surprise that Mendoza managed to sneak in a point in their game of foosball. Not that Mendoza needed him to be distracted to accomplish that.

“Yeah.” Mendoza grabbed the ball from the side cabinet it rolled out onto and sent it back through the serving hole. “Do you have any plans?”

Considering Evan worked tonight, Daniel’s plan was to simply eat reheated leftovers and then find something on TV to fill the silence. But that sounded pathetic, even to Daniel’s own ears, so he just shrugged.

“Not really,” he said. “Why?”

“Cool.” Mendoza flicked his wrist, sending the little soccer ball across the table. “’Cause you do now.”

Daniel was not proud of how easy it was for Mendoza to talk him into going out after shift. Honestly, he was just thankful to have an excuse not to go to an empty and silent apartment just yet. Which was how he found himself stepping into a hook-and-ladder joint downtown, thankful it wasn’t the bar his brother worked at.

He knew damn well what kind of menace Evan could be.

“Finally!” Mendoza’s voice carried over the din of the crowd. Daniel spotted him at a table on the other side from the entrance, arm in the air to catch his attention. “Over here!”

Daniel sighed but went where he was beckoned. Mendoza wasn’t alone at his table, there were a couple guys with him that Daniel didn’t recognize. One looked bored, with a handsome face that might as well have been a mask. The other was openly looking him over with a smirk.

“What took you so long?” Mendoza demanded, kicking out the chair across from him, the one beside the bored looking guy. “We left at the same time.”

“Wasn’t sure if I wanted to come,” Daniel said as he dropped into the offered seat.

“Why wouldn’t you? I’m awesome,” the paramedic said. “Guys, this is Dan, he’s the new driver-engineer at my station. He’s from Texas.”

“Howdy, y’all,” Daniel drawled.

“Casey, One-One-Five,” said the one beside Mendoza. He held out his hand for Daniel to shake, his hold lingering a bit longer than necessary. “Nice to meet you.”

“Stop that.” Mendoza reached up to smack at Casey’s hand. “You have a boyfriend.”

“So?” Casey simply shrugged.

“That’s Austin, driver-engineer at the One Eighteen,” Mendoza continued, indicating the one beside Daniel with his beer bottle. “Don’t mind him, he doesn’t talk much.”

“Maybe he just doesn’t want to talk to you,” Daniel quipped. Austin let out a soft snort, which had Mendoza and Casey’s eyebrows raising for whatever reason. “How did the two of you get stuck with that one?”

He pointed at Mendoza, who scoffed in offense.

“Same class at the academy,” Casey answered. “Jon here’s a bit clingy.”

“Clingy!?” was Mendoza’s offended response.

The pair started sniping at each other. Daniel rolled his eyes and flagged down a server so he could order a drink. It was probably the only way to deal with Mendoza tonight.

“Are they always like this?” he asked, once the server came and went with his order.

“Yup,” was Austin’s quiet answer.

“Better than being home alone, I guess.”

“Is it really?’

It was a response that had Daniel snorting out a laugh.

“Not like I had anything better to do,” he said.

“Jon would’ve bugged you until you said yes.” Austin said it with the confidence of someone who’d fallen prey to that tactic often. “Casey just doesn’t have anything better. His boyfriend’s working and his minority support group’s stopped meeting.”

“Minority support group?”

“It’s what I called it.” was said with a shrug. “I was never invited. He said I didn’t count.”

Daniel frowned and wondered what that meant.

In the end, he was glad he didn’t just blow off Mendoza. Drinking at a noisy bar with other people was far less pathetic than drinking alone in the silence of his apartment. And there was a new audience to tell tales to, though they weren’t awed by them as Evan and the other one used to be.

Austin was cool, too. Even if he spent most of the time listening instead of talking.

Now

Daniel set the beer bottles down on the table and tossed the lids into the trash.

“And what does Nash think about you coming over again?” he asked as he took a seat.

It was a question that earned him an eye roll from Athena. She’d showed up at his doorstep, as she had done a handful of times now, and he simply let her in before grabbing them both something to drink. He’d finished dinner – a tupperware container of food that Buck managed to sneak into his car when he left Eddie’s that afternoon – and had just finished rinsing the lone dish he’d used.

“Bobby can think whatever,” Athena declared. “He’s hovering. Usually I don’t mind it, but I’m already dreading tomorrow.”

“What’s tomorrow?” Daniel asked.

“Hudson’s trial.”

“Oh.”

Jeffery Hudson, also called the Realtor Rapist. He got the jump on Athena a couple years ago during a minor blackout. Everyone was forced to listen to the sounds of the struggle until they switched channels. Daniel still remembered the stricken expression Buck wore at the time.

“We have some of his victims going on the stand,” Athena said before taking a swig of her drink. She made a face at the taste and then scowled at Daniel, who simply shrugged. If she had problems with his beer, she could bring her own next time. “It should be open and shut, I don’t know why it wouldn’t be.”

“Do you think his fan club’ll be there?”

“Probably,” Athena muttered darkly.

The fan club in question was a bunch of women who, for whatever reason, were fawning over the bastard. They had a whole “Free Jeffery Hudson” group on Facebook that Daniel could barely look at without losing his temper.

“Buncha freaks,” he stated, and got a nod in agreement. “Would’ve been easier if that shot had killed him.”

“It was dark,” she said. “I didn’t get the chance to aim.”

“Oh, I don’t know. You shot his dick off,” Daniel reminded her. “I think you aim was pretty good.”

Athena’s smirk was nothing but vindictive glee.

Chapter End Notes

Casey of the 115 isn't an OC, by the way.

Chapter 3

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

This was not the supermarket Eddie usually went to. He went to the one beside Park La Brea or the store just up the street from the station. Normally, he did his shopping in the morning; if a shift wasn’t too exhausting, he stopped on the way home because he was already out. There was less of a crowd at those hours, less people to weave around and wait to get out of his way.

Buck, however, insisted they stop at a Whole Food’s not far from the apartment. Unfortunately, “not far from the apartment” meant that the Whole Food’s was smack dab in the middle of downtown Los Angeles. In the middle of the afternoon.

It was crowded.

“Buck, I love you,” Eddie said as he followed along his boyfriend with the cart. Chris, whom they’d picked up from school before heading here, was ambling just ahead. “But why are we at this store?”

“Because it sells the brand of apple butter Danny likes. I mean, he says he doesn’t care, but I can tell,” Buck answered, as he reached around someone to snag a jar of something that was distinctly not apple butter. “And the only other places that sell Lebanon bologna are across the city.”

“What’s Lebanon bologna?” Chris asked, intently pronouncing each word. “Is it like regular bologna?”

“It’s, uh.” Buck scrunched up his face in thought. “Fermented beef sausage-”

“Gross.” Chris drew out the word. “I don’t think I like it.”

“You’ve never even tried it.”

“I don’t need to,” was the haughty response.

Buck let out a loud, disgusting sounding snort of laughter that had a lady side-eye him before scurrying away.

There were far too many people in this store, Eddie thought. He shuffled aside as yet another person went by, feeling sweat trickle down his neck from the heat of so many bodies in one place. He really, really wished Buck would hurry up and decide on something to buy here, so they could get the apple butter and the bologna and the whatever else and then get the hell out of here.

(Did crowds always bother him this much?)

“Are we even in the aisle for apple butter?” Eddie asked, grimacing when the words came out a bit sharper than he meant for them to be.

Buck cut a glance his way, frowning.

“Yeah, it’s right down there,” he said, flapping a hand towards the other end of the aisle.

He made no move to head there, however, and Eddie was left to sigh and hunch over the handle of the cart.

“Why are we getting the bologna?” Chris asked. “That’s not what we’re eating for dinner, is it?”

Eddie’s kid pushed so much judgment into those words. It was honestly impressive.

“Nah. Danny likes putting it on sandwiches,” Buck told him. “With mayo, barbecue chips, and cheese. And potato bread, which we also need to get.”

Outright horror crossed Chris’ face, which Buck thankfully did not see. The boy turned to his father, clearly hoping that Buck was simply lying to him for fun, and despair joined the horror when Eddie silently nodded. He’d seen Danny eat those sandwiches before, back when they were all living in the apartment together during lockdown. The man even had boxes of a specific brand of chips delivered to him straight from Pennsylvania just for those sandwiches.

Maddie had Lebanon bologna in their fridge, too, according to Chimney. Apparently, it was a Pennsylvania thing. Apparently, Danny had been pretty bummed about not being able to find any in El Paso and then ecstatic when Buck said he found it at a store while out and about one day.

Eddie was simply thankful that packages of it hadn’t made its way into his own fridge. Yet.

The store was still too crowded. Maybe more crowded than it had been before, Eddie wasn’t sure. It certainly felt like there was a mob of people pressing in on him. Especially in this aisle. Why were so many people in this one particular aisle?

“Where do they sell this awful bologna?” Eddie asked, rubbing at his face. “I’ll go get it.”

“Oh.” Buck sent him a sunshine smile. “It’s in the deli aisle, with all the other bologna.”

“Okay. I’ll go-”

There was a loud shattering noise from just beside Eddie. Bits of red sauce splattered everywhere, and bits of glass scattered along the floor. Buck and Chris both jumped where they stood, twisting around to spot the source of the noise.

“Oh, hey, are you okay?” Buck asked someone, Eddie didn’t know who.

He tugged at the collar of his shirt, because he suddenly couldn’t breathe.

“I’m fine,” someone, some woman, said. “I’m so sorry.”

His chest. That was feeling tight, and painful.

“Hey, no, don’t worry about it,” Buck told the woman. “We’ll get an employee or someone who can clean this up.”

Eddie was not getting enough air, and his chest felt like it was being stabbed, and nothing around him was feeling steady.

“Dad?” Chris sounded worried. Why was Chris worried?

Oh.

Why was Eddie on the ground?

“Well, it wasn’t a heart attack,” the cardiologist, a Dr. Salazar, explained.

Eddie was laying in the hospital bed, ECG leads stuck to his bare chest. Buck spent the time before the doctor’s return pacing the small room in fretful worry while Chris sat in the provided loveseat, just as anxious. An ambulance had brought Eddie to the ER, with Buck following behind in the jeep with Chris, who then sent him up here. There’d been tests, and then there’d been waiting.

And now they were being given an answer that made no sense to Eddie, even as Buck relaxed in clear relief.

“Are you sure?” Eddie asked her.

“You sound disappointed,” Dr. Salazar replied.

“No. I’m just…. I’m just surprised,” he said. “It sure felt like one.”

Not that he had anything to compare it to, to be fair.

“Panic attacks can often present as heart attacks.”

Panic attack? No, that made even less sense.

“That is not what this was,” he insisted. “It’s not possible. I- I don’t panic.”

Buck did not even attempt to muffle his scoff, nor hide the way he rolled his eyes. Eddie glowered at him.

“Because you’re a guy?” The doctor did not sound impressed with him.

“And a firefighter,” Chris chimed in, as proud of his father as he ever was.

“We both are,” Buck added, frowning at Eddie.

“Now that’s a stressful job,” Dr. Salazar commented.

“But this didn’t happen on the job,” Eddie pointed out.

It happened at a supermarket of all places.

“Panic attacks aren’t rational, Mr. Diaz,” she said. “But they can be an indicator of some underlying anxiety. Have you experienced any lifestyle changes lately? Any new stressors?”

“No.” Eddie shook his head. “Nothing. It’s been great.”

“Seriously, Eds?” Buck snapped, and then turned to the doctor to tell her that, “He was one of the firefighters that got shot a few months ago.”

“The sniper,” the doctor said.

“Yeah.”

“But he’s dead and I’m not,” Eddie said. “I don’t even think about him anymore.”

“The makes one of us,” Buck grumbled beneath his breath, just loud enough to hear.

“That could be called repression,” Dr. Salazar pointed out.

“Or just moving forward,” Eddie argued.

“Maybe.” But the doctor did not sound like she agreed. “But I would like to give you a delightful and informative pamphlet outlining what to do if you start experiencing symptoms again.”

She took one out of the line-up of pamphlets along the counter and held it out to Eddie. Eddie simply stared at it.

“I won’t be needing that,” he told her.

Buck rolled his eyes, again, and took it from her. He immediately started perusing it, his phone appearing in his other hand.

Eddie wasn’t stuck in the hospital for much longer after that. The doctor left, after Buck grilled her with unnecessary questions, and then she went to get the paper work arranged. The drive home, after Eddie was once again fully dressed and free, was spent with Buck updating Danny over the phone. The other Buckley had been blowing up Buck’s phone the entire time they were at the hospital, Eddie knew.

They got something from a drive through along the way, since their plans for dinner with Danny were interrupted. They ate in the dining room when they got home, and then Chris ran off to his room and Eddie moved to the couch. He was fully intending to relax, exhausted as he was by a hospital trip and a fake heart attack.

Except Buck, who was once again reading though that damn pamphlet, started talking.

“Video or in person?” he asked, apropos of nothing.

“What?” Eddie asked a bit dumbly.

“Therapists,” Buck said. “Do you want an in-person one or over video?”

“I- What?” Eddie repeated, and then shook his head. “I’m not going to a therapist, Buck.”

“Why not?” the other man demanded. “I saw one. Danny still sees his.”

“Because I don’t need a therapist.”

“You had a panic attack so bad you thought you had a heart attack,” Buck said, biting out the last couple of words.

“I don’t panic,” Eddie insisted, managing to keep his voice down only by reminding himself that Chris was just down the hall.

“That-” Buck cut himself off with a glance towards Chris’ room. When he spoke again, the words were much quieter, but also much sharper. “That is such bullshit, Eddie. All you do is panic!”

“That’s not-”

“Shannon.”

The name was hurled like a brick through a window. Eddie ground his teeth, doing his best to keep his temper in check, and horribly reminded of the arguments he’d had with Shannon before she went running.

“Can we not do this right now?” he managed to get out without snapping or shouting. “Our kid is right down the hall.”

“I know.” Buck, however, did snap. But Eddie also saw the fight seep out of him. “Fine. But I’m not letting this go.”

Unfortunately, Eddie was well aware of that.

“It was a what attack?” Daniel asked, tossing down the rag he’d been using to wipe off his hands.

He’d just finished doing the weekly maintenance on the trucks with Flowers, everything from kicking the rims to opening up the ladies’ guts. They’d dragged Albert into helping, though his role was mostly just fetching tools and writing stuff down for them while their hands were busy and grease covered. Now the probie was getting started on washing the engine, hauling buckets of sudsy water across the bay with sponges and washcloths.

“A ransomware attack,” Buck repeated. “Or that’s what Hen said that Karen said that one of her friends said.”

There was a moment of silence.

“That made sense, right?”

“Yeah, squirt, it made sense,” Daniel assured him with a laugh. “But how do they know this?”

“Well, the cartoon head on the LAX computers saying ‘give us money’ was a bit of a hint.” The sarcasm in his brother’s voice had Daniel rolling his eyes. “But Karen’s friend said this isn’t the first. One in Texas last week, Michigan the week before. According to Bobby and Hen, whoever it is demanded twenty-five million dollars.”

“They’ll probably get it, too. LAX?” He let out a long, low whistle. “They’ll end up losing more than that if they can’t land any planes.”

“Yeah, the entire system was down when we got there. Stressed the air traffic controllers out so much they were dropping with heart attacks and panic attacks left and right.”

“Christ.” That was a good segue, though. “How’s Eddie?”

Buck’s disgusted scoff conveyed far more than any words ever could.

“That bad?”

“We had a fight last night.” Buck sounded tired. Immediately, Daniel started going over pros and cons, content in the certain knowledge of where Eddie’s will dictated Chris went. “He keeps insisting he doesn’t panic.”

Daniel laughed aloud at that, the sound bursting out of his throat unbidden.

“Right!? But I just know this is Ramon’s bullshit again,” Buck said, and then pitched his voice mockingly as he said, “Men don’t panic, men don’t do therapy, men don’t stay home to take care of their kids.”

The words had Daniel frowning, wondering if it was just Ramon’s bullshit. Dr. White might question his ability to empathize with people, but he didn’t need to stretch himself to put himself into the shoes of a kid taking care of their younger siblings. That was half the reason Eddie ended up at their apartment so often during those years in El Paso, because when he was there, he wasn’t expected to make up for Ramon’s absence.

“Men don’t do therapy?”

Eddie was healed by this point, a least physically. He could deal with being smacked once or twice or maybe a dozen times.

“Oh, he didn’t actually-” Buck sighed across the line. “That was how the fight started. I asked him how he wanted to do therapy, and he insisted he didn’t need a therapist.”

Oh, Daniel thought as something unpleasant uncoiled in his stomach. That might not be Ramon’s bullshit. Because he remembered how long it took Austin to argue him into going to therapy himself. But when he spoke next, it wasn’t to voice that thought.

“Says the guy who refused to go to therapy until the world shut down.” Though, the reason for that, which still made Daniel seethe even years later, was wildly different from Daniel’s and now Eddie’s stubbornness. “Maybe you could ask Austin how to bully him into it. Or Copeland, when you see her next.”

“Oh, um…”

That wasn’t suspicious at all.

“Buck?”

“So. Eddie got shot right before my session with Copeland, so I missed it. And then I kinda… Forgot? To reschedule it?”

Daniel reached up and rubbed at his forehead. Honestly, he wasn’t even surprised. Not just the ball getting dropped during the whole mess, because that was entirely understandable, but also the fact that Buck went entire months without remembering to reschedule. Or, more likely, he’d thought about it and then got distracted because Eddie or Chris needed something, or because of work, or because something caught his attention along the way.

“Danny?”

“Go do that now, squirt,” he instructed. “Austin’ll tell me if you don’t.”

“Seriously!?”

“Seriously. I’m gonna call him as soon as-”

The screeching of tires, the crunching of metal, the long drone of a car horn. All of that interrupted what Daniel was saying. He ran to the bay doors so he could see what happened, spotting the forming pile-up of cars at the intersection the station sat on. Buck would be worried, he knew, for the way he just hung up without explanation.

But he’d understand.

“Get Cap and the others!” Daniel barked at Albert, who’d started coming over to look, as well. When the probie didn’t move fast enough, he shouted, “Now!”

“Right! Right, yeah!” Albert nodded like a bobblehead as he sprinted off into the guts of the station. “Getting the others!”

The first thing Daniel did was to shoulder on his turnout coat and gloves. The second thing was to grab a Halligan from off the engine, as well as the spare medkit. The third thing was to grab a radio, which he thumbed on and spoke into as he jogged to where the cars were all smashed together.

“Dispatch, this is Firefighter Buckley, One Thirty-Three,” he said. “There’s a been a MVA at the intersection in front of Station One Thirty-three. Multiple vehicles.”

“Copy that, One Thirty-three,” replied the dispatcher. Daniel thought her name might’ve been Linda, but it wasn’t like he was friendly with every single one of his sister’s (old) coworkers. “You said right in front?”

“At least we don’t have to go far,” he joked, then glanced up when flashing lights caught his attention. “Traffic lights are going crazy.”

She responded, but Daniel was no longer paying attention. He’d gotten to the nearest car, a four door sedan. He peeked through the first window, back passenger, and spotted a car seat with a squalling, red-faced infant. The driver, the mom, was sitting up and rubbing at her head. Her face was red, too, but from the airbag that was slowly deflating.

When Daniel knocked on the window, she looked to him and tried to open the door, which refused to budge. The passenger side had another car blocking it, and while the back door might have been able to open, the car seat was right in the middle of the bench and therefore blocking any easy way to reach the woman without removing it first. But it was for exactly this reason that he brought a Halligan with him.

A bit of leverage, applied force, and some sweat had the driver’s door swinging open with the groan of stressed metal. The infant’s screeching got louder.

“You got here fast,” she commented. She tried to turn her head, but he stopped her. There was a small goose egg starting to form on her forehead.

“We’re right over there,” Daniel told her. He dug through his kit with one hand for the c-collar. “I’m gonna put this around your neck to keep it still.”

As he checked her over for any other injuries beside her head and face, he worked her through the basic concussion checks. Questions about the day and the month and the year and the date and the time, questions about what she remembered from before and during and after the crash, those kinds of things. All signs were pointing to her just having a headache and some whiplash later, to go along with the mild burns from the airbag, but he didn’t get her out of the car. Instead, after instructing her to stay where she was, he moved on to the still quite unhappy baby in the back.

She – or, Daniel was assuming the baby was a girl, with the pink ribbons adorning her outfit – was very clearly unhurt, though he still ran his hand along her to make sure. When that was done, he cut through the straps holding the seat in place and brought out the entire thing.

“What have we got?” Mendoza asked as he and Huff arrived, bags bouncing on their hips.

“Hit her head on the window,” Daniel relayed. He set the car seat on the ground and started rocking it with his foot, and the cries eventually eased into sniffles. “Passed all the concussion checks I gave her.”

“Alright, we’ll get her on a gurney and get her out of here.”

Daniel nodded, grabbed his kit and the Halligan, and moved to the next car.

The baby started crying again as he left.

Chapter End Notes

Pennsylvania White Boy Sandwich.

Chapter 4

Then

The sad fact was that Daniel knew why he was still letting himself be dragged out by Mendoza. He wasn’t the only one, they were occasionally joined by one of the others. Usually Nola, another paramedic, or the apparatus operator for the 133’s ladder, whose name was Florian but everyone called Flowers, much to his easy-going annoyance. But usually, it was just Daniel, Mendoza, and Mendoza’s friends.

He still disliked being alone in the apartment. A holdover from his childhood, when he was trapped in that clean room for weeks that became months. It was too silent, too still, too… Too lonely. So, it didn’t take much effort for Mendoza to coax him out for some drinks, at least on the nights that Evan was working. Unfortunately, one of those nights they ended up going to the bar that Evan was currently employed at, but it had thankfully been busy enough that neither side could embarrass the other too badly.

(It was absolutely intentional, too, because that wasn’t their usual bar. Because Daniel made the mistake of mentioning which bar his brother was working at and Mendoza was a bastard like that.)

Austin helped, though. The other driver-engineer was funny and sarcastic, once he got to talking. Hell, even when he was his usual silent self, Daniel would catch his eye and have to stifle his laughter when he saw the other man roll his at whatever Mendoza or Casey was saying. Half the time, more than half the time, Daniel would ignore the other two in favor of talking to Austin.

“You look like something’s bothering you,” Daniel remarked while he passed out the next round of drinks to everyone. “What’s up?”

“My mother,” was Austin’s answer, and Daniel nodded.

“I ran across the country with my baby brother to get him away from mine,” he stated, and Austin’s eyebrows rose by a quarter inch, which stood out when compared to his usual stoicism. “What’s up with yours?”

“Nothing worth fleeing the state over. Maybe.” Austin frowned for a moment, as if he really wasn’t sure. “She’s trying to set me up again.”

“Who is it this time?” Mendoza asked, tuning into their conversation. “Coworker? Friend’s nephew?”

“Someone’s granddaughter, I think.” Austin shrugged and took a swig of his drink. “She told me her name, but I’ve already forgot it.”

“She set you up a lot?” Daniel asked, leaning back in his seat until the front two legs were off the ground.

“He comes in complaining about it every other month.” Casey rolled his eyes. “He’s a prude, ignore him.”

“I’m not a prude,” Austin said, a bit sharply. “I’m just not interested in all that.”

“You don’t even try with any of them.”

“Because I’m not interested.”

“Have you told your mom you’re not interested?” Daniel asked. He shot Casey a glare, and Casey glowered back.

“Yup.” Austin’s answer was flat and tired. “Repeatedly. I’ve given up by this point. She and Dad just don’t get it, so they keep trying to set me up with someone because they think that surely this one will be the special one.”

“I don’t know why you’re complaining,” Casey said. “At least yours are open minded.”

“Last time it was…” Mendoza scrunched up his face as he tried to remember. “That weird twink, right?”

Austin scoffed but didn’t answer, and Daniel was suddenly very interested in the story. But the conversation moved on, at least enough for the other two to forget about it and drag Daniel into some argument. Not that he could figure out what the argument was about.

When the night ended, when they’d switched from beer to water for long enough that they weren’t at risk of becoming a cautionary tale, they started splitting off. Casey first, to beat his boyfriend home, and then Mendoza. It left Daniel and Austin at the table alone in comfortable silence that neither of them seemed in any hurry to break.

But Daniel’s curiosity, much like his brother’s, eventually got the best of him.

“Not interested?” he asked as he ran a finger along the edge of his glass of water.

“Not interested,” Austin echoed, a bit defensively. “Never have been.”

“In… Any of it? Boys? Girls? Both? Neither?”

“Nope.”

“Huh,” Daniel said, and then, “I could pretend to be your boyfriend.”

Austin stared at him for a moment.

“What?” he intoned.

“If it would get your parents off your back,” Daniel continued. “They’ll be happy that you have someone, and you won’t have to deal with them trying to set you up anymore.”

“Seriously?”

“Seriously. I’ve been in worse arrangements.”

Austin frowned at him, and Daniel couldn’t tell if it was in concern at his statement or if he was actually contemplating the offer. But then he shook his head and stood up.

“Nah,” he said. “I’ll go on the date, they’ll be disappointed, and I’ll be good for another couple months.”

“Fair enough.” Daniel stood up as well, pulling out his wallet to throw down a couple bills next to the ones Austin already contributed. “Offer’s still there though.”

“I’ll… Keep that in mind.”

Now

Things had gotten a bit chaotic across the city, thanks to the ransomware attacks. It seemed like almost every system somehow connected to the internet was being hit, from the traffic lights to the hospitals. That last one was most pertinent to Buck and the rest of the 118 at the moment, seeing as it meant they were roped into assisting the doctors and nurses.

See, they’d just arrived with patients in tow – at the same hospital Eddie went for his panic attack and with Dr. Salazar greeting them in surprise at the entrance, which was awkward for everyone – when it became obvious just how badly the hospital was being affected by the ransomware attack. The staff had to brute force their way into their prescription cases to give the patient drugs, they didn’t know who to give those drugs to because they couldn’t look up the patient charts, IV drips were being calculated the old fashion way, and all the ventilators on one of the floors went down. Life-saving tasks that were trusted to machines just that morning were now having to be done analog style.

To help, Bobby assigned each of them a task that would lessen the burden on the frazzled nurses and doctors. Which was how Buck and Eddie ended up manually pushing air into the lungs of two bagged patients, in tense and awkward silence. Not that the tension was new, because it had been there since their argument the night before.

Back in high school, the two of them never really fought like this. Since getting back together, their squabbles were just that, squabbles. They were addressed quickly and solved quickly. This, though, reminded Buck far too much of Eddie’s first year at the station. Those first couple of days before their truce, when every conversation between them hid a verbal knife. Or maybe it reminded him of those months between Shannon’s return and her death, when Eddie wanted to have his cake and eat it too, and Buck finally put his foot down about it.

Buck didn’t like conflict. If given the choice, he did everything he could to avoid it. From ignoring the issue, to pretending like everything was fine, to just outright running away. It was a trait the Buckleys all shared. Even Danny did it, at least when the conflicts weren’t about Buck or Maddie.

But sometimes, ignoring the issue or pretending like everything was fine or running away simply weren’t options. Buck couldn’t ignore the fact that Eddie had a panic attack so severe that they all thought he was having a heart attack. Buck couldn’t pretend like everything was fine when it so obviously was not. And Buck couldn’t run away from it, not without running away from Eddie and Chris.

However much he disliked conflict, he didn’t have much choice in the matter right now.

“So-” he started to say, only for Eddie to interrupt him with a huff and a,

“Can we not right now?”

“You don’t even know what I was going to say,” Buck tried, and got a nasty little glare for it. “Okay, fine. We can’t keep not talking about it, Eds.”

“You really think this is the best time and place for it?” Eddie demanded, the tone coming out sharp and snappish as he gestured around them at the unconscious patients and at Austin, who was a couple beds down with his own bagged patient and looking for all the world like he wasn’t shamelessly eavesdropping.

“Yeah, I do,” was Buck’s blunt answer. “Chris isn’t here and you can’t run away.”

“Like you’re one to talk,” Eddie muttered, and then immediately looked contrite.

“You panicked so badly-”

“I don’t panic,” was the immediate denial.

Buck scoffed.

“Tell that lie to someone else, not me,” he said. “Come on, Eddie. Danny goes to therapy, I went to therapy – I’m gonna go back to therapy before Danny drags me to it. Why won’t you?”

“’Cause I don’t need therapy,” Eddie insisted. “I’m fine. So let’s just drop it, okay?”

“No, not-”

“Think the trauma bay’s on its feet for now.” Chimney’s voice, loud enough to carry across the room, startled Buck with its suddenness. He half-twisted, seeing Chimney and Hen coming over while pulling on disposable gloves. “We did all we could. Little snip there, little stitch here.”

“You guys want us to tag you out?” Hen asked. “Give you a break?”

“Yes!” Eddie’s answer was a bit too eager, and he seemed to realize that, because he followed it with a more subdued, “Yeah, a break sounds good right about now.”

“Eddie.” Buck glared at his boyfriend as the paramedics moved around them to take over pumping the oxygen bags. “We’re not done here.”

“They’ve got it,” Eddie said, even though he knew Buck didn’t mean the bagged patients “I’m gonna see what else needs doing.”

It was impressive how quickly Eddie vacated the area. Or it would be, if it didn’t serve to piss Buck off in that moment. Buck huffed and didn’t chase after him only because they did have a job to do here. Instead, he tore off his own disposable gloves and threw them into a bin with more aggression than was necessary.

“What was that about?” he heard Chimney asked, followed by Austin’s flat,

“Don’t worry about it.”

Buck got out of the way of a nurse with a radio – “Chopper Three-Zero-Five, I’m heading up to the roof now. Over.” – who was marching with a purpose. There was a waiting room nearby, with multiple televisions mounting to the columns. One of them was turned to Channel 8, which was covering the mayor’s press conference about the ransomware attack.

“Today,” the mayor was saying, “Los Angeles has been held hostage by an unscrupulous band of criminals who prefer to hide in the shadows. Well, let me be clear: the city of Los Angeles does not negotiate with terrorists.”

It was almost as if whoever was behind everything was watching the news while waiting for their cue, because the moment the mayor said that – almost to the very second, even – the power went out. Everything went totally and completely dark, so suddenly that it left Buck reeling. Everyone around him let out startled cries. Emergency lights mounted into the walls came to life with buzzes and clicks.

Buck waited and, when it was obvious the power wasn’t coming back on, hurried to find the others. He ran into Eddie as they both converged on the nurses station, where Chimney and Hen already were.

“Generators should have kicked in automatically,” Eddie stated, all remnants of their ongoing argument wiped away by the seriousness of the situation. “Something’s wrong.”

“Chim’s finding out where they are,” Hen said, pointing to where the other paramedic was conversing with the woman behind the desk.

“Back of the building,” Chimney said when he turned to them, gesturing as he spoke. “Near the services doors.”

“Buck.” Bobby had joined them, now. “Get the tools, meet us there.”

“Copy that,” Buck said with a nod, turning on his heel to rush for the engine.

Only to stutter to a halt as, above them, a crash that shook the whole building. Buck ducked instinctively, as did everyone else. Their priorities changed, and they ran outside to see what had happened. Which, as it turned out, was a helicopter parking itself so it was straddling the edge of the roof, its propellers still slicing through the air.

The sight was not any better when they rushed to the roof, Chimney splitting off to grab Austin and any gear they might need from the engine. In fact, being able to see everything closer only made it clearer how perilous the situation was. The only bright side of the whole thing was that the propellers were angled away from them, which meant they weren’t in immediate danger of being diced and sliced and made into julienne fries.

(Alma used to say it all the time when she was teaching him how to cook while Danny worked; “It slices, it dices, and it makes julienne fries.” She’d laugh a little, only to be visibly disappointed when Buck just stared at her blankly the first few times.)

The AirMed helicopter was rocking where it sat on the edge, leaning heavily towards the side of empty air. The only thing keeping it from tipping over and falling were-

“Window washing cables.” Eddie, voice raised to be heard over the chopping cacophony of the propellers, pointed at where they were tangled up around the landing skids. “This thing’s hold on by a thread, Cap.”

Bobby nodded in grim agreement and then grabbed his radio.

“One Eighteen, I want ropes, webbing, and the heavy winch to the rooftop,” he spoke into it. “Double-time.”

“But we’re not waiting for any of that.” Buck rocked on his heels, fighting the instinct to sprint over and start getting people. He didn’t think he could handle it if the helicopter fell in front of him and he didn’t even try to save anyone. “Right?”

“No,” Bobby confirmed. “First thing we do is get those rotors turned off. Hen, Eddie, check the cabin. Buck, you’re with me.”

Buck watched as Hen and Eddie slid into the cockpit of the helicopter, Hen in the back and Eddie in the front. Hen assessed one of the patients, requesting a backboard over the radio. There was a flickering orange glow, the telltale light of fire, that Eddie put out with a fire extinguisher. Then he started flicking switches on the dash, the rotors slowing to a stop.

Which, while it made it safer for them to approach the helicopter, it also meant part of what was keeping the helicopter up was gone. It started to slide over the side, more weight falling on the poor window washing cables. Buck lunged for the nearest landing skid, grabbing hold of it as if he would be able to make any difference, though he felt less stupid when Bobby did the exact same thing.

Things jarred and he lost his grip, falling back a bit. He heard what sounded like one of the doors swinging open and his heart stopped for a moment.

“Eds!?” Everything was at an angle that kept him from seeing the inside. “Hen!?”

“We’re fine!” Eddie shouted back.

Buck clambered onto the side of the helicopter, wincing at every groan of metal. The patient in the back, in his blue jumpsuit, was laying against the side-turned-floor. The pilot was still strapped in, thankfully in the seat on the side that was now “up.”

“Buck?” Eddie looked up at him from the pilot’s other side, already undoing straps.

“Yeah, yeah.” Buck reached down to grip the guy under his arm pits. “I’ve got him.”

Once the pilot was lifted out and handed off to Bobby, Eddie climbed into the back with Hen. Buck, straddling the empty air and doing his best to ignore it as wind tugged at him, looked up as Chimney came rushing to them with the backboard. He took it from the paramedic, passing it down to Hen and Eddie.

“Hen, what do we got?” Chimney asked, ignoring the interaction.

“Spinal, I’m guessing cervical,” Hen answered. “We need to flip him onto this backboard.”

“That’s a two-person job.”

“I’m available!” Eddie chimed in from where he was crouched inside. “Trick is, how do we do that here?”

It was a fair question. The back of the helicopter’s cabin was quite cramped with three people in it, especially at the angle they were working with. And the drop.

“The backboard’s long enough to cover this opening,” Hen pointed out.

The opening in question was the now empty frame of a window. It was something the patient was not particularly jubilant about, though he wasn’t really getting much of a choice. Hen and Eddie got the backboard in place and then flipped him onto it, strapping him in while Chimney dashed off again. Buck was too busy watching Hen and Eddie work, extra conscious of every movement of the helicopter, to pay him much mind. Not until he and Bobby returned with the emergency fire hose.

“Eddie!” Bobby tossed the end down to the paramedics. “Attach this to the board.”

Eddie did, and they were about to bring the patient up when he suddenly shouted for them to wait. Because the helicopter was a transplant flight, and there was a heart onboard waiting for a new home. Eddie spotted it and Hen tried to grab it, but it was out of her reach and they didn’t have time.

Once everyone was out of the helicopter, which was sinking lower and lower, they focused on the patient. Most of them did, anyway. Buck saw Bobby eyeing the helicopter below them and then grab the same hose they’d used for the backboard.

“The winch is here, Cap,” Buck pointed out.

Austin had finally joined them on the roof with the requested gear, though he came to a stop at a noticeable distance from the walled edge.

“No time for that,” the captain replied as he tied the hose around himself.

That was how they ended up anchoring Bobby as he climbed into the helicopter, the hose his only safety line. Buck could hear the creaking of metal and the audible tension of the window washer cables that were the only thing holding up the helicopter. The way that the captain was moving around as he got the heart was not helping any.

And then, finally, the cable reached its breaking point. It snapped, the ends whistling through the air, and Bobby was suddenly dead weight. Buck and the rest scrambled to keep hold of the hose, until Bobby managed to pull himself up with the heart in its container. Buck manhandled him onto solid ground as a nurse grabbed the heart.

“That was amazing.” Dr. Salazar had joined them on the roof at some point, without Buck noticing. “Any chance you can do a transplant in the dark?”

It was a question that had Bobby laughing.

“We’ll go get the generators running,” Eddie said, already moving for the stairs. Or maybe he was running away from the doctor.

“And I bet that by the time we do,” Buck said as he followed, “the city will have the power back on.”

Chapter 5

Chapter Notes

cw: References to Daniel's time as a prostitute in El Paso.

A bit more than half of this was written under the influence of a low-grade fever, so...

Then

It was weird for Daniel to have a social life these days. Sure, when they were in Hershey, he still kept in touch with the friends from school that, like him, didn’t end up going on to higher education. But in El Paso, his time pretty much revolved around Evan, work, work, and… Him. Daniel would hang out with Alma, too, when he had time, especially after Evan left on his Neverending Gap Year Road Trip. But beyond all that, his social life was pretty much dead, which was ironic for a man who loathed being alone.

Somehow, he’d managed to make and keep a friend over the two years since moving to Los Angeles. Thanks to fucking Mendoza, of all people, constantly dragging him out for drinks with academy friends. That had more or less stopped, though: Casey’s boyfriend got a job offer in San Francisco, and off he went.

Daniel wasn’t all that upset to see him go, to be honest. Mostly because of the way Casey talked to and about Austin. He always talked like Austin was broken or pretending. By the time Casey left the city, Daniel was debating the merits of punching Mendoza’s friend’s lights out.

Daniel liked Austin. He was an asshole and kinda funny, once you got him going. The two of them started hanging out before Casey left, though hanging out still meant meeting at a bar. They’d share gossip, because Austin was an insatiable gossip hound – apparently, the new captain for the 118, the second since they met, was cruising through his last few months before his retirement in the Summer. Austin would talk about his nephews and his niece, proudly showing pictures of them, and didn’t bat an eye when Daniel talked too much about Evan.

Sometimes, Austin would show up in a mood. This time it happened in the second week of February, leading up to Valentine’s Day. The cause of it was almost always the same: his parents and their need to make sure he didn’t die alone or something. It was almost sweet, their insistence on finding their son someone to be happy with, because they and their other kids had found partners and they were happy for it. The Joneses sounded like far better parents to Austin and his siblings than his own had been to Evan and Maddie. But it was a love that could wear away at a person.

“Who is it this time?” Daniel asked when Austin flopped into the seat across from him. He pushed Austin’s drink towards him. “Wait, lemme guess, friend of a friend’s gardener’s niece?”

The other man blinked at him and then rolled his eyes.

“They’ve got Emmett in on it now,” Austin said, already sounding exhausted. “It’s some woman at the daycare Dean and Sam go to.”

“I’m pretty sure you’ve been set up on dates with half the city by this point,” Daniel said. “The half my brother hasn’t slept with.”

Not that there was anything wrong with his baby brother’s proclivities. As long as Evan was being safe and wasn’t getting hurt, it was no one’s business but his own and the people he slept with.

“It feels like it, sometimes,” Austin complained.

“The offer’s still there, y’know,” Daniel reminded him, as he did every time this topic came up. “I’ve been told I’m excellent arm candy.”

Austin stared at him, face conflicted, as if he wasn’t sure if he was allowed to find that funny or not. Daniel had confided in him about what he did on the side in El Paso one night, several drinks in, not too long ago. The other man had been distinctly non-judgmental, except to state, with clear disbelief and confusion,

“I still can’t believe people actually pay for it.”

Now, months later, Austin still hadn’t expressed any scorn or looked at Daniel any differently. He couldn’t even say that for all of his johns and janes, and had grit his teeth under the disparaging gaze of more than one doctor when he went in for screenings.

(He didn’t want to ever tell Evan. He wasn’t sure he would survive if his baby brother looked at him differently for it.)
