“C’mon.” Daniel nudged at Austin’s foot under the table when the other man was quiet for a beat too long. “It’ll be fine, unless it’s too awkward for you.”

Austin’s brow furrowed in thought as he glanced away. And then he took a long draw of his beer, looked back to Daniel, and said,

“You know what? Sure. Why not?”

Now

Buck was wrong, very, very wrong. The city did not have the power back on by the time he and Eddie got the hospital’s generators running. The city didn’t have it back on by the next day. The city didn’t have it on four days later. And worse, the first day of the blackout was also the first day of an awful heatwave that just seemed to be getting hotter with every sunrise.

He’d been working the entire time, too. The emergency recall procedure had been put into action, meaning every shift was on duty. Not that he could understand how having more people did anything to help when they still had the same number of emergency vehicles as before, but he wasn’t the person in charge of those decisions. As it was, there were two shifts of people left twiddling their thumbs in the dark every time the trucks were sent out, usually to respond to another case of heat exhaustion or heat stroke.

The station had a generator, but by the fifth night without power, Bobby declared they needed to start rationing it to cut back on fuel use. That meant the fridge was emptied and unplugged, along with everything else that was deemed unnecessary at the moment. Fortunately, phones were not on that list, because the captain would have a riot on his hands if his people couldn’t charge their phones to check in on loved ones. But that didn’t mean phone charging was a free-for-all.

That was where Buck came in. His current job, given to him by Bobby himself, was to oversee the distribution of department issued portable chargers. He was given a clipboard and everything, much to his coworkers’ dismay, along with an assistant in the form of the B-shift probie who jinxed everyone earlier in the year.

They were set up at a folding table in the truck bay, just in front of the locker room. He’d taken a picture of it on his first day back and sent it to the non-work 133 group chat, receiving shock and horror from everyone in response. With all the power out, and all the lights but their portable lanterns gone dark, this might’ve been the most privacy anyone had ever gotten in that room.

“After a requisition form has been submitted, you will be issued a personal charging device,” Buck told an annoyed seeming Chimney, pushing the small stack of forms across the table. Buck had given the spiel at least two dozen times by this point, the speech learned by rote. “Once it is dead, you return it to the probie charging pool, and it will be recharged for you.”

“You can just say Ravi,” the probie interrupted to say. “Ravi will recharge it for you.”

“One charge per twenty-four hours is the limit,” Buck continued. “Do not attempt to bribe me for more, or you will be docked two charges.”

Chimney scowled down at him.

“Give me a charger,” he said. “Or Uncle Buck will never see his niece again.”

Buck blinked up at him, and then turned to Ravi.

“That was a threat, right?” he asked the probie.

“Sounded like it,” Ravi replied, a bit uncertain.

“I thought so.” Buck faced Chimney again and said, with all the confidence of a man whose big brother would regularly send his childhood bullies crying to their mothers, “Threats will get your charging privileges revoked. But since you are my niece’s father, I will embrace nepotism and ignore it. Fill out the form and you’ll be issued a charging device.”

“Buck,” the paramedic ground out.

“Chimney,” Buck mocked and then held out a pen to the man. “It’s one form, it takes less than five minutes. You’re not special, you’re not the only person in this building with a kid at home, you’re not even the only person in this station worried about Maddie. But I am the person whose ass is on the line if one of these chargers can’t be accounted for after this.”

Chimney glared and huffed and then tore the offered pen out of Buck’s hand. He grumbled as he filled out the form, most of it unflattering comments about Buck. Buck ignored it, however, as he set about getting the man’s charger out. There were stickers, with peoples names, serial numbers, and QR codes, on each of them – Buck had done that ahead of time, just for efficiency’s sake – so it was just a matter of finding Chimney’s, making sure it was fully charged, and then holding it out of reach of the man until the form was filled out.

“Thank you,” Buck said as he took the form

“This is what you get when you give him a clipboard,” Chimney told Ravi, a bit snidely, as he took the charger.

“Efficiency,” Buck said with a bright smile.

The paramedic merely grunted. Buck twisted in his seat and watched him stomp up the nearby stairs, passing Eddie on the way. When he turned back around, it was just in time to hear a cry of “Dad!” and spot Chris entering the station with Pepa.

“Hey!” Eddie jogged across the truck bay and scooped Chris up, crutches and all. “What are you guys doing here?”

Buck, as he got up to head over as well, saw Pepa was holding a pair of reusable shopping bags, both of them bulging.

“Food,” was her simple answer.

“You guys didn’t have to do that,” Eddie told her.

“Ha!” Pepa shook her head and tutted. “That one kept saying how he missed you, and then said he thought you were hungry. In front of Mama.”

Buck laughed as he joined them, catching Chris’ attention. He knew very well what Isabel Diaz was like, so it was not very surprising that Pepa came here loaded for bear. Isabel had done her best to keep his and Danny’s fridge filled to bursting after his leg injury.

“Buck!” Chris cheered.

Eddie passed him off, and Buck didn’t waste time giving the kid a bear hug of his own. Chris giggled into his shoulder.

“You’re the new fire chief, right?”

“No.” Chris drew out the word as he laughed.

“No? Darn.”

“Oh, Ravi.” Before Eddie’s words, Buck didn’t know that the probie had followed him over. “Guys, this is Ravi Panikkar. Ravi, this is my son, Christopher, and my aunt, Josephina.”

“Call her Pepa if you don’t want a shoe to the head,” Buck warned as he set Chris back on his feet. “And she has brought us… Uh, what did you bring?”

“Food,” was her answer once again, accompanied by a shrug. “The church has a generator, so Mama’s been over there cooking. She just gave me a stack of containers to bring over. I didn’t ask questions.”

“Who asks questions when Isabel Diaz is giving you food? Not me.”

“You’re a firefighter?” Chris asked, peering up at Ravi from beneath his bangs.

“I’m what they call a probationary firefighter,” the probie said.

“Did you do something wrong?”

“Constantly,” Buck quipped before Ravi could answer.

“Daniel’s at a different firehouse, isn’t he?” Pepa asked. “How is he doing?”

He and Danny started a system of turning on their phones just long enough to check in with each other, and then try to touch base with Maddie. It mostly meant they were playing tag with text messages, but he knew that if anything serious happened, someone would reach out over the radio. Thankfully, nothing serious had happened yet.

“He got puked on today,” he said. It wasn’t even Danny who told him, it’d been the others, with glee-filled text messages popping in the moment he turned on his phone that afternoon.

“Gross!” Chris and Ravi said at the same time.

“C’mon, we can take all this upstairs.” Eddie took both bags from his aunt, who seemed relieved to be free of the weight. “You’ll be the station’s favorite person, Bobby’s down to making powdered eggs for dinner.”

They were speeding through Los Angeles, sirens blaring, and Eddie’s boyfriend was very much concerned about the details of the emergency they were responding to.

“What kind of animal?” Buck demanded to know over the headset. He was in the jump seat beside Eddie, face covered in dirt and grime and generally grungy. They were all grungy at this point, honestly.

“We’ve been over this,” Bobby said, with the exact same kind of tone Eddie used with Chris when he had to explain something for the umpteenth time. The captain was twisted partially around in his seat to look at them. “We don’t know. It could be anything, the caller got cut off during the attack.”

“Okay, so- so the ransomware attack busts open all the cages at the LA zoo, and- and they call us?” Buck asked. “No one thought to call, I don’t know, Animal Control?”

“I asked about Animal Control. They’ve been busy since the breakout.”

No shit, Eddie thought. If there was an entire zoo’s worth of animals on the loose, Animal Control was definitely getting stretched as thin as they were. Personally, he was just thankful to get out of the crowded station before the walls started closing in on him. But also, the whole thing was ridiculous, so he couldn’t help poking fun at it.

“Yeah,” he said, not looking at Buck. “They, uh. They tranq’d a lion at Griffith Park.”

“That’s right, Mufasa was at the Greek Theatre, and they got Sarabi and the cubs heading down Los Feliz towards Cahuenga,” Chimney said, voice pitched just enough to be mocking. “I guess all mammals know to use the Franklin Pass.”

Bobby shook his head at the paramedic in disapproval.

“No, I don’t think you guys are hearing me,” Buck argued. “Okay? This is not Austin and Eddie bagging a turkey in South Pasadena.”

Buck had gotten quite the kick out of that story. Eddie wasn’t as appreciative, especially when Buck decided to cook a turkey for dinner.

“Wh- What gear do we even bring?” he continued. “You know, they have serious predators at the LA Zoo. A snow leopard, a Sumatran tiger…”

“He takes Christopher there all the time,” Eddie told the others. “Got the place memorized.”

It’d started while he was still laid up with his shoulder, with Danny showing up to tag in – and Eddie knew damn well that was what was happening – while Buck took Chris to the zoo for a bit. It was nice, actually, because when he was in front of Danny, he never felt like he had to put on the performance that his parents practically demanded of him. It was why he spent so much time at the apartment in El Paso.

Danny didn’t even bat an eye when Eddie stopped pretending like he wasn’t constantly aching, and instead let out all the curses and sobs and mild tantrums that he refused to let Chris and sometimes even Buck witness.

“A chimpanzee!” Buck exclaimed into their headsets. “A chimp will literally rip your face off without even thinking twice about it. You remember, right, Eddie? Th- The recording, with the lady?”

Buck shook his shoulder until he sighed and nodded and said,

“Yes, Buck, I remember.”

Did he particularly want to remember that recording of the 9-1-1 call? No, not really. But he did. Coincidentally, the woman whose face was torn off was a Nash, too.

Hen, looking extremely tired of everyone’s bullshit on day five of the blackout, simply told Buck to,

“Just smile.”

Buck did not seem to appreciate the sentiment, and appreciated even less the way Eddie and Chimney did smile at him.

Hollywood Boulevard, with its tacky rows of stars in its sidewalks, was a mess when they arrived. Trash covered the streets and cars were left abandoned. The latter meant Austin parked in an intersection and the rest of them got out. And as much as they’d teased Buck for being overly worried, when he grabbed a Halligan, so did Eddie.

The Los Angeles Zoo was in Griffith Park, quite a bit away from Hollywood. The birds he saw – vultures of some kind, maybe? – made sense, because they could fly. The giraffe, however, he had to question the logistics of, along with the troop of chittering monkeys that he could hear, but not see, and the camels they found in the middle of the little path of shops that led to the Loews Hollywood Hotel, after they went up Highland a bit.

Eddie didn’t ask why, he just followed Bobby, but he figured the captain wanted to avoid getting too close to the two or three story tall herbivore. It was a moot point, however: they were so focused on the tableau of destruction in front of them that an elephant somehow managed to sneak up on them. Maybe Buck’s worry wasn’t something to be laughed at.

“They said an animal attack, Cap,” Hen said as they looked around, wary of anything that might jump out. “But I’m not seeing any humans.”

“Well,” Bobby said, gaze fixed on the shuttered front of one of the stores. “They did say it was a souvenir store.”

“Cap?” Chimney stopped in place. “You hear that?

“That” was a guy calling for help. He was at the base of one of the stairs, hidden by a red patio umbrella. When they uncovered him, his eyes were swollen and his leg had a compound fracture. Thankfully, Eddie had grown desensitized enough that he barely grimaced at the sight of jagged pieces of bone, cover in blood and gore, sticking out.

“Sir,” Hen said as she and Chimney worked him over. “What did this to you?”

“I- I- I don’t know. It screeched and spit some kind of poison at me. I can’t see anything,” the guy stammered out. “I- I tried to run and I fell. Did- Did you find Ivan?”

“Who’s Ivan?” Eddie asked, looking around as he tried to spot for the other victim.

“My boss, I think he’s still trapped in the store. With that thing!”

“Okay, Eddie, you assist Chim,” Bobby instructed. Eddie nodded and knelt down, laying the Halligan to the side to free up his hands. “Hen, you’re with me.”

“Wait, you’re going in there?” Buck asked him.

“We are. You’re on animal control.”

“What? Wh- What am I supposed to do?”

“The animal makes a move… Control it.”

Eddie glanced over in time to see Buck glower at Bobby’s retreating back. Nonetheless, his boyfriend took up position over them, the Halligan held like Danny’s bat.

“Thanks, Cap, that’s so helpful,” Buck grumbled, flinching at a camel getting a bit too close. “I’ll just control the elephant if it decides to come over here. I’m sure it’ll stop if I get stuck in between its toes…”

Eddie rolled his eyes at Buck’s theatrics.

“Be useful-”

“I’m trying to be!”

“-and call Austin, get them to bring a gurney over.”

“Two gurneys,” Chimney amended with a glance thrown at the store Bobby and Hen went into.

By the time they got the gurneys, and got the guy onto it, the two had evacuated the shuttered store with Bobby dragging an insensate Ivan along the ground. Eddie was fairly certain he caught a glimpse of a screeching alpaca before the door closed again and blocked off his view. Buck, from his chuff of a laugh, had seen it, too.

To be professional, they silently agreed to save the mocking until they were back at the trucks.

“So you ran from an alpaca?” Buck asked. Taunted, more like.

“Two alpacas,” Bobby corrected a bit sharply. “And there was no running.”

“I’m sorry.” Buck cupped his ear with a hand. “Weren’t you one of the people mocking my concerns earlier?”

Bobby scowled.

“Get in the truck.”

Eddie could swear he caught a glimpse of a rhino in the road as they drove away.

Chapter 6

Chapter Notes

CW: Mental health issues (Maddie's PPD) and the author's ongoing ACAB propoganda.

(once again, half of this was written under the influence of a mild fever.)

Daniel turned on his phone for the first time all day, for the check-in with Buck. His phone chimed with the expected texts from Buck – a dozen, one after another, in a language that was only English-adjacent – plus the ones from the two 133 chats, professional updates in the one and dirty jokes in the other. But it was the text from Austin that caught Daniel’s immediate attention and had his stomach dropping to his knees, even though it was just two simple words: Call me.

He wasted no time in following the instructions and thankfully didn’t have to wait long before the other man answered.

“Is Buck okay!?” he barked out almost immediately.

“Buck’s fine.” Austin’s words had Daniel breathing out a sigh of relief. “He and Eddie pushed a pair of cots together. They’re curled around each other like puppies.”

“Adorable,” Daniel drawled. “So what’s the emergency?”

“Gossip,” Austin answered. “I overheard-”

“You mean eavesdropped on.”

“Chimney talking to Hen. About Maddie.”

That had Daniel’s attention. He straightened up where he stood just outside of the station, one of the few places to get any privacy at the moment.

“What about Maddie?”

Austin’s sigh was quiet, even in his ear.

“Chimney was worried about her. You know, alone with a baby and no power.”

“Right…”

“Because she has postpartum depression.”

It felt, for a moment, as if everything froze. Daniel wasn’t certain he was breathing, wasn’t certain his heart was beating. His mind, though, was running through everything he’d taken note of since Maddie quit her job as a dispatcher, everything he’d been worried about, and putting it under the new lens.

“She has postpartum depression,” he repeated, the words clumsy on his tongue. “When- When was she...?”

“I didn’t stick around for long, but he said it started a few months ago. He also said she didn’t want other people to know.”

“How long have they…” Daniel let the question trail off and shook his head, blood turning frosty as a realization struck him. “She- She’s alone. With the baby. He left her alone in the dark with the baby and didn’t tell anyone what was going on!?”

“He told Hen.”

“After days of-!” Daniel realized he was on the verge of shouting at Austin and he bit back the words. “He’s a paramedic, and I know he’s been doing this job at least as long as me. He should know better.”

As if to make up for the frozen moment earlier, his heart and breathing sped up. He was panicking, he knew. Panicking because he’d responded to the end result of this kind of situation. Panicking because his sister hadn’t responded to his texts from yesterday, hadn’t answered his calls all week.

“Thank you,” he breathed out. “I’m- I’m gonna-”

“Go.”

Daniel hung up and sprinted into the station, dancing around cots and firefighters that turned their heads to follow him with curious eyes. He wasn’t certain where Mehta was right then, but he found him quickly enough. The man was in his office, of course, trying to do paperwork by the light of the portable lantern sitting on the desk. He looked up when Daniel burst in, expression going from shock to concern edged with apprehension.

“Dan,” Mehta said, putting down his pen. “What’s wrong?”

“I need to do a wellness check,” Daniel told him, and decided that he didn’t much care about Maddie’s privacy right then. Mandatory reporter, after all. “For my sister. I just- I just found out she has postpartum depression, that she’s had postpartum depression, and she’s alone right now. Just her and the baby.”

The captain’s eyes went wide.

“Have you heard from her?”

Daniel shook his head.

“Not for a couple days.”

“Alright, take the battalion truck. Woods and Stoppard are both here, we can live without you for the moment.”

Daniel barely remembered to thank the man before he was dashing through the station again. He heard Mendoza call after him, but he ignored the paramedic as he slid to a stop in front of the rack for keys. The battalion truck’s key was hanging at the end, along with the spare keys for the ambulances and trucks. The battalion truck itself was parked behind the station, just outside the set of bay doors on the backside.

The Los Angeles streets were empty and dark, for the most part, the sirens echoing in the stillness and their light casting everything he passed in angry red. In the distance he could see police helicopters searching for something, spotlights beaming down. One went over the road, momentarily blinding him.

Maddie and Chimney’s apartment building was as dark as everywhere else in the city. He parked in the fire lane and jumped out to rush inside. When he reached their door, flashlight beam bouncing as he sprinted up the stairs, he gave several firm knocks.

“Maddie?” he called out. “Maddie, it’s me.”

Daniel was debating the merits of kicking in the door when his sister finally answered. She had a flashlight of her own in one hand and seemed confused at seeing him. Daniel was just relieved to see her. Even if she looked horribly exhausted.

“Dan? What are you doing here?”

“Checking on you,” he told her, entering the apartment when she stepped aside enough. “You haven’t responded to any texts and you aren’t answering the phone.”

“It’s a blackout, Dan. My phone’s dead.”

She rolled her eyes at him. Daniel scowled, because that would have been a good excuse if calling her got him sent directly to her voicemail. Instead, it would ring out first. Dead phones didn’t do that.

“How are you?” he asked, instead of pressing the lie. He could call her out on her bullshit after he made sure both his sister and his niece were okay. “How’s Jee-Yun?”

“We’re… Fine,” Maddie said, and while her face was too shadowed to give anything away, something about her voice had warning bells ringing in Daniel’s head.

“She asleep?” he asked her as he made his way through the apartment to the bedroom, his flashlight illuminating the way in front of him. “I haven’t seen her all week, Mads.”

She didn’t say anything. Jee-Yun was tucked inside her crib, looking cozy in footie pajamas. Daniel kept the light angled down to the floor, but there was enough to do a quick check of her. He felt the knot of worry begin to untangle itself.

“Why did you come?” Maddie asked. Demanded, almost. “I thought it was the emergency recall procedure, all hands on deck.”

“It is,” he told her. “Mehta let me come do a wellness check.”

“A- What? Why would you need to do a wellness check on us, Dan?”

Daniel debated how to do this. He could try to ease into it, somehow, but that wasn’t really his style. Hell, he did a sexuality crisis speedrun in a motel in the middle of his very first time with a guy. He didn’t do easing in, even when he made an honest effort. He always ended up leaping feet first, with all the consequences that followed.

So, he didn’t ease into it.

“Austin overheard Chimney talking to Hen,” Daniel said. His sister’s face, partially masked by shadow, went slack with surprise and then went concerningly blank. “About you. About how you’re sick.”

“Howie promised that he wouldn’t tell anyone,” she whispered.

“It was a stupid promise.”

“I didn’t want anyone to know!” Maddie snapped. “Of course your fake boyfriend ran to share the gossip the moment he heard it.”

“He only told me.” Daniel was certain that if other people knew, if Buck knew, then this apartment would be far more crowded. “Because you’re my sister. Because keeping it a secret is stupid, Mads.”

“It’s my business!”

Jee-Yun made a fussy noise at the rising volume. Daniel and Maddie soothed her back to sleep together, so similar to how they’d done Buck, when he was this age, and then moved to the living room.

“I know it’s your business,” Daniel said, keeping his voice low. “But Maddie, c’mon. This is- It’s just been you and Jee-Yun here, alone, for a week. Without power.”

“We’re fine,” his sister stubbornly insisted.

“Okay, I’m sure you are,” he lied. “But- But you don’t have to stay here alone. You could go to the Wilsons, or- or Chimney’s folks, or, hell, I bet Tabitha and Alan would let you stay with them if we asked.”

“I don’t want to intrude on anyone.”

“You wouldn’t be. You know you wouldn’t be.”

“We’re fine here.”

Daniel stared at her, familiar despair welling up. They’d been here before, he knew, though the “here” last time had been in the hallway of a hotel that was technically outside of Hershey. He could push and push, but she would just dig her heels in. The Buckley stubbornness.

The only way he’d get his sister to stay somewhere else right now would be by physically dragging her there. It was tempting, to be honest. It was what he should’ve done when he found out about Doug. But he couldn’t drag her along and carry Jee-Yun at the same time, not in the dark like this.

“Okay, fine. Fine! Then- Then answer when I call, reply to my texts-”

“My phone’s dead,” she lied again.

“Then I’ll do another wellness check tomorrow,” he told her. “Not even just me. I’ll ask Athena to do one, too.”

His sister’s mouth drew into a tight, displeased line.

“Don’t you have to get back to the station?”

“Maddie…”

“We’re fine, Dan,” she insisted again.

When Daniel finally relented, after making her promise to call if she needed him, he stepped out into a hallway that seemed somehow darker than before.

Some time in the night, while they were all trying to get some sleep in between calls, Bobby suddenly rushed out of the station. He put Chimney in charge, said he’d explained later, and then sped off in the captain’s truck. He looked vaguely panicked. It was concerning, to say the least.

While he was gone, they were called to a shooting. The only problem was that their victim happened to be in the Autonomous Cop-Free Zone that had popped up in the middle of the blackout’s chaos. Honestly, these days, Buck could get behind that. Sure, his opinion of cops was much higher than his brother’s, but that really was not saying much.

Unfortunately, the people running the Zone saw their uniforms and made no distinction between cop and firefighter, which was unusual. Generally speaking, even the ones most distrusting of cops were at least willing to tolerate firefighters. There were occasions, back in El Paso, where people in their apartment building went to Danny before they even contemplated calling 9-1-1.

And at the moment, however much Buck could understand the stance of the people here, it was kinda annoying.

“Dispatch says there’s a Gold Line Metro station in the middle of this thing,” he commented to Hen as the two of them went to join Eddie and Chimney at the barricades that marked the Zone’s boundaries, the former clutching at a Halligan, who were trying to negotiate entry with a lady who seemed to be leadership adjacent. At the very least, she was the one talking and she had a rifle hanging by a strap from her shoulder. “Can’t we use that to sneak in?”

“I don’t sneak,” was Hen’s answer.

“I don’t know who called you guys, but no official passes through these barricades,” the lady was saying when he and Hen got there. People in the crowd behind her shouted their agreement. “We don’t need you. We have volunteers to take care of that.”

“But they’re not,” Chimney told her. “Someone in your camp called Nine-One-One. That’s who we are. We are Nine-One-One.”

Approaching sirens had Buck looking behind them, to see the 118’s captain’s truck pulling to a stop.

“You’ve got a man in there bleeding out,” Chimney tried.

“You have your rules,” the lady responded. “We have ours.”

“Why is everyone just standing there?” Bobby demanded as he stormed up to them, the panic from early having turned into a truly dark mood.

“They won’t let us in,” Buck answered with a frown, wondering what happened that he’d missed. “Miss Mayor is blocking the way ‘cause of some stupid rule.”

“Really?” Bobby said. “Since when do anarchists have rules?”

“Adapt or die,” Eddie remarked.

“There are no rules here,” Bobby declared, taking the Halligan from Eddie.

He lifted his leg, clearly intending to kick over the barricade. Buck lunged forward to grab him by the shoulders and drag him back before he could. He turned and pushed Bobby even farther back, ignoring the man’s outraged scowl.

“Okay, all of you go that way,” he demanded, making shooing motions. “Come on, go.”

“Buck,” Bobby growled out. “We don’t have time for this.”

“Just… Go away for a bit,” Buck reiterated. “All of you.”

They were reluctant, Eddie especially. But it was Eddie who gripped Hen and Chimney by their sleeves and tugged until they started moving on their own. Bobby didn’t budge; Buck just ignored him as he turned back to the lady and the others.

“Let’s try this again,” he said, slapping his hands down on the barricade. “Cops suck. Trust me, I know.”

“Really?” the lady scoffed, eyeing up his uniform and his very clear lack of melanin compared to her.

Buck sighed, glanced over his shoulder at the others, and then dropped his voice.

“A cop… A cop hurt my brother,” he told her. He looked her in the eye as he did. “He hurt him a lot.”

He looked her in the eye, willing for her to get the unspoken message, until her own eyes widened with dawning comprehension, then flickered with disgust and outrage.

“So yeah, I do know cops suck. I know how much they suck. I don’t blame you for wanting to keep them out,” Buck said. “But we’re not cops. We’re firefighters and paramedics. Someone in your camp is hurt, and someone called us for help, and that’s all we want to do.”

She was wavering, the crowd behind her silent. But her reluctance was still clear.

“It doesn’t need to be all of us,” Buck said, and then pointed at where Eddie, Hen, and Chimney were clumped together. “See them? They’re paramedics.” Technically a lie, because Eddie never got paramedic certified, but a very small one. “They can go in with a gurney, get the guy loaded up, and then come right out.”

She scowled for a moment longer, and then nodded.

“You and those three,” she said, jerking her head at someone close to them in the crowd. “But not him.”

She indicated the still stormy captain. That was probably for the best, because Bobby’s attitude was not gonna be helpful for keeping the peace here.

“Not him,” Buck agreed.

He jogged over to the others as the barricade got moved out of the way. Hen and Chimney stared at him in puzzlement, clearly trying to figure out how he’d got the impressively stubborn lady to agree to anything. Eddie, though, just had an expression of grim understanding.

“The four of us are going in with the gurney and then we’re coming straight out,” he informed them.

“What did you say to her?” Chimney asked as Hen and Eddie grabbed the gurney from where it was waiting just outside the open ambulance doors.

“The truth,” Buck answered with a shrug as the four made their way into the Zone. Bobby started walking with them and Buck once again stopped him. “Just us four, Cap.”

“Buck-” Bobby started, but Buck kinda ignored him as he jogged to catch up with the others. The barricade got moved into place behind him.

“He’s over here,” one of the members of the crowd said, very grudgingly, as he led them through.

Their gunshot victim was propped up on a cot with a mound of pillows, blood running down their arm and torso from the wound in his shoulder. Chimney and Hen knelt down, already pulling on their blue gloves. Eddie was a moment behind them, getting the gurney situated.

“Looks like a through-and-through,” Chimney declared. “But the brachial artery looks intact. Buck? Call it in, let the hospital know.”

“Copy that,” Buck agreed, stepping back to let the paramedics and Eddie work.

Just as he promised, they got the guy stabilized for transport with gauze wrapped around his torso, and then got him onto the gurney. It wasn’t as quick as the people around them might’ve preferred, but Buck never promised a set time for it. The lady that he negotiated with gave him a nod as they moved the barricades again to let them through once more, and he nodded back.

Bobby was still rather stormy, glaring at the people in the Zone and everyone else his eyes landed on.

“Were you really going to kick over that barricade?” Buck asked the captain, genuinely curious, after they got the gunshot victim onto the ambulance. “Just not having it tonight, huh?”

Normally, Bobby was one of the more levelheaded firefighters at the 118. Doing something like that, especially when there was a gun in the mix, was not normal behavior for the man. Which meant something was going on, probably the same thing that had him rushing out of the station earlier.

Hen seemed to agree, because she stepped up next to Buck and asked,

“Everything okay?”

Bobby didn’t answer for a beat. And then-

“Harry’s been kidnapped.”

The ride back to the station was silent, the atmosphere in the truck almost oppressive. Even Austin was feeling it, Buck catching the man’s eyes bouncing between the road and rear view mirror to look at them.

It was Jeffery Hudson, Bobby had said, the Realtor Rapist. The man who attacked Athena just before the pandemic locked everything down. The man whose penis Athena had shot off.

When they got back to the station, Bobby secluded himself in the locker room without a word. Buck could see him through the glass walls, sitting on the bench with his head bowed and something clutched in his hands.

It was chance whether or not Danny’s phone was on right now. Buck decided to make that gamble as he stepped outside the truck bay doors and turned on his own phone. It was a gamble that paid off, because it rang in his ear instead of sending him directly to voicemail.

“Everything okay?”

“Yeah- No, not really.” Buck could hear wind in the background. “Are you driving? That doesn’t sound like the engine.”

“I was out doing a wellness check.”

“They had you doing a wellness check?” Buck asked, letting his disbelief color his words.

“I am a paramedic, you know.”

“They still wouldn’t send you to do a wellness check, they’d send a cop. What’s going on?”

“Nothing. Everything’s fine.”

“You promised,” he reminded his brother.

Danny sighed down the line.

“I’ll tell you after this mess with the blackout is over.”

“Danny-”

“I promise. Pinky promise, even. When we can go home long enough to change our underwear, I’ll tell you.”

“Gross.” Buck glanced up at the night sky, so much more full than the dark, inky void it usually was. “Fine. But I’ll put chili sauce in all your food if you don’t.”

“How threatening,” was Danny’s dry response. “What were you calling me for? You said things weren’t okay.”

“Yeah, um. Harry’s missing. Kidnapped.”

“Harry? Harry Grant?”

“Yeah. It’s that Hudson guy, the-”

“The one whose dick Athena shot off.”

“Yeah.”

“Fuck,” Danny sighed. “Okay. Keep me updated, I guess.”

“Yeah,” Buck said. “Um, how about you? Is- Is everything okay over there?”

“No,” was his brother’s honest answer. “After the blackout, squirt. I’ll tell you then.”

Chapter 7

Chapter Notes

CW: canon typical violence, off-screen/implied suicide attempt

Bobby was still in the locker room, sitting in the exact same spot, silent and unmoving. It was yet another thing that sparked Buck’s anxiety, along with Harry’s well-being and whatever was going on with Danny. Even while trying to complete his post-call chores, Buck’s eyes kept sliding back to the locker room.

“What is he doing?” he finally blurted out, cracking under the pressure. “He still hasn’t moved.”

“Leave it alone,” Chimney said. He had a cloth in hand, using it to polish the engine. “Nobody’s going in there.”

That was a statement that rankled Buck on multiple fronts. Because it didn’t sit right with him to just leave someone alone when they were going through something like this. More practically, though, and this rang through his head in Danny’s voice, the locker room was not an area they could just bar people from entering. It wasn’t quite a public space, however much it was open to the public, but it was a shared space. Their things were in there, after all, and it was the only way to the showers.

If it was privacy Bobby wanted, the captain’s office had a door with a lock.

“I can’t imagine what Cap’s going through right now,” Eddie remarked.

“Can’t we?” Buck asked him under his breath as settled beside him, leaning against their gear cages.

Eddie cut a glance his way, confusion marring his face before it turned into a grimace. Because the two of them actually could imagine how Bobby was feeling in that moment. Chris being lost in Santa Monica after the tsunami might not have been the exact same, but it also wasn’t all that different. The stomach-churning, heart-stopping terror and worry that Bobby was certainly feeling in that moment? Buck and Eddie had both felt it.

“But why Harry?” Buck was louder this time as he asked. “Thought this guy’s thing was attacking women.”

“Pretty sure that’s exactly what he’s doing,” Hen said as she zipped up the med kit she’d been restocking.

Buck grimaced and kicked himself, because yeah, now it made sense. He felt kind of stupid for not making the connection on his own, all things considered.

For the first time, Bobby moved. The motion in the locker room caught Buck’s eye and he immediately turn to see the captain pick up his phone. And then, moments later, they were rushing for the engine. Well, Bobby was, jumping straight into the driver’s seat. They just hurried to clamber inside before they could be left behind.

As they were pulling out, Buck caught sight of Austin in the side view mirror. The driver-engineer was watching them drive off with his head cocked like a confused puppy.

“Uh, Cap?” Buck asked as he fumbled to get his seat belt on. He’d managed to catch the front seat, the ghost of old anxiety briefly fluttering over him. “Where, um. Where are we headed?”

“The Gold Line Metro,” was the captain’s answer.

He apparently meant that literally, because after a bit of a drive, the captain was maneuvering them onto the Gold Line Metro. As in, on the tracks. Buck wondered how he knew there weren’t any stalled trains in the way as they drove, Bobby craning his neck to check the signage of every station they passed, until they reached the one he seemed to want and parked them.

Then he killed the engine and shut off the lights.

“So…” Eddie’s voice carried from the back. “Where are we?”

“The station inside the Autonomous Cop-Free Zone,” Bobby said, voice tight.

“And why are we here?” Hen asked this time.

“Hudson.”

The cab of the engine fell into a heavy silence then as they were all left to wait in the dark. Buck twiddled his thumbs and fought the urge to start bouncing his leg. The others, from the sound of rustling clothes and creaking seats, were not doing any better than him.

Except Bobby. Bobby seemed to just be staring straight ahead, still as a statue.

A figure dashed across the tracks in front of them, hitting the wall and coming to a stop. Buck sat up, nerves singing with anticipation. Soon after, the beam of a flashlight swept around to land on Jeffery Hudson pressed up against the wall. Bobby hit the engine’s lights, all the lights, flooding the station with illumination.

Athena was the one with the flashlight. Dressed in civvies and armed with a gun – they were both armed, Buck could see Hudson’s now – that she had fixed on Hudson. They bore witness to the drama playing out in front of them: Athena ordering Hudson to drop his gun and tell her where Harry was; Hudson being the exact kind of smarmy asshole that he appeared to be.

And then, he pointed the gun at the engine.

Athena did not hesitate. She emptied her clip into him, the sound loud and echoing in the enclosed space. Buck flinched back in his seat. The sound was still ringing in his ears even in the dead silence that followed, as everyone jumped out and ran to the downed assailant.

Hudson died with his eyes open and Athena demanding he tell her where Harry was.

The detail that all of them managed to forget until the last moment was that Jeffery Hudson was a realtor before that night with Athena. A realtor whose company had a dead project out in Valencia, a neighborhood where no one lived and where no one would see anything suspicious. Once it became obvious that was where Harry was, they wasted no time speeding that way.

There was a gate that blocked entrance into the cul-de-sac, but it didn’t slow them down. They parked in front of a ring of half-finished houses covered in graffiti, and scattered out to find something, anything. The inside of the houses, as marked and tagged as their outside, gave nothing. Behind one of them, however, were cans of paint the same shade of non-offensive white as the interior walls. The can wasn’t dirty like one would expect if it had been there since the project shut down during the pandemic, and the paint was still tacky to the touch.

“Cap!” Buck followed the raised voices of Michael and Athena, while Eddie followed him, to one of the front rooms on the ground floor of the house. Bobby was there, too, as were Hen and Chimney. “I found this out back. I don’t know if it means anything.”

He lifted up the empty paint can. Everyone’s eyes zeroed in on it, and then started darting around at all the empty spaces of white on the walls.

It was Athena that found the spot of wet paint, in the corner of the room and near the floor. It was Eddie that slammed the Halligan into the wall. It was Michael who jumped in and tore it open with his bare hands.

Harry was inside the wall, bound and gagged, and alive.

The city’s power came back on as they drove away, lights blinking to life along the skyline. The air of the station was filled with palpable relief and the coolness of air conditioners as they pulled the engine in. The ambulance, carrying Bobby, Hen, Chimney, and three out of four Grants, went on to the hospital. When it returned, only the paramedics got out of it.

“Look at all this glorious electricity,” Chimney said, frowning down at his phone. “Guess the power czar is out of business.”

Right, that’s what people had started calling Buck while he oversaw the distribution of the personal chargers. It sounded like playful banter coming from them, but it had a different feel when it came from Chimney. Or maybe Buck was still sore over Chimney threatening to ban him from seeing Jee-Yun over a requisition form.

“I gladly hand my power back to the power company,” he said.

“Good, ‘cause my phone is dead. You did a horrible job.”

How was that Buck’s fault?

Eddie rolled his eyes and then asked Hen,

“How’s Harry doing?”

“As well can be,” she answered. “After being abducted by a lunatic and sealed in a wall to die.”

“Did Cap not come back with you?” Buck asked, craning his neck in an attempt to spot him in the cab of the ambulance.

“He’s still at the hospital with Athena.”

Fair enough, but…

“So who’s in charge until he gets back?”

“I am,” Chimney immediately said.

Hen scowled at him.

“He did not say that.”

“Not in words,” Chimney said as he started to walk away. “Didn’t you catch that manly glance of unspoken confidence he flashed me before he left.”

“I did not.” Hen’s voice was filled with doubt. “Cap said that if he isn’t back by end of shift, he’d close it out in the morning.”

“Would you guys say this is the longest shift we ever worked?” Buck asked them.

It was almost a week straight that they’d been clocked in. So much overtime.

“It definitely ranks,” Eddie agreed.

The apartment was silent when Daniel walked in, and the air warmer than he’d like it because the AC hadn’t been set to combat the heatwave when he last left it. It was too empty, too. While Daniel knew Austin had been suffering the entire week, struggling not to lose it on someone because he didn’t have that blessed alone time he needed in order to decompress, Daniel had been having the opposite. He’d been reveling in a station filled to capacity, or at least in not having to go home to an empty apartment.

He plugged his phone in and left it to charge after making sure it was turned back on, then he went through the motions of emptying his work bag. His uniforms, the change of civvies, all of it was over due a wash. Sweat, dirt, and worse covered them, and he made a face as he took off badges to be set aside while doing his best not to touch soiled fabric. They all went into the washer afterwards, along with the clothes he was wearing then. They were not the freshest, either.

Afterwards, he took the hottest shower he could manage. He scrubbed at his skin until it was pink, and didn’t step out until he was in no danger of being mistaken for Pig-Pen. Changing into fresh clothes afterwards – and especially changing into fresh underwear – had him feeling more human than he had in days.

That left the fridge. The fridge that had been without power for more than five days. The fridge that had not been empty when he left, and certainly hadn’t been empty when the power went out. The fridge that no doubt had monstrosities growing inside it that could rival what he and Maddie found underneath Buck’s childhood bed, that one time.

Daniel desperately did not want to open that fridge. Daniel also desperately needed to open it, to clean it out now, before it got worse and the CDC had to be called. He didn’t think his neighbors or the building manager would appreciate-

The ringtone he’d set for Maddie cut through his thoughts. The screen of his phone, sitting on the kitchen counter where it was plugged in, lit up with his sister’s name. He did not waste time answering it, all worries of biological horrors forgotten.

“Maddie?”

“Danny,” his sister sobbed. “I need…”

“Where are you?”

Zuma Beach, apparently. In Malibu. An hour’s drive from his apartment, though he managed to cut it down to forty-five minutes. He threw out all regard for traffic laws when Nash, of all people, called him on the way to say Maddie had left Jee-Yun at the station.

The beach was mostly empty at this time of day, after the chaotic and tiresome week they’d had. There were only two cars in the parking lot when he got there, both spread out to either ends. He parked next to his sister’s and drew in a fortifying breath before he got out.

Maddie herself was huddled in the sand just in front of her car, knees pulled up and face buried in her arms. Her clothes were on the drier side of damp, and there was sand clinging to her hair. Daniel approached her slowly and crouched down beside her.

“Maddie,” he whispered, reaching up to brush her bangs aside.

She lifted her head to look at him, and her face scrunched up as she broke into sobs. Again, from the looks of it. He gathered her into his arms and squeezed her tight, uncaring of the tears staining his shirt. He just held her and rocked her until she stopped shaking.

“What’s going on, Mads?” Daniel asked, as gently as possible. “What are you doing here?”

“I couldn’t stay.” Maddie’s words came out as another sob. “I can’t stay, I can’t, but I couldn’t…”

“Couldn’t what, Maddie?” Daniel asked, but he looked over his sister’s head at the ocean and he knew without her having to say the words.

He wanted to ask when she’d decided to do this. He wanted to ask if she already knew this was what she going to do when she stood in front of him last night, when she said she was fine and insisted she didn’t need help. He wanted to ask if this was why she refused to stay with anyone, if she didn’t want anyone to be able to stop her.

He wanted to ask, but he didn’t want to know.

(Daniel remembered Buck, curled up in his bed. Quiet, small. He remembered Eddie taking his turn trying to coax Buck out. He remembered Eddie saying how the blood clot could have killed Buck, but it didn’t. And he remembered….

“Maybe it should have.”)

“What happened?” he asked instead.

“Jee-Yun,” she managed to say. “I- I- I almost drowned her, I almost…”

“Jee’s fine, Maddie,” Daniel said, though his heart was racing. Drowned her? “She’s fine.”

If she wasn’t, Nash would’ve said something. Even as much as they hated each other, Nash would’ve told him if Jee-Yun wasn’t okay when he called to say she was at the station. Or Daniel thought so, at least.

“But she isn’t safe with me! She isn’t… I can’t be there.”

“Okay, okay, it’s okay-”

“No it’s not!”

“You’re right, you’re right, it isn’t. But it will be, okay? It’ll be okay.” Daniel pulled away from her enough to lift up a hand and stick out his pinky. “United front, remember? You and me. Remember?”

Maddie nodded, face twisting again.

“What do you want to do?” Daniel asked her. “If you don’t want to go back, what do you want to do?”

The first part of that answer involved going to Oxnard. They made a stop at a Wal-Mart, Daniel running inside to grab clothes that weren’t ocean-soaked for Maddie to change into. Then he found a motel that looked like the kind of place where nobody asked questions, even if they saw a bedraggled woman in a car. Every town had one; El Paso had several, in and around it.

Next, while Maddie was in the shower, in a bathroom that Daniel had made sure was clear and with a door left unlocked, Daniel called Mehta. His captain did not sound very surprised when told that he was putting in for emergency leave. Even if the gossip about Jee-Yun being left at the 118 didn’t reach the 133, Daniel had told Mehta that his sister was diagnosed with postpartum depression before tearing out to do a wellness check.

And then, after hanging up with his captain…

“Yeah?” was how Austin greeted him when he picked up.

“I need you to do me a favor,” Daniel said, and didn’t stop the words from trembling as he had with Mehta. “Please.”

It was hours later that there was pounding at the motel door. Daniel didn’t bother checking the peephole before opening it. Austin, grim and stoic, stood on the other side with a bag. Buck was next to him, a live wire of energy.

“What’s going on, Danny?” Buck demanded as Austin simply went inside. “Austin wouldn’t tell me anything, just that we were meeting you here. Why the hell are you at a motel in Oxnard? Maddie left Jee-Yun at the station, she….”

He trailed off when he spotted their sister sitting inside the room, perched on the end of a twin sized bed.

“Danny.” Buck’s voice was small, and a little bit scared. “What’s going on?”

“Do you remember how I promised I would tell you after the blackout?” Daniel asked. His baby brother nodded. “It’s after the blackout.”

Buck sat beside Maddie, clutching at her as Daniel explained. A not quite censured explanation, an explanation that danced around why Maddie was at Zuma Beach at all. Because Daniel hadn’t asked, and Maddie hadn’t said, and the longer that stayed true then the longer they could both pretend.

“Why didn’t you tell us?” Buck asked. It sounded closer to begging, maybe. “We would’ve helped you. We could’ve…”

“I didn’t… I didn’t want anyone to know.”

“So- So what are we going to do?” Buck turned to Daniel as he asked, in that exact same way he had as a kid and still thought his big brother could fix everything. “We’re going to do something, right?”

“There’s a women’s health clinic in Boston that she trained at,” Daniel told him. “I’m going to take her there.”

“Take her…” Buck blinked and then looked down to Maddie. “You’re leaving.”

It wasn’t a question, but she nodded all the same.

“Wh- What are we gonna tell Chimney?” Buck asked. “We’ve gotta tell him something, right? We can’t just not tell him something.”

“I left him a message.” Maddie wiped at her eyes, her nose, her face. “He- He already knows I’m leaving.”

That had not been what she was actually telling Chimney with whatever message she left him, Daniel knew. But it also was.

“Okay, but-” Buck glanced to Daniel again and then Austin, who was as silent as ever. “Chimney’s freaking out. Are we just… Are we just not gonna tell him where she is?”

“No.” Maddie shook her head, hair flying a little with the motion. “He’ll come after me, he’ll bring Jee-Yun. No. He needs to stay here. He needs to stay here and take care of her.”

“Alright.” Buck pressed his face into Maddie’s hair. “We won’t tell him.”

“And please don’t tell him about Jee. Please.”

This time, Buck just nodded.

“Her car is still at the beach,” Daniel told Buck and Austin. “I don’t want to leave it there. If anyone reports it abandoned, it’ll just make them all more worried. Take it to the apartment, maybe? Park it in one of the guest spots?”

“Cynthia and Emmett have a garage that they barely use,” Austin said. “They won’t mind.”

“Are you sure?”

“Yeah.”

Austin said it so casually, like they weren’t conspiring to hide all traces of Maddie’s flight from all their friends. Like asking his sister and his brother-in-law to be accessories was just an every day thing that Austin did.

Daniel had to once again choke back the words that seemed to always be on his tongue nowadays.

Austin took the keys to Maddie’s car when Daniel handed them over, pocketing them with a jingle of metal. Buck was reluctant to go, slow to pull away from their sister, and only did so when promised that they’d see him before they left. Then it was just Daniel and Maddie, clinging to each other on one of the twins, wrapped around each other as if they were little kids again until Maddie fell into a restless sleep.

When he was certain she wouldn’t wake up, Daniel got up, grabbed his phone, and padded out the door. He wasn’t entirely sold on what he was about to do, even though it was his idea and no one else knew he was doing it. It felt like betraying his sister’s trust. But he also wanted to cut off the possibility of a manhunt getting started.

“Athena,” he said when the sergeant answered the call. “I’m going to tell you something. But you have to promise me something else first...”

It was Daniel who bought the tickets for the flight the night before, on the laptop that Austin slid into the bag he packed for him. Daniel’s phone charger was in there, too, along with enough changes of clothes to last him a few days. Including one of Austin’s sweaters, and not even one that had managed to find its way into Daniel’s closet.

They went to a bank, when they finally untangled enough to get up, and Maddie took out money from Doug’s estate that she had never touched. Twenty-thousand dollars. In cash.

Christ.

Buck met them at the glass doors that were the entrance to LAX. He perked up at the sight of them, duffle bag bouncing on his hip as he jogged to meet them halfway. Daniel wondered what excuse he’d given Eddie for where he was, what excuse he’d given the day before.

“Here.” Buck handed the bag to Maddie. “I went by yours and packed you some clothes. Chimney wasn’t there.”

The last bit was in answer to the worried question on her face. She relaxed a bit at the words.

“You’re gonna come back, right?” Buck suddenly asked. “You’re- This isn’t for forever. Right?”

“Yeah, Buck.” Maddie wiped at her face. “Yeah, I’ll come back.”

It did not escape Daniel’s notice that, unlike all their other promises to one another, this one had not been sworn on a pinky.

Chapter 8

Chapter Notes

cw: oblique references to sex work; something that could, perhaps, be construed as gaslighting

Then

“We have a problem.”

Daniel looked up from where he sat. Austin looked… Well, as stoic as he typically did. But Daniel was starting to be able to pick out the subtle lines of agitation and anxiety that marred his face after this long of knowing him.

“What kind of problem?” he asked, sliding the drink he ordered for the other man – a basic, run of the mill lager. He himself had gone with something colorful and fruity tasting, because he was comfortable in his masculinity. And because he didn’t see the point in going out to a bar just to get a drink he could’ve bought at a store, especially if he was the one paying for it.

“Mom wants to meet you.”

Daniel choked on his colorful and fruity drink.

“Wh- What?” he managed to get out between coughs. “You told her this past January, right? Last month? Not January of last year?”

“I did,” Austin confirmed. He pulled out the chair across from Daniel and sat down, and then immediately took a draw of his lager. “I even bullshitted an actual date. It’s the eighteenth, by the way.”

“The eighteenth? Did you seriously give her your station number for our fake anniversary date?”

The other man just shrugged. It wasn’t even self-conscious or sheepish. It was simply unbothered.

“I think that she thinks I made you up,” he told him. “To get out of that Valentine’s date with what’s-her-name.”

“Yeah, I can see why your past dates went so well,” Daniel teased, and then let out a breath. “We knew this was gonna happen eventually, right? I just didn’t think it would happen a week later.”

It was still February, for crying out loud. As far as Mrs. Jones knew, they’d only been together for a month and a day. That was way too soon for Austin to be bringing Daniel home to meet his parents, right? None of Daniel’s other – none of his real – relationships had gotten to that level of seriousness so fast. Then again, he’d had few relationships that lasted this long. He’d had trouble keeping girlfriends in high school, still had trouble the last couple times he dipped a toe into the pool, and he hadn’t really been of a mind to try in El Paso. Or at least, he didn’t try for anything in El Paso that didn’t fatten up his wallet come morning.

“Well it’s happening.” Austin did not sound very happy about it. “You, me, and dinner with my parents.”

“Luckily for you, I’ve bullshitted my way through far worse dinner parties.” Daniel grinned at him and sat his elbows on the table as he leaned in. “Let’s talk boundaries.”

“Boundaries?” Austin echoed, eyebrows inching up.

“Yeah. We can’t go in there and act like a pair of dead fish someone tossed into a bucket together,” Daniel said. “We’ve gotta sell that we’re a couple. Which means you need to tell me what you’re comfortable with.”

“No kissing,” was the immediate response.

“No kissing,” he agreed. “What about hugs? Hand holding?”

“They’re fine. I guess.”

This wasn’t too entirely different from what Daniel had done before, but in truth, that had been more like haggling. What were they willing to pay for? What was he willing to be paid for?

“What else?”

“I don’t know,” Austin confessed with a frown. “I've kissed people before, and I’ve had sex, but I don’t know…”

He didn’t know what he’d be comfortable with when it came to this, was the unspoken rest of that statement.

“Okay,” Daniel said, even as he filed that little tidbit away to ask about later. “Let’s try this, then.”

He got up and moved around the table, dragging his chair along with him. Austin stared at him as he sat back down, this time so they were right next to each other. Then he threw his arm around the back of Austin’s chair.

“Too much?” Daniel asked. Austin’s frown grew heavier, but he shook his head. “Tell me when it is.”

Austin was fine with that same arm around his shoulders. A hand on his knee didn’t bother him, either. Then Daniel started moving that hand up his thigh, slowly, almost like a game of gay chicken, to give Austin time to slap it away. Austin caught his wrist when his hand was halfway up, muscles tense beneath his palm, and shook his head.

“Alright,” Daniel said.

He took his hand back and scooted his chair farther away, so there was actual space between them again. Austin noticeably relaxed.

“Last questions.” Daniel reached across the table to grab his drink. “What time do we need to be there, which of us is gonna be driving, and what should I wear?”

Now

The door was locked, but there was someone talking on the other side. Not quite loud enough to make out, or to recognize. But someone was in there, awake and alive.

“Chimney!” Buck continued to pound on the door. “Come on, I know you’re home. I can hear you.”

Because who else would be inside Chimney and Maddie’s locked apartment? Well, besides Jee-Yun, but she wasn’t talking yet. Not as far as anyone had told Buck, at least.

Screw it. He had a key. It was how he was able to pack Maddie’s bag, after all. It went in just as easily this time as it did then, and turned all the same. When the door swung open and he was able to get inside, he spotted Chimney at the dining table, laptop open in front of him. He was replaying parts of Maddie’s goodbye message to him, over and over.

“-don’t need to go to the police or come looking for me-”

Buck’s stomach squirmed with week-old guilt.

“Chimney?” He approached the other man, getting no reaction. “Chimney.”

“-or come looking for me-”

“What is it, Buck?” the paramedic asked sharply, not even taking his eyes away from the screen.

“-no one is making me do this-”

“What are you doing?”

“What do you think I’m doing?” Chimney asked, still focused on the screen of his laptop and the video he kept playing back. “I’m trying to figure out what happened to your sister.”

She checked herself into a clinic in Boston, is what Buck didn’t say.

“Nothing happened to her,” was what Buck said instead. “She just left.”

“She just left?” Chimney snapped, finally looking at him. “And that makes sense to you?”

“She’s a Buckley. Buckleys… Leave. They run.” God knows he’d proven that true himself, time and time again. “Sometimes Maddie just likes to put distance between herself and her problems.”

“So then what’s the problem in this instance? Is it me or is it her daughter?”

A tiny little part of Buck, a part that sounded so very much like Danny, wanted to say that the problem was Chimney leaving a woman with postpartum depression alone for a week in the middle of a blackout. But Buck was aware enough to know how much that wouldn’t actually help anything, so he bit back that observation. Besides, he got the feeling Chimney was already beating himself up over that decision enough as it was.

“You know what her problem is.” Buck sat down in the empty chair beside Chimney. “She told you what the problem is. She felt like Jee wasn’t safe with her right now.”

Where was Jee-Yun, anyways? How long had Chimney been sitting here, obsessing over Maddie’s goodbye message to him?

“Here.” Chimney spun the laptop around a bit, angling it so Buck could see the screen. “Let me show you something.”

Maddie on the screen, in the video, was sitting at this exact table. It was daylight, and had to have been recorded on the morning that she ended up in Oxnard with Danny. She looked tired, Buck noted. That deep exhaustion that was felt in the bones.

Chimney hit play again.

“-or come looking for me. I’m not in any danger and no one is making me do this-”

Chimney paused the video.

“Did you see that?” he asked, gesturing at the screen. “Did you see that, right there?”

“See what?” Buck asked in turn, trying to figure out what the hell he was talking about.

“She shakes her head as she’s looking right into the camera! It’s like she’s telling me to believe the opposite of what she’s saying. Like- Like there’s someone making her do it.” Chimney looked to him and whatever expression Buck was wearing had him deflating and falling silent. “That sounds crazy, doesn’t it?”

“No,” Buck was quick to say, even though it absolutely did.

“It does. I know it does. I just- I just need to know. I need to make sure there’s no one out there trying to hurt her.”

“No. No, Chimney.” Buck shook his head. “No one wants to hurt Maddie.”

“Doug did.”

“Doug is dead.”

“Is he, though?” Chimney asked. “You weren’t there, Buck. Did Dan see a body? Did anyone see a body?”

“Come on-”

“The day after Maddie disappeared, she withdrew twenty-thousand dollars-” Christ, Maddie. “-out of a settlement account from a bank in Oxnard. Doesn’t it sound like maybe somebody’s trying to coerce her!?”

“No.”

“That money came from Doug’s estate!” Chimney said. “You know he would have considered it his.”

“Do you really believe anything you’re saying right now?” Buck asked, that guilt squirming in his gut like a bag of live eels.

“I really don’t want to hate her,” Chimney said after a long moment of silence. “I don’t want to believe that she could do this to us, to me, to Jee-Yun.”

“You just have to give her time,” Buck told him. “She’s gonna come home.”

“How do you know that?” was the skeptical question.

“’Cause I know my sister.” And because Maddie told him so before she left.

“Yeah, well, I did, too,” Chimney said. “Now I’m not so sure, which is why I have to keep going back to this. This is the only thing she left me with.”

From the bedroom, the sounds of an upset Jee-Yun made their way to their ears. She sounded upset, like she was hungry or needed a diaper change or maybe even both.

“Not the only thing,” Buck whispered.

Chimney sighed and buried his face in his hands for a second, before pushing himself out of his chair. Buck didn’t bother to watch him go, too busy swallowing back those guilt-squirming eels that were wanting to make their way up his throat.

He didn’t know if he could keep this up.

Then

Daniel whistled a tune as he combed his hair, eyeing himself in the bathroom mirror. Austin had been unable to give him anything except “something nice” when Daniel asked him what he should wear, so he went with a simple blue button up and pair of black slacks. Showing up in a suit would probably be a bit too much, even if he kinda wanted an excuse to dress fancy again.

His whistling cut off with a frown as he heard the apartment door open. He poked his head out of the bathroom in time to see Evan tossing his keys onto the table. His baby brother looked dejected, managing to achieve extra levels of pathetic when it was combined with the work uniform he was wearing.

“You’re home early,” Daniel remarked. “By about four hours.”

“Got fired.” Evan sighed and flopped onto the couch, ridiculously long legs stretching out until his feet hung over the armrest. It reminded Daniel so much of when he was a teenager that it hurt. “It was stupid.”

“Getting fired was stupid? Or what you were fired for was stupid?”

Daniel threw the comb into a drawer and debated cologne, not that it was a very long debate. He didn’t have very many reasons to go out these days, so he might as well take advantage of the ones he had.

“Both,” his brother answered. “I tripped over something and dumped food all over a buncha people.”

“Woops.”

Daniel thought he should get a medal for not laughing, because it was a challenge.

“Yeah. There was a baby, in what was the cutest little white dress. Her mom was not happy with me.” Evan sat up with a sniff. “Is that- Are you putting on cologne?”

“I might be.”

“Are you going out?”

“I might be,” Daniel said again. He grinned at his reflection when he heard his brother huff in annoyance. “What of it, squirt?”

“Nothin’.” His brother shrugged. “You just don’t go out as much now. You were always going out before my, uh. Road trip.”

“And now you’re the one going out all the time,” Daniel shot back, and then opted to change the subject. He liked the fact that he managed to keep his baby brother convinced that he was simply going out on constant dates back then. “Are you gonna be okay tonight? Not gonna drown yourself in your woes?”

“No.” Evan scoffed, as if insulted by the very idea. As if Daniel didn’t come home to find two empty tubs of Ben & Jerry’s in the trash the last time Evan got fired from a job instead of quit. “I hated that place, anyways. My boss was a dick.”

“You say that every time.”

“’Cause they’re dicks every time!”

“Uh-huh.” Daniel made no attempt at hiding his doubt as he double checked his appearance. “Call me if there’s an emergency, squirt. After you call Nine-One-One.”

“I turn twenty-four in June, not fourteen, Danny.” He rolled his eyes.“Go have fun. And, you know. Fun.”

The little shit gave him a grin and wagged his eyebrows. It was Daniel’s turn to roll his eyes as he shoved at Evan’s face on the way to the door.

As it turned out, Austin lived in an apartment building not too far from Daniel and Evan’s own. Maybe not as fancy – Daniel might have splurged a bit, after years of suffering that shitty building in El Paso; he only kinda regretted it – but far from a slum. After a quick stop at a nearby shop, Daniel parked on the curb, ignoring the signs posted that explicitly said not to park there, and texted Austin that he was waiting. The man came down a scant few minutes later, dressed somewhat more relaxed than Daniel was.

“Nice polo,” Daniel commented when Austin got in. “Is that what you usually wear out on dates?”

“Yeah.” Austin tugged at the hem and frowned. “What’s wrong with it?”

“Nothing. You just look like a dork,” Daniel was quick to say as he pulled away from the curb. It was better than admitting that the tight sleeves of the polo shirt made Austin’s biceps very distracting. “Where’re we going?”

“Glendale.”

“Glen-” Daniel grimaced. “Oh, Christ, do your parents live in suburban hell?”

“Suburban hell?” Austin sounded amused.

“Yeah, you know. All those little ticky-tacky boxes in rows that look pretty much the same, where people smile to you and act all friendly and then talk shit about you behind your back.”

“I wouldn’t say the houses on Mom and Dad’s street look the same.” Austin sounded amused as he eyed up Daniel. “Have much experience with suburban hell?”

“A bit. A lot of Hershey was suburban hell adjacent. And there were a lot of ticky-tacky houses in El Paso that I ended up making house visits to.”

Austin snorted out a laugh and Daniel tossed him a grin.

Contrary to Austin’s (admittedly weak) insistence, Mr. and Mrs. Jones’ house was smack dab in the middle of a neighborhood that served as a good example of suburban hell. Sure, the houses weren’t just identical boxes made of tack and cardboard, but they had the spirit. The lawns were all green and perfectly manicured – and why the hell would anyone even bother with watering their grass in this dry-ass city? – with not even a single dandelion marring the place. And most of the houses had cutsey, kitschy decorations on the porches or lining the paths that led to them.

“Your face,” Austin laughed. “It’s this one down here, with the yellow bug.”

There was a tiny little VW bug parked in one of the driveways, next to a boring white sedan. Daniel parked his car, which was beat up and dirty and stuck out horribly in this place, on the front curb, right at the end of the sidewalk leading to the front door. Austin sat in his seat for a moment, simply staring out the window, before blowing out a breath.

“Right, let’s get this over with, then,” he said as he got out.

“Don’t worry, I’m gonna charm the pants off both of ‘em,” Daniel assured him as he reached into the backseat to grab what he bought from the store. “Not literally, of course.”

Austin raised an eyebrow at the box of store bought cinnamon rolls and the flowers.

“Really?”

Daniel shrugged.

“My parents are WASPs.”

Austin led the way to the front door, which had one of those decorative wreaths made out of artificial flowers hanging on it, and rang the doorbell. After an awkward moment, where he looked entirely unsure of himself, he slipped an arm around Daniel’s waist as they waited. Seconds later, the door opened to reveal a brightly smiling Mrs. Jones. Her blond hair, streaked with gray, was in an up-do and she had crow’s feet that wrinkled the corners of her eyes. Her husband stood behind her, and it was clear he was who Austin got most of his looks from. They even parted their hair the same way.

“Austin!” Mrs. Jones exclaimed, pulling her son into a hug.

“Hi, Mom, Dad,” Austin gamely replied, hugging his mother back before he managed to get free. “This is Daniel.”

Immediately, Mr. and Mrs. Jones’ eyes zeroed in on him. The weight of their gaze was almost tangible. He lifted up the cinnamon rolls and flowers, partially to distract them and partially to shield himself.

“I’m, uh. Not much of a baker,” he told them as he gave a self-deprecating laugh, ducked his head a little, and gave her a shy smile. The little bit never failed to charm ‘em. “But my own mom would never let me hear the end of it if I showed up empty handed.”

“No, no, this is perfect!” Mrs. Jones’ exclaimed, taking both from him. The flowers were shoved into her husband’s chest, with an order of, “Go find a vase for these.”

“Yes, dear,” Mr. Jones dutifully replied, smiling fondly at her, before disappearing deeper in the house.

“Come in, come in!” She bustled them through the door, and then shut it behind them. Like she was afraid they’d flee if given the chance. “Dinner’s just about ready.”

“It smells wonderful, Mrs. Jones.”

“No, please, Daniel, call me Tabitha!”

“Then call me Dan.”

The smile he gave her was just as bright as her own, and it was only a little bit fake.

Daniel caught Austin rolling his eyes while his mother wasn’t looking his way, and he thankfully had enough experience at this that he managed to contain the laugh that threatened to bubble up. In retribution, and because he was a bit of an asshole, Daniel wrapped an arm around Austin’s shoulders, gave him a smile that felt far more real this time, and told Mrs. Jones,

“Austin’s so tight lipped all the time. I haven’t gotten a single story from him about when he was a kid.”

Mrs. Jones lit up with glee.

Austin stomped on his foot.

Now

“We have a problem,” Buck announced the moment he walked into the apartment.

“We have several problems.” Danny didn’t even look up from the bag he was unpacking, the one he took with him to Boston. “What’s this one?”

“Chimney thinks Doug took Maddie.”

This did get his brother’s attention. He dropped a shirt onto the table and turned to fix Buck with an incredulous stare.

“He what?”

“Okay, I don’t think he actually believes it,” Buck hurried to say. “But he was watching the video over and over. Like, he was looking for signs that Maddie was taken. He was looking for secret messages in the video.”

“Christ,” Danny groaned and rubbed at his face.

He’d gotten home from Boston just that morning. Buck knew that Maddie admitted herself into that women’s clinic the same day they touched down, Danny made sure that she talked to him on the phone one last time before she went in. The rest of the time Danny spent there was in the event that Maddie changed her mind and wanted to come back home.

Buck wasn’t certain if the clinic would’ve let her leave just like that. He was certain, however, that Danny would’ve gotten her out if that was what she wanted.

“I can’t just- Danny, I’ve got to tell him something.”

“Maddie doesn’t want us to tell him anything,” Danny reminded him. “Not about where she is, not about Jee-Yun. And I’m more inclined to do what she wants than make that asshole feel better.”

Buck winced at the vitriol in his brother’s words. Danny was very unhappy with Chimney for the way he handled their sister’s postpartum depression diagnosis. Not that he hadn’t told them about it when she’d gotten diagnosed months ago – that hurt, but that was on Maddie just as much as it was on Chimney, and they could both understand keeping a secret like that – but the fact that Chimney hadn’t said anything even after the city lost power and Maddie was alone in the dark, the fact that he still hadn’t actually said anything about it to them.

“I just… Danny, I walked in there to find him making up conspiracy theories for why she didn’t just leave him,” Buck said. “I don’t know if I can do this.”

Danny sighed and padded over to him. He cradled Buck’s face between his hands and forced Buck to look him in the eye.

“It’s gonna be okay, squirt,” he said. “This isn’t for forever, this is just until Maddie herself feels ready to talk to him. Alright?”

“But what if she never…?”

“She will.”

Danny sounded so very confident about it. Buck, as he always did, decided to put his trust in his big brother.

Chapter 9

Chapter Notes

CW: references to suicide attempt; canon typical violence

The traffic collision they were called to was especially bad, one of the worse that Buck had seen. Mostly because it was caused by someone driving into a high school’s homecoming parade. There was confetti, bits of float, and car parts strewn across the pavement when they got there, along with victims either sprawled out or stumbling along with the help of others. He saw cheerleaders, members of the marching band, and football players. All of them were kids.

Bobby was on the radio as soon as they got out, calling for more assistance beyond the other two stations already called upon, but Buck didn’t stick around for him to finish. He’d already been given his marching orders on the drive, and he wasted no time in grabbing both the gear he needed and Ravi, and then jogged to the first wrecked vehicle. It was a moving truck of some sort, plain white and unmarked, resting on its side. It was the vehicle that drove into the parade.

“Alright, Rav. Pull it right there,” Buck instructed, pointing to the corner of the truck’s windshield. The safety glass was a smashed, a spiderweb of fractures emanating from the epicenter.

Ravi stabbed the end of his Halligan into the corner, wedging it between the windshield and the body of the truck, and started leveraging it loose. Buck helped, gloved fingers digging into the gap and yanking. The windshield peeled away and they could see the driver easier now. He was laying against the door in a heap, face a bloody mess.

Buck bent down to feel at his neck, and was not surprised when he didn’t find a pulse.

“No belt, no airbags,” he remarked as he stood back up, shaking his head at Bobby when he noticed him there.

“Do you think he did it on purpose?” Ravi asked.

“Callers told dispatch he was honking like crazy just before the crash,” Bobby said. “That doesn’t sound like somebody who’s trying to hurt people.”

“I don’t see any skid marks.” Buck peered about, just to make sure, but there wasn’t any fresh signs of friction against the asphalt. “I bet the brakes went out.”

“Probably,” the captain agreed. “Tag him and keep moving.”

That was the pattern that picked up for a bit, though there were thankfully better results. They would extract a victim and then pass them off to the paramedics standing by with a gurney. Buck spotted Nola and Jon and the others dotted about, rushing back and forth, but barely had the time to greet any of them with so much as a nod as they passed each other, either to an empty ambulance or on the way to the next person who needed help.

A football player calling for help disrupted the pattern, Buck subconsciously noting he was an offensive lineman based on his jersey number. The kid led them to a trapped teacher, Mr. Foster. He was stuck in among a bunch of metal that was now so twisted, Buck couldn’t even begin to guess what it had once been, though he he could make out barred barricades, bleacher seating, and netting in the mess.

He called for Bobby and the kid explained that,

“I tried to pull him out. But- But I can’t.”

“Okay, it’s a good thing you left him where he is,” Bobby said as he joined them. “What’s your name.”

“Tino. That’s Mr. Foster.”

“Okay, Tino, just give us some space. Okay?” Bobby knelt down as the football player moved back a bit. “Word has it you’re Mr. Foster.”

“Hugh,” the teacher said. He had a bleeding gash to the forehead.

“Well I’m Bobby. Is Tino one of your students?”

“Science.”

“Oh, that’s my favorite subject.” Bobby reached out to the man’s open hand, which had blood pooling in it from a cut somewhere up his arm. “Can you squeeze my finger?”

Buck and them waited anxiously as the man’s own fingers didn’t even twitch.

“I- I can’t feel a thing,” Hugh said. “Is that bad?”

Yes.

“It’s tough to say anything at this point,” was the neutral answer that Bobby gave. “But we’re gonna get you stabilized and we’re gonna get you out of here. Does that sound good?”

The teacher nodded. Bobby stepped back and lowered his voice enough that only Buck could hear him as he said,

“It’s a probably spinal injury. We’re gonna have to extract really gently to avoid any further damage.”

“That’s easier said than done,” Buck pointed out, though he was already thinking of how they’d manage it. Cutting through the metal would probably be safer than trying to lift anything, but they’d still have to shore up the metal to keep it from shifting too much on them. “He’s tangled in there good, Cap.”

“Then we do it slow, and we do it safe.”

“You got it.”

Extra hands would be needed for this, too. Maybe not for the cutting part, but to get everything stabilized and then drag the guy out. Buck didn’t think he could do it by himself and keep from moving Hugh too much. Maybe with Ravi and Bobby’s help, but he wanted to be sure.
