He looked around and spotted who he wanted easily. Eddie was busy helping to load a patient onto an ambulance, and he’d been tasked with helping Hen besides. But the other...

“Danny!” he called out as he jogged to the 118’s engine.

His brother’s head shot up, eyes landing on him instantly. He came over, Austin not far behind him, as Buck started getting everything out he thought he might need.

“S’up, squirt?”

“That.” Buck pointed at the mess of twisted metal with one of the wooden blocks they used for chocking. “Guy stuck in there with a spinal injury.”

Danny made a face when he glanced over.

“What’s the plan?”

“Uh, shore it up then cut him out,” Buck said. “He’s on a bleacher, or I think it’s a bleacher. It could serve as a backboard if we secure him to it somehow.”

“We could always duct tape him.”

“Duct tape’s there.” Austin pointed at a compartment on the engine, and then another. “Medkit’s there.”

Together, Buck and Danny managed to secure Hugh to the bleacher-turned-ad hoc backboard well enough, for the moment at least, with multiple rounds of duct tape. Next came the shoring up, hammering the blocks of wood at points that they thought – hoped – would stabilize the whole mess. Ravi helped with most of the grunt work for that one, while Danny got a c-collar secured onto Hugh to keep his neck immobile and then started tracking the man’s vitals.

“Tino seems pretty loyal,” Bobby remarked to the teacher. His grimace when Danny joined them had been subtle, but Buck still caught it. Danny, for his part, had simply called him “captain” and got to work. “He hasn’t left your side.”

“You’re still here, Tino?” Hugh asked in surprise.

“Yeah, I’m not going anywhere, Mr. F!” the kid called back.

There was bantering going on between the three of them, Bobby and Hugh and Tino. Buck barely paid it any mind as he focused on the shoring up everything. It didn’t take much longer, and they were about ready to start cutting when Ravi interrupted the proceedings with a shaking,

“Uh, Cap?”

He was pointing to the moving truck that he and Buck went to first. There was now smoke leaking out of the cargo area. Buck wasn’t sure if it was just smoke, though, because there was a concerning green tinge to it. Bobby led the way to checking it out, Buck and Danny following him out of concern and curiosity.

When Buck opened the back doors, more smoke came rolling out. There was a fire consuming the inside of it, maybe started by one of the still sparking cables that were hanging loose. But the most concerning thing were the tanks of chlorine gas in the middle of it all. Danny’s “oh, fuck” really summed it up quite well, in Buck’s opinion.

“I want everybody in breathing apparatus,” Bobby instructed after he updated dispatch about the hazardous material. He was moving quickly, as were Buck and Danny. “Evacuate the area. Anybody who can walk, get ‘em a thousand feet. And I want that fire knocked down, and a stream of fog on top of that smoke.”

Buck voiced his understanding while Danny simply nodded, the pair of them splitting up to their respective trucks. Buck got his SCBA gear on and grabbed the saw; he saw his brother and Austin haul one of the hoses to the truck and start spraying. Another pair of firefighters from the 133 were putting fog on top of the rising cloud, creating a ceiling that kept the chlorine from rising any higher and spreading any farther.

Thankfully, with Hugh secured in place and everything shored up, the task ahead of Buck now was more time consuming than difficult. The bleachers were new and made of aluminum, so they weren’t hard to cut through. There was just a lot of it, and he needed to be careful about where he put the saw. Not because he was afraid of cutting Hugh, but because he could still cause a minor collapse.

When he was done, extricating Hugh from the wreckage was a matter of delicately pulling out the bleacher bench-turned-backboard. By the time they did that and were loading him onto a gurney, Danny and Austin had contained the fire.

Buck was very much looking forward to putting his feet up after this one.

“How’s your sister?”

Daniel sighed and sat his beer down beside his phone on the counter, the glass clinking against the wood top. Alma had called as he was trying to figure out what to do for supper, his baby brother having heartlessly abandoned him to be with his boyfriend and his boyfriend’s kid. He’d just settled on a sandwich – he still had a couple bags of Middleswarth BBQ chips and some slices of Lebanon bologna – when his phone rang, and hadn’t let it go for too long before answering it.

Now, after the usual small talk and updates from Alma’s side – one of Celia’s professors spent all semester so far being a dick, Delia was debating changing her major again, and Iggy bought another godawful lawn gnome – they were on to the heftier subjects.

“Dunno,” Daniel answered her. “They ain’t exactly letting her call people from her padded cell.”

“Dan.”

“Yeah, yeah. The place is all about patient privacy, so they ain’t about to tell me shit. I gave them my number and Evan’s, so if we need to know about something, they’ll tell us. Until then…”

“I can’t imagine what she’s going through.” Alma’s words were soft. “It was rough before and after the girls were born, but at least I could trust myself.”

She’d given Daniel the rundown of her introduction to single motherhood years ago, had called the story a cliché herself. A silly girl in her last year of high school, a handsome boyfriend, a broken condom, and very conservative parents. The boyfriend ran the moment the test came back positive and her parents disowned her not long after. Daniel kinda wanted to meet all three of them in a dark alley.

“They were still babies when that Andrea Yates woman...”

“It could’ve gone so much worse, Alma.” Daniel remembered how Maddie had looked on that beach, soaked head-to-toe in the sea. He took a large drink of his beer to wash the taste of that moment from his mouth. “There was a, um. A call my first year here, a jumper. Except she’d already jumped by the time we got there. Just had her second kid.”

He heard Alma mutter something in Spanish, and he was able to understand enough of it to make out the shape of a prayer.

“Any idea how long Maddie’s gonna stay in Boston?”

“I don’t know. She promised Evan she’d come back, but…”

“But you don’t think she will?”

“I don’t think I’d be surprised if she decided to stay in Boston after they discharge her. Us Buckleys, once we run, we don’t really go back.”

Speaking of running, there was knocking at the door, sharp and quick. It wasn’t Athena, her knocking was firm, heavy, the knocking of a cop that still made him uneasy when he heard it. Unfortunately, everyone else that would reasonably show up at his door at this hour all had keys, which meant he was down to people that would unreasonably show up.

Daniel already knew he didn’t want to deal with it, even before he ended the call with Alma and found Chimney on the other side of the door.

“Maddie took Jee-Yun to the emergency room,” the other man said, shoving a piece of paper at Daniel on his way inside. It was an insurance in-voice.

“She took Jee-Yun to the emergency room,” Daniel echoed, biting back the choice list of curse words that wanted to spill out.

“It was during the black out,” Chimney said. “There was some kind of accident. Maddie was bathing her and-”

“Hold on. How do you know this?”

“I went to the ER she took Jee-Yun, the doctor that saw her told me,” he said. Daniel wondered if that violated patient confidentiality somehow. “But she was bathing her and Jee-Yun slipped under the water. That’s what she meant when she said Jee-Yun wasn’t safe with her.”

“But Jee-Yun is fine,” Daniel pointed out, and wondered where his niece was right now.

“Exactly! She’s fine! But Maddie is out there somewhere, beating herself up because she lost grip of a slippery child. She needs to know that this wasn’t her fault.”

Chimney was full of manic energy as he spoke, gesticulating widely. Buck had mentioned his worries, mainly because the man was going off on conspiracy theories like he was a guest on a History Channel show. Was this how he’d been then? Or was this him calmer, after finally finding a reasonable answer?

“Okay, so it wasn’t her fault,” Daniel blandly agreed. “What of it?”

“Wh- What of it!? I told you, she needs to know!” Chimney exclaimed. “She needs me. I’m going after her.”

Fuck.

“I’m pretty sure that’s the opposite of what she wanted,” Daniel pointed out. “Most people, when they run away, they don’t wanna be chased by whatever they’re running from.”

“But she exiled herself because she thinks she’s a danger to the baby,” was Chimney’s retort. “I can’t leave her alone like that, believing that, thinking the worst of herself. She needs to know.”

“What she needs is time. She just needs time and then she’ll come home.”

Daniel really hoped he was convincing when he said that.

The other man fell silent, a furrow deepening between his brows that suddenly cleared. He raised a hand, finger out as if he’d realized something.

“Why weren’t you surprised?” Chimney suddenly asked. “When I told you that Jee-Yun almost drowned, you didn’t even blink. It wasn’t until I said I wanted to go after Maddie… Did you know?”

Daniel cut a glance at the metal bat by the door. Old and reliable, a tool that had broken bones and warded off countless people that wanted to start something, and it was out of his reach. They were in the middle of the apartment, and Daniel never considered needing the bat for someone already inside.

“Yeah, I knew,” he casually confessed, shrugging at the other man’s outrage. “She told me.”

“She- She told you?”

“The morning she left Jee at the station,” Daniel said. “But I already knew about the postpartum depression.”

“How?”

“That truck bay ain’t exactly the best place to have a private conversation, man.”

Chimney blinked at him, and then he glowered.

“Jones.”

“Yeah. ‘Cause unlike you, he figured I should know that something was wrong with my sister.”

“She didn’t want you to know!” Chimney snapped at him. “Where is she, Dan?”

“She doesn’t want you to know.” It probably wasn’t smart, or nice, to grin at the man’s outrage. Daniel did it any way. It was all teeth. “And what she wants matters to me a helluva lot more than you do.”

“Eight days! I have been going through hell for eight days-”

“Oh, fuck you!” Daniel snapped. “You left her alone in the dark for five days, knowing that she was sick! She’s where she needs to be, getting the help that she needs to get. I took her to get the help she needs, which is more than you were doing for her. You couldn’t even be assed to ask Nash to let you do a wellness check, or have someone stay with her. So I don’t give one single solitary fuck about you, or what you want. Not when it’s your fault my sister almost-”

The fist connected with the side of Daniel’s face. It sent him staggering a bit before he regained his balance. Chimney was glaring at him, chest heaving. Daniel spat a wad of blood at his feet.

“She doesn’t want to see you,” he told the man. “She doesn’t want to talk to you. She. Doesn’t. Want. You.”

For a moment, Daniel thought Chimney was going to punch him again. Instead, the man stormed out of the apartment, slamming the door behind him with enough force that the pictures on the wall beside it rattled where they hung.

Eddie remembered what Buck had said about Chimney, when he came back from checking on him. He’d said the man was manic and chasing down rabbits. Eddie didn’t see it, or maybe Chimney had calmed down since then, because the man in his living room was looking rather settled.

“Buck’s at the store,” he told him, digging out a controller that was sticking halfway out from the cushions.

“Whatever,” was Chimney’s response. Something stormy passed over his face, but Eddie didn’t get a chance to figure out what it was about before he was talking again. “I did want to ask you about him, though.”

“Shoot.”

“Do you regret not chasing after him when he left?”

“Every day,” was Eddie’s immediate and honest response. Because it was still true, even after they found each other again. “You’re thinking about chasing after Maddie.”

“Yeah.”

“Well, if it’s my opinion you want,” Eddie said, “I think it’s the right thing to do. It’s what I should’ve done when he ran that day, instead of giving him space.”

“Okay.” Chimney nodded, seemingly more to himself than to Eddie. “I already thought that, I just…”

“Wanted someone else to tell you that you were doing the right thing.”

“Yeah.”

“You probably want to talk to Bobby, then,” Eddie said. “I’m not sure FMLA covers runaway baby mamas.”

Chimney snorted.

“I will.”

Somehow, Austin managed to exude judgment even as his face remained as implacable and stoic as ever. Daniel thought it was his eyes, or rather the little muscles around them and around his mouth. Tiny little microexpressions that managed to convey how much of an idiot Austin thought you were as easily as if it was on a billboard.

Or maybe Daniel was just able to read him that well.

“I’m not going to the ER,” he told Austin, rolling his eyes and lounging in a dining chair. “I’m fine.”

“Yeah, sure.” Austin shoved a fresh ice pack at Daniel’s face, making him yelp. He’d come over when Daniel told him what happened, even making a stop to buy everything needed to treat a black eye. “It’s perfectly fine that your eye is starting to swell shut.”

“It is not.” Daniel took over holding the pack in place. “’Sides, I’m a paramedic. I checked myself out and declared myself to be healthy.”

This time, Austin’s judgment was conveyed with a scowl.

“C’mon, you know me,” Daniel said. “Do you really think this is the first time I’ve gotten punched in the face?”

“That’s a good point. I’m surprised more people aren’t doing it.”

“Hey!”

“Word through the grapevine is that Chimney is taking off for a while.”

Daniel scoffed and let the hand holding the ice pack drop. Austin reached over, manipulating Daniel’s arm until he was holding the ice pack to his face once more.

“Oh, you know I like how you manhandle me,” he said with a grin, and didn’t receive any reaction. Austin didn’t even roll his eyes. “He’s chasing after Maddie, I guess.”

“Does he even know where to go?”

“No, not unless Buck told him. And I haven’t heard anything about Jee-Yun, so I’m guessing he’s taking an infant with him on his goose chase.”

It pissed Daniel off if he dwelled on it for too long. Chimney had no damn clue where to start looking for Maddie, he had no idea how long it was going to take, so of course he was gonna take his daughter along for the ride. Daniel just had to hope that the man wasn’t such a dumbass that he would keep Jee-Yun in a car seat for hours upon hours at a time.

Personally, he wasn’t about to hold his breath.

“Hand me my phone, would you?” he asked Austin. “A thought just occurred to me.”

“We should celebrate the occasion,” Austin dryly quipped as he handed the phone over.

Daniel stuck his tongue out at him, and then found the number in his contacts that he wanted. It was a recent addition.

“Hey, yeah, uh. I’m calling about one of your patients? Maddie Buckley? Something’s happened that she should probably know about...”

Chapter 10

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

“That is a nasty shiner you have there,” Dr. White said.

Daniel was sprawled out in one of the chairs in her office, taking up as much space as he could. The doctor was across from him in her own chair, perched primly, notebook closed in her lap. They’d returned to in-person sessions over the summer, at the same time they went to one session a month.

The last time he’d seen Dr. White was before Maddie, before the blackout. Then, they’d talked about Eddie returning to work finally, about how Buck was handling being back at the 118 under Captain Nash. They’d talked about Daniel’s worries regarding Maddie pulling away again, and they’d talked about Austin.

Dr. White liked getting him to talk about Austin. And that was fine, even great. Daniel loved talking about Austin, almost as much he did Buck. The problem was that she kept circling back to the topic of Daniel’s feelings and his silence there upon. She seemed to think he was practicing self-destructive behavior in the way he stayed in a “fake relationship” – she didn’t make air quotes when she said it, but he knew she wanted to – when he had actual feelings for Austin and he knew Austin didn’t return them.

Daniel, very politely, reminded her that he wasn’t seeing her for relationship advice and to kindly keep those opinions to herself.

Only, it wasn’t actually very polite. Or even a little polite.

“My sister’s baby daddy punched me.”

Dr. White arched an eyebrow, so blond it was nearly her namesake color.

“Why would he do that?” she asked, in a tone that suggested she knew that not only was there a reason, but that there was a very good reason.

“’Cause he’s a bitch that can’t take criticism,” Daniel stated. Dr. White was not impressed with that answer, he could tell. “He found out I knew where Maddie was and didn’t tell him.”

She regarded him in silence for a moment, and then let out a little hum.

“We appear to have skipped over some of the story,” she said. “It sounds like you had an eventful month since you were last here.”

“Yeah.” Daniel glared at the wall and then sighed. “My sister has postpartum depression, that’s why she was being weird. I found out during the blackout.”

He told her the story, from Austin’s text near the end of the blackout, to the wellness check on Maddie that he did, to her calling him the next day. He told her about secreting her away to that hotel in Oxnard and then to the women’s clinic in Boston. He told her about Maddie swearing both him and Buck to secrecy. He told her about Chimney finding out and confronting him.

“It was basically a love tap.” Daniel shrugged. “I’ve been smacked around worse.”

“Just because you’ve had worse doesn’t mean you should downplay it,” Dr. White tutted. “So your sister is in Boston? You’ve talked to her since you escorted her there?”

“She’s still an in-patient, but yeah. They let her call after I told them about Chimney. I didn’t tell her about the punch, though.”

“Why?”

“’Cause it didn’t seem worth it? I told you, this is nothin’.”

“But you didn’t believe it worth mentioning that her romantic partner lashed out physically at you before chasing her across the country.”

Daniel gave her a narrow-eyed glared. He knew what she was getting at, he knew the comparison she was trying to oh-so-subtly make. He just wasn’t sure he appreciated it.

“If I thought he was Doug-lite, I’d’ve already bashed his head like a watermelon by now,” Daniel bluntly told her. She didn’t even bat an eye at the statement. “’Sides, it doesn’t matter. He doesn’t know where she is and, even if he did, they wouldn’t let him see her.”

“And you aren’t concerned about your niece?”

“What was I gonna do? Keep him from taking her with him?” Daniel scoffed. “She ain’t my kid.”

Yeah, he was worried about Chimney being an idiot, worried about Jee-Yun being stuck in a car seat for too long. But there wasn’t anything he could do about it. Father and legal guardian trumped uncle, after all, and while CPS trumped legal guardian, there was no way a case would’ve been picked up to be investigated in that small window of time before Chimney left the city, county, and then state. Especially not over worries about what might happen.

“She tried to kill herself.” Daniel felt hollow as he said it. He stared at the wall again, just over Dr. White’s shoulder, instead of at Dr. White herself. It was painted an inoffensive pale blue. “She tried to drown herself. She didn’t say it, I didn’t ask. But I know she did. She'd already decided to do it when I saw her the night before, when she told me everything was fine. She looked me in the eye and didn’t even tell me goodbye.”

There was a tiny part of him that he wasn’t certain would ever forgive her for that.

When the session was over, Daniel went, as he often did afterwards, to Austin’s place. Not because it was particularly harrowing, but simply because he wanted to. He let himself in, finding Austin stretched out on the couch with a book that he must’ve just started.

“I hope that’s not one of those Harcourt books,” he said, making a beeline to plunder the bowl full of candy that sat on the coffee table.

The benefit of Halloween being right around the corner: bags and bags of fun-sized Skittle packets.

“Why would I read one of those?” Austin asked without looking up from the book. There was a chick with a bow and a guy with a sword on the front cover. “They didn’t magically get better when he died.”

“A bodice ripper?”

This time, Austin deigned to give him a flat look. Daniel grinned in response.

“It’s the new R. A. Salvatore book,” Austin said. “New-ish. It came out in August.”

“It looks like a nerd book.” Daniel flopped onto the couch, Austin wordlessly making room for him. They still ended up overlapping and twining together. “I told Buck I was in a bar fight.”

“A bar fight.” Austin’s words were flat and toneless, yet still managed to come across as judgmental.

“Yeah. If he asks for details, I dunno, try and make me sound badass.” Daniel tapped the book in Austin’s hands. “You should read to me.”

He heard (and felt) Austin sigh then flip through the pages.

“Prologue. The Year of the Nether Mountain Scrolls. Dalereckoning 1486,” Austin read, a bunch of nerd words that might as well have been Greek, for all the difference it would’ve made to Daniel. “Sleep seemed shorter and shorter. Why hadn’t twilight come? Where was the night? Had she missed it during her many naps? Had she lost track of time itself? It seemed to her as if most of the day had passed...”

It was weird at the station after Chimney took off in search of Maddie. Maybe it was the way that it felt as if everyone at work were stepping around the subject. Maybe it was being partnered with the probie while Eddie was with Hen. Or maybe it was just Buck making things weird.

Things were a bit weird outside of work, too. Not only was Chimney being very short with him over texts and calls, sometimes ignoring him entirely, but when he came over to check on Danny after he declined an invitation for dinner at Eddie’s – something he would never do normally – Buck found him with a black eye. Because he’d gotten into a bar fight of all things.

Buck wasn’t sure he believed him, but it was the only explanation he was getting. He knew he didn’t get it at work, because he asked the others. Also, Albert would’ve told him if Danny got hurt on a call like that. Or he was supposed to, anyway. What was the point of bribing the guy with food if he didn’t do what he was bribed for?

None of this was helped by Buck still feeling guilty over keeping secrets from Chimney. Sure, him being gone helped a little, if only because out of sight was almost literally out of mind for him sometimes. But it didn’t just go away, especially when something reminded him about Chimney being gone.

“Okay,” Bobby said as they clambered out of the engine. They were in residential territory, at an apartment complex. The ambulance was parked right behind them. “Buck, Eddie is with Hen, so you and Ravi grab the forcible entry tools.”

“He means the ram,” Buck translated for the probie as he went for the proper compartment.

“Follow my lead,” he heard Hen tell Eddie. They’d ridden in the ambulance, another bit of weird. “And try not to step on my toes.”

“Luckily for you, I’m an excellent dancer,” Eddie retorted, and then sent a warning glare Buck’s way when Buck laughed.

It wasn’t because Buck didn’t believe him or because it wasn’t true. Quite the opposite, it was because Buck had seen the trophies for ballroom dancing competitions, hidden away in the closet of Eddie’s childhood bedroom in El Paso. Buck could say something, make it into a thing like Eddie and the others made Bobby’s ice skating into a thing. Later, maybe.

They all crowded into the entryway of the apartment, space at a premium because of the narrowness of it and the metal staircase going to the next floor. Bobby pounded on the door, but when he didn’t get an immediate answer, Buck handed the probie the ram. The probie excitedly – was Buck like that when he was starting out? – forced the door open, swinging the ram neatly and splintering wood.

Their patient – Craig, according to Bobby – was in the bathroom just down the hall from the front door. The poor guy was on the toilet, oddly bent over at the waist, a roll of toilet paper on the floor in front of him and just out of reach. Buck grimaced in sympathy.

There were few things he could think of that were worse than needing to be rescued from the bathroom in the middle of taking a shit.

One of those Narcissus exercise mirrors, either horribly or very appropriately named, was leaning against the wall. It was part mirror, part video screen, and was basically a really fancy version of the old exercise tapes they used to sale. Buck had seen the infomercial for it one night during a twenty-four, at that awful time when nothing was on but he was too wired for sleep.

“Nice swing,” Buck told Ravi, snapping a splinter the rest of the way off from the door frame. “C’mon.”

The others, Bobby and Eddie and Hen, had gone to the patient. Buck took the probie to put away the ram and grab the gurney, which he figured was needed. If Craig could move on his own, he would not be on the toilet still.

“You have to put the battering ram back with the handle facing outwards,” Buck explained to an attentive probie. “It saves time that way. And Austin gets upset if you don’t put things back the right way.”

“Who’s Austin?” Ravi asked.

Buck sighed.

“Jones. The driver.” Who was probably taking a nap in the driver’s seat right now.

“Oh!”

Ravi nodded, but his attention clearly wandered. Buck was just closing the compartment door when he heard whirring motors.

“This mechanized gurney is awesome.” Ravi was playing with the buttons, raising it up and down. “The B-shift guys never let me use it.”

“That’s ‘cause it’s not a toy,” Buck told him. “Stop playing with it.”

Except the probie didn’t listen. Buck huffed, rolled his eyes, went over to make him stop and wondering the entire time if this was how Danny felt with him.

“Probie,” he bit out, “I said stop playing with-”

The ends of the gurney snapped up as it folded like a taco. Buck didn’t even know it could fold like a taco. Buck didn’t know how someone could make it fold like a taco.

“What did you do?”

Ravi shrugged, sheepish and helpless and looking very much like someone who needed yelling at.

“Where’s that gurney, Buck?” Bobby asked over the radio.

Still glaring at Ravi, Buck thumbed the radio to answer.

“Uh. Small technical issue with that, Cap.”

“What kind of issue?”

“Bringing the stair chair ASAP,” he replied instead of answered, then let go of the radio to fix a stern finger at the probie. “Keep your hands to yourself.”

The stair chair was stowed away in the ambulance, and its wheels squeaked the entire way to the apartment.

“Wheels could probably use some WD-40 when we get back to the station,” Buck commented to Ravi as he backed the chair through the front door. He debated whether or not to make the probie do it, caught sight again of the gurney-taco outside, and decided that was too much responsibility to trust Ravi with.

Speaking of-

“Have you seen these workout mirrors?” Ravi was gazing into the Narcissus, posing a little. “So crazy.”

“Hey! Focus.” Buck was making Danny whatever dinner he wanted after this, because it was the least he deserved if this was even a fraction of what he had to deal with when Buck was a kid. “You get distracted and people can die.”

“I’m gonna die,” Craig whimpered from the bathroom.

Whoops.

“No, you’re gonna take it easy,” Hen told him. She and Eddie had Craig in a c-collar and hooked up an IV. And one of them had flushed the toilet. “No more crazy workouts trying to fit in. Just do you.”

“Someone went too hard, too fast?” Buck guessed.

“Yup,” Eddie confirmed. “Three-hundred sit-ups.”

“Yeowch. Gotta start small, man,” Buck told Craig. “And don’t forget the warm-ups.”

Hen and Bobby moved out of the way, allowing Buck and Eddie to lift Craig off his porcelain throne and into his new wheeled one. After pulling up the guy’s shorts for him. It probably demolished more of Craig’s dignity, but he figured letting him go to the ER with them around his knees would be worse.

Also, the skid marks.

“You guys are pretty jacked,” Craig said when he was lowered into the chair. “What kind of workout do you do?”

“I give up,” Hen sighed.

“What happened to the gurney?” Bobby asked as they got Craig strapped in.

“Yeah, probie.” Buck raised his voice a little so it carried. “What happened to the gurney?”

“I don’t want to know, do I?”

“You’re about to find out.”

Hen, who’d gone ahead of them, definitely did. And she was not happy about it, based on her angry shout of,

“What happened to my gurney!?”

Daniel thought, perhaps a bit foolishly, that Buck graduating high school meant that he wouldn’t receive any calls from any principals asking him to come in to discuss behavior or grades. But apparently, it was silly of him to think that the tiny fact of him not even having any kids enrolled in any schools would mean he wouldn’t be getting calls from principals. It would make sense if it was Durand calling about Chris, because he knew he was on that list. But it wasn’t.

It was Powell Middle School.

It was Harry Grant.

It was a very sulky looking Harry Grant, who refused to lift his head to meet Daniel’s eye. The office they were in appeared much the same as any other school office, with posters on the wall that were different from when he and Buck were in school only because they were newer. Daniel hadn’t been told why he needed to come down, beyond there being an incident involving “your son.” An odd word choice, and a telling one.

“Thank you for coming down, Mr. Grant,” said the principal, a Ms. Pincus.

Their system must still be a mess from that ransomware attack, if they thought he was Michael.

Daniel debated correcting her, wondered what she would do if he did. He debated calling Athena or Michael, even briefly thought about calling Nash. But while Harry wasn’t his kid, he’d still called him, specifically. Or had the school call him, at least. That meant something, and the least he could do was find out why before ratting the twerp out.

“So what did he do?” Daniel asked.

It was easy to call up that old practiced tone of exasperation and disappointment that the teachers ate up. A lot of drama was prevented just by giving at least the appearance of agreeing with them at the start, and it usually made the indignant anger a bit more effective later, if necessary.

The principal sighed and gestured for him to follow her into her smaller, personal office. Plants in the window, degrees on the wall, name placard on the desk. Daniel sat down in one of the chairs in front of the desk, while she sat in the rolling chair behind it.

“I want to be clear,” Ms. Pincus said. “We’re all very sensitive to the fact that Harry has been through a significant trauma. We’ve done our best to be patient, but his acting out has reached the point-”

“Acting out?”

“He’s been ill-tempered,” she said. “Not turning in his homework, using expletives with his teachers. But now we’ve moved past the point of understanding, and we need to take action. Harry is being suspended for one week.”

“Suspended? Detentions would make sense for all that, but suspension seems a bit much.”

“He’s not being suspended for that,” she said. “He’s being suspended for punching another student.”

Ah. There was really nothing Daniel could do for Harry there.

“Thanks for coming,” Harry said as the left the building. They’d stopped to get his things from his locker.

“You had them call me,” Daniel pointed out. “The kid you punched, did he go down?”

“Yeah!”

“Good job.”

It probably wasn’t appropriate to praise Harry for something like that, but he wasn’t the kid’s parent. A good punch was a good punch. Especially since it didn’t seem like he broke anything or bust his knuckles; Daniel made sure to check when they got to his car.

“You know I’m telling your parents about this,” he pointed out as he turned the ignition. “Right?”

“What? No! That’s why I gave them your number!”

“And if you hadn’t gotten suspended, that would’ve worked. They’re gonna notice it when you don’t go to school tomorrow. Next time, do this shit off school grounds or somewhere you won’t get caught.”

That was the problem with schools these days, too many security cameras and not enough places to have informative talks with snot nosed bullies without people getting upset about it.

“I could stay with you?” Harry was so hopeful with his suggestion.

Daniel crushed that hope without an ounce of guilt.

“I still have to work- No, you’re not coming to the station. Mendoza or Albert would probably mention it to Buck, and then he’d ask Nash about it.”

Not to mention all the problems involved with having a minor staying in his apartment for any length of time without said minor’s parents knowing. Especially a minor that was kidnapped not too long ago. And while he thought he could possibly take Athena in a fight, he was not willing to put it to the test. Especially if it was over one of her kids.

Harry sulked adorably all the way to the Grant-Nash residence. Athena wasn’t home, which was not ideal. Daniel would much rather deal with her. Instead, he got to see the gobsmacked expression on Nash’s face when he opened the door to see them there.

“Harry. Dan.” the captain stated a bit dumbly as the kid pushed his way past into the house. “What?”

“Athena’s gonna raise hell at that school,” Daniel remarked. He leaned against the wall, not especially desiring to go in when it was just Nash. “They didn’t even check my ID.”

“His school. What?”

“Harry’s suspended for the week-”

“What?”

“-for punching a kid-”

“What?”

“And apparently he’s been cussing out his teachers and not doing homework.”

Nash stared at him for a long moment, unblinking.

“Why did his school call you?” he asked, the question understandable but not the scathing tone it was asked in.

“’Cause he gave ‘em my number,” Daniel told him. “Kid knows how to throw a proper punch. You guys should be proud.”

Nash slammed the door in his face.

Chapter End Notes

The timeline gets kinda funky around this part of the season, especially since they rearranged some episodes prior to airing. 5x05 through 5x09 land somewhere between early October and Christmas. There's been enough time for Buck's bruise to go away completely, and 5x12 has Maddie finishing her in-patient stay, completing her treatment for her postpartum thyroiditis, and then moving into an apartment in Boston before she calls Buck at the end of 5x05, which... I don't know how long the in-patient stay and the thyroiditis treatment would take, so I can't really judge how much time 5x12 retconned into existence between episodes. But in 5x05 there's the implication of a short window of time (everything's still weird, Hen and Eddie are still figuring out how to work together - and Bobby felt the need to specify he was pairing them up because of Chimney's absence, so there's that implication of them just now starting.) while 5x12 makes it seem like the window of time is much longer.

Chapter 11

There was, perhaps, the possibility that Buck was taking things a bit too seriously, that he was taking it a bit too far. Going overboard with things was a pattern with him since adolescence, part and partial of what made him too much for so many people throughout his life. So, yeah, he probably was going too far with this.

That didn’t stop him, though.

“Probie!” Buck bellowed as he stomped up the stairs, and then again, “Probie!”

Bobby had assigned Buck to be Ravi’s training partner. And Buck was taking that task very seriously, making sure Ravi knew everything he needed to know. He even had a clipboard with something like a lesson plan, or perhaps it was more accurate to say that he had a clipboard with a list of things he kept adding onto every time he thought of something the probie should know. Like how to take the blade of a chainsaw out of the motor.

Last shift, Ravi choked on a call. It wasn’t even one of their more gruesome calls, just a dude with a chainsaw stuck in his neck. Bobby asked for the two of them, Buck and Ravi, to remove the saw’s blade from the motor. And that’s when Ravi choked. Or, rather, went off to go puke somewhere he wouldn’t be in the way while Bobby and Buck did the removing.

Thus, now. Buck was adamant about making sure Ravi could disassemble a chainsaw, because that was not the first chainsaw accident they responded to and it would not be the last. If Ravi was gonna be on their shift, if Ravi was gonna be filling in for Eddie while Eddie filled in for Chimney, then he needed to know this shit. Hell, even if he went back to B-shift or moved to another station, he would still have people expecting that they could depend on him.

So Buck was gonna teach Ravi how to take apart the chainsaw, and then he was gonna make Ravi do it over and over again until he could do it while puking. Unfortunately, Ravi seemed to not be in agreement with this plan. The probie had pulled an Austin, managing to disappear somewhere in the station. The latest spot Buck had checked was the loft, but he wasn’t there.

Hen was, though.

“Have you seen the probie?” he asked her.

She dropped the book she was reading.

“So this is what it’s come to?” she asked in turn. “He gets a little queasy at a scene and now it’s off with his head?”

“No.” Buck drew the word out as he shifted his grip on the chainsaw he was carrying. “I just want to show him how to disassemble one of these.”

“Ah.” Hen’s nod felt condescending. She simply didn’t understand. “No, Buck. I haven’t seen him.”

“If you do, let him know I’m looking for him.”

Where were all the best places to hide in this station? Buck didn’t know them, not all of them. But he knew someone that did, and luckily, the person wasn’t squirreled away like he usually was. Instead, the man was on the creeper beneath the engine.

“What do you want, baby Buckley?” Austin asked.

“How did you know I was here?” Buck asked.

The man was currently beneath the engine, after all. There was no way he could’ve seen him.

Austins arm gripped the outside of the engine and pushed, sending himself rolling out from under it. The man stared at Buck, thoughts impossible to read.

“Nothing about you is quiet,” Austin said. “What do you want?”

“Where’re all the places to hide in here?”

Austin stared at him again. If Danny was here, he’d be able to read him so much better than Buck could.

“The probie wouldn’t know those yet,” Austin said, and Buck didn’t ask him how he knew it was about Ravi. Even if Buck wasn’t shouting it out, Austin just knew things. Buck had come to accept this. “Try the captain’s office.”

“Why the captain’s office?”

“Because it’s the place you hide when you think you’re clever, but you’re still a rookie.”

Buck, personally, would never hide in the captain’s office. Admittedly, that was because if he were to try and hide, it would probably be because he was trying to hide from Bobby. But Ravi wasn’t him, and he wasn’t hiding from Bobby, and Buck figured Austin was the expert.

Faith was rewarded when Buck opened the door to the office to find not only Ravi, but also Hen. They seemed to have been in a middle of a conversation, Hen breaking off in the middle of her sentence as she turned at his entrance. Ravi went wide-eyed and then ducked beneath the desk.

“The drill sergeant from hell has arrived,” Hen said, a bit dryly.

“He knows that I know he didn’t just disappear, right?” Buck asked.

“Yes I did!” Ravi said from beneath the desk, which rather ruined the intent of hiding.

“I think you’re going a bit too hard on the kid,” Hen said, after rolling her eyes in the desk’s direction. “He deserves a break.”

“He got twenty minutes while I was trying to find him,” Buck pointed out. “That’s a break.”

Hen’s stare expressed very well how not impressed with him she was.

“Is someone going to die because he doesn’t know how to take apart a chainsaw?” she asked and then, the moment he opened his mouth, cut him off with, “In the next thirty minutes.”

“Probably not,” he admitted. “Okay, fine. But if our next call out involves another chainsaw, I reserve the right to be really annoying about it.”

“You’re always annoying. Go bother Eddie.”

Buck stuck his tongue out at her and blew a raspberry, but he went and did as she said anyway.

The next time the alarm rang and they were sent out, though, was not about another chainsaw accident. Instead, they were sent to a fire at a nursing home, or a retirement center, or whichever euphemism the place used to describe where the elderly were deposited when they couldn’t live by themselves. It looked like one of the nicer ones, too, the kind where the employees were paid enough to actually care.

Their trucks and ambulance pulled to a screeching halt in front of stairs that probably weren’t elderly friendly. There was a fountain in the middle of the drive, where an orderly was seeing to an old man. Other residents and employees were dotted about, making as quick of an evacuation as they could.

“Dispatch said the fire started with an explosion inside the chapel,” Bobby told them as they pulled on their SCBA gear. “Basement level, bravo side of the building.”

“Chapel?” Eddie sounded incredulous. He’d once again driven the ambulance with Hen.

“Someone piss off the big guy?” Hen asked.

“No word on point of origin,” Bobby continued. “Buck, Ravi, you guys are in the lead. Run a hose through the front door and start knocking it down.”

Ravi leaped up and grabbed a hose.

“Wrong hose, probie,” Buck barked out, reaching up to grab the correct one. “Inch and three-quarter.”

The probie didn’t say anything, but he grabbed the other 1 ¾ inch hose.

Buck led the way into the building, trusting that Austin would get them hooked up to the tank. The stairs to the basement were in the common area of the center, smoke billowing up from it like a chimney. When they went down, they found mostly darkness, their lights cutting through the haze to show them the way. The chapel was easily found, doors blown clean off and flames devouring the interior.

“Cap!” Buck called out, and then pointed to where a stirring orderly was beneath one of the doors. “Got a live one here!”

“Copy that.”

He and Ravi entered the chapel and turned the hoses on at full blast. Water sprayed out, hitting the flames. Behind them, Hen was seeing to the orderly, Bobby radioing for a gurney. Eddie shouted out as he found another victim, under a pew.

They got the orderly out, but Eddie was still treating the old man. Buck only half Except, it just kept spreading. It didn’t matter how much water they doused the flames with, they burned and burned and burned. They weren’t the normal orange anymore, either, they’d turned into a bright, nearly cherry red. Something was wrong, but hell if Buck knew what that was. And the only solution he had for it wasn’t working. He could only hope to keep the fire from spreading farther, however fruitless that hope seemed to be at the moment.

And then the probie shut off his hose.

“What the hell are you doing!?” Buck snapped at him.

“Those chemicals over there, they’re reacting to water.” Ravi pointed and Buck finally spotted the set-up hidden between the now burning pews. He’d been too focused on the fire itself to see it. “We need CO2.”

Buck swore and twisted his nozzle off, too, dropping the hose and followed the probie as he turned to go back to the engine.

“Good catch,” Buck told him, and then grabbed his radio. “Austin, we need CO2.”

By the time the pair of them got back with the extinguishers, the fire had spread, and Bobby and Eddie were trying to carry their patient out. Thankfully, the CO2 did what the water could not, killing the ravenous monster before it could cause more damage. Bobby switched with Ravi, making the probie help with carrying the patient out, while he investigated the source of the fire.

“That’s a meth lab,” Buck observed when he joined Bobby’s side. He figured even Danny would frown at someone setting up a meth lab in a chapel, of all places.

“It’s someone’s idea of a retirement,” the captain countered. “The manager said Felix insisted on being down here, but she didn’t know why.”

The problem with the nicer retirement centers was that they were expensive. Apparently, Felix, the old man, came up with making and selling meth to cover the costs. He also used it to buy his wife pretty jewelry, because they were literally trying to keep up with the Joneses.

(No relation to Austin. Buck asked.)

When they returned, when they hung up their gear and washed off the smoke and did all the post-call out chores, Buck hunted down Ravi again. It was easier this time, because Bobby was using his office. A lot of paperwork came with uncovering a meth lab at a fire, after all.

The rooftop of the station was actually one of the hiding places Austin never used. Buck wasn’t sure why that was. Ravi had decided to use it, though, and he about leaped out of his skin when Buck slammed open the door to the stairwell. The poor probie looked like a deer that couldn’t decide which way to run.

“Danny and I had this neighbor, Crazy Carl,” Buck said. He went up to the wall surrounding the roof and leaned back against it. “He had his set-up in his kitchen until Danny made him move it to a shed behind the apartment building. He helped me pass chemistry.”

Ravi gaped at him.

“You… You were tutored in chemistry by a guy who made meth in his kitchen?”

“Yeah. Why not?” Buck shrugged. “You haven’t lost anyone yet, have you? On a call I mean.”

“Oh, um.” Ravi was rocking on his heels anxiously. “No.”

“Lucky. I lost my first one my fourth month on the job,” Buck told him. “At the beginning, when I lost people on the job, I couldn’t help but to go over how it could’ve gone better if I’d been better. If I’d been able to do this faster, if I’d known what to do here...”

Ravi blinked at him, lips parted a bit.

“Oh.”

“It’s time, by the way. That’s usually where we mess up. In this job, every second counts. If you don’t know what you’re doing, if you grab the wrong thing and have to go back, that’s less time you have to help. You want to be able to do something without having to stop and think about it.”

“That… That makes sense.”

“But it’s not just…” Buck turned his head and glanced down. Austin had the engine pulled out and was doing the maintenance check with Ortiz; the cab was up and the innards were exposed. “I went to my first line-of-duty funeral when I was fifteen. They’d been called to a structure fire, two alarm. Danny was there, actually. The guy fell through the floor because he wasn’t paying attention. It was closed casket.”

Buck remembered, at first, how he’d thought his big brother being a firefighter was so cool, as if Danny wasn’t already the coolest. He remembered hanging out at the station, sometimes, when he could get away with it, and he remembered bugging Danny for exciting stories. And he remembered sitting in that pew, in a crowded church, in his brand new dress clothes, and realizing that it could’ve been Danny in that casket.

“Hen said you were hyper-focused on protecting everyone here because Chimney and your sister were gone,” Ravi said. “She also said you were trying to make sure I was ready and that I was worthy.”

Buck made a face at that.

“I don’t get to decide if you’re worthy, Ravi. That’s all on you.” He pushed himself off the short wall. “Cap said dinner should be ready soon. C’mon.”

Buck knew that Danny didn’t like being alone at the apartment, that he didn’t do well alone. Normally, Buck would entice his brother into coming over to Eddie’s. Any excuse worked, really, because Danny didn’t want to be alone either. But he and Eddie had also started doing surprise sleepovers at the apartment. Chris, as Eddie predicted when he suggested the idea, didn’t complain. He loved it, in fact.

(There was, admittedly, a system in place where Buck would check in with Austin before doing either one. He didn’t think Danny had yet to realize that the invites and the sleepovers only happened whenever he wasn’t at Austin’s.)

Tonight was a surprise sleepover night. Buck showed up with Eddie and Chris, and a couple pizzas. Danny, as always, brightened up when they let themselves in, sitting up from the sad nest he’d made on the couch while he watched trashy television and dusted off the crumbs from the chips he’d been eating. Games were played, food eaten.

And then Danny’s phone rang.

It didn’t immediately cut through the conversation. Chris didn’t pause in his story of his school day, and Eddie didn’t stop listening to him. But Buck recognized the ringtone and his attention zeroed in on his brother’s phone, sitting on the kitchen counter. He got up at the same time Danny did and padded over to it with anxious anticipation bubbling up in his gut.

Danny picked up the phone, but he hesitated a moment, sending an unreadable expression Eddie’s way. Then he answered with,

“Hey, Maddie.”

“Hey, Dan,” she replied, sounding so much less tired than when Buck heard her last.

Something hit the ground. Buck turned to see that it was the controller Eddie had been using. Eddie himself was staring at them, eyes wide and jaw hanging loose. That anxious anticipation in Buck’s belly joined the guilt and created a pit.

“How’s it going?” Danny was staring straight at Eddie, Buck noticed.

“Good. I- I’m doing the outpatient treatment now.”

“That’s awesome!” Buck blurted out.

“That’s a bit soon, isn’t it?” Danny asked. “The PPD is all fixed up, just like that?”

“It wasn’t postpartum depression, it was postpartum thyroiditis. It was my thyroid. They gave me some hormone therapy and…”

“That means you can come home. You can do the outpatient here,” Buck said, letting the excitement and hope build a little. “R- Right?”

“Um.” A single syllable extinguished the excitement and hope. “I don’t think I’m ready for that yet, Evan. I still have a lot to work through. I just signed a short-term lease for an apartment.”

“Oh.”

Yet. She said yet. She said it was short-term. That meant she wouldn’t stay in Boston forever.

“He probably hates me anyway,” Maddie said. Buck didn’t need her to specify who she was talking about. “After Jee-Yun and the way I left.”

“He doesn’t hate you.” Danny rolled his eyes. “Not you. Don’t think he’d be running blindly across the country if he did.”

“I’m still not ready to see him,” she said. “He’s in Utah, now. Or was. He sent me a video of Jee-Yun crawling.”

Danny flinched at the words. Neither he nor Buck had been told about that. Neither of them were really being kept up to date about their niece.

“Email me the video.” Danny’s voice was even as he made his request. “I think he forgot to send it to me.”

“I will. She’s adorable, Dan.”

“That’s the Buckley in her.”

Maddie didn’t stay on the phone for much longer, much to Buck’s disappointment. When she hung up, when he refocused on his boyfriend, he found the man with a stormy expression on his face. Eddie didn’t say anything, not then; he started to, and then glanced Chris’ way. But he was noticeably more distant afterwards, colder, until Chris was tucked away in Danny’s bed.

And then he spoke his mind.

“Have you two known where she was this entire time!?” He was making an impressive effort to keep his voice down.

“Yes.” Danny looked and sounded like he didn’t care that Eddie was so obviously pissed off at them. He was relaxed against the kitchen counter. “I took her there.”

“You- That week Buck kept making excuses for why-” Eddie’s glared focused on Buck. “You lied to me.”

Buck opened his mouth to defend himself, couldn’t think of any words that would, and shut it again.

“You’ve both known where she was,” Eddie said. “And you didn’t think to tell anyone? Tell Chimney? Tell me that you knew?”

“I didn’t want you to have to lie to Chimney.” Buck wrapped his arms around himself. “He’s your friend.”

“I wouldn’t have to lie if you’d told him!”

“She didn’t want Chimney to know where she is,” Danny said. “She still doesn’t. You heard her. He shouldn’t have chased after her anyway.”

“It was the right thing to do,” Eddie said. “It’s what I should have done.”

“If you’d chased me when I left, I never would’ve stopped running,” Buck told him. It was the truth, in all its blunt glory. “I would’ve run farther.”

“He deserves to know where she is,” Eddie bit out. “He deserves to know that she’s okay.”

He glared at them both, then turned on his heel and stomped into Danny’s room. The door didn’t slam shut behind him only because, even pissed off, he was thinking about Chris. The living room was silent in his wake.

(Buck sent Chimney a text that night, laying alone in his bed. Three simple words. It didn’t do anything to make the guilt go away.)

Chapter 12

Chapter Notes

cw: canon typical violence and the aftermath

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Then

Austin had given him a key a while ago, saying it only made sense if they were going to successfully fake being boyfriends, but Daniel still wasn’t quite comfortable with just letting himself in. Instead, he knocked beneath the shiny 5C and waited for the door to open. He brought with him a six pack from the store, a lager like Austin preferred, as his contribution to the dinner he knew the other man was cooking.

“You could have just let yourself in,” Austin said when he opened the door.

“Could’ve,” Daniel agreed as he entered the apartment. He sniffed the air, stomach rumbling at the aroma of cooking food filling the air. “Got your beer. What’s for dinner?”

“Stir-fry. Chicken and broccoli.”

“Nice.”

Daniel stuck the six pack in Austin’s fridge, covered in take out menus and coloring book pages and pictures of other Joneses, snagging a couple bottles before closing the door. He opened them both, setting Austin’s by the stove where the man went back to the food. He took his own to the couch, settling into it and toeing off his shoes.

There was a sweater thrown over the back of the couch, hanging right beside where Daniel sat. It was dark blue, knitted, with diamond patterns. It smelled like Austin, like that vaguely chocolate scented body spray he used that always reminded Daniel of days spent at Hershey Park with Evan and Maddie. Daniel had the oddest, strongest urge to crawl into it.

“Buck got high scores all around, apparently,” he said, turning away from the soft looking temptation. “He’s out celebrating with those friends of his.”

His face twisted at the mention of them. He’d met the bunch, Connor and the others, a couple times, and been to that house in Arcadia once. If that was anything close to what the college experience was, then he was glad to have missed out on it.

“What’s his station assignment?” Austin asked. “Did he get it yet?”

“Uh, well.” Daniel laughed as he twisted around to grin at Austin over the back of the couch. “About that…”

Austin paused in the process of portioning out the stir fry onto plates. He set the skillet back down, and the spatula he’d been using, and turned to face Daniel. Curiosity flickered across his handsome features.

“I didn’t think they’d put him in your house.”

“They didn’t.” Daniel felt his grin splitting his face. “They put him in yours.”

Austin blinked. It was his only expression of surprise.

“Oh,” he said, going back to their dinner. “I guess he’s replacing Kinard. Do we tell him?”

Even without the vague motion he made with the spatula, gesturing between the both of them, Daniel would know what Austin was referring to. They were a year and a half into their fake relationship now, and Daniel had made several more appearances at family functions since that first dinner with Mr. and Mrs. Jones. He’d charmed the pants off both of them, and then the extended members of the family. He’d even got something like a shovel talk from Austin’s younger brother, Ethan, which he managed not to laugh through. It was like being threatened by a puppy.

Austin’s sister, Cynthia, was a bit of a mystery, though. She’d seemed so confused the entire time Austin was doing introductions, the first time Daniel met her, and Austin had seemed vaguely embarrassed. Now, whenever they crossed paths, she always gave the impression of being in on a joke that Daniel wasn’t privy to.

Buck didn’t know about it, though. Daniel couldn’t say why he hadn’t told him, except that it was close enough to what he did in El Paso that the old instinct to keep it separate from his baby brother reared its head. Now it was just habit, one that should probably be broken.

Except…

“Do you remember what I said about my parents?” Daniel asked. “How yours were so different?”

“Yeah.” Austin came over with the plates, managing to balance them and his drink. “You love ‘em, but you hate ‘em.”

That hadn’t quite been what Daniel said, but it also wasn’t a bad summation.

“Right.” He sat properly on the couch as he took his plate. “Buck used to hurt himself for Mom’s attention. Get himself hurt. It got better as he got older, but he also decided on football as the sport he wanted to play, so maybe it didn’t.”

Daniel remembered how hard some of those tackles were. It was a miracle that Evan hadn’t gotten seriously hurt during those years. But no, he managed to eke through with his worst injuries being some broken ribs and a particularly concerning concussion.

“There’s a lot of ways to get hurt on this job,” he said. “I just...”

“You’re about to ask me to spy on your brother.”

“I’m asking you to share work gossip, like you already do!” Daniel countered. “If you happen to tell me whether or not he did something stupid ‘cause that was how Mom taught him to get positive attention, that’s a total coincidence.”

“I’ll keep an eye on him,” Austin promised, and Daniel was surprised at how much he relaxed at those words. “That didn’t answer my question though.”

“Kid’s gonna be a brat when he finds out someone’s squealing to me. It’ll be fun making him guess.”

Annoying Buck with it was a big brother prerogative, after all.

Now

Things were tense between Buck and Eddie in the days after Maddie’s phone call, mostly on Eddie’s end. He let up a little bit when Buck told him that he’d told Chimney that Maddie was in Boston. That didn’t really remove the sting of knowing that Buck had lied to him for weeks. Not just by omission: that week Danny was in Boston, Buck had come up with excuses for why Danny wasn’t coming over and why they couldn’t just drop in on him.

Buck figured the lying was what Eddie was most pissed off about.

Still, the tension was there and it was obvious to the others. Even Ravi, who barely knew either of them, noticed. Bobby and Hen took to frowning at them in concern within hours of their first shift after the fight. After Hen mentioned that Chimney was going to make his way to Boston, after the tip from Buck, and the conversation that followed, that concern took a turn closer to disapproval.

“You knew where she was the entire time?” Hen was upset, understandably so.

“Yeah.” Buck shrugged and looked down at his feet. “I did.”

“And it took you this long to say anything to him?”

Buck shrugged again, and Hen did not look like she appreciated the lack of answer. They were up in the loft, which was currently sparsely populated. Eddie was somewhere else in the station, working through his list of chores. Alone.

“How could you be so- so- Selfish!?” Hen snapped. “Chimney was driving himself crazy with worry!

“It probably would have been good to let him know,” Bobby commented. The words were neutral, the tone was neutral, everything was carefully neutral.

The disapproval was still very loud to Buck’s ears.

“You should have told him, Buck,” Hen said. “His girlfriend up and disappears, and you know exactly where she is? You should have told him the moment you heard. There’s no-”

“Shut up,” Austin snapped. The suddenness of it, the fact that it was Austin of all people saying it, served more to silence all of them than anything else. “You should have told Cap the moment Han told you about the PPD. You should have gone to Cap and asked to do a welfare check. Especially when you knew Han wasn’t going to do it. You’ve both been paramedics for too long not to know how stupid it was not to.”

Bobby and Hen stared at him in shock. It was perhaps the most that either of them had ever heard the man say. It was definitely the most emotion they’d seen from him, Buck knew.

“Han fucked up, you fucked up, and Maddie left.” Austin glared at Hen for another moment, and then turned to Buck. “Come help me with the maintenance.”

Buck did not linger for long once Austin started for the stairs. He scrambled to catch up to him, eager to put distance between himself and his angry friend. He was just starting down the stairs when he heard Bobby tell Hen, quiet and solemn,

“He’s right, you know. The both of you should have come to me.”

Eddie was over at the lockers, washing the glass walls. He looked up and smiled at Buck as they went by, because he might’ve been upset with Buck at that moment, but they’d still been Buck-and-Eddie since they were fourteen years old. But he didn’t come over, because it was Buck who was quick to forgive and Eddie who clutched at grudges, even if he wasn’t nursing one now, and he was still a bit upset with Buck.

“Why did you tell Han where she was?” Austin asked as they headed for the captain’s office. That was where all the documents for the truck maintenance, the checklists and all, was kept.

Because Buck had too much heart, like Danny liked to say.

“’Cause Eddie was right. He deserved to know that she was okay,” was what Buck actually said. “Is Danny angry? That I told Chimney?”

“Why would he be angry?” Austin went through the open door of the office and headed for the drawer of the filing cabinet with everything he needed. “Especially since he doesn’t know. Unless you or Eddie told him.”

“No, no, I- You haven’t …?”

“Nope. Come on, I want this done before we get called out.”

They did, though only barely, with them being called out for a small kitchen fire almost as soon as Austin marked his initials down in the last column. But the day was, for the most part, slow and calm and perhaps even a bit q-wordy. There was nothing big until that afternoon, a bit after four. And then it was really big.

It was with sense of ominousness that Buck watched the entrance gate to the state prison open for them. The inmates had rioted, were still rioting, with the building’s alarms blaring to fill the silence left by the engine’s sirens not being on. He wasn’t sure why they were the station called in for this, but they were.

One of the guards, maybe a warden, briefed Bobby as they geared up and Buck listened. Prisoners took out the fire suppression system then started setting things on fire. They were being sent to the one burning in the HVAC corridor, the biggest of the fires and the only one the guards hadn’t managed to put out. The main corridor, the one they were going through, was cleared and locked down. Any comfort from that fact was diminished to nonexistence by the way they were escorted by guards in full protective gear and carrying “non-lethal” weaponry that still looked a lot like rifles.

The corridor past the point they needed to be buzzed in was… Calling it a mess would be accurate, but too small to really convey it. Besides the random things tossed onto the floor – clothes, blankets, mattresses, whatever could be torn free – there was blood on the white brick walls. Lines of it, smears of it, splatters of it. There was a red hand print just before one of the gates and a white, bloodstained shirt hanging on a cell door, and that told Buck more about what they were walking into than any word could.

The HVAC corridor required a key card to get to, and smoke billowed out when one of the guards opened the door. The fire itself, once they reached it, was not hard to contain for all that the HVAC system itself was feeding it while circulating the smoke everywhere. They got the hoses hooked up to the prison’s water supply and then he and Eddie put up a water curtain until Ravi found the HVAC’s off-switch, at which point they put out the fire out the fire.

On their way out, however, things took a turn. First, one of the other cell blocks was “blowing up,” in the words of the lead guard, and required most of their escort. They were left with just the single guard to walk them out, a guard who looked about as nervous as Buck felt. Second, the sound of gasping breaths led them to a pair of prisoners laid out on the floor and hidden behind some machinery. They were both unconscious and covered in blood.

“Holy crap, that’s Mitchell,” their lone escort said. “I wonder who got the drop on him.”

“Maybe one of your colleagues. It doesn’t look like he held back, either,” Hen said. She was looking over the gasping man. “Airway’s compromised. We’re probably looking at a crushed trachia.”

“Alright, we’ve gotta move,” their escort said.

“Woah, hey. These men are injured,” Buck told him.

“And the guy in the shackles has a nasty gash in his lower abdomen,” Eddie said, having lifted the prisoner’s previously white, but now definitely red, shirt to peer at it.

“They’re scumbags,” the guard said. “Leave ‘em here.”

“We don’t get to decide-” Buck started to retort, when the guard’s radio interrupted with,

“We’ve lost control of Block One. All guards, fall back.”

“We’re not leaving anybody,” Bobby announced. “Let’s get ‘em up and out. No time for gurneys.”

Buck and Eddie got the guy with the breathing problems while Ravi and Hen got the guy with the gut wound. They were almost to the gate, and to the ambulance, and to a more secure sense of safety than what Buck was currently feeling, when Mitchell, gut wound guy, started seizing. Bobby waved them on, and their lone guard led them outside before going back in.

They’d just gotten their patient into the ambulance when the pair of guards that would serve as prisoner escort joined them. One went into the back with Eddie and the patient, the other got upfront with Buck.

Buck had never been more relieved to put a place to his back as he was when they finally left the prison behind.

Then

The ringing of his phone had Daniel turning away from watching their latest probie fumble with the hoses. He and Mendoza had an ongoing bet about how long this one would last: Daniel had half a year, while Mendoza had high hopes and put money on making it to the end before transferring stations. From the looks of the kid, and the way he trembled like a sad little chihuahua just from Daniel being this close to him, Daniel was pretty sure he was gonna win.

“What’s up?” was how he answered the phone.

“Your brother just got reamed out by a cop,” Austin said.

“What?” Daniel bit out.

The probie let out a squeak, dropped the hose he was trying to fold, and ran away. Daniel watched him go and idly wondered what he should buy with Mendoza’s money.

“It was Grant, that sergeant that’s friends with Hen and went to that minority support group with Casey,” Austin said. “Buck didn’t want to let a patient onto the bus, and she took exception to it.”

“Why didn’t he?”

“They just rescued a baby out of a pipe, and she was the mother.”

Oh.

“Cap had to stop him from following the baby into the hospital, by the way,” Austin continued.

“Of course he did. Kid’s always had too much heart.” Daniel sighed. “If that cop gives him anymore trouble…”

“I’ll let you know.”

Now

Austin had been called many things in his life. Calm, stoic, quiet, creepy. He was easily forgettable, able to blend in with a crowd even when there wasn’t a crowd to blend into. He’d been part of the 118 for years, thankfully managing to miss Gerrard but still suffering through a good number of the awful captains that followed him. He’d listened to and watched his coworkers as they drove to and from scenes, as they chatted mere feet away from him at the station, and knew far too much of their personal lives.

(Not enough people in his life shared his appreciation for gossip. His sister, Cynthia thought it was mean to talk about people behind their back and his brother, Ethan generally didn’t care about what other people got up to. It was one of the things he loved most about Dan, the fact that he would happily sit and listen and laugh about it with him. Even if it was mean.)

At the moment, however, Austin was not feeling very calm or stoic. At the moment, he was on the wrong side of a gate and in the middle of a prison riot. At the moment, he and the others had a patient in severe distress. At the moment, there was a group of prisoners running straight for then.

He did not want to know what those men would do if they reached him and the others. Firefighters, generally, had a good rap and most people let them be. But this was a riot, and there was blood on the walls, and he did not think being firefighters would protect them from the frenzied violence once it got them in its grasp.

The guard in the security pod, more a disembodied voice than an actual person for Austin, seemed to agree. He told them to move, directing them to the prison infirmary since their exit was compromised. The captain and Ravi grabbed Mitchell’s arms, Austin grabbed his legs, and they all hurried to get the hell out of there.

They reached the infirmary just ahead of the horde, the doctor letting them in. After getting the wounded prisoner onto the bed, Bobby went to help the doctor pull the door closed while the prisoners tried to keep it open. Austin heard a cry of pain just as they managed it.

“What’s going on with the patient?” the doctor asked, clutching at his hand.

“He’s got a stab wound to the lower abdomen. He’s lost a lot of blood,” Hen reported. “He was convulsing, but it’s slowed.

“It’s probably from the BP, he’ll need fluids. Wait.”

The doctor stared at the face of the unconscious man and then looked up.

“That’s not an inmate,” he said. “That’s Freddie Vaughn. He’s a guard.”

What?

“What?” the captain echoed Austin’s thoughts.

“Where’d you find him?” the doctor asked.

“In the machine room, next to the other prisoner,” the captain said. “But if this one’s a guard…”

“Then the other one probably is, too,” Hen finished the thought.

“So where are the prisoners?” Ravi asked, the most obvious and most pressing of questions.

The answer came in the form of a lack of an answer. The captain radioed for Buck and Eddie, and neither of them replied. Austin wasn’t sure who was more surprised, himself or the others, when he let out an emphatic,

“Shit.”

Chapter End Notes

Someone in the comments of the last chapter wished for something from his POV and, in a moment of serendipity, doing so at this point was actually very convenient for the story.

Speaking of Austin, now that we've got some of his POV, this might be a good time for this post about the difference between Daniel and Austin, and the problem of the L-word.

Chapter 13

There was a gun pointed at Buck. Again.

He was driving the ambulance, Eddie was in the back with one of the injured prisoners they’d found in the prison. The prisoner had a crushed trachea and was gasping for breath. A pair of guards were in the ambulance with them, one in the back with Eddie, the other in the passenger seat beside Buck.

Buck had the assumption that, once they’d gotten away from the prison and the riot therein, that he and Eddie were in the clear. A faulty assumption, by all counts. Because the guard in the seat beside him had a gun pointed to Buck’s head.

“Buck, Eddie.” Bobby was radioing them, had been radioing them. The gun had come out when Buck went to reply. “If you can hear me, return to the prison. Come-”

The guard turned off the radio.

“Kill the lights and sirens,” he ordered, and Buck had enough self-preservation to do as he was told.

The next direction was to pull over on the side of the road, and then to get out. Buck was herded at gunpoint to the back of the ambulance and then farther still, to stand beside Eddie. The other guard – “guard” – was stripping his jacket, and there was a red stain on his uniform shirt. It was right where that one prisoner, the one left behind with Bobby and the others, was stabbed.

The one with the gun patted Buck and Eddie down, throwing their radios aside and taking their phones and wallets.

“Okay, so you broke out. What now?” Eddie glared at Bloodstain. “Patient in there currently fighting for every breath. That your handwork?”

Bloodstain just shrugged.

“So what do we do?” Buck asked Eddie under his breath, once Gun went back to his buddy with their belongings and began pawing through them.

“Don’t know yet.”

“Maybe they just want the ambulance?”

“Why would they search us if they’re gonna leave us behind?” Eddie countered. “There’s two of us. There’s two of them. There’s two-”

“Cute kid.” Bloodstain pulled the picture of Christopher out of Eddie’s wallet. “Yours? I’m guessing he lives with you at Four-Nine-Nine-Five South Bedford Street.”

Buck wasn’t sure what he thought he could accomplish, if he was thinking at all when he started towards the pair. The blow to the temple with the butt of the gun sent him staggering back, pain lancing through his skull. Eddie reached out, gripped his coat, and tugged him closer.

“So now that we’re all on the same page,” Bloodstain, the talker of the pair, said. “Let me tell you what we’re gonna do next. I know these things have GPS on them, and I know they can track us that way. So one of you is going to find it and rip it out.”

Buck, still clutching his head, glanced Eddie’s way. His boyfriend was still silently fuming, glaring at the pair as if he could will them into bursting into flames with just his eyes.

“I know where it is,” he said, and then quieter, to Eddie, “Danny showed me.”

It had been after dispatch was held hostage by those would be art thieves. From dispatch, they used the GPS on the police cruisers to track them and send them off on nuisance calls when they got too close to the scene of the crime. Athena disabled the GPS on her cruiser by yanking out something important, and then she passed on the knowledge to her colleagues.

Danny had shown him where the transponders on the ambulances and trucks were as an emergency, a just-in-case kind of deal. Knowledge to have if something like that were to happen again. He definitely had not been thinking of Buck in this situation.

Once the transponder was disconnected – Buck simply reached in and yanked, much like Athena had done; it hadn’t turned off like hers did, so it must’ve been on its own power source somehow – it was tossed to the ground along with their phones and radios and everything else the two not-a-guards took from them. Then they were herded into the back of the ambulance with the patient, Bloodstain getting in with them, his own gun in hand, while the other guy got behind the wheel and started driving. Their patient, in the time they spent on the side of the road, had only gotten worse.

“Is he alright?” Bloodstain asked.

“Other than choking on his own blood?” Eddie shot back. “Buck, hand me the Yankauer suction tube.”

It was already out from before, so Buck just grabbed it and tore it out of the packaging. He kept his eyes on the gun the entire time. The fear was creeping in with the reality of the situation, with the knowledge that nothing good would happen from this point on, that nothing good happened at secondary locations. Because he and Eddie were so very vulnerable right now, with no way to call for help, with no way for people to know where they were going.

Yet Eddie just seemed annoyed with the situation.

“Just make sure he doesn’t die in here,” Bloodstain said.

“Suddenly you care about the guy you almost beat to death?” Buck couldn’t help but ask.

“If I wanted this guy dead, he’d be dead,” Bloodstain said. “I kept him alive. Now, you do the same.”

And that was odd. It was so very odd that the guy who was probably going to kill the three of them by the end of the day was so concerned with them keeping his victim alive. If it weren’t for the mind numbing terror from being held at gunpoint, and perhaps the minor concussion from being pistol whipped, he might wonder about that more.

Austin was in a bit of a conundrum. It was the kind of conundrum he would’ve thrown at Dan and his nephews while dungeon mastering. Before him was the choice of whether or not to keep the promise he made to Daniel, the day Buck got his station assignment.

He never told Daniel everything, he didn’t give Daniel a play-by-play of what his little brother got up to. That wasn’t what Daniel wanted, anyway. Daniel wanted to be told things like when Buck pissed off a cop, or when the captain got a bit too physical with him, or when a bowling ball hit him upside the head, or when strange women stormed into the station to assault him. Austin had seen Buck be stupid on calls so many times and hadn’t told Daniel a thing, except to sometimes make fun of the kid for it, because doing something dumb was different from needlessly throwing himself into danger, as Daniel feared he might do.

This was not Buck being stupid on a call. This was Buck being in danger, this was Buck – and Eddie – being held hostage by a pair of convicts. This was the exact sort of thing Daniel would want to know about.

But what good would it do to tell Daniel? What would it accomplish, other than to send him into a worried frenzy? Austin didn’t even have a direction to send Daniel in, and wouldn’t do it in good conscience even if he did. Neither guard had their sidearms with them when they were found, with the obvious conclusion being that the convicts had taken them, and guns beat a metal bat or whatever weapon Daniel would grab on his way out of the station.

So, conundrum. One that could’ve been avoided if he’d simply left his phone in the engine, instead of bringing it with him in the pocket of his coat. Then he’d have an excuse for why he didn’t call and tell Daniel, at least not right away. Because he didn’t think Daniel would forgive him for not doing it.

The captain was still at the infirmary door, watching through the reinforced glass of the window as the inmates ran wild outside. Hen and the prison doctor were discussing the patient, Vaughn, talking about the next steps they needed to follow to treat him. Ravi, much like Austin himself, was standing around uselessly.

And somewhere else in the prison, there was banging. It was just off rhythm enough to be caused by a person, or maybe people, and it was insistent. It didn’t sound like they were hitting a door, or a wall, or a pipe. There was a hollowness to it, but it was dull and didn’t have the ringing quality that came from striking metal.

Austin wondered if anyone else had noticed, or if they failed their perception checks.

“Looks like the blade must’ve hit his splenic artery,” the prison doctor said, still keeping his injured hand tucked in.

“We can clamp it,” Hen said. “Slow his blood loss.”

“We should give him something for the pain first.” The doctor turned to Austin and Ravi. “Top drawer. One vial of morphine.”

“Stop, don’t sedate him,” the captain ordered. “I need you to wake him up.”

The doctor’s “what?” was accompanied by incredulous laughter.
