“You said he’s in security, right? I need somebody who can tell me about security protocols here.” The captain sounded worried and stressed. “Who has keys, what they open, and if we’re safe in here.”

“I’ve been a doctor here for a decade. I can tell you there are three keys to this room: mine, the warden’s, and the shift commander's. We’re perfectly safe.”

“The shift commander in the Lexan booth who let us in here?”

Oh.

“Yes,” the doctor said. “The security pod. Why?”

That would explain the hollowness of the banging.

“Listen,” the captain said, having apparently passed his perception check, too. “You hear that?”

“What is that?” the probie asked, after a moment where they all spent listening.

“If I had to guess, I’d say that’s a bunch of guys trying to break into the security pod.”

“There’s no way-”

“Doc, I have been a firefighter for decades, and trust me when I say there is not a locked room anywhere that the right tools and enough time can’t break open,” the captain bluntly stated. “Now, please, wake him up.”

The patient, Vaughn, did not enjoy being awake. Or at least, Austin inferred as such from how the man's pain-filled cries. Ravi, the probie, was at the door now, cringing at the sound as he kept watch on the horde outside. Austin didn’t quite enjoy it either, there was a reason (several reasons, actually) why he chose to focus on the engine over any other path in the department, but one did grow a bit numb to the screaming after doing this line of work for long enough.

“Can you hear me, Vaughn?” the captain asked.

Vaughn’s cries died down and he must’ve given some sort of indication that he did. Austin couldn’t see, his view blocked by both Hen and the doctor.

“Okay, good. I’m sorry about this, and I don’t know how much you remember, but I am with the Los Angeles Fire Department. There was a riot here. We had to put out a fire and we got stuck in here on our way out. Do you understand me?”

Austin heard a grunt.

“Yeah? Good. Listen, the guards lost control of this cell block, and I don’t know how much time we have in here until the rioters start coming for us. And I need to get you and my people out of this infirmary.”

“Cap, he needs pain management,” Hen said.

“You need to stop this,” the doctor added.

“Vaughn, is there any way out of here besides through that door?” the captain asked the suffering man. “Can we go through any air ducts or tunnels or crawl spaces?”

Seeing as they were not in a dungeon designed by Wizards of the Coast, Austin highly doubted there were any secret tunnels in this room that they could open up if they could only find the right trigger. The air ducts would be a no go, too, because this was not a Die Hard sequel, and the people who designed this prison would not have wanted to give the prisoners any means of bypassing the choke points. The only way out of this infirmary was through that one door and the horde beyond it, they all knew that.

Vaughn tried to say something.

“The pod?” the captain guessed. “The security pod is under siege.”

“G- G- Gas,” Vaughn managed to gasp out.

“Tear gas?” Hen guessed.

“I suppose we could try to contact the guards, if there are any real guards left, and have them run up to the HVAC,” the captain said. “But we turned it off ‘cause of the smoke.”

“Are we even sure tear gas would work?” the probie asked. “Kind of feels like it would just make everyone mad.”

Right, that was the problem with the plan. Not the fact that the infirmary was on the same HVAC system as the rest of the building and they’d be getting tear gassed right along with everyone else. Austin had never been tear gassed before, and he would very much like to keep that streak going.

“Nitrous Oxide,” was the doctor’s suggestion.

“Laughing gas?” Hen sounded dubious. “That would make them less mad.”

“Alright, doc,” the captain said. “Draw me a map and I’ll turn the HVAC on, and I’ll go into the dental office and run whatever’s in there through the vents. And then we hope that works.”

“That sounds like a lot of distance to cover, Cap,” Hen pointed out. “I should go with you. I can turn on the HVAC, you can get the gas.”

“No, you’re needed here. Doc only has one hand and Vaughn could go south again.”

“I’ll go,” Ravi volunteered. Which was good, ‘cause Austin sure as hell wasn’t. “I can do it.”

“You’ll be a target,” the doctor cautioned. “Heroic firefighters might make especially good ones.”

“What if he doesn’t look like a firefighter?’ the captain countered.

That was how a queasy looking Ravi ended up stripping out of his turnouts and uniform to change into Vaughn’s bloodstained prisoner clothes. He and the captain went out, and Austin closed and locked the door behind them. Austin watched them go through the window, the pair separating after a moment.

“Better them than me,” he said aloud.

“Jesus!” the doctor suddenly shouted, good hand to his chest, eyes wide as he stared at Austin. “I didn’t- I forgot you were there.”

“That happens a lot, actually,” Hen told the doctor. “Let’s take a look at that hand.”

“Yeah, okay.” The doctor still sounded shaken as he went to sit down. “Why didn’t you volunteer to go?”

“Because I’m not stupid,” was Austin’s answer.

He watched Hen wrap the doctor’s hand, and then watched them both freak out when Vaughn’s vitals crashed. They did surgery on him, Hen as the hands while the doctor supervised and Austin stayed out of the way. It was a good opportunity, actually, for him to do the thing he was dreading.

“It’s never a good thing when you call me during a shift,” was how Daniel answered, voice already a bit tense. “What’s going on?”

“Well.” Austin saw Hen look up at him, frown, and then go back to what she was doing. “I’m trapped in the middle of a prison riot-”

“What!? Where’s Buck? Where’s Eddie?”

“Yeah, that’s the other thing…”

Daniel took the news about as well as Austin expected, and about as badly as he feared. Daniel was silent as Austin explained what happened, from the reason for their arrival, to the two injured guards in prisoner garb, to taking refuge in the prison infirmary while Buck and Eddie got out, and then the pair of them going radio silent. It was the heavy, dreadful silence that Austin had come to associate with imminent violence.

“What are you doing now?” Daniel asked, voice flat.

“Waiting for Cap and the probie to fill the place with laughing gas so we can finally get out of here.”

“Okay.”

That was all Austin got before the call dropped. He wasn’t surprised, and he took comfort in knowing that Daniel had no idea where to storm off to. Not on his own, anyway.

“Was that a good idea?” Hen asked as she shrugged back into her turnouts and gear. Vaughn was all sutured up, the paramedic and the doctor having done all they could do with what they had.

“Probably not,” Austin confessed. “But I made him a promise.”

“Hen, Jones.” The captain’s voice crackled over their radios just as Austin took note of the hissing noise. “The gas is a go.”

Austin hurried to put his oxygen mask back on, Hen doing the same. She gave the doctor a small oxygen tank.

Ravi arrived back first, followed by the captain. The hallways, when they were finally heading for the exit, were filled with laughing and insensate convicts. It was easy to step over them or around them as needed.

Austin was very, very relieved when he was sitting in the driver’s seat of the engine again.

Chapter 14

Chapter Notes

CW: canon typical violence; minor discussion of past sex work

Then

The first time Austin realized that Daniel was… Different. That was a good word for it, though he knew that it wasn’t the only word for it. He knew that several people would use other, less polite words to describe Daniel. But Austin was just going to say he was different.

The first time Austin realized that Daniel was different from everyone else, in a way that was nowhere near how Austin himself was different from everyone else, was actually fairly early on in their friendship. Not that first day, when he showed up at their table while Austin was sitting through Jon being obnoxious and Casey just being annoying, wondering why he kept coming out with them. Though he’d gotten an inkling of it even then, and even more of an inkling as the man kept showing up.

Normal people, after all, did not casually offer to fake a relationship with a guy just to get his parents off his back. They definitely wouldn’t have tagged onto the offer the fact that they’d been in “worse arrangements.”

It wasn’t when Daniel – on the far side of tipsy and sliding well into the territory of drunk, enough so that Austin had insisted Daniel crash at his – casually mentioned being an ex-prostitute. Actually, what he’d said was a comment about how long it had been since he’d gone home with a guy that wasn’t paying for it. Upfront, because apparently he’d learned from that mistake when he nearly missed a rent payment.

Austin was still confused by the fact that people, real life people, paid for orgasms when they could just have one on their own for free.

No, the first time that Austin realized Daniel was different, that he was different, was long before that night. It was maybe the fourth or fifth time it was just the two of them out, no Jon or Casey. They were sitting at the bar that time, all the tables and booths taken, while they shared the latest gossip from their stations.

Daniel froze in his seat, cutting himself off mid-sentence, and his face lost all expression. He silently slid off his stool and stalked down to the end of the bar, plastering on a smile that was somehow more unnerving than the blankness that it replaced. He slapped his hand down between a couple, a man and a woman, and asked the man,

“What did you put in her drink?”

The man stammered out denials, the woman half-shrieked questions. Daniel dipped his pinky into the woman’s drink – something amber colored – and tasted it.

“Salty,” he commented, sounding perfectly pleasant. “Was it supposed to be salty?”

The woman shook her head, hair bouncing with it. Daniel nodded, the smile unwavering.

And then Austin watched Daniel slam the man’s head into the bar top.

They never went back to that bar.

Now

Having a gun constantly pointing at you, Buck was finding, was rather stress inducing. A different kind of stress from what their job usually entailed. He was hyperaware of it at all times, and he couldn’t keep his eyes off it for longer than a second. For the entire ride, since they started off again and left the ambulance’s transponder behind, the gun had taken up the vast majority of Buck’s world.

Eddie, for once, was simply on the fringes of Buck’s attention. Their legs were pressed together in a way they didn’t need to be within the space they were filling. It was a line of warmth that would normally be comforting, and Buck knew that Eddie was trying to be comforting with the way he made sure they kept touching no matter how the other moved, but it just wasn’t enough to make up for the gun.

And his head was still throbbing.

“What are you doing now?” Bloodstain asked.

“Intubating him,” was the answer that Eddie gave. “Buck, hand me the bougie. Long blue tube in the cabinet.”

Buck reached into the open cabinet for the bougie, trying his best to keep his eye on the gun while also finding what Eddie asked for. Mostly, he ended up just looking back and forth until he found it.

“Almost there, Mitch,” the one in the driver’s seat said.

Between the seats, Buck could see out the windshield as he opened the bougie’s package and helped Eddie get it out. That was how he spotted the lit up sign for an emergency room. Disbelief filled him, pushing back the fear and anxiety that seemed to settle into his bones.

“Are you guys serious?” he blurted out. “We’re really going to a hospital?”

“Thought that’s what you wanted,” Bloodstain – Mitch – replied. “You’re so concerned about the health of your friend here. Now you can walk him through the front doors.”

Nothing about this made sense to Buck. Leaving the injured guard for dead on the side of the road while they took off with Buck and Eddie as hostages, that would make sense. Park somewhere and switch vehicles, raid the cabinets for drugs, those things would make sense. It would make sense for escaped convicts to focus on putting as much distance between themselves and the prison as possible. But going to this length just to get to a hospital? That did not make sense.

They parked in the drive in front of the ER doors. As soon as the engine was shut off, the other convict was slipping out of the driver’s seat and rounding the ambulance to throw open the doors.

“Doesn’t look like we have any company,” he said.

“Alright, pretty boy stays with me. Dom, you and the one with the eye herpes take our friend inside,” Mitch instructed, and Buck felt his heart drop. “Hand him off, then you know what to do.”

“Hey, man, listen,” Buck started. “I don’t know what you want, but there is a hospital full of sick people-”

“Just go, or I’ll shoot you. Or better yet.” The gun turned to Eddie. “I’ll shoot him. And then I’ll find his kid and shoot him, too.”

“Buck.”

He met Eddie’s eyes and swallowed back the bile. He did not, did not want to leave Eddie behind with this man. But the choices before him weren’t choices at all.

“My brother’s going to kill you,” he said, as certain as he’d ever been about anything in his life.

Mitch and Dom both simply laughed.

Buck helped get the injured guard unloaded and then wheeled to the sliding glass doors. As soon as they were inside, it was very clear something was off. Emergency rooms in Los Angeles were never empty, yet this one was. There was not a single person in the waiting room, no one waiting with broken arms, no exhausted nurses. Emergency rooms in Los Angeles were never quiet, yet there wasn’t a noise in this one except the wheels of the gurney. There was no shouting, no crying, no cursing.

“Where do we go?” Dom asked.

“Right this way.”

He tried not to bring attention to the wrongness of the situation. Everything was wrong, and he’d been tense the entire day, or so it felt. There wasn’t anything he could do but lead him farther into the hospital and try not to think about what might happen next.

“Where the hell is everyone?” Dom asked.

As if that was their cue, uniformed officers suddenly spawned into existence. They mobbed Buck and the convict, hands grabbing them and throwing them to the ground. Buck found himself with his face smashed into linoleum and his arms pulled painfully behind his back, no matter how much he tried to tell them that he was a firefighter, that he wasn’t one of the convicts. But they wouldn’t listen-

“Get your fucking hands off of him!”

Buck looked up at the sound of Danny’s voice, spotting him – still in uniform – storming across the emergency room with Athena right on his heels. He practically melted into the floor with relief at the sight of him, most of the fear and anxiety disappearing. Because the part of him that was still that little kid he’d been years ago would always be convinced that his big brother could fix everything.

Danny was not gentle with the way he grabbed the first officer and tore him off of Buck, how he pushed them all away. He and Buck both ignored their complaints, and Athena soothing their tempers, as Danny helped Buck to his feet.

Buck knew the moment Danny saw the injury to his head. There was a flash off rage, the most rage that Buck had ever seen on his brother, before Danny’s face went completely and utterly blank. It wasn’t an expression, it was the absence of one. He turned on Dom, who’d been struggling against the hold of the officers arresting him. The convict froze in the officers’ grips, terror creeping over his features.

“Get him the fuck away from me!” he screeched, doing his best to backpedal.

“Daniel.” Athena’s voice had a hint of warning to it. “Buck, are you good?”

“Y- Yeah.” He was breathing heavily, his head still ached, and now his shoulders were sore from the hold that the cops had on him, but there wasn’t a gun pointed at him anymore. He counted that as a massive improvement. “How’d you know we were coming here?”

“We figured out why Mitchell broke out,” the sergeant answered. “Where’s-?

“Where’s Eddie?” Danny asked. He’d kept his gaze on Dom until he was out of sight.

“He’s still in the ambulance with Bloo- Uh, Mitchell,” Buck said. “Danny, he’s-”

“I know.” Danny’s eyes went to the wound on Buck’s head again, face momentarily twisting with anger once more. “C’mon, squirt. Let’s get that taken care.”

Danny practically shoved him into a chair while Athena went off to consult with whoever was in charge here. He left to raid the supplies, and Buck watched him get into an argument with a nurse that he either won or ignored entirely, because he was soon back with what he needed to wash away the small trickles of blood and then cover it up. Athena was back by the time he was finished and throwing away the wrapping.

“Tac team is working on a plan to take the ambulance,” she told them.

“What? No.” Buck stood up, shook his head, and regretted it. “As soon as they make their move, he is going to shoot Eddie. There’s got to be a way to talk him down, get him to surrender.”

Athena and Danny looked at each other, something grim in both their expressions.

“It’s a little more complicated than that,” Athena said. “Surrender is not what Mitchell’s after.”

“He’s here for his kid.” Danny’s words came out flat. It was a statement that, from him, made Buck’s blood run cold. “His son’s here in the ICU. Congenital heart failure.”

“He’s been on the donor list for a year.”

The thing was that if Mitchell had pulled this with any other firehouse, or, hell, if he’d held anyone else (with a singular exception) from the 118 hostage, Daniel would probably be supporting him. No, scratch that, Daniel would definitely be supporting him. There was no maybe or probably about it.

It was because Daniel knew the lengths he’d go if it was Buck in need of a new heart. The depths he would go. And he’d do it without hesitation and without guilt. Hell, his parents made a whole ass person to fix him when he was sick, so he came by it naturally.

“Here’s your phone, by the way.” Daniel held it out to Buck, who took tit with a wordless noise of question. “They found it with the transponder, along with Eddie’s phone and your wallets.”

“Thanks,” Buck muttered as he tucked it away into a pocket. He was still in his bunker boots and pants, but the coat must’ve been in the ambulance still.

“This is Savanna Richards.” Athena had left to go do… Daniel didn’t actually know what she went to go do, it hadn’t been important enough to pay attention to. There were many things that were higher on the list of priorities, such as Eddie still being held hostage. But now she was back, accompanied by a blond woman who looked tired on the spiritual level. “Mitchel’s ex, Nolan’s mom.”

“She’s the reason we-” Daniel rolled his eyes when Athena pointedly cleared her throat. “The reason Athena knew Mitchell would be coming here.”

“And Nolan’s the reason he broke out,” Buck said, the words coming out almost like a question.

“He started writing me letters last year, saying he wanted to save his son,” Savanna told them. “I still don’t know how he found out about Nolan.”

“You mean you didn’t tell him?”

Savanna’s scoff was an answer unto itself.

“That last time I saw Mitchell was in a courtroom eighteen years ago.”

Though Daniel didn’t know Nolan’s age, he was willing to bet he was seventeen and change. Savanna probably wasn’t even showing at the time, might not have even known that she was pregnant yet.

“He tried to jump the defense table to get to me, screaming he was gonna kill me for betraying him,” Savanna continued. “I told him to rot in hell. Didn’t think there was much to say after that.”

“Pretty much said it all, really,” Daniel agreed.

“Mitchell’s been waging a campaign to save Nolan,” Athena said. “He’s contacted the State Board of Prisoners, the governor, asking to be executed.”

“I thought we didn’t do that anymore,” Buck said.

The governor had signed an executive order about it a couple years back, putting a moratorium on capital punishment. And yet, they still had death row inmates. Make it make sense.

“It’s legally complicated,” Athena told him. “But he wants the state to kill him so he can donate his heart.”

“All his requests were denied,” Savanna said.

“So he decided to force the issue.”

“I’m kinda starting to respect the guy,” Daniel commented.

Athena was unimpressed, while Savanna was visibly disgusted with him. Daniel just shrugged. Here was a guy who was literally trying to give his son his heart. There was no way Daniel wouldn’t appreciate that kind of sentiment. Again, if it had been any other firehouse…

“Mitchell still hasn’t responded,” Athena’s captain – Maynard by her name tag – said as she joined them.

“Does he need to?” Buck asked, a reasonable question. “We know what his demands are, don’t we? He wants to donate his heart to his kid.”

“That’s not on the table,” the sergeant said.

“It’s not even on the menu,” the captain said. “The state doesn’t allow death row inmates to donate their organs.”

“Come on,” Buck protested. “That’s just a technicality.”

“Yeah, no, squirt.” Daniel shook his head. “Things don’t end well when the state starts killing people for parts. ‘Cause eventually, everyone starts lookin’ like spare parts.”

Buck looked ill at the implications.

“Okay, but- but there’s gotta be something we can do,” he protested. It was almost begging.

“We’re still trying to talk to him,” Maynard said. “Give him a chance to surrender peacefully.”

Daniel stared at her.

“Respectfully, ma’am,” he said, and Athena immediately started sending him warning looks that he ignored. “But how stupid are you? He wants to give Nolan his heart. He wants to die. He’s not gonna surrender peacefully, anyone smart enough to not become a cop can see that.”

Neither cop he was talking to looked particularly impressed with what he just said.

“I understand what you meant, now,” Maynard told Athena, before stomping away.

Savanna wasn’t far behind. Athena lingered long enough to roll her eyes at him before pacing away a few feet when her phone began to ring. Daniel tuned her out when she greeted Nash, to face his baby brother.

His baby brother who was looking vaguely upset with him.

“What?” Daniel demanded, but Buck just sighed, so he moved on. “Anyways, you know how Maddie’s always saying that Mom and Dad aren’t bad people-”

“They’re just bad parents,” Buck finished. “Yeah.”

“Does this guy count as ‘not a bad parent, just a bad person?’”

Buck seemed guilty about the laugh that bubbled forth out of him. He slapped a hand over his mouth to stop it.

And then a gun shot rang out.

Daniel watched Buck’s face pale as the blood drained out of it, as nurses and doctors and patients all screamed. As they ran away from the sound, Buck ran towards it. Daniel followed, knowing there was no way he could catch the long-legged brat before he made it outside.

They found Eddie crouched over the still form of the man that must have been Mitchell, surrounded by cops. He was doing compressions, arms frantically pumping at the dead man’s chest, stopping only to bat at the arms of the cops that tried to pull him away.

“Grab a crash cart and help me keep his heart pumping,” he demanded the moment they reached him.

Daniel was already turning on his heel to do just that, even as Buck dazedly asked him what was going on.

Austin did not go to the hospital with the others at the end of their shift. He knew that both Buck and Eddie were okay, he knew that some kid got a new heart, and he knew that Daniel returned to his station when the first of those things was certain. He knew this because Daniel had told him this.

Austin also knew where Daniel was most likely to go when he got off shift. He knew it with the same kind of certainty that he knew Daniel would do something to the captain once Daniel learned about him shoving Buck into a wall. He knew it with the same kind of certainty that he knew Daniel would storm off the moment he heard what was going on with the escaped prisoners, though he would have no idea where to even storm off to.

He knew where Daniel would go, and he knew why Daniel would go there, so that’s where Austin went. Buck and Eddie were both visibly surprised to find him sitting in his car outside the latter’s house, but let him in all the same. They both disappeared into the kid’s room, and Austin waited.

Not that he had to wait long.

“Is that- Oh.” Daniel fell silent when he saw it was Austin that answered the door. “I guess that is your car.”

“They’re bothering your nephew,” he said, stepping back to let Daniel in.

“’Course they are,” Daniel said. “And you came here to…?”

“Figured you’d want everyone close,” was Austin’s bland answer.

Daniel’s face took on that expression then, the expression Austin was certain that Daniel never realized he was making. The gooey one. The one Austin had seen his parents look at each other with, his sister and her husband, his brother and his wife. He knew what the expression was, if only because he had seen it so many times on other people’s faces. He’d have to be an idiot not to know what it meant.

So, like he so often did, Austin completely ignored it.

“They’re probably going to crash,” he said, turning around and heading for the couch. “There might be something trashy on TV.”

Daniel joined him on the couch moments later, stretching out along the length with him. It wasn’t a very big couch, and their tangle of sock-covered feet ended up hanging over the end. It was comfortable enough, though, and they managed to find a rerun on watch that was sufficiently mind numbing.

Buck and Eddie were not the only ones who ended up crashing.

Chapter 15

Chapter Notes

Welcome back, everyone, from the unannounced hiatus I took while focusing on my other series for a bit. But speaking of my other series! There's an AU over on my tumblr that is basically "what if Daniel was alive for it."

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Contact with Maddie when she was in the clinic had been sporadic. The only time he’d heard from her directly then was when she called after he told the clinic about Chimney. Since she’d been released into the out patient program, though, they’d talked a lot more. More than they had before she went to Boston, even.

“How’s Buck?” she asked.

Daniel leaned against his counter, pinning the phone against his shoulder as he tore open a bag of Middleswarth chips. He was, sadly, out of Lebanon bologna and low on the potato bread, so a sandwich was currently unavailable to him.

“The squirt’s doin’ good, all happy family with the Diazes still,” he told her. “The mark from the pistol whipping isn’t quite as bad, either. Just like, a yellow bruise and a tiny scab.”

Whenever he saw it, the rage in his veins blazed to life once more. He made the mistake of Facetiming Buck while he was on shift one day, and whatever expression he wore had several people stepping lightly around him until they went home. Mendoza just reminded him not to do anything in his uniform, which was one of the reasons Mendoza was his favorite at the station.

“Good,” Maddie said. “Every time I’ve asked, he just says he’s fine and then starts asking about how I’m doing.”

“The standard Buckley response,” Daniel joked, because he was guilty of it as much as Maddie and Buck were. “How, um. How are you doing?”

“I’m fine,” she said, and then had the audacity to laugh. Daniel rolled his eyes. “Still going to groups. The woman leading it, sometimes she’s a bit too…”

“Upbeat?”

“Yeah. Sometimes I don’t want upbeat. Sometimes I want bitchy.”

“That’s why I like Austin, he’s as much of an asshole as I am.”

“Interesting that you just… Bring him up like that.”

“Shut up.”

“Uh-huh. Buck’s mentioned you’ve been hanging out with Taylor Kelly. I’m not sure if that’s an improvement, but I’ll take it.”

“Shut up.” Daniel tossed the bag of chips onto the counter. “Firstly, Taylor is… I don’t know how old she is, but she’s closer to Buck’s age than mine. Secondly, I don’t need to- to – to move on, or whatever it is you think.”

“The portions of your life not focused on Buck or firefighting are spent with a guy who doesn’t love you back, Dan. That’s not healthy.”

“Says the woman hiding from her baby daddy.”

“You are an asshole,” Maddie quietly said after a moment of silence.

“...do you want to hear about the trip to the Trading Post that Buck dragged me to?”

“Are you about to tell me you bought something for Austin while you were there?”

“No, seriously, shut up.”

“Victim’s name is Edgar Hill,” Bobby said when they arrived at the scene. They were not the only units there: actual Search and Rescue, cops, even a helicopter with a spotlight that cut through the night. Taylor Kelly was here, too, reporting on it. “He told dispatch that he was carjacked, got shot and buried in some sort of coffin.”

Buck grimaced as he pulled out the gear they would need. It was a bit like a metal detector, in that it was on a long stick with a handle. There were what looked like radio dishes running along it, getting smaller the closer to the end they got, and a set of headphones were attached directly into it.

“Based on the size of an average male, that bot is probably eighty-six by thirty inches, so forty-five feet of cubic space,” Bobby said, and Buck would just take his word on it for the numbers. He’d hobbled through his math classes with pity and charity. “Most of that is taken up by Edgar, so he’s got anywhere between ten minutes to an hour of air left.”

“And we don’t know how long he’s been underground,” Buck guessed.

“Right,” Bobby confirmed. “Or even which piece of ground he’s under. The cell towers can only limit the search by so much.”

They fanned out in all directions, a line of people combing through for anything out of place. The problem with searching for a man this way, with a device that could hear the sound of his breathing, was that the device was sensitive by necessity. Buck put the headphones on, though only over one ear, and he could hear everything as he swept the rod along.

In one direction, a fox ran through the bush, tiny out beating out a frantic rhythm as it fled from them. In another direction, he heard someone’s stomach grumble, another complaining about being out in the cold. In this direction was a twig that snapped under someone’s foot with a deafening crack, and in that direction the crackling of radios.

“One Eighteen, be advised. Infrared penetration is not optimal at this time.”

“Copy that, Fire One. Keep doing what you can,” Bobby replied to the pilot above them. “Buck, you get anything on sound detection?”

“Nothing, Cap,” Buck replied.

They trooped along, beams of light bouncing as people walked. Buck swept the rod right to left to right again, hearing nothing but-

Wait.

A scream. Or something that sounded like a scream.

“Cap,” Buck radioed. “I got something.”

“What?”

“I thought I heard a voice.”

Buck focused the rod in the direction it came from. Smaller sweeps this time, a more narrow focus. It was quiet now, though, which was what he told May, the dispatcher for the call, when she asked.

May offered to get them a sustained sound to listen for, and Bobby radioed for everyone to, in essence, shut up. The helicopter above, with its noisy propellers, pulled away. Everyone on the search line froze where they were, each quiet except for heartbeats and breathing.

Buck put the headphones on fully. It didn’t go silent, it went the opposite of quiet. Owls hooted in the trees, a cricket played a song somewhere in the grass. It was just the sound of nature as Buck crept forward.

And then, a scream. Faint, muffled. Buck focused in that direction and a beam of light hit an area of upturn dirt. They converged on the spot, Ravi starting to dig with the shovel he was carrying. With all the people involved, it didn’t take long until they were hitting and then opening the wooden box that was intended to be a coffin.

A police detective was pulling up just as their ambulance was driving off with Edgar.

At some point, Daniel and Taylor Kelly had become friends. He wasn’t sure when that happened, but it was sometime between the failure of a treasure hunt and Eddie passing his recertifications to start back at work. They weren’t close friends, he wouldn’t even call them good friends, and maybe calling them friends was to use too strong a word, but they were definitely friendly.

Being friends with Taylor was simple and easy, much like the friends he’d managed to keep in Hershey before moving away. There wasn’t the constant danger of accidentally puking up his feelings over everything, like with Austin. They didn’t have to trust each other with their lives, like with Mendoza and Nola and the rest. He definitely wouldn’t go to her for refuge and succor, like he would Alma and Athena.

Their friendship was more akin to the one he had with Michael. They’d get together, talk shit, and usually drank too much. Even at brunch. There was nothing weighty to it, nothing valuable. They were less of friends than he was with Michael, even. If Taylor fucked off one day and they never crossed paths again, his life would not become horribly bereft. The same, he knew, was true for her in return.

Honestly, their friendship was never going to be one of story and song. Not with one of its key foundations being so questionable to begin with. See, Buck got along with her, was better friends with her than Daniel was, but Eddie? Eddie loathed her. He acted like a territorial dog around her, coming just short of actually growling at her as he circled with his hackles up.

It was objectively hilarious to watch. A part of Daniel, the part that would never quite forgive Eddie for the Shannon of it all, took great pleasure in it. The best part was that it was a way to poke at Eddie without upsetting Buck, since, as mentioned earlier, he and Taylor were friends. He hadn’t noticed what Daniel was doing, those times Daniel arranged for Taylor and Eddie to be in the same room together, and would tell Eddie off for being an unfriendly grump.

But their friendship wasn’t all about tormenting Eddie. Most of the time, it consisted of him listening while she bitched about someone or something. They’d meet somewhere and get a round of those colorful drinks that Daniel got when he was out drinking, instead of at home with Austin, and he’d drink and pretend to listen while she bitched and kvetched. She always paid for the drinks, so it wasn’t much of a hardship.

It reminded him a bit of what his friendship with Attica consisted of these days. He and Attica were simply too different for anything to last, and he figured that their relationship was doomed to fizzle out even without Buck’s unintentional assistance in the matter. Mostly, she called him over to have someone to vent to and then work out her frustrations with. Even that wasn’t happening much of late, since she had someone else to fill the role for her in the form of a handsome paralegal named Augustus. The asshole volunteered at food banks, so Daniel couldn’t even find it in himself to make fun of his name or the way she called him Auggie.

Today, Austin was busy helping his sister with something – Daniel avoided Cynthia when he could; she was always looking at him as if she knew, though he only had fears about what she it might be that she knew – so Daniel sat at a table outside, protected from the bright Los Angeles sun by a large umbrella. There was a multitude of note cards on the glass tabletop, each categorized by color: the green cards were filed under “they said,” the reddish pink were “the marriage,” the blue were “motives,” while the yellow were “the neighbors said/saw.” It was an orderly mess, and Daniel was half-expecting for red yarn and thumbtacks to come out to play.

“He’s not telling anyone how it happened,” Taylor stated, gnawing at her thumb. “He was buried alive. One would think he’d be more willing to talk.”

Right, the reason she needed someone to vent to. Edgar Hill, some guy that was buried alive after being shot. Buck and the 118 were the ones that dug him up while Taylor was in the middle of reporting on it.

“Sounds like you’re trying to do the police’s job,” Daniel commented as he munched on a fry.

He’d learned the hard way that the fun drinks he got whenever he went out to drink often hit harder than the “manlier” stuff. Or at least the alcohol in them snuck up on him easier. Probably because he threw them back faster when they tasted particularly fruity. Either way, he’d learned the lesson about drinking on an empty stomach a long time ago.

“They don’t have any idea what they’re looking for,” Taylor retorted, barely looking up from the note cards.

“They rarely do. You could replace most of ‘em with trained dogs and they’d be just as effective.”

She eyed him curiously for a moment, before picking up one of the cards and tapping it against the corner.

“When the One Eighteen pulled him out of the hole, he said, I’m sorry Lindsay-”

“Who the hell told you that?” Daniel had to ask.

She just smirked and shrugged.

“They’ve been married for a decade. He claimed she’s currently on a cruise to Alaska, but I’m doubting that,” the reporter continued. “The neighbors said she doesn’t have any friends-”

“Do you have any friends?” he blurted out without a thought.

The stink eye she gave him was answer enough. It wasn’t a surprising one, either, because there was a reason she was inviting him out and paying for his drinks for nothing except to have someone at least pretending to listen. If he told someone that people hired him in El Paso because of that sort of loneliness just as often as he was hired for anything else, they probably wouldn’t believe him.

“But her neighbors noticed a van parked down the street the past few weeks,” Taylor said, primly ignored his question. “One of them saw Lizzie getting out of it.”

“Weird place to be having an affair.” He made a face at the taste of his drink. There was no risk of this one sneaking up on him: too much daiquiri, not enough strawberry. “Normally went for hotels.”

Taylor cocked her head a bit, almost visibly chewing on that comment.

“She may or may not have been having an affair,” she said after a moment. “But there was definitely something wrong with that marriage.”

That was an understatement. Three life insurance policies on the wife, while the husband let the one on himself lapse. Mail carrier witnessed them fighting, including the wife out and out saying he’d rather see him dead, money or no money. Taylor was of the opinion that the wife hired someone to kill her husband so she could sell off his assets and collect his retirement accounts.

She was half right: the husband hired a hit man to murder his wife so he could collect on the life insurance policies. Except the hit man fell for the wife and she convinced – read: slept with – him to kill her husband.

“He agreed to kill her husband, just because she had sex with him? When the husband was offering him actual money?” Austin’s tone was full of disbelief and doubt. The two of them were sat on Austin’s couch, drinking beer and watching a Lakers game. Like bros. Bros who sat pressed together from shoulders to thighs. “That seems kinda stupid. I would’ve preferred to be paid in cash.”

“Well, yeah,” Daniel chuckled. “Of course you would.”

“Anyone smart would,” his friend retorted. “The kind of money he was hoping to get could have paid for a lot of sex. Right?”

It was accompanied with a slight nudge of Austin’s elbow into Daniel’s side. The asshole was grinning at him over the rim of his beer.

“Was that a prostitution joke?” Daniel scoffed, turning his head to hide the smile he couldn’t quite quash. “I can’t believe Buck thinks you’re funny.”

“I’m hilarious.”

He was, but honesty wasn’t the game they were playing right then. Instead, Daniel just made a show of rolling his eyes and said,

“You’re a bitch, is what you are.”

He watched Austin open his mouth, pause, and then say,

“How much are tickets to their home games, anyway?”

“More than I’d want to spend, probably,” Daniel answered, and wondered what Austin had been going to say.

Chapter End Notes

"Yeah, yeah, love you, too."

Chapter 16

Chapter Notes

Happy 9-1-1 day, everyone. Let us ready ourselves for disappointment.
cw for minor reference to homophobia, the reference and use of a slur, and canon typical violence.

Then

Austin was not like the other boys. He knew that he was different. He also knew that different was not good. Especially when he was different in the way he suspected that he was.

By middle school, the boys in class were not shy with voicing their opinions about the girls. Austin would sit there and listen to them, listen to them lie about what they did and listen to them talk about how pretty the girls were. And Austin would look at the girls they were talking about and… They were pretty, he guessed. Pretty like flowers, pretty like sunsets, but he didn’t feel anything that his friends were talking about when he looked at them.

The boys were pairing up with the girls and he was left feeling like he’d missed something vital. He felt like they were all in on some sort of joke and he just kept missing the punchline, or worse, he was the punchline. He felt like some sort of defective freak.

His first kiss was at some stupid eighth grade dance. Her name was Tiffany, and his friends – what few he had, with friendships built more on how they didn’t mind the way he’d rather sit and listen than talk – had more or less bullied him into asking her out. She wore a powder blue dress with matching bows in her hair, a necklace made of mismatched beads, and lip gloss that made her lips shiny and that tasted of watermelon.

There was no spark, no fireworks, nothing like what his friends talked about or the people on the television. It was just awkwardness. It was just her making a ridiculous face and him not knowing what to do. It was just him realizing, with a finality, that he didn’t like kissing her, that he didn’t like her that way.

He didn’t like girls the same way all the other boys did. And if he didn’t like girls, then…

Austin hadn’t really, truly been paying attention to all of that. Not the stuff in the news, not really. But he had been paying attention to the way his friends spoke, his classmates. The casual way they’d say things, how smear the queer was one of the more popular games the other boys played. How the McPhersons down the street disowned their oldest son and were no longer invited to his mother’s potlucks.

“I think I’m gay,” Austin blurted out one day, all in one breath.

Cynthia paused in the middle of doing her make up. She was sitting in front of her mirror, getting ready to go out with that asshole, Chad, again. Even though they’d broken up twice because he kept cheating on her. But he’d come crawling back and plead and she gave him another chance. Sometimes Austin wished he was a little bit stronger and a little bit braver, that he was someone who could send the creeps running.

Instead he just had to watch as his sister got herself ready to be hurt again.

She set down the brush she’d been using to put on eye shadow, a deep purple, and turned to face him. There was nothing on her face like he may have imagined or feared. There was just a slight frown, her mauve painted lips downturned.

“Why do you think that?” Cynthia asked, deliberately casual.

“I don’t like girls,” Austin said.

“You took that girl to the dance, didn’t you? Tiffany whatever.”

“Yeah, but.” Austin clutched at the hem of his shirt, fingers twisting the fabric. “I kissed her, and it just felt weird.”

“Kissing’s always weird at first,” his sister said.

“You like kissing Chad.” He couldn’t help the way he spat out the guy’s name. His sister huffed and rolled her eyes. “Don’t you?”

“Yes, I do. He’s a very good kisser.” Maybe that was why she kept taking him back. “Maybe you and Tiffany are just bad at kissing.”

“All the other boys, they- they talk about what they want to, you know. Do. With their girlfriends.” Austin felt his cheeks heat up as he said it. Cynthia’s eyes scrunched up at the corners, but she thankfully didn’t laugh. “And I… Don’t.”

“Don’t talk about it?”

“Don’t want to do it.”

“At all?” she gently probed. When he just silently shook his head, she asked, “And you… You want to do that with boys?”

“I…” Austin hesitated, unsure of the answer. “I don’t know. But I don’t like girls, so…”

He didn’t like girls, so that meant he was gay. Right? Wasn’t that how it worked? Either you liked one or you liked the other, or maybe you liked both. But he didn’t like girls, which really only left him the one option.

Didn’t it?

He heard his sister sigh and then stand up from the chair. She padded over on stockinged feet to where he sat on the edge of her bed, and then cupped his face with her hand. Then she bent down and pressed a kiss to his cheek. He winced and resisted the urge to wipe off the lipstick stain he knew she left behind.

“You’ll be okay,” she told him. “We can find you a cute boy to kiss instead.”

Austin scrunched up his nose.

“You’re dating Chad,” he pointed out. “I don’t think I can trust your tastes.”

Cynthia huffed and shoved at his face with a muttered,

“Brat. Do my braid?”

This time it was Austin’s turn to huff.

Now

The last person Daniel was expecting to find at a massive hospital fire was Michael. Daniel especially didn’t expect to find him in a suit.

They’d been called to Alvarado Union Hospital because of a major explosion on the fourth floor that led to a fire that was quickly spreading, thanks to the sprinkler system losing pressure. The 133 had been the fourth station called, dispatch catching them on their way back from another scene. That meant they arrived just after the 118, Daniel spotting his Buck and Eddie getting out with the others.

The 133’s ambulances were busy hauling a pair of patients to ERs, Mendoza and the other paramedics with them, so Mehta sent Daniel off with the engine’s medical bag to help where needed. Tents had been set up in front of the hospital, managers doing their best to wrangle patients and workers alike. Ambulatory patients, those who were able to walk under their own power, were sent to parking lot A and that’s where Daniel went, since there weren’t many triage patients evacuated yet and the others were all simply waiting on an ambulance to be freed up.

Of the people there, Daniel expected to see patients in unflattering gowns, visitors in fretful worry, nurses in scrubs. He did not expect to see Michael, dazed and scruffed up.

“What the hell are you doing here?” Daniel asked as he guided – or maybe pushed was a more apt word – the man into a nearby wheelchair.

“I was gonna surprise David after work. Take him to dinner, propose,” the man explained. “The explosion kinda… Threw me back.”

“This is David’s hospital?” Daniel glanced at it over his shoulder as he dug out a penlight. He saw the 118’s aerial had been extended, a line of people climbing it to the fifth floor, to one of the windows right above the raging flames. “Did you hit your head? Lose consciousness?”

“Yes, no, and no,” Michael answered. He flinched when Daniel shone the light in his eyes. “My ears are still ringing.”

“That’s to be expected.”

“Listen, Dan, thank you, but I need to get in there.”

Daniel turned off the penlight and debated whether or not hitting Michael with it would be excusable. It wasn’t like he could actually hurt the guy (much) with it.

“In there? The building that’s on fire? I didn’t realize you’d gone through the fire academy. Congrats, what station did they assign you?”

Michael did not seem very amused. He glowered up at Daniel, and then glowered harder after Daniel pushed him back into the chair when he tried to get up.

“I will call Athena and have her come cuff you to a parking meter,” Daniel threatened.

“David is still-”

“The building’s being evacuated.” Daniel shouldered his bag as a woman called for a paramedic. “Someone’ll drag him out. The last thing any of us needs is you going in there and having to be rescued because your stupid ass inhaled too much smoke or tripped head first into something.”

Was it blunt and rude? Probably. But Daniel wasn’t about to coddle the guy when he was being an idiot. Sometimes the blunt and rude truth was what was needed, as evidenced by the way Michael slumped where he sat and didn’t argue any further.

Eddie could feel the heat from the fire just beneath his feet and outside the nursery door. The babies, too many babies, were all screaming their heads off as they experienced one of the worst first days ever. There was no way to get them out through the stairs, the fire blocking the exit, so they were having to rely on a human bucket brigade down the aerial ladder.

“You’re okay, you’re okay,” Buck cooed to the infants, and then asked Eddie, “Good?”

“Yeah.”

Eddie was the first man on the ladder, right at the window. A precarious position, especially with the precious cargo they were about to be handling. Javier Ortiz, the apparatus operator, was right behind him, and Ravi was at the very foot of the ladder below.

Austin had firmly refused to get near the ladder, and so was the one on the ground that Ravi was going to be passing the infants off to.

Buck picked up the first infant, swaddled up nice and tight, and passed the red-faced and wailing bundle through the window to Eddie. Eddie, in turn, backed down as carefully as he could and turned enough to hand the baby off to Javier, who did the same to pass the bundle off to the firefighter below him. Eddie climbed back up to the top just in time to take another bundle.

After the fourth trip, Bobby’s voice crackled over the radio.

“How’s it looking up there, Buck?”

“Smoke is starting to breach the door, Cap,” Buck replied when his hands were free.

Eddie’s eyes went to the nursery door automatically, spotting the wisps of smoke starting to crawl beneath it. He hurried down a little bit faster as he heard Bobby say to double-time it and then hurried up the same way.

They’d just got the last infant squared away and were about to climb off the ladder when Bobby came over. The seriousness of his expression had Eddie coming to a halt.

“I need you on the third floor,” he told them. “We got an eight year old boy, Parker. He’s trapped up there with his baby sister.”

“I thought that floor was cleared,” Buck said.

“They must’ve missed him in all the chaos. He dialed nine-one-one from a patient’s room, three-one-eight.”

“Back up the ladder,” Eddie announced, already turning to climb back up.

Buck blocked the way, however, and shook his head.

“The fire is right outside the nursery doors,” he pointed out. “Stairs would be better.”

Bobby moved out of the way, letting them shimmy down off the ladder truck and onto solid ground. They headed for the hospital entrance as the captain informed dispatch they were on their way. With the way they rushed, it did not take them very long to hit the smoke-filled stairs and start racing up them.

“Unit One Eighteen, this is Dispatcher Collins,” came over their radios as they were halfway up. “We’ve lost connection with a minor caller in room three-one-eight. Line is dead.”

That wasn’t good.

“Copy that, Dispatch,” Eddie replied. “We’re almost there.”

“This is us,” Buck announced when they hit the landing for the third floor.

The third floor was doing a good approximation of Hell. The lights were out, so the only illumination came from their flashlights and the flickering orange of the raging inferno on the other side of the fire doors. The room they needed to get to was also on the other side of those doors.

“Dispatch, we’re heading that way,” Eddie reported. “But this floor is fully involved.”

“I told him to take shelter in the room, shut the door and seal it with a towel. Room three-one-eight.”

Collins’ voice shook a little with unvoiced stress.

“We can’t go any farther without a hose line,” Buck argued as the pair of them paired through the windows.

Eddie spotted extinguishers on the wall. He knew his boyfriend saw them, too, by the look in his eyes when they faced each other. With a nod, they burst through the doors and each grabbed one of the extinguishers, pulling the pins and pulling the triggers. The extinguishers did next to nothing as they crossed the inferno to the next set of fire doors.

A ball of flames erupted in their faces when they were opened. They fell backwards to the sound of the dispatcher trying to raise them for an update. Eddie was too focused on the burning remains of the hall and the rooms they needed to reach to answer her.

Buck did it for him.

“There is no room three-one-eight,” he radioed. “The fire took it.”

When the request for more paramedics on the fourth floor came over the radio, Mehta sent Daniel to assist. He reached the stairwell on the eastern end of the hospital just as Hen and Ravi did, the pair not even questioning as he fell into step with them. The stairwell was filling with smoke, making visibility difficult. They almost literally stumbled into a nurse helping evacuate a roughed up looking patient, the pair of them covered in dust and soot.

Both of them could walk, though, so they were of low priority.

“How many are still up there?” Hen asked.

“Hard to tell,” the nurse answered. “But there’s a guy helping dig people out of the rubble.”

“One of ours?”

“No, one of ours.”

“A nurse?”

“A patient.”

This part of the fourth floor was free of fire, still, but not free of damage. The explosion had shaken the walls and the ceiling, sending bits crashing to the floor. Firefighters from other houses were carrying people out.

“Who else is up here?” Daniel stopped one to ask.

“One more,” was the answer. “But he won’t leave.”

“Must be the guy helping,” Ravi concluded.

The found the man in question shortly after, trying to move a piece of debris. Old, gray haired. He pretty much ignored Hen’s attempts to get him to leave until they helped lift the debris, revealing the body of a clearly deceased nurse. Blood from the head wound that killed her covered half her face, and it was already congealing.

“No pulse,” Hen said, checking the woman’s neck to be sure.

“It’s not Callie,” the patient remarked, sounding relieved.

“Sir, the hospital’s being evacuated,” Ravi explained while Hen tagged the body.

“No, there’s still someone here that needs help,” the guy insisted, already moving on. "Callie!”

“Sir, sir, hold up.” Hen stumbled to catch him. “You need to leave the building, sir, and let us do our job. It’s not safe. Sir!”

“It’s Rupert.”

“Rupert, the building’s on fire and it ain’t stopping,” Daniel bluntly told the man. “It’s time to get the hell out.”

“I’m not leaving without Callie.”

“You wanna bet?”

“We appreciate your help,” Hen cut in, giving Daniel a chiding glare. Daniel rolled his eyes. “But it’s too dangerous for you to stay here. You need to leave. We’re not asking, we’re telling.”

“I survived stage four cancer!” Rupert declared. “I can survive this.”

He stomped off, still in search of whoever Callie was, leaving a very annoyed Hen to send Ravi after him. The guy was fast for someone of his apparent age and health.

“Dragging him out is still an option,” Daniel remarked.

“Don’t tempt me,” Hen said.

They were left to trail after Rupert, looking out for anyone else who needed and were willing to be evacuated. Ravi was a bit ahead of them, so they had to jog when he called for the pair. They found him following a trail of blood to a storage closet, where Rupert was lifting a set of shelves off another nurse. Callie, going by the way Rupert was fretting.

She was unconscious with a bleeding wound to her leg. Daniel got a tourniquet around it while Hen woke her up and proceeded to assess her for spinal damage. When the results of that was aces across the board, Hen pulled out a c-collar to stick around Callie’s neck.

“I can’t believe you’re here,” Callie mumbled to Rupert. “You’re not a patient anymore.”

“I guess I got used to this place,” Rupert told her. “I’m not leaving you just yet.”

“Probie, there was a gurney right over there,” Daniel said. “Grab it?”

Ravi nodded and hurried out, coming back with it quickly. They transferred her over to it just as something above them creaked ominously, which had them rushing even without Rupert’s insistence. Rupert shouted “Get out!” behind them and Daniel heard the ceiling give, turning just in time to catch the probie when he was shoved to safety.

Rupert was not so lucky. He was caught in the pile of debris, head and arms hanging limply where they stuck out. Ravi was stuck staring at him in stunned silence until Daniel pulled him along.

“C’mon,” he told the probie. “We need to go before more gives.”

The probie just nodded, still daced, and followed behind like a lost puppy.

Chapter 17

It was with a grim air that Buck and Eddie marched back down to the hospital parking lot. Neither of them spoke, neither of them had anything to say. That part of the third floor had been an inferno, and there was no way that anyone could survive that. There was no way that an eight year old and an infant could have survived that.

Buck took up the job of stowing away their gear and their used oxygen tanks, slipping out of the harness he wore to carry the tank and then helping Eddie out of his. His boyfriend was concerningly, but understandably, silent. He’d just put the last tank up and closed the compartment door when he glanced over to see Eddie using the fire engine to hold himself up, a hand to his chest.

A couple months ago, this would have had Buck calling out for a paramedic and easing Eddie into sitting. Now, though, he recognized it for what it was. Another panic attack from the guy who doesn’t panic.

“You okay?” he asked, already knowing the answer.

“That kid, he was eight years old,” Eddie ground out. “And his sister?”

Buck let out a sigh and got into Eddie’s space enough to place a gloved hand over his cheek.

“I know, Eds.”

“What are we supposed to do with that?”

“I’ll tell you what Danny told me,” Buck said. “We pack it away and move on.”

“Pack it away and move on,” Eddie repeated, sounding more than a bit dubious.

“To the next one that needs us.”

Buck could tell that it wasn’t helping Eddie anymore than it did when Danny told him the exact same thing. But it was the best Buck had for the moment, the advice to push it off until they weren’t on the scene or on the job. He pulled out a fresh tank and started on fastening it into their harnesses.

“Firefighter Diaz, this is Dispatcher Bates. Do you copy?”

Eddie sighed and grabbed his radio to answer, face already clearing.

“Go ahead, Dispatch.”

“Did you get to room Three-One-Eight?”

“Negative.” Eddie was blank faced as he stared into the side view mirror of the engine. “It was lost to the fire.”

“Well, the little boy just called me back.”

Buck grabbed his radio as he watched Eddie’s eyes go wide in his reflection.

“Are- Are you saying he’s alive?”

“Where is he?” was Eddie’s question.

“He’s still saying the same place as before. Is it possible you went to the wrong room?”

“No,” Eddie said, drawing out the word a bit as he stared more intently at the mirror. “Is there an Eighty-One E in the hospital?”

“Uh… Yes. On the fifth floor.”

“He was- He was reading a reflection!” Eddie nearly shouted, excitement palpable. He scooped up his harness and started running. “Let’s go!”

Buck ran after him, helping him get his oxygen tank harness back on as they went. The inside of the hospital was even smokier than before, a cloud of it filling up the stairwell. They ran as fast as they could up the stairs, Bates – Buck was pretty sure her name was Linda, just from listening to Maddie talk about her coworkers – giving them directions once they reached the fifth floor.

When they reached 81E, which was third one on the left past the elevators, just as Linda said, they could hear the beautiful sounds of an upset infant. They pulled open the door and stepped over a towel that had been stuffed beneath, finding both Parker and his sister on the floor. Parker was unconscious, and the baby was flailing her little arms as her swaddling was slowly coming undone.

“Pulse is stable,” Eddie reported as he checked on the boy. “Must’ve been the smoke, but he can’t have been out for too long.”

Buck left the job of taking care of Parker to Eddie. Instead, he scooped up the infant and her blanket, and stuck a too large oxygen mask over her face. When Eddie had the boy in his arms, they did not waste any time lingering. The trip back to the parking lot was, if possible, even faster than the trip up.

“This hospital has stupid room numbering,” Buck remarked once they’d dropped brother and sister off with the paramedics and nurses. “Eighty-One E? Seriously?”

“Yeah,” Eddie agreed.

“I get why he did what he did,” Ravi said.

Hen turned to look at him, but he was facing away from her. He sat perched on the back of the ambulance while she was pulling out more supplies.

“Rupert?” Hen guessed, because it was that hard of a guess.

They’d found a letter on Rupert’s body and gave it Callie, which Ravi had to read for her. A love letter of sorts, from friend to friend. Callie was a nurse in the cancer ward and until today, Rupert had been a patient in the very same.

“I spent practically my entire childhood in hospitals,” the probie told her, still not looking her way. “Those doctors and nurses become your family.”

“I didn’t know that,” Hen admitted, coming to sit next to him.

One would think, after Buck, that she would have learned that just because someone seemed open, it didn’t mean they actually were. Ravi didn’t talk much about himself, she was beginning to realize. Despite being generally upbeat and friendly, he was about as closed off to them as Bobby had been at the beginning. She wondered if that was on purpose or if it was like Buck, simply not realizing that he’d forgotten to share important details of himself that were never intended to be secrets.

“It’s not exactly fun party talk,” Ravi said, obviously never having been to a party with Buckleys. Neither Daniel nor Buck were shy when it came to talking about it. “Chemo on the weekends instead of soccer tournaments, but those people cheered me on just the same. They saved my life. So I get why he ran back into the building. To pay it forward.”

“I guess that’s why you do it, too,” Hen said. “That’s probably why Dan wanted to be a doctor.”

Ravi turned his head, the entirety of his dust covered face turned in a curious frown.

“Buck’s brother?”

“I guess you wouldn’t know.” Sometimes common knowledge wasn’t actually common. “Buck’s a savior sibling.”

She saw Ravi’s eyes go wide.

“What? But that’s- When he was born, that would’ve been- That’s so early.”

“Yeah,” Hen agreed. The “first” one, Adam Nash, was born in 2000. That’s how early Buck was. She’d looked it up, once, and wondered how desperate the Buckley parents must’ve been. “They’ve got a complex about it. Dan’s a bit protective of his brother.”

She still remembered the day Daniel stormed into the station, all seething rage. She’d been certain she was about to see Bobby be murdered right before her eyes. Jones, she knew, had feared the same; the way he hung close to Daniel had been less about showing his support and more so he could grab the man if the need arose. Their engineer had been tense the entire encounter, until Daniel walked away.

Not even Athena showing up had Jones relaxing any.

“Buck isn’t all that shy talking about it,” Hen told Ravi. “Not sure about Dan, though.”

They weren’t precisely close, after all. It was definitely a safe assumption, with their relationship, that he talked to Jones about it. But Hen wasn’t certain of how open Daniel was when it came to his childhood cancer.

“A savior sibling.” Ravi shook his head. “Wow.”

“Like I said, they’ve both got a complex about it.” Hen pushed herself to her feet and went back into the ambulance. “So be prepared for that if you ask Buck about anything.”

“Yeah.” The probie sounded distracted. “Yeah, I will…”

Daniel decided the hospital must’ve bribed its way through fire inspections. He’d never seen fire, regular fire that was fueled by nothing more than air and whatever it managed to catch, eat through what was supposed to be fire rated doors and walls so quickly. It acted as if every obstruction was just a plywood facade on a film set while they tried their best to keep it at bay long enough for David to finish actual brain surgery.

The hospital was a loss. Once they got everyone out, once they got David and his patient out, they all resigned themselves to keeping the fire from spreading farther while they watched it gut the hospital. They were on scene for hours until other houses could come in and relieve them. Daniel spent maybe a bit too long in the shower, once they got back to the station, simply standing in the tiled cubicle and letting the hot water run over him.

The last bit of shift involved making sure everything on the trucks and ambulances was restocked before they got any more calls or the next shift came in, whichever came first. That unfortunately took long enough that there wasn’t much of a point to even attempting at getting any sleep. They’d be getting up and going home before they ever hit REM.

Going to Austin’s place instead of his own could have been excused with it being closer, but that was a lie. It was actually a little bit farther away, if there was a difference at all. Mostly, he was tired and he didn’t feel like sleeping on Eddie’s couch again. Nor did he feel like going home to an empty apartment.

Truthfully, he never felt like going home to an empty apartment.

He got there before Austin himself did, and he let himself in with his key. It was with exhaustion quickly settling into his bones that he slipped out of his shoes and dragged himself to the bedroom, kicking off his jeans along the way and snagging a sweater to crawl into. The unmade bed was soft and welcoming when he collapsed onto it, and he started dozing off almost the moment he closed his eyes.

He vaguely knew when Austin arrived, pulled out of sleep by the way he was jostled a bit as the other man got into bed, too, rousing enough to acknowledge his presence. When he woke up hours later, Daniel found himself in the iron grip of a human koala, Austin’s face tucked into the back of his neck. Because Austin was a cuddler, and Daniel knew that, and Daniel actually managed to feel a bit of guilt for taking advantage of that. But it was Austin who’d started it, so it was only a little bit of guilt.

There was a text on his dying phone waiting for him when he woke up, though he could only read it once he was freed from the human bear trap. It was from Michael, an invite to come over. An actual date and time, too, so nothing like their past get-togethers.

Unsure exactly what it was, Daniel grabbed the usual six pack on his way over on the night of. Michael answered with a smile not long after he knocked.

“Hey, I brought-” Daniel paused in the middle of lifting up the six pack, spotting the whole Grant horde just inside. Even Nash was there, doing his best to maintain the veneer of Midwestern politeness. “This is awkward.”

“Hello, Daniel,” Athena greeted him with a nod and a smile. “I didn’t know you were invited, too.”

“I didn’t know what I was being invited to,” Daniel replied as he stepped inside, letting Michael carry off the beer. After he pulled one out for himself. “Still don’t.”

“They’re engaged,” May supplied, gesturing between Michael and David. “David, show him.”

David bashfully held up his arm. On his wrist was a new watch, fancy and shiny. It looked very expensive.

“You proposed to him with a watch?” Daniel asked as he sat down at the dining table.

He went with the side opposite Nash, and on the other end. It stuck him next to May and across from Harry, but he didn’t particularly mind. He used a bottle opener on his keys to pop the cap off his drink.

“I like it,” David defended his fiance. “He got it engraved.”

“What would you have proposed with, huh?” Michael demanded, grabbing a bottle of wine off the counter and refilling Athena’s glass at her silent request. “Actually, what would Buck or Eddie propose with? Neither of them seem like rings guys, either.”

“Hell if I know,” Daniel said. “Knowing my brother, it’ll probably be a pretty pebble he found.”

“Penguins,” was all Nash said to that, and Daniel silently pointed at him in agreement.

It seemed he wasn’t the only one subjected to a marathon of penguin trivia.

“This isn’t just an engagement announcement, though.” Michael set the wine bottle down on the table and stood at the head of it. “As all of you- As most of you know, David has decided to go to Haiti to help with the relief efforts there.”

“There are worse places you could move to,” Daniel remarked. Because he couldn’t help himself, he followed it with, “Like Minnesota.”

“Daniel,” Athena tutted, shaking her head at him.

“When are you both leaving?”

“He leaves in a couple weeks, and then I-” Michael cut himself off. It seemed to have been an automatic response, because he blinked and shook his head and said, “Wait, no, that was going to be my other announcement.”

Daniel stared at him, then he slowly turned his head enough to look past May and meet Athena’s gaze. Except, instead of anything he expected, he just saw the same surprise as on her husband’s and children’s faces. Which he could not understand, because he honestly thought it was a no-brainer. The moment Michael said that David was going to Haiti, Daniel had known that Michael was going, too.

“I am so sorry for not being stupid,” Daniel said with an eye roll.

Michael huffed at him.

“I want to help rebuild,” he explained, though Daniel honestly didn’t think he needed an explanation beyond the fact that David was going. “After everything, the brain tumor and the pandemic and the- the everything, I was feeling a little lost. And then I watched a bunch of people come together to save someone they didn’t know. More than one. So I just…. I want to do the same.”

Athena was silent and looking everywhere but at Michael, making no effort at hiding how upset she was at the idea of him moving so far away. But eventually she stood and hugged him, and the others joined in. Even Nash.

Not Daniel, though. Daniel was perfectly content being on the outside of that Hallmark moment.

Then

Austin lost his virginity in high school. There was nothing particularly special about the occasion, nothing memorable. It was with another guy his age, Justin, who thought his “strong, silent routine” was hot.

His first thought when it was done was, “that was it?” It felt good, sure, as did making Justin feel good, but it was also so very underwhelming for how much hype it received. The afterwards, with Justin curled up into his side and seeping warmth into Austin’s skin, that was more pleasant. But sex didn’t seem worth all the effort people put into chasing after it, not when he could go masturbate in his room and achieve the same end.

Worse, he hadn’t felt any different when he was kissing Justin – when he was doing all the other things with Justin – than he had kissing Tiffany at that eighth grade dance. He hadn’t felt any different looking at Justin than he did Tiffany. There’d been hunger on Justin’s face when their clothes were coming off. It was a hunger that was foreign and unknowable to Austin, because Austin had never felt it.

He was beginning to think that he was simply broken. Abnormal. Normal people, they liked girls or they liked guys or they liked both. He was beginning to realize that he liked neither. Not in that way. He looked at Justin the same way he looked at Tiffany, and he looked at Tiffany the same way he looked at one of the more elaborate pieces of graffiti he passed whenever they drove into Los Angeles proper.

Cynthia was going to college upstate, which meant he only saw her when she came home on breaks. They’d talk on the phone though, and she listened to him as he confessed to being broken. And when she came home for Christmas break, she hadn’t taken long to get him alone while leaving Emmett – her newest and currently longest lasting boyfriend amongst the string of boyfriends that followed her finally kicking Chad to the curb for good – to fend for himself with her family.

Last Austin knew, Ethan was giving the guy his version of the shovel talk, which involved a car battery and body wax.

“So.” Cynthia sat primly at her desk, one of her bags in her lap. “At college, you learn a lot of things.”

“I’d kinda hope so,” Austin told her as he sat on her bed. It had been waiting for her, pristinely made, since she left at the end of the summer. “Why else would you be going?”

“All the cute boys,” his sister quipped, but she eyed him as she said it like she was gauging his reaction. “But as I was saying, you learn a lot of things at college. Not all of it in class, either. Like what not to mix together at a party, and how to tell your roommate that they need to clean their side without starting a fight.”

Austin nodded. He’d already decided he wasn’t going to college, having settled firmly on his desired career months ago and working towards it ever since. He had to be fit to get accepted into the LAFD, after all.

“And there are some things you learn by talking to people.” Cynthia unzipped her bag and dug into it, pulling out a bunch of printed off pages. “Like places to go looking to learn other things.”

She held the pages out to Austin, who leaned forward to take them. The first thing he noticed was the upside down triangle at the top of the first page, mostly empty except for its filled in black tip. The second thing he noticed was that these were printed off pages of a website. The third thing he noticed was the name of the site, the Asexuality Visibility and Education Network.

“Cynthia, what is this?”

“You, I think.” She sat the bag down and got up. “I should go rescue Emmett. He’s too sweet to let Ethan scare him off.”

Austin nodded absently as he stared down at the papers in front of him. He barely noticed her crossing the room to stand in front of him, not until she was bending down slightly to press a kiss onto his forehead.

“You’re not broken,” she whispered, before giving him a smile and sweeping out of the room.

Austin sat there for a very long time afterwards, reading and rereading the answers she’d found for him.

Chapter 18

The argument Buck and Eddie were having was not a new one. In fact, it was months old. It was just that, between the black out and then Maddie, they’d stuck a pin in it and then promptly forgotten about it. But the events of the hospital fire, or rather Eddie’s reaction when he thought the little boy and his baby sister were dead, brought it back to the fore.

“I don’t need to see a therapist,” Eddie bit out.

His temper was steadily fraying, Buck could tell. It frayed a little bit more every time Buck revived the topic. It probably also didn’t help that this time they were at the station, doing their best to keep their voices from carrying across the apparatus bay.

“You were about to have a panic attack,” he retorted. “Another panic attack.”

“I was not-” Eddie cut himself off and came to a stop, glancing around for an audience. No one else was close enough to hear them, at least not that Buck could see. “I was not having a panic attack, Buck. I was upset because I thought children were dead. Because I thought a baby was dead.”

“You were clutching your chest like you were about to go into cardiac arrest.” Buck reached out a poked Eddie in the chest, right on the LAFD logo on his shirt and over his heart. “So either you were having a heart attack, and we should’ve taken you to a cardiologist a week ago. Or! You were having a panic attack, and you need a therapist.”

“I don’t need a therapist,” his boyfriend repeated for what was probably the hundredth time that week. “Let it go, Buck. I’m fine.”

He stomped away before Buck could keep kicking the dead horse. Buck seethed in frustration, left with the choice of chasing after Eddie and maybe pushing this into an actual blow up of a fight. Or he could let it go, like Eddie asked. For the moment.

He went with the latter, because his own temper was starting to wear, and he stomped up the stairs to the station’s loft. Ravi was awkwardly hanging around, trying too hard to seem inconspicuous to not have been eavesdropping on him and Eddie from above. Not that Buck thought he heard much, but anyone could tell by sight alone that there was a bit of tension between them at the moment.

“Just say it, probie,” Buck sighed.

He crossed the loft to the fridge and pulled out a bottle of water while the probie dithered. Finally, the younger man seemed to pull himself together to say,

“Hen said you were a savior sibling.”

“What?” Buck blinked at him, taken aback by the topic. He’d been expecting a comment about him and Eddie. “I mean, yeah. I am. It’s cool.”

Ravi stared at him.

“It’s cool?” he echoed.

“Yeah!” Buck nodded. “I was literally made for Danny, to make him better. Unless I royally fuck up again, I’ll always be good for something. I’ll always be useful.”

The probie stared at him with something akin to horror, though Buck couldn’t for the life of him figure out why. Was it something he said? Was there something on his face?

“Useful,” Ravi once again echoed, and then said, “Oh my God, this is what she meant.”

Before Buck could ask about that, the tones went off and it was a rush to the trucks. It was a medical call, according to dispatch, so only the engine and the ambulance went screaming out of the bay. The atmosphere was a bit tense between Buck and Eddie, still, but they were sat across from each other all the same and tangled their legs together in the limited space of the engine cab as they always did.

He wasn’t really paying attention to what was being said over the headsets, busy as he was trying to make sure he and Eddie were okay without asking the question aloud. But his attention was grabbed by the throat when he heard Hen say,

“May walked in on him and Athena the other night. Connecting.”

“Oh, God,” Eddie laughed. “Parental nightmare.”

Buck heard Bobby sigh over the headset.

“Apparently my wife has been oversharing,” he lamented.

“I just wasn’t aware that we were able to bring our gear home for recreational purposes,” Hen said, wagging her eyebrows a bit at those of them in the back.

Were they allowed? Buck nudged Eddie with this foot until his boyfriend paid attention to him, and then raised an eyebrow in question. Eddie stared and then rolled his eyes. But it wasn’t a no.

“You’ve been playing sexy firefighter, Cap?” Ravi asked, voice teasing, though there was still a vague expression of shell shock on his face. For whatever reason.

Bobby turned in his seat to look Ravi in the eye, and then asked,

“What makes you think that I was the firefighter?”

“Oh, come on. I don’t want to think about that,” Buck complained. He shut his eyes and was bombarded with visions of Athena in Bobby’s turnout coat. Because his brain was often times not his friend. “Ugh, now I can’t stop thinking about it.”

While the others laughed, he was left wishing he could somehow go at his gray matter with bleach and a scrub brush.

Bobby was sat at one of the small round tables up in the station’s loft and was, until five seconds previously, discussing one Clive Wexler with Hen.
