He was the patient on the call they’d had earlier in the day, and Hen had just returned with Eddie from transporting him to the hospital. It was an unremarkable call, except for the fact that Clive somehow knew Hen’s mother. Clive knew her well enough, in fact, that he called her by her government name and referred to Hen’s father as the shrapnel that led to Toni Wilson being reluctant to leave herself exposed.

But five seconds ago, Ravi came and sat down across from them, and they dropped the conversation to focus on him. He’d been carrying an air of upset since just before the call with Clive, enough so that Bobby had been debating taking him aside and asking him about it. It was especially concerning, because Bobby couldn’t think of anything that could’ve happened in that time to cause it.

“He called himself useful,” the probie blurted out.

“You talked to Buck,” Hen guessed with a nod.

Bobby quickly looked about to make sure Jones wasn’t around, even glancing up at the rafters above them in a fit of paranoia. The last thing any of them needed was Ravi saying something unwise about Buck and Daniel hearing about it. Which he would, because Jones seemed perfectly willing to throw people under the bus when it came to the brothers. He’d certainly done it to Bobby, and without an ounce of guilt or regret afterwards.

“Talked to Buck about what?” Bobby asked, though he could make a confident guess.

“I asked him about being a savior sibling,” Ravi told them. “And he said it was cool, because at least he’d always be good for something.”

“I told you they had a complex,” Hen said.

“I thought you meant that he was sensitive about it or something! Not- Not whatever the hell that is.”

“I also said he wasn’t shy talking about it.”

“His probie year, he sat down and started talking,” Bobby said, trying to decide if it was a good memory or not. “It was right after the blood drive Chimney threw. Remember?”

Hen nodded.

“I’d just been told my blood had a lot of anti-D antibodies.” Ever since then, or rather, ever since he threw away his book, Bobby went and donated plasma at least once a month. He was still uneasy around needles, however. “And I guess he overheard me and Chimney talking about it and wanted to bond or something. Just sat down and started talking about Daniel and…”

Bobby shook his head. Simply thinking about it made his blood boil in a way it hadn’t when he was first hearing it, too shocked to fully absorb what was being told to him. He’d never met them, but he very much supported Daniel’s decision to keep Buck away from their parents.

“I don’t know if it was a direct quote,” Bobby said. “But there was something about how the only reason their parents kept him around was in case Dan got sick again. He called himself spare parts.”

Hen, having already heard this before, or at least the line about being spare parts, pursed her lips and shook her head. Ravi, however, was staring at Bobby in silent horror.

“How did this even come up?” Bobby asked, curious.

“I, uh. I had juvenile leukemia as a kid,” the probie explained. “I don’t know… How desperate were they to make an entire person?”

“Apparently very desperate.”

“You said they both had a complex about it,” Ravi said to Hen. “If Buck’s like that, how bad is his brother?”

Bobby couldn’t help the bitter laugh he let out at that question.

“He’s protective,” Hen answered with a sidelong glance at Bobby.

“He threatened my life once,” Bobby said, and watched as Ravi’s eyes went wide at the statement.

“When did-? No, I bet I know when. Buck’s probie year, after you-” Hen cut herself off, eyes flickering quickly to the probie. “After the plane crash.”

“Yeah.”

Bobby still remembered leaving the station that night, tired and weary and just wanting a drink. He remembered Daniel appearing out of the shadows in the parking lot, face a blank mask. And he remembered the way he was shoved harshly into his own truck, agitating his old back injury, and held there with an arm to his throat while Daniel threatened him.

And to think, Daniel had struck him as pleasant the first time they met.

Then

When he accepted Daniel’s proposal, Austin had been too focused on how his mother would now stop trying to set him up with random people. It had been a relief, it still was, to have something to ward off his mother’s well-meaning attempts at forcing him into her idea of happiness. The kind that involved romantic dates, marriage, and two-and-a-half kids. The kind that held no appeal to him whatsoever.

He liked his nephews and his niece, but the idea of having his own ankle biters sounded awful. And dates, marriage, romance? That all sounded like a chore, honestly. A warm body was nice to wrap himself around, but he’d decided a long time ago that the effort it took to reach that point was not worth it.

When he accepted Daniel’s proposal, Austin had not been thinking about anything beyond finally being free from his mother’s well-meaning efforts. He had not thought ahead, had not considered his mother’s excitement or the way she’d want to tell everyone. He had not considered what would happen when he introduced Daniel to everyone as his new boyfriend.

They’d gone out to eat for his mother’s birthday in May, as they always did. That year, she had encouraged him to bring Daniel and he couldn’t think of any excuse to not bring his supposed boyfriend along when his in-laws were also coming. Besides, as Austin was quickly learning, Daniel was not one to turn down the opportunity to be fed. Especially for free, with Daniel laughing when the waiter put their check down in front of Austin after a moment’s hesitation at the end of the meal.

Daniel had charmed Ethan and Irene and Emmett as he had Austin’s parents. The boys loved him, as did Rosemary, and Daniel didn’t seem to mind at all when the three of them decided to climb all over him while they waited to be seated. Austin had the briefest vision of him allowing a much younger, much smaller Buck to do the same.

The only one who hadn’t been instantly charmed was Cynthia. Not because she didn’t like him, or at least the version of Daniel that Daniel was pretending to be. The one that was an attentive boyfriend, constantly touching Austin and smiling at everyone and joking with everyone and calling Austin’s parents “ma’am” and “sir.” The version of Daniel that was so similar to the one that Austin had gotten to know over the past couple of years, yet was so different as to be almost alien.

Cynthia liked him, but she was clearly confused the entire time. Because she knew. She knew, and Austin had not considered her at all between accepting Daniel’s proposal and introducing him to everyone. Austin managed to avoid her interrogating him at the dinner, and managed to dodge her afterwards with an excuse about Daniel needing to get home.

Daniel had made a quip about being a pumpkin, making everyone laugh.

But the interrogation would not be put off forever. As a firefighter, it was common sense to give someone the spare key to his apartment just in case something happened. He’d chosen Cynthia, because she’d made the most sense at the time. Now he was regretting it, because she didn’t even give him the courtesy of knocking before waltzing right in.

“I could have been busy, you know,” Austin complained as he set aside the book he was reading. “What if I had a guest over?”

“Then I could ask lovely Daniel what was going on, too,” she said as she made her way to his couch. Instead of taking a seat beside him, however, she just picked up one of the throw pillows and asked, “Seriously, Austin?”

It was the pillow she’d gotten him for his birthday just that January, along with a magnet that was currently stuck onto his locker at work. Both were striped black-gray-white-purple, the asexual pride flag that he hadn’t even known was a thing until she was giving it to him. Just like he hadn’t known asexuality was a thing until she gave him those printed off pages so long ago.

“It was his idea,” Austin said.

“That’s not an explanation, that’s assigning blame.” Cynthia flopped down onto the couch and smacked him with the pillow. “There I am, drinking a bottle of wine while I watch Emmett fail at convincing Sam to put on clothes, when Mom calls to tell me that you went and got yourself a boyfriend. And I thought, Mom, it’s too early to put you in a nursing home-”

“Cynthia,” Austin sighed, already feeling the headache forming.

“But if you’re this demented already, maybe we should start looking for one for you, or maybe we should check the house for black mold, ‘cause there’s no way Austin, my brother Austin, went and got himself anyone-”

“Cynthia, please.”

“And if he had!” Cynthia smacked him with the pillow again. “I would have heard it from him.”

“I’m sorry?”

His sister huffed and rolled her eyes.

“How did this even happen?” she asked. “What, was he just like, ‘hey let’s lie to your mother and pretend we’re dating’?”

“Actually, that was pretty much how it went.” Austin shrugged at her incredulous expression. “He’s… Different.”

Understatement of the century.

“Pot, kettle.” Cynthia settled back into the couch, gripping the pillow to her chest. “Alright, tell me about this guy. How did you meet? And don’t give me whatever bullshit you fed Mom.”

“It wasn’t bullshit, I really did meet him through work friends…”

Now

As much as Eddie tried to pretend otherwise, he loved gossip and drama. Especially if it didn’t involve him. In high school, he would sniff out anything juicy and happily share it with Buck, and later Shannon. Even now, he practically salivated over any tidbit of playground gossip that Chris brought home and would probably join the PTA if he had the time just for a new source of it. So it only made sense that, as soon as he got a whiff of Wilson Family Drama, he would pounce on it and force Hen to tell him everything.

They were parked after a call, a minor one that was almost not worth getting out of the station for, while they waited for Ravi to get back from a coffee run. Hen and Eddie were perched on the side of the engine, and Buck was leaning against it, while Hen fed them the latest update on the saga of Clive, who definitely somehow knew Hen’s mother. Though neither Clive nor Toni were giving up the pertinent details.

“From the neighborhood?” Eddie repeated, a bit incredulously.

“Yep. That’s all she said,” Hen confirmed. “Like he was just some guy she used to see on the corner or something.”

“Oh, she used to definitely see him, alright.”

“Right? I wasn’t just imagining that.”

“Clive and Toni were definitely a thing,” Eddie confidently declared. “A big time, long-lost-love kind of thing. My abuela would eat this up, she loves a good telenovela.”

“Oh, ‘cause you don’t?” Buck smirked and pushed off the engine, moving to stand in front of Eddie so he could properly mock his boyfriend. “I know you watch ‘em with Christopher.”

Eddie got into it, too. He’d get pissy whenever Buck talked over the characters, or if something came up that pulled him away from it.

“That’s how we practice our Spanish,” Eddie lied.

“Uh-huh. And when you watch ‘em while he’s at school-”

“The real question is,” he quickly said, talking over Buck. Buck rolled his eyes with a laugh. “What happens next in the story?”

“Well, I do have his number, I could call him,” Hen said. “I mean, just to see how he’s doing after his fall.”

“Of course,” Eddie said with an encouraging nod. Because he loved drama that didn’t involve himself.

“Hold on, you don’t know why they broke up,” Buck pointed out. “What if there’s some deep trauma that shouldn’t be poked at?”

He smiled brightly at his boyfriend, who glowered at him in return.

“I doubt there were any prom night regrets,” Hen said, causing Eddie to turn his glower upon her. “And it didn’t send either of them fleeing the city, state, and country.”

“Still, though-”

“Three minutes!?” Hen suddenly exclaimed as she got up, checking her watch.

Buck glanced over and saw Ravi had come back. He had drink trays in his hands, and Buck helpfully snagged his and Eddie’s drinks. Austin appeared from seemingly no where – it did not escape Buck’s notice that wherever he came from, it was close enough to eavesdrop on the Wilson drama – to get his drink, too.

“How the hell did you manage to shave almost three minutes off Buck’s best coffee run time?” Hen asked Ravi as she snagged her own drink.

“Mobile order.” Ravi shrugged. “Walked in and the drinks were waiting.”

That sounded a lot like cheating to Buck’s ears, and therefore did not count.

Daniel perched on his counter, swinging a leg so his bare heel thumped against the cabinet as he waited for his sister to answer. He had his phone pinned between ear and shoulder to free his hands up to eat cereal. When she finally picked up, he set the bowl aside to take his phone in hand.

“Sorry,” she said. “I was talking to Kira.”

“Your group bestie.”

“Yes, my group bestie.”

Daniel could almost visualize the way his sister was no doubt rolling her eyes at him.

“What are you doing for Christmas?”

“Um,” Maddie answered, and Daniel took a calming breath. “I- I don’t know, Dan, I- Something small, probably?”

“Something small,” Daniel repeated. “Probably.”

“I just- I’m not ready to go back to Los Angeles, Dan. I’m not ready to see everyone.”

“Chimney hasn’t come back. You wouldn’t have to see him.”

There was guilty silence in his ear.

“You know, when Buck missed Christmas, I flew all the way to Peru.”

“Dan…”

“Your choice, Mads,” Daniel said. “Are you flying to LA for Christmas or are we flying to you? There is no third option.”

More silence.

“I don’t really have room in my apartment…”

“That wasn’t an answer,” he pointed out. “But a couple days in Boston it is. I’ll tell Buck. He’ll probably want to go back to that boat.”

“Yeah, okay.” This time, Maddie’s voice had a smile in it. “Just the three of us. We can go to the Constitution together, if it’s open.”

“If you don’t have at least a small tree and lights, Buck will pout until we hit up the store.”

“There’ll be a tree with lights,” she promised.

They talked a bit longer, mostly discussing what dates would work best. Not Christmas Day proper, Buck wouldn’t want to disappoint Chris like that. They were gonna try for the week after, or maybe the first week of the new year.

His brother answered far quicker than Maddie when Daniel called him.

“So, squirt, we’re making a trip to Boston after Christmas...”

Chapter 19

Then

Daniel would be lying if he claimed to not be at least a little bit nervous about introducing Austin to Buck as his fake boyfriend. Technically speaking, it was his longest and most serious relationship by that point, sad as it was. Plus, there was no telling what Buck’s reaction would be to finding out that Daniel had been pretending to date his coworker since before he was Buck’s coworker and never told him about it.

Maybe Daniel would have broken and confessed to it earlier if Buck had chosen to do something fancier with his shield ceremony. Instead, with Maddie in Pennsylvania, Alma in Texas, and those frat guys he hung out with unsuitable for public events, he just opted to not make a big deal out of it. Daniel still insisted on taking him out to celebrate, and even got him a cake.

So, Christmas. When Tabitha, through Austin, insisted he bring Buck along to the Christmas get together, it seemed like the perfect opportunity. Even more so when Maddie rebuffed their attempts at a Buckley Sibling Christmas. Because she wanted to be alone on the holiday, except not, because she was apparently spending it with that Chimney guy. So maybe she just didn’t want to spend it with them.

(Athena invited them to spend Christmas at the Grant house, but it was also to celebrate her engagement to fucking Nash, so that was a solid no even without the Joneses.)

The Christmas this year started out much the same as their previous Christmases, even on the years they had to celebrate the day before or after. Buck got up far too early, though at least he was past the stage where he’d burst down Daniel’s door and wake him up by cannonballing into him. Then they had breakfast before they opened the presents that had gathered under their tree, including the ones that migrated there over night.

Those presents were labeled “from Santa,” even though Buck stopped believing in Santa when a little shithead in elementary school made fun of him for it. Daniel didn’t think Buck remembered it, though he did. And he insisted on the Santa presents even now, still spiteful that he wasn’t allowed to throw that kid into traffic.

After the presents, they first called Alma and chatted with her. Then Daniel called Maddie to wish her a merry Christmas, because she was still his sister, even if sometimes he thought she would rather be anything but. She once again refused to do anything with them that day, which surely had nothing to do with the fact that Chimney wasn’t going to Athena’s, either. Hen had mentioned it to Buck, when she texted to ask if he was going.

Most of the morning afterwards was spent vegetating on the couch while some Christmas movie played on the TV. It wasn’t one of those claymation ones, sadly. Daniel kinda missed watching them. Christmas mornings were one of the few times their parents didn’t shoo Buck out of the living room, no patience for the way he couldn’t sit still or stay quiet for the time it took to watch things.

Speaking of their parents, his phone began to ring with the tone he assigned to Home (Hershey). Daniel didn’t answer until he’d taken the phone into his bedroom and shut the door behind him. Because even like this, even just their voices over the phone, they were still too close to Buck for his liking.

The conversation he had with his mother pretty much went the same as it always did. She was eager to talk to him, tried to guilt him for once again not visiting for Christmas – as if he would just leave Buck alone on the holidays – and didn’t once ask after her youngest son. She didn’t ask about Maddie, either, but that was because they still thought she was with Doug. Unless Maddie herself had told them otherwise.

Eventually, the time they needed to leave approached. For a single moment, Daniel thought about backing out. Maybe he could send Buck to Athena’s after all, tell Tabitha that Buck just had other plans and couldn’t make it. Or maybe he could insist on spending the Christmas with Buck alone, tell Austin they weren’t coming. It wouldn’t be the first time he’d missed Christmas with them, he had to work it last year.

(Austin once quietly confessed to him that even without Daniel physically being there, things were better now than they had been before their fake relationship started. He was more relaxed around his parents, he’d said. He didn’t spend every visit or phone call expecting them to try and foist a date onto him.)

It was a fleeting thought, however. He knew that Buck would find out about Austin eventually, Daniel definitely wasn’t going to pretend he didn’t know Austin if they saw each other with Buck there. So he bullied Buck into getting dressed like he was a civilized person and off they went.

His brother’s reaction to meeting Austin as his fake boyfriend and not just a forgettable coworker was actually not that bad. More confusion than upset, and the upset was only from how long it had been going on without him being told. The longer it went that Buck only had questions, the more Daniel relaxed.

He wanted his brother to be okay with it, to get along with Austin, more than he thought he did. Austin was the best friend he’d ever had, and that was including Alma. He wasn’t sure what he would do if Buck turned out to be stridently against him being anything with someone he worked with, fake or not. Thankfully, that wasn’t a problem.

Car rides with Austin were rarely completely silent. The man did talk, after all. But it was usually up to Daniel to keep the conversation going, because Austin preferred to listen unless he had new work or family gossip to share. Daniel never minded, and even when there were lapses where the only noise was the radio, it was still a comfortable silence.

Car rides with Buck were not like that. Car rides with Buck were filled with the man yapping away. The entire drive to where the Joneses lived in Glendale was filled with Buck peppering them with more questions and then, much to Daniel’s relief, attempts to embarrass his brother. Like with the story of the time Daniel took… Whatever her name was out on a date and accidentally chose the place where Buck was working at the time. Which meant he was there when she went to the bathroom and never came back.

It was Buck’s favorite go-to story when attempting to embarrass Daniel these days.

Daniel knew Buck was a little weirded out by how warm Tabitha’s greeting to the both of them was. Especially with the way she said she was excited to finally meet him. A phrase that had Austin and his siblings turning to stare at Daniel, the three of them wearing the exact same expression of teasing, because Daniel might have mentioned his brother a handful of times to them before.

Daniel also knew that Buck was a bit weirded out when Rosemary, all of three years old and wearing a bright red Christmas dress, ran up to hug Daniel's leg with a cry of,

“Uncle Dan!”

It was weird to Daniel, too. It would never not be weird to Daniel. Because it wasn’t real. The thing between him and Austin, the relationship the entire family thought they had, wasn’t real. Would never be real. And having Austin’s niblings refer to him as their uncle always left a squirming feeling in his stomach that took far too long for him to identify as guilt.

It made sense, though. Dean was the oldest of the three, and Daniel had been to his fourth birthday. He’d known Austin for longer than Rosemary had been alive. But none of that made Daniel’s stomach any less squirmy when one of them called him uncle.

What didn’t make his stomach squirmy was how didn’t bring presents for anyone. Austin just slapped both their names on the ones he got and they called it a day. It was just coupley enough to sell the lie, and meant Daniel could both be lazy and save money. Although, judging from one of the presents this year, maybe he should start thinking about giving his input.

“Seriously?” Cynthia looked like she couldn’t decide between being amused or offended as she held up the second of the two presents addressed to her from Austin and Daniel. “The firefighter calendar?”

Most of them were seated on the two couches that decorated Tabitha and Alan’s living room. Daniel and Austin were standing, as there weren’t any seats left. Buck was on the floor with the kids, helping them get their new toys set up.

“Flip to July,” Austin suggested with a smirk, because he was an asshole.

Cynthia did and then started cracking up. She showed it to Irene, Ethan’s wife, and she did much the same. Daniel knew exactly what they were laughing at: him. Specifically, the picture of him shirtless in bunker pants and wet from being hosed down.

Somehow, the entire experience of that photoshoot made him feel more like a piece of meat than most of the people that paid for him in El Paso. He was less a person to the ones running everything and more of a very realistic mannequin to pose as they saw fit. Thankfully, he was sitting for his shoot, as opposed to the ones who were stuck standing in ridiculous poses.

“Nice, uh. Nice hose placement, Dan,” Irene commented with a snicker. “Now we know what Austin sees in you.”

Cynthia’s eyes shot to Austin. For a moment, Daniel could’ve sworn he saw more of a question in them than laughter. But then Austin sighed and shook his head and said,

“It’s definitely not his personality.”

“Hurtful,” Daniel complained while everyone, including Buck on the floor with the kids, laughed at the quip. “If that’s how you feel, maybe I’ll just leave.”

He went to take a step, like he was actually going to leave. But then Buck’s eyes went wide and he pointed above Daniel and Austin’s head. Daniel looked up, spotting the mistletoe stuck to the ceiling. Austin looked up as well and said,

“Oh.”

“Who put that up there?” Daniel asked, even as he eyed the other man.

Austin was looking distinctly uncomfortable, as subtle as it was. He wasn’t sure the others noticed. If they knew him well enough to see it, Daniel wouldn’t be in this position to begin with.

“Oh yeah.” Ethan tilted his head back to look at the mistletoe. “I forgot I did that.”

“Of course,” Austin quietly scoffed.

“C’mon!” Irene crowed, Cynthia silent beside her. “Give us a kiss!”

Daniel looked to Austin. There was that discomfort again, more plain for Daniel to see than before. This was one of the lines Austin had marked at the start of this thing, one of the boundaries they’d agreed not to cross. So Daniel opened his mouth to tell the others that he wasn’t quite comfortable with that sort of PDA.

And then Austin rolled his eyes with a huff and dragged him in.

As kisses went, it was very tame and very dry. It was just lips on lips, with a man who clearly didn’t have much experience with kissing. The only thing that kept it from being a peck was how long it lasted. Tabitha cooed, and Dean started making exaggerated sounds of disgust that Sam mimicked.

It took a moment for Daniel to get with the program, but when he did, he decided to make them look a bit less like two dead fish. If that involved taking the opportunity to cop a feel, so be it. When Austin pulled back to glare at him in annoyance, Daniel just smiled.

Buck was staring at them, or maybe at Austin, like he just saw a dog speak or a cop help someone. It was an expression he carried through the rest of the day.

Now

Eddie thought Buck was bad when it came to Christmas. His boyfriend had an actual calendar reminder for 12:01, December 1st, and the decorating start practically immediately. Buck and Danny’s apartment always ended up looking like a Christmas aisle vomited up its contents all over the walls. All bright lights and gaudy decorations, with barely any semblance of matching. If it was Christmas, it was going to end up somewhere in that apartment.

Except for that Elf on a Shelf. Both times that Buck got one, Danny disposed of it. The first time, way back in El Paso, he tossed it out their window. The second time, Buck hadn’t even bought it for them, it was for Eddie’s place. Danny still ended up committing vehicular manslaughter on it.

Anyways, Buck was a bit overwhelming while possessed by the holiday spirit. Somehow, though, Christopher was worse this year. He was a short, bespectacled dictator and the critic of nightmares while they were putting up the tree.

First, it was facing the wrong way. The skinny side, Chris insisted, had to face the window. Even though to Eddie’s eyes, there was no skinny side. He and Buck just ended up spinning the tree in place until Chris decided it was the correct side through some arcane method that Eddie couldn’t comprehend.

Second was the decorations for the tree. Eddie blamed his boyfriend for this, what with how many lights he put on his and Danny’s tree. Eddie also blamed Buck for procuring the box after box after box after box of Christmas lights that Chris pulled out.

Buck claimed innocence, but Eddie didn’t see any other way that Chris would’ve gotten them.

Then came the garland. Chris insisted it had to be handmade, so Buck got popcorn and cranberries. Except Eddie learned, in the middle of stringing them all together, that there was a ratio of popcorn to cranberries – five to one, for the curious – that he was supposed to follow. He ended up having to redo it all.

Eddie sent his son to bed when he started on about tinsel and sparkle. Insisting on everything being perfect was cute, but also exhausting. Eddie honestly preferred Buck’s lackadaisical attitude towards Christmas decorations, even if he could get a bit overwhelming with it.

“So what’s wrong with the tree?” Carla asked a day later, after Eddie made an offhand complaint.

“Everything,” Eddie and Buck said at the same time.

“Jinx,” Buck said, grinning.

Eddie rolled his eyes.

“It was facing the wrong way,” he said. “He didn’t like the way I was stringing the lights-”

“You put one string of lights on it and called it a day,” Buck said, and ducked Eddie’s playful swipe.

“Then I was putting the ornaments in the wrong places,” Eddie continued. “I felt like I was being judged by Martha Stewart. Pre-Snoop Dogg. This one wasn’t helping, either.”

“I think you’d find that I was very helpful,” Buck argued, sticking his nose in the air.

“In what universe?” Eddie scoffed.

“He’s growing up,” Carla laughed. “Becoming his own person, forming his own opinions.”

“Opinions about the ratio of popcorn to cranberry,” Buck said. "At that age, I was just happy when my parents wanted to do something with me for once.”

Eddie opened his mouth to voice, once again, his low opinion of the Buckley parents. Except that was the moment Chris screamed from his bedroom, and then there were no other thoughts in Eddie’s head. He and Buck sprinted the short distance to Chris’ room, slapping the light on and finding him sitting up in his bed and visibly upset.

“Hey, hey.” Eddie slid into a crouch by the bed, hands hovering over his kid. “Are you okay?”

“I’m fine,” Chris said.

“That was a pretty loud scream,” Buck said.

“What happened? Did you have a bad dream?” Eddie asked. “Do you want to talk about it? Sometimes talking about it makes it less scary.”

“It was about Mom,” Chris whispered.

Eddie heard Buck let out a quiet oh. Then soft footsteps as he backed out of the room with Carla. They closed the door, and then it was just the two of them. Them, and the ghost looming over them still.

The station was decorated top to bottom in Christmas cheer, with a tree twice as tall as Buck and wreaths hanging from the loft railings, which were decorated with garland and lights. They were once again hosting the department’s toy drive. This meant the station was once again filled with people coming and going as they dropped off their donations. There should have been an air of sunny cheer, but Eddie managed to drag in a cloud with him.

Buck didn’t think it helped that Taylor had been there reporting on the toy drive. She was friends with Danny now, which was weird. In his opinion, at least. It was weird that Buck’s brother was friends with Buck’s past bar bathroom hookup.

(He had a suspicion that it was mostly to torment Eddie, but he couldn’t prove it.)

“So what did the doctor say?” Buck asked his boyfriend as they carried stacks of presents to put under the tree.

After Chris’ nightmare, Eddie managed to get an emergency session with the same guy he saw after the tsunami. Buck hadn’t been able to be there this time as he picked up an extra shift. Also, he managed to not use the opportunity to nag at Eddie again. He was proud of his self-restraint there.

“That one nightmare isn’t necessarily a reason for concern,” Eddie said. “That it could be an isolated incident. But…”

“But?” Buck urged when he trailed off.

“I don’t think it’s an isolated anything. He’s obsessed with the holidays this year, how everything has to be perfect.”

They reached the tree and knelt down to unload their haul. Finding space for what they had among what was already there felt a bit like playing Tetris.

“You named him after me and Danny,” Buck pointed out. “Were you expecting him to be a Grinch?”

“You’re not obsessed with the holidays, you just love them,” Eddie countered. “You don’t care if the tree’s perfectly placed and perfectly decorated. You just care that there’s a tree and that it’s decorated.”

Buck couldn’t really argue that one, because it was true. Half the time, the decorations just went where they could fit. Which was how they ended up with garland on the toilet one year.

“He woke up screaming, dreaming about his mother, Buck.”

“Well.” Buck tried to think what to say. “He got his mom for Christmas one year. Maybe that has something to do with it?”

Though, he’d have thought it would have been a thing that came up at some point over the past two years.

“The holidays were their thing,” Eddie said, sounding a little bit wistful. “Cookies and stockings and gingerbread houses. Maybe I’m not doing it right.”

Sighing, Buck set down the last of the presents he’d carried and reached out to run his finger through Eddie’s hair. Eddie leaned into the touch. His smile was soft, and weighed down.

“He misses his mom,” Buck told him. “That’s not your fault. That’s normal.”

“Maybe.” Eddie sighed and pushed himself up. Buck’s hand dropped to his side. “So much for the most wonderful time of the year.”

Buck watched him go, wishing he could do more to help. He was also, though he wouldn’t admit this if Danny was being waterboarded in front of him, a tiny bit annoyed that Shannon was still managing to mess things up for them even in death. It felt petty to still be holding a grudge against a dead woman, especially since he’d moved past Eddie’s involvement in all of it.

It was once Eddie had turned the corner – he had a nice butt, okay? Buck couldn’t not stare at it when given the chance – that Buck finally noticed how Austin was staring at him. He looked as if he was contemplating something. Finally, he nodded to himself and came over.

“What’s up?” Buck asked as he stood.

Austin opened his mouth, hesitated, closed it. Buck waited, raising an eyebrow as he did. Finally, Austin opened his mouth and said,

“I don’t know what to get Dan for Christmas.”

“Seriously?” Buck blurted out. “You’ve been whatever the two of you are for like, six years.”

“We weren’t supposed to get presents for each other. Nothing big,” Austin said, looking more and more uncomfortable. “And then Dan went and bought those figurines for me.”

Buck remembered. Technically, he’d been the ones to pick them out, Danny had just given them his stamp of approval. He’d been a bit high-strung on the day he dragged Buck to that nerd store, relaxing once they’d located something he deemed suitable.

“What did you get him last year?” Buck asked.

Austin pursed his lips.

“A sweater.”

“Well, that’s- I mean, did he like it?”

“I guess.” Austin shrugged. “He wears it.”

Danny wore a lot of sweaters, Buck thought. Or, no, Danny wore the same sweaters a lot. None of them looked very new, even though Buck couldn’t remember them being in his brother’s wardrobe for long enough to get as threadbare as a couple of them were.

“What did you get Eddie?” Austin suddenly asked.

“Tickets to that UFC match in Anaheim next month.” Buck shrugged. “He likes watching two half naked guys wailing on each other.”

“Dan likes basketball,” Austin remarked, sounding thoughtful. “I could get tickets, I guess.”

He didn’t sound particularly enthused about it, which Buck could sympathize with. As much as he enjoyed being allowed to slam into other guys when he played football in high school, and watching Eddie get sweaty in his baseball uniform, he didn’t actually like sports all that much. There were a hundred other things he’d much rather do than watch ‘em.

(Eddie didn’t like it when Buck was over while he was trying to watch a game. Mostly because Buck was very, very good at pulling Eddie away from the TV and he ended up missing most, if not all, of it. Not that he ever complained about it at the time.)

“You could, uh…” Buck scrambled for an alternative, and then snapped his fingers when one dawned on him. “You could get him a leather jacket!”

“A leather jacket.”

“Yeah! He had this one that he wore all the time, back in the day. He loved it. But it got lost not too long after we moved to El Paso,” Buck said. And then, because he was no longer a self-centered teenager and now had a far better understanding of how dire their circumstances had been at the time, exclaimed, “Oh, God. Did he sell it for rent money?”

He hadn’t questioned it when it disappeared. They were in Texas, after all. It was much warmer, usually, than it was in Pennsylvania. Why would Danny be wearing his jacket in that heat?

“Leather jacket.” Austin nodded to himself, ignoring the crisis Buck was going through in front of him. “Okay. Thank you.”

“You’re welcome.”

Buck snapped a mock salute at the man, who merely shook his head and walked off.

(Later, when he asked Danny about the leather jacket, he learned that it hadn’t been sold for rent money. It was sold for grocery money.)

Chapter 20

Chapter Notes

It's another longer than normal chapter, to make up for the fact that it took longer than I intended.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Then

They weren’t supposed to be exchanging gifts like this, Daniel knew. Nothing big. Small things, like candy, that was okay. That was what he and Austin had silently agreed on that first Christmas they were “together” nearly four years ago. It was why Austin usually just got him a thing of Skittles and Daniel got him those Peppermint Crunch Junior Mints that came out around Christmas.

But that was before. Before Daniel spent an evening terrorizing everyone in a hospital waiting room, and Austin weathered it without complaint while all others ran away from him. Before Austin went and hunted down a vending machine just to get him his favorite candy. Before Daniel looked at Austin and that winged thing unfurled in his chest and he realized-

So Daniel dragged Buck to that nerdy store with him, in search of something that Austin would like. The first time Daniel had ever gone Christmas shopping for a- Not boyfriend, Austin wasn’t his boyfriend. It wasn’t real, it was pretend. Play acting. But it was the first time Daniel had ever gone Christmas shopping for a significant other, play acting or not.

Thirty-four years old, and Daniel was hitting a milestone that his baby brother managed as a teenager.

Buck, because he was the best baby brother, went along with it. Barely any questions asked, beyond why they were at that store and then what they were looking for. He was even the one to find the figurines that Daniel settled on, a packet of little people and then a dragon. The dragon he didn’t remember seeing on Austin’s shelves, and it looked suitably cool.

Even after he paid, even after he brought them back to his apartment to be wrapped with the rest of the gifts that were yet to be wrapped, he was uncertain about it. Would Austin appreciate them? Would he be upset? Would he think Daniel was pushing for more? Because he wasn’t, he wouldn’t, no matter how much he wanted it.

That wasn’t what they were. That wasn’t what they would ever be, what they could ever be. Daniel accepted that in the moments that followed him realizing the depths of his stupidity. And yet, still, Daniel found himself putting more care in wrapping Austin’s presents than even the ones he did for Buck.

On Christmas Day, he spent the morning debating with himself, arguing with himself. Even through he and Buck tearing into their own presents for each other, through lounging in front of the TV before it was time to go, through teaching a bunch of orphans how to hustle people at pool, through dinner with the 118 and their families. The doubt about the presents was a constant, ever present thing.

Right up until he was standing in front of Austin, heart in his throat, and timidly holding out both boxes to him.

Austin didn’t say anything as he took them, and for the first time in a long time, Daniel could not read him. It made his nerves about the matter even worse. Because what if he stepped too far? What if Austin guessed how Daniel felt and ended things?

“All I got you was Skittles,” Austin finally said, staring down at the gifts. “It’s at the apartment.”

“Yeah, I figured. I just…” Daniel shrugged self-consciously and shifted where he stood. “I don’t know.”

The other man looked up at him, the spot between his eyebrows furrowing. Then he carefully, so very carefully, pulled at the tape holding the wrapping in place on the first one. He must’ve been as uncertain of this as Daniel was, since Austin normally didn’t care about the state of the paper afterwards any more than Buck or Daniel himself did.

When the box was unveiled, the red dragon clear on the front, Austin simply stared at it. He tilted his head a little, and was silent for so long that Daniel was about to apologize for ever thinking this was a good idea.

“This is expensive,” was what Austin finally said.

Daniel shrugged.

“I didn’t remember seeing it on one of your shelves,” he said. “It kinda reminded me of Smaug.”

Austin raised his head to stare at him, mouth pulled in a small, curious frown. The question was very clear, if unspoken, and Daniel felt himself flush.

“I read the Hobbit as a kid. And maybe watched the movies,” he said, and then, “Shut up.”

The other man quietly laughed and then looked back down at the presents in his hands. The next one, with the little people, was unwrapped just as delicately as the first. They weren’t as cool as the dragon, certainly, but they seemed to hold muster.

“Thank you,” Austin whispered.

“You’re welcome.” Daniel ducked his head and shifted on his feet a bit, feeling awkward now. “I’m going to, um. Eddie’s present.”

It probably counted as running away. Not that Daniel could tell anyone just what he was running away from.

(He caught Austin showing the dragon off to his parents, later. Not even Maddie calling him out on his gooey eyes – and he really hoped they weren’t that obvious – could sink his mood.)

Now

“So.” Eddie’s tone was even as he poured the chili flakes into the hot chocolate he was making, calm. Purposefully so. “When are you and Danny leaving?”

Buck let out a sigh and propped himself up against the kitchen counter. He’d told Eddie that they were planning on going to Boston to see Maddie, because he wasn’t going to keep that from him. He didn’t exactly enjoy keeping Danny’s last trip there a secret from Eddie, after all. But telling him about it seemed to have revived that old bit of tension between them regarding his sister and Chimney.

“Day after Christmas,” he answered. “We’ll be back in time for my shift on New Year’s Eve.”

Eddie let out a hum, spoon clinking as he stirred in the flakes.

“And what are you going to tell Chimney?”

And there they were.

“Eddie,” Buck groaned. “What do you want from me? You want me to betray my sister’s trust? I’ve done that already just by telling him she was in Boston.”

“He deserves to know where she is,” Eddie said, just like he insisted the last time this came up.

“And he does!” Buck quickly looked towards the living room, where Chris was sat in a chair at the coffee table and hard at work building a gingerbread house. The kid, thankfully, didn’t seem to have heard him. “He does, Eddie. He knows she’s in Boston, he knows she’s okay.”

His boyfriend scoffed. Buck wanted to tear at his hair.

“You think this is easy for me?” he asked. “You think it’s easy standing between my friend and my sister? But she doesn’t want him to know, just like she didn’t want me and Danny to know she was sick. And Chimney had no problem respecting her wishes then.”

“That still doesn’t mean-”

“Yes it does. I helped hide her the last time she ran from a partner. I’m not going to betray her to this one just because he’s my friend. Not more than I already have.”

Eddie sighed.

“You’re right.” He pinched the bridge of his nose between forefinger and thumb. “You’re right, I’m sorry. I just keep thinking about when it was me and you…”

“But it’s not me and you,” Buck told him. “Maddie didn’t run for the reasons I did, she ran because she’s sick and scared. And she went to get help, actual help, and she promised to come back. All Chimney had to do was stay here with Jee-Yun and wait.”

“Is she, though?”

Buck blinked.

“What?”

“Is she coming back?” Eddie asked. “She signed a lease for an apartment. Remember?”

“It’s- It’s a short-term lease.”

Eddie stared at him. There was something soft in his eyes that was painfully close to pity, and it was making Buck want to run and hide. Thankfully, Chris shouted from the other room that he was done with his gingerbread house. Eddie stuck the hot chocolate on a plate with some cookies they’d baked earlier, and carried it out.

Buck did not get much time to relax. It seemed that within moments of Eddie going into the living room, Chris let out a cry of,

“My house! It’s ruined!”

He got to the living room in time to see Chris shove the gingerbread house he’d been making onto the floor. It broke into pieces and Eddie crouched down, desperately trying to piece it back together while also trying to piece Chris back together. From the mess on the table, there’d been an oopsie with the hot chocolate.

“Maybe we can fix it,” Eddie said.

“It’s ruined,” Chris reiterated, sounding on the verge of crying.

“And we can build a new one.” Buck knelt down beside Eddie and started helping him gather the soaked pieces. “It’ll be super awesome.”

“Super awesome,” Eddie agreed with a nod. “I’ll help.”

“There’s no time, it’s almost Christmas,” Chris protested. “And you’re working tomorrow.”

“Okay, what’s going on?” Eddie asked his son, leaving the gingerbread house – or rather, its remains – to Buck. “It seems like you’re putting a lot of pressure on yourself to get Christmas right. Where is this coming from?”

“I wanted everything to be perfect.”

“You, me, Buck, Danny. Presents and stockings,” Eddie desperately tried. “That sounds pretty good to me, right?”

All it got in response was a sniffle. The sound of it broke Buck’s heart, and clearly Eddie’s, as well.

“It’ll be okay,” he reiterated. “We’ll make an even bigger gingerbread house next year.”

“You could be dead next year!” Chris blurted out.

It was like the words froze the entire room. Buck stopped in the middle of cleaning everything up. Eddie just sat there, staring at his son with his mouth hanging slightly open. He didn’t seem to know what to say at first. Until, finally,

“I’m- I’m not going anywhere, bud.” His voice was strained. “I’m not- I love you, you know that.”

Chris just shook his head.

“Hey. Hey, Eds.” Buck shook his boyfriend’s shoulder a little to get his attention. “How, uh. How about you go get more hot chocolate?”

“What?”

“More hot chocolate,” Buck repeated, meeting Eddie’s eyes and hoping he got the message. “Try not to spill it this time.”

“Uh, yeah.” Eddie pushed himself up. “Okay, sure.”

Buck watched him go into the kitchen, then he focused back on Chris. The kid was still upset and sniffling.

“This is about last spring, isn’t it? When your dad was hurt?” he asked, and got a jerky nod in return. “I thought Danny was so cool when he became a firefighter.”

“It is cool,” Chris mumbled.

“Right? But scary, too. So scary that I saved up money to buy a scanner, just so I could listen in. I was worried every time he came home hurt.” Buck reached out and squeezed Chris’ knee. “But he kept coming home. And your dad, he has me, right?”

“Y- Yeah,” was the sniffed response.

“Yeah. He has me, and I’ll always make sure he comes home,” Buck told Chris. “Just like I did before. Okay?”

Chris nodded, rubbing at his nose and eyes as he did. Buck got up, bending down enough to hug him real quick, before padding into the kitchen. He found Eddie at the counter, propped up by his elbows and his face buried in his palms. At the sound of Buck’s footsteps he looked up and cleared his throat.

“He, uh. He good now?”

“Yeah.” Buck nodded. “I think so.”

“Good.” He grabbed the new cup of hot chocolate. “I’ll, uh. I’ll take this out to him. And hopefully I don’t spill it on anything else…”

He brushed past Buck, shoulders stiff. Buck watched his back as he left and heaved out a sigh.

Holiday calls were awful. The only ones worse than a Christmas Eve call was a Christmas Day call. This one, mere hours away from Santa making his visit, was to an apartment complex. Buck was intimately familiar with crappy apartment buildings, but this one seemed to beat the one in El Paso: a dad was backing into his designated spot, hit a support column, and a wall came tumbling down on top of him.

Residents were outside to gawk, either from the apartment complex or neighbors. The car was hidden beneath a pile of rubble in the drive, the remaining section of the wall tilted to one side and propped up by it. A string of lights were hanging down, caught in the debris.

“Please, you have to help us. My husband’s in there,” a woman clinging to a boy begged as they passed. “We can’t get to him.”

“Everybody step back,” Bobby urged the gather crowd as they approached the buried car. “It looks like he hit this post and took some of the structure down with it.”

He indicated the post in question, skinny and now at a distinct lean.

The wife was speaking again while Buck looked over the debris. She’d been on the phone with her husband when it happened, apparently. He went from talking to silent, which was not a heartening sign. But it was a distinctly car shaped pile, which seemed to suggest the car hadn’t been crushed much, if at all.

Bobby instructed them to carefully dig out the debris. Ravi was sent off to evacuate any residents who remained inside. If this section went so easily, there was no telling how the rest of the building was faring. Everything was supposed to be retrofitted after Northridge’s 6.7 magnitude quake, but clearly this landlord was as concerned about building codes as Buck and Danny’s in El Paso.

They’d managed to move enough debris to get to the driver’s side. Like Buck had thought – hoped – the car wasn’t all that crushed. The husband was unconscious inside, covered in dust. They couldn’t tell if he was breathing, though, a statement which prompted his son to ask if he was dead.

Eddie stared at the kid, and Buck figured he was thinking about Chris.

They managed to get airbags between the top of the car and the debris on top of it. When they inflated, they had enough space for them to open the car door with the jaws. The husband roused while they extracted him, and he managed to stumble part of the way to the gurney. Buck would’ve preferred to have eased him out better, but there wasn’t much room to maneuver and life trumped limb. Or in this case, keeping him from being crushed if more came down on the car trumped the concern that they could aggravate a spinal injury.

And then came the grim task of telling everyone that they were going to be homeless for Christmas. Bobby had them red tag it, deeming it too unsafe to allow anyone back inside. They left the group of them standing outside, because there was nothing else they could do.

(Buck, believer in the universe’s ability to scream as he was, really should’ve taken that as a sign for how the holiday was going to be.)

The original plan for Christmas initially involved spending part of the day with the Joneses and then, urgh, at the Grant-Nashes. Things changed due to a bunch of recently homeless people, a slew of cancellations, and Nash’s apparent need to force charity upon people. He and Athena decided to move Christmas to the parking lot of a motel that the Red Cross stuck the residents of a now condemned apartment building. Even the Joneses were going to be there, apparently, the whole clan of ‘em.

With how quickly the entire thing got set up, Daniel wouldn’t be surprised if Tabitha took the reins and then whipped everyone into action. He knew she used to do this kind of thing on her street, so she’d have the practice. He also knew that doing it this way was a relief for Buck, who’d been fretting between wanting to spend the holiday with Daniel and with everyone else.

Even Albert and the Lees were going to be there, and that was mostly Daniel’s doing. He’d already been thinking about dragging Albert into something, and so he just took the opportunity given to him. Anne had been very thankful at the invite when he spoke to her over the phone.

(Chimney might’ve been far from Daniel’s favorite person these days, but he’d laid claim to Albert that very first night Buck brought him home like a sad stray kitten and he was not going to let him go just because his brother was a dick.)

“Danny!” greeted him the moment he parked and got out, Buck making his way directly to Daniel.

It was the first Christmas in a very long time that Daniel had woken up in an empty apartment. The usual pile of presents was currently sitting untouched beneath the tree, waiting for Buck to come upon them. Daniel had brought along the ones for Eddie and Chris, along with the ones he got for Albert and Austin.

“S’up, squirt.” He wrapped an arm around him when his brother reached him, displacing the Santa hat on his head enough to knuckle his head. “Who baked those cookies?”

Buck was holding a platter of them, chocolate chip and fluffy looking.

“Tabitha,” Buck said, and then, “You can have one.”

“Seriously?”

“She has me giving one to everyone, Danny! There’s gotta be enough.”

Daniel rolled his eyes and snagged a cookie.

“How’s Chris?” he asked.

Apparently, the kid was more than a bit anxious about Eddie’s mortality. Sadly understandable, all things considered.

“He’s…” Buck shrugged, half-turning to look at where Chris was sitting. “I dunno. He seems to be enjoying himself today.”

“Well, I’ve got presents for him and Eddie.”

Daniel opened the door to the back seat, stuffing the cookie into his mouth to free up his hands. While Buck went back to his Keebler elf circuit, Daniel swung by Albert to give him his gift, before going to Chris and Eddie. The former seemed to be enjoying himself well enough, and he certainly tore at the wrapping of his present with all the eagerness of previous years.

Eddie, though, was a different case. There was a cloud around him. Daniel figured it was unavoidable, what with the way Chris bluntly stated his fear of his father dying within the next year. He would probably be the same in his stead.

Those two presents deposited, and after a hug with the second coolest kid, Daniel went in search of the Joneses. He spotted Tabitha and Alan talking to the family of one of the other 118 firefighters, ones that had been there for the station party a couple years back. The rest of them weren’t too far away.

Austin was easily spotted. He was talking with his sister, a present wrapped in snowflake covered blue paper in his hands. It was large and rectangular, and Daniel wondered what could be in it.

Cynthia’s eyes darted past Austin to land on Daniel, and Daniel froze. He liked Austin’s sister well enough, but the way she always seemed to be laughing whenever she looked at him, like she was in on a joke that he didn’t know about, made him uneasy around her. Like now, when she smirked and said something to Austin that had him rolling his eyes even as he turned to see Daniel for himself.

Daniel gave them a small wave from where he stood, which seemed to only amuse Cynthia even more. She said something else, and whatever Austin’s reply was, it made her laugh. Austin left her shaking his head.

“Hey.” Daniel felt the edges of his smile threaten to go gooey and did his best to catch it. “What was your sister bothering you about?”

“She was just being annoying,” was Austin’s answer. “Merry Christmas.”

He held out the wrapped box. Daniel put down the gift for Austin onto a nearby table so he could unwrap it. The wrapping paper was placed onto the table as well, along with the box’s lid. The box itself got dropped to the ground as Daniel held out what it contained.

“You got me a biker’s jacket?” There was no preventing the gooey edges this time, and he lifted the jacket higher to hide it. “Wait, is this why Buck was asking me about my old one?”

“Maybe.”

Austin seemed nervous. Not that there were many outward signs, but Daniel caught the slight shift in posture, the tightness in his shoulders. It took a moment, and then he realized Austin was waiting for him to put it on. When he did, slipping his arms into the sleeves, he found it fit pretty well.

“Thank you,” he whispered, doing his best to keep from nuzzling at the jacket’s collar like a weirdo. “Oh, um. Here, yours.”

He shoved Austin’s present at him. Austin took it and peeled back the paper. The contents earned a tilt of his head and a quirk of his brow.

“How much did this cost?” Austin asked, staring at the stacked set of books. Lord of the Rings, the Hobbit, and even the Silmarillion, each bound in green leather with golden letters and illustrations on the inside covers. “Do I want to know?”

Daniel shrugged.

“They’re like, the only nerd books you don’t have,” he said. “You’ve gotta earn your nerd cred, man.”

Austin softly scoffed.

“Right,” he said, and then his eyes flickered past Daniel. “What’s going on over there?”

Daniel turned to look, spotting Eddie standing in front of Buck, Hen, and Ravi. Whatever was going on, it seemed tense just going off the way Eddie seemed to be standing at parade rest. Buck had that Santa hat clutched in his hands, twisting it more and more as Eddie spoke. Then, shaking his head, Buck dropped the hat to the ground and stomped off.

Eddie watched him go, face drawn, but didn’t go after him.

Daniel did, however. He didn’t feel like chasing his brother across national borders again.

“You’re still angry,” Eddie said, and Buck wanted to laugh.

“What gave you that idea?” he asked, a bit sardonically. He shut off the sink and slotted the last of the dishes from dinner into the rack to dry. “You just decide over night-”

“It wasn’t over night,” Eddie interrupted.

“Bullshit,” Buck spat. “You weren’t thinking about quitting your job yesterday, or the day before.”

“I’m not quitting, Buck.”

“No, you’re right. You’re running away.”

“That isn’t what this is either,” Eddie argued. His voice was tense, his shoulders were tense. Everything about him, everything between them, was tense. “But you heard Chris, he’s afraid. Of the risks I take, of me not being here next Christmas.”

“And you think quitting your job will make him not afraid?” Buck shook his head at him. “Shannon got hit by a car, Eddie. Crossing the street. Are you gonna stop crossing the street?”

“Of course not-”

“Of course not! Because this doesn’t have anything to do with Chris, this is you panicking. You’ve been panicking, and now you have an excuse to run away.”

“Oh, fuck you,” Eddie spat, face twisted with anger.

Good. Because Buck was pretty fucking angry himself.

“You know what the worst part is? Besides the fact you’ve made another shitty decision on your own, another decision that effects everyone else, because you’re scared-”

“I swear to God, Buck-”

“The worst part is that you didn’t even talk to me about it,” Buck said. “I stood right fucking there and talked to you before the transfer request was even printed off, remember? Because you’re my boyfriend, you’re my best friend, and I respected you enough to talk to you as my partner.”

“I don’t need to talk to you about doing what’s best for my son!” Eddie snapped.

Buck reeled back, heart stuttering. Regret quickly pushed away the anger on Eddie’s face.

“You know what? You’re right.” Buck wiped his hands off on a towel before throwing it down onto the counter. “You don’t need to talk to me about your son.”

“Buck, that wasn’t…”

He ignored his boyfriend’s attempts to walk back the words. Instead, he went to the bedroom. It was a simple task, gathering what he needed and wanted, and then he was coming back out. Eddie crept out of the kitchen, and his face dropped at the sight of the packed bag hanging from Buck’s shoulder.

“Where are you going?” Eddie’s words shook, all traces of the earlier anger gone.

“Home, Eddie. I’m going home.” Buck yanked open the front door. “I’ll call before we take off tomorrow.”

The door was very loud when it clicked shut behind him.

Chapter End Notes

Facts About the Joneses.

Chapter 21

Chapter Notes

See the end of the chapter for notes

Boston was much colder than Los Angeles was. It was a bit of a shock when they stepped out of the terminal and was blasted by the winter air. Danny had cursed, loudly, while he zipped up the jacket Austin had given him and twisted a scarf around his neck. Buck would’ve made fun of him for it, except he was too busy trying to turtle into his own coat.

“California has ruined us,” Danny complained when they got on the airport’s free shuttle, throwing themselves into seats. The sun was well below the horizon at this point, the five and a half hour flight and the time difference meaning they landed quite late. “Remember when we used to think this was t-shirt weather?”

“We never thought that,” Buck pointed out, a bit miserably. He’d been feeling miserable since last night, and the chill wasn’t helping. “We were just as cold then.”

“Maybe you were,” his brother grumbled.

“Where are we going, anyway?”

“Shuttle’s taking us to the blue line, and that’ll take us to the orange, and that should get us close to the hotel.” Danny’s tongue stuck out a bit as he dug out his phone. “I think.”

“You think!?”

“Do I look like I live in this godforsaken city?” he asked, and got a couple glares for it from nearby locals. Which he ignored, if he even noticed. “Our hotel’s on the wharf, near that church you obsessed over for a week.”

“The Old North Church?”

“Sure.” Danny turned his phone in circles, glaring at it. “They couldn’t use an actual street map for this? Screw it, we’ll take the rail to downtown and walk if we have to.”

“Maybe you’re just bad at reading maps,” Buck teased, and didn’t quite manage to dodge the swipe to his head. “You’re just mad ‘cause it’s true, you got us lost three times on the way to El Paso. Two of them in Nashville.”

“Fuck Nashville, and fuck whoever planned their roads. Oh, wait, here we go.” Danny angled his phone to show Buck the street map version of everything he’d been looking for, as a convenient PDF. “Yeah, blue line to State, then taking the orange to Haymarket should put us close and at some point, yes, we’ll go to the aquarium. You child.”

Buck hadn’t even said anything. He had, however, taken note that one of the stations they’d be passing was at an aquarium. His brother knew him well enough to guess as much.

He still blew a raspberry all the same.

“You said the hotel’s on the wharf?” Buck pointed at the screen.

“Here. Ish,” Danny answered, pointing at a spot just north of the Sumner and Callahan tunnels.

“So couldn’t we just get out at the Aquarium stop and walk from there?” Buck’s phone chirped in his pocket and he pulled it out with a frown. “It doesn’t seem much farther than Haymarket.”

“Eddie?” Danny asked, eyeing Buck’s phone as he typed back a reply.

“Yup.”

Buck had called him before they took off from LA, and then texted him when they landed.

“The offer to take the bat to his knees is still there, y’know,” his brother said. “Not like he’s gonna need ‘em for work now.”

“We’ll see how I feel when we get back. So, Aquarium?”

“But it’s cold,” Danny whined as he tucked his phone away. “Fine, I guess.”

“You’re the one who decided we weren’t renting a car,” Buck pointed out, grabbing his bag as the shuttle reached the rail terminal. “Hey, I forgot to ask yesterday. What the hell is up with Albert’s mustache?”

“It’s awful, right? We’ve been trying to bully him into shaving it.”

It was a very short ride from the airport to the aquarium rail station. They climbed the stairs to the surface level, coming out at an intersection with a large brick building to their right. Buck stared at it, or rather at the letters on its side that spelled out MARRIOTT, and then at Danny. His brother’s response was a simple,

“Shut up.”

“You couldn’t have gotten us a room there?”

“I said shut up.” Danny started walking, following the brick sidewalk. “I wasn’t looking at the rail lines, okay?”

Buck scoffed as he followed, only to come to an immediate stop.

“Danny!”

“What?” his brother asked, pulling his scarf tighter. He looked around, and then huffed when his eyes landed on what Buck had seen. “Ghosts and Gravestones.”

“I think it’s a trolley tour.”

“We’ll see about going on it before we leave.”

His brother was the best.

It wasn’t an awful walk to the hotel, cold or not. Especially with the Christmas lights decorating everything. It reminded Daniel a lot of the trip they took to see Maddie, before they moved to Texas. The walk along the Freedom Trail that he was forced along by Buck. Except Buck wasn’t being creepy in cemeteries this time.

But that reminded him.

He took out his phone, sending Maddie a short text: Hey we’re in town.

Maddie was calling him just seconds later.

“What do you mean you’re in town?” she asked, laughter hiding just behind her words.

Daniel grinned, glad that she remembered.

“I mean that we’re in town,” he replied, and then looked around for a suitable landmark to give her. “We are currently at, uh, Union Wharf. Please tell me there aren’t any creepy cemeteries along this trail.”

Maddie laughed in his ear. Buck complained in his other.

“What hotel are you staying at?”

“Uh, the Battery Wharf? Not far from that church, across the water from the naval yard.”

“Oh, by the Coast Guard museum.”

“Sure,” Daniel agreed easily, even though he had no idea if that was true or not. “Hey, squirt, Mads says there’s a museum by the hotel.”

“Sweet.”

“I’m surrounded by nerds,” he complained.

“It’s free.”

“Sweet,” he echoed to Maddie’s laughter. “How do you feel about ghost tours?’

“The Ghosts and Gravestones one? I’ve wanted to go on it, but… Never really had the chance.” Read: Doug was a dick and probably decided they had better things to do. “I’ll see about tickets.”

“Make room on the calendar for the aquarium, too,” Daniel told her as he eyed their brother. “He’ll whine if we don’t.”

“Fuck you.”

He stayed on the phone with Maddie until they reached the hotel and had to check in. It was a nice place, and Daniel might’ve splurged a bit on it. He was long past the stage of his life where he was willing to settle for less, though, so he didn’t have much regrets about it. He even paid extra for a room that overlooked the water instead of the courtyard.

“You couldn’t book a hotel with a pool?” Buck complained as they rode the elevator up.

“We live twenty minutes from the beach.”

“But it’s always so crowded.”

Daniel rolled his eyes and shoved at him.

“It’s got a spa,” he said.

“You’ve already booked it.” Buck didn’t phrase it as a question, so Daniel chose not to answer. “Wow, Danny.”

“I was stuck next to you for a five and a half hour flight,” Daniel reminded him. “I’ve earned some pampering.”

Buck scoffed and then asked how much it cost. Because Daniel’s brother wasn’t any more immune to the siren song of deep tissue massages and chilled cucumber slices than he himself was.

Daniel hadn’t splurged on the best room at the hotel. Mainly because that was the presidential suite, and he didn’t see the point in paying for a hotel room large enough to have a living room when they were only going to be in Boston for four nights. It was very tempting, however, because it came with a jetted tub and Daniel envisioned soaking in that for an hour or five. It would’ve been worth sharing a king sized bed with Buck and his ice cube toes.

But their room wasn’t bad. Buck flopped onto one of the doubles within moments of Daniel unlocking the door, only barely pausing to drop his bag and slip off his shoes first. He sunk in a bit and groaned.

“It’s so soft,” he praised, words a bit muffled by the tucked in comforter his face was pressed into.

“The listing said it was a ‘luxurious’ bed,” Daniel remarked.

He set his bag down on his bed and pulled out his phone again. Telling people back home their hotel and room number was only common sense, because they all knew the kinds of disasters that could happen. Austin, of course, but also Athena. Maybe Buck told Eddie, he probably did, but their current divorce era made him unsure of it.

Austin was calling him within moments of the text being sent off.

“How was the flight?”

“Long.” Daniel put his phone on speaker and set it on the bedside table. Then he unzipped his bag and started pulling out the clothes he’d packed for their stay. “And it’s too damn cold here.”

“You grew up in Pennsylvania.”

“And we fled to warmer pastures.” He watched with some amusement as his uncoordinated brother nearly fell out of the other bed trying to get up. “We were meant for sandy beaches, not snowbanks.”

“There were a couple times growing up that we got enough snow to bury someone in,” Buck remarked as he started unpacking his bag, too. “Danny threatened to bury me, once.”

“You remember that, but you’ll forget your own birthday without a reminder.” Daniel shook his head at him. “Hey, you’ve got a shift tomorrow, right?”

“Right…”

“If Eddie comes in-”

“Danny,” Buck huffed.

“I’ve already got the itching powder at the ready.”

“I knew that was you!”

“When are you seeing Maddie tomorrow?”

“Buck’ll probably be dragging me out before noon.” Daniel shrugged, even though Austin couldn’t see it. “I think she might have her group thing tomorrow? I’ve got a spa appointment, too.”

On the other end of the line, Austin suddenly gave a snort of laughter.

“What?” Daniel asked.

“Nothing.”

“No, what was so funny?” Buck asked, ever curious. “Besides Danny going to a spa.”

“Just…” Austin laughed again. “It’ll be the first time Daniel’s paying, instead of being paid, to get a fac-”

“And we’re hanging up now, bye!” Buck lunged to slap at Daniel’s phone, somehow managing to end the call with the flailing. “Your zucchini is a dirty minded pervert, what the hell?”

“Stop calling him a vegetable!”

“I think it’s a fruit, actually.”

Daniel threw a shirt at his face.

Maddie’s new apartment was small. Quaint? Quaint seemed more polite. There were paintings and pictures on the wall that Buck figured were already here when she got the place. In the corner was a table with a small, sad looking Christmas tree on it, with just a couple strings of lights, a single box of ornaments, and a star on top. But it was something, at least.

“It’s better than our place in El Paso,” Danny remarked, after they finally stopped hugging their sister for long enough to look around.

Buck nodded in agreement.

“The elevator didn’t make scary noises.”

“The clinic’s not too far away,” Danny said. “Is it?”

“Yeah.” Maddie nodded. She was looking so much better than when Buck last saw her, right after the blackout. Still a little tired in appearance, maybe, but not the kind of exhaustion that seeped into your very cells and made getting out of bed an impossible endeavor. “Being part of the program was why the landlord was willing to accept enough cash for six months.”

“Jesus Christ, Mads,” Danny swore, while Buck just found himself staring at her.

“I didn’t want to leave a paper trail.” She sounded sheepish. “In case, you know. In case he got the police involved.”

“Yeah, that…” Now it was Danny’s turn to be sheepish for some reason. “That- That makes sense.”

“The jacket’s new.” She reached up and tugged at the lapel. “It looks like the one you had in Hershey.”

“Oh, yeah. Austin got it for me for Christmas.”

Danny was actually blushing as he tilted his head down to look at it, and his face had gone disgustingly gooey. Buck met his sister’s eyes, both of them trying very hard not to laugh. He really hoped he never looked like that when talking about Eddie, but he was sadly certain that he did. When Eddie wasn’t being a jerk, at least.

“Um, speaking of.” Danny cleared his throat. “We brought you presents.”

Buck held them out, his and Danny’s. Both were wrapped in green, snowman patterned paper. Maddie took them from him with a smile, small and warm, and placed them on the table by her sad little tree.

“So, bad news,” she said. “That next Ghosts and Gravestones tour is Thursday night-”

“Aw, man,” Buck whined. “We’re flying back that afternoon.”

“But I did get us tickets for the aquarium for today.”

“You got them? Maddie.” Danny was frowning in worry. “Are you okay for money? You just said you paid for six months in advance, you haven’t mentioned anything about working anywhere…”

“I’m fine, Dan,” she assured him. “I still have more than enough of the money I pulled out in Oxnard. But we have time to get something to eat-”

“Which I’m paying for.”

Maddie rolled her eyes.

“There’s a Marriott right next to the aquarium,” Buck told her. “With a pool. But no, Danny had to go swanky.”

“We live twenty minutes from the beach!”

“But it’s always so crowded,” Maddie said, and then laughed when Danny threw up his hands.

“He got it for the spa,” Buck stage whispered.

“An eighty minute deep tissue massage, Maddie!”

“That does sound nice, actually,” she said.

“Thank you! And this one-” Danny pointed at Buck. “-was looking up how much they cost last night.”

“Just because I’m thinking about booking an appointment, doesn’t mean I can’t make fun of you for it.”

“That is exactly what it means, you brat.”

“Okay, kids,” their sister broke in before they could continue sniping at each other. “Let me get my coat and we’ll find somewhere to eat, okay?”

Predictably, there turned out to be more than a few choices for food near the aquarium. They made the trek to the nearest station and road the rail until they reached Park Street – “Hey, Danny, I think the Freedom Trail starts near here.” “Awesome, Maddie can watch you be creepy in cemeteries in person.” – at which point they changed rails, and then had to do it again at the next station before they got on one that actually took them to the aquarium. They picked a tavern that was, more or less, straight across from it. Mostly because Buck pointed out it offered chicken-and-waffles when he checked out the menu and Danny made an executive decision.

Midway through, the draft beer that Buck had ordered – Danny got some cocktail that came with a rubber ducky floating in it – caught up to him. He wasn’t paying much attention to anything posted outside the bathrooms on his way, in a hurry as he was, but he came to a stop on his way out.

Because Maddie’s face was staring at him from a Missing Persons flyer.

It was an old picture of Maddie, maybe from a couple years ago, with MISSING right above it on a bright red border. There was the usual blurb of information, her height, her age, where she was last seen. Then, at the bottom, contact information for Chimney.

Buck was ripping the flyer down before the thought even finished forming. He hurriedly shoved it into his pants pocket, afraid that Maddie or Danny would see it and know what he’d done. As casually as he could, he went back to their table and retook his seat, finding his brother with his head hanging back and groaning.

“What’s his problem?” Buck asked, and hoped his voice didn’t give anything away.

“My group at the clinic is doing a friends and family session on Wednesday,” Maddie said, sounding a bit hesitant.

“Of course we’ll go.” Danny lifted his head. “But I reserve the right to be dramatic about it.”

“So that’s where Buck gets it from.”

Danny threw a fry at her.

Daniel could not get out of there fast enough. It wasn’t that the group therapy thing was bad, necessarily. It was more that even his own therapy sessions threatened to give him hives sometimes, and Dr. White was used enough to him now that his comments barely drew a reaction. That had not been the case here, today, and he just really wanted to put space between it and him.

Buck was a yapper, though, and Maddie was sorta friends with some of them. They’d hung back to talk, and so Daniel walked out by himself. Which, as it turned out, was a good thing.

Because Howard fucking Han was sitting at a bench, watching the clinic entrance like a hawk.

There was no hope of the man not spotting him, because he was bolting to his feet. Daniel heaved out a sigh and started walking, delving into the small crowd of pedestrians. He undid the scarf around his neck as he went, glancing back every so often to make sure Chimney was still following. He took a corner, then another, never going so fast that Chimney lost him. Even crossing the street was done at a relaxed pace.

There was no one around after the next corner he took, so Daniel came to a stop and wrapped the scarf around his hand. Then, it was merely a matter of waiting. Honestly, an argument could be made that Chimney ran face first into his fist.

“Eddie’s already on my shit list, so I’m choosing to blame him for this,” Daniel told the man, who was busy groaning on the ground. He unwrapped the scarf and put it around his neck again. “I bet Buck could’ve held out longer if it wasn’t for him.”

Because of course it was Buck who told Chimney about Boston. The kid had too much heart.

“Where is she, Dan?” Chimney demanded, propping himself up on an elbow and wiping at his bleeding nose.

“I have a better question.” Daniel stuck a boot on the man’s chest and pushed him back down. “Where the hell is my niece?”

“She’s with Eli’s wife,” was the strained sounding answer. “He used to work at the One Eighteen.”

Daniel had no damn clue who that was. He also didn’t exactly get a say in who Chimney entrusted his daughter to.

“At least you weren’t stupid enough to have her out in this cold while you played lookout.” He took his foot off him and stepped back a couple paces. “Do you like the new jacket? Austin bought it for me.”

“I don’t care about the jacket,” the other man spat as he got up. “Where’s Maddie?”

“You can not seriously think I’m gonna tell ya.” Daniel scoffed and shook his head. Then he smiled. “Have you asked Athena yet?”

Chimney froze, the question of “What?” coming out sounding like it was squeezed through a straw.

“Yeah, I told her what was going on the day Jee was left at the station,” Daniel taunted. Because people would be upset if he hurt him, more than he already had, but he could still hurt him. “Did you ask her? Did she say she didn’t know?”

“She told me the detective wouldn’t file a missing persons report…”

“She probably didn’t even bother speaking to a detective.”

It was definitely not a good quality in him, that he took such pleasure in the other man’s seething anger. He’d accepted he wasn’t exactly a good person a long time ago.

“You’ll want to find a new bench, by the way.” Daniel grinned at the man, all bared teeth, as he backed away. “I’m gonna tell the people at the clinic you’re stalking Maddie and she’s hiding from you. Which is more or less the truth.”

Daniel turned his back on him then, practically daring the man to do something as he walked away. When nothing happened, he resolved to mock him for it the next time they crossed paths.

Chapter End Notes

In 5x07 (Ghost Stories) and 5x12 (Boston) Chimney, Hen, and Albert are all three doubtful about Buck figuring out where Maddie is based purely on church bells he heard in the background. Meanwhile, Maddie starts panicking the moment they start ringing while she's on the phone with Buck, to the point that the next scene is her packing up as she prepares to run again, because she knows Buck can and will guess her location based only on that. Just something I thought was interesting.

Chapter 22

It had been weeks since Christmas, since Eddie’s announcement that he was leaving the 118 and the ensuing argument in the kitchen. Weeks since Buck packed a bag and went back to the apartment, weeks since his and Danny’s return from Boston. Enough weeks to qualify as months, even.

Things were… Well, not perfect. Not even particularly close to perfect. At work, Hen was being saddled with a new floater every shift, filling in the void that Chimney and then Eddie left in the ambulance. She’d started calling them by the days of the week, because they never stuck around long enough for anyone to actually remember their names.
