Buck was still partnered with Ravi, but sometimes he was with Bobby, like it was his probational year again. Eddie’s absence would probably have been an awkward weight around his shoulders, except he’d worked without him for far longer than he’d worked with him by this point. It wasn’t much different from when he was at the 133, except he wasn’t being treated like the station’s collective little brother.

It had been a nice feeling. He kinda missed it.

The real problem wasn’t at work, it was the hours outside of it. Things between Buck and Eddie were still a bit off after their argument on Christmas. Buck would spend the occasional nights there, but more often than not, he went back to the apartment. Danny, he could tell, was torn between being overjoyed by the development and worried.

Mostly, it was the fact that Eddie was miserable at his new job as the Public Service Officer at Metro Dispatch, but he wouldn’t admit it. He kept insisting he was perfectly fine, that he was happy, whenever Buck brought it up. He’d grow defensive and then they’d find themselves right back where they were on Christmas night. Especially whenever Buck spent the night and got to see, in person, just how poorly Eddie was sleeping.

Buck was worried. Buck had been worrying since that panic attack at the store. It just seemed to be getting worse, and Eddie simply dug his heels in whenever Buck tried to address it. More fights had erupted from him voicing his concerns in the last couple of months than they’d had in all the previous years combined.

Buck was worried, and he had no idea how to fix anything.

The dispatch floor looked much the same as it had the last few times that Daniel visited in recent weeks. Still busy, still filled with the constant chatter of one-sided conversations. He spotted dispatchers at their stations in their blue and maroon uniforms, and wondered once again what dictated who got which color. May and Josh both had blue, for example, while Linda wore the same maroon as Maddie had.

Maybe it was simply random.

“Hey, Dan.” May stopped on her way from the break room to greet him. “Back again?”

“I bring provisions.” Daniel lifted up the bag of food, and then yanked it away when she made as if to grab it. “Not for you.”

“Rude!”

“You’ll live,” he told her without any ounce of sympathy. “Where is he?”

“In his cave.”

The room they stuck the Public Service Officer in was very cave-like, Daniel had to agree. It was small, with cement block walls painted off-white, with maps of LA and a small window near the ceiling.. Most of the space was taken up by filing cabinets and the desk, behind which was a trio of corded phones. There was another phone on the desk itself, and Eddie was fielding a call on it when Daniel came to hover in the open doorway.

“Uh, yes.” Eddie’s fake smile was more of a grimace as he leaned back and looked away. “I am a real firefighter.”

Daniel managed to contain the scoff that statement deserved. Because no one who worked in this office was a real firefighter. The closest that anyone working in this office would ever come to firefighting would be if someone got a paper cut or burnt something in the break room’s microwave. At the moment, Eddie was doing less first responding than the dispatchers on the call floor. A person didn’t even have to go through the academy to do the job Eddie was doing right now.

And he was miserable for it, Daniel could tell. They could all tell. It was etched into the new lines on his face and carried in the bags that were growing beneath his eyes. He tried to put up a good show, tried to claim that being able to take his kid to school every morning and spend every afternoon with him was enough to make up for the position he’d exiled himself to. It wasn’t. That much was obvious from the way he shrank in on himself after hanging up.

It was Eddie’s choice, though. A very stupid choice on several counts. The change in position meant a cut in pay, and Daniel knew Eddie was not financially stable enough to simply suck that up. Buck had started picking up the slack in some areas and the only reason the two of them weren’t fighting about that, too, was because Buck was doing it behind his back. Because Buck, unlike Daniel, was perfectly willing to protect Eddie from the consequences of his stupidity.

If it was Buck, Daniel could see himself doing the same. But Eddie wasn’t Buck, and so Daniel was of the opinion that he should’ve been left to drown until he came to his senses. A missed month of rent and the threat of eviction would be enough to have Eddie scrambling for turnouts again. God knew the fear of that had kept Daniel from even contemplating something as foolish as this, not when he had a kid depending on him.

“I bring burgers,” Daniel announced as he entered the tiny room. Eddie jumped in his seat, startled. “Had to settle for In-n-Out.”

“I’m too hungry to really care,” Eddie admitted.

Daniel set the bag and drinks tray on the desk to dig out Eddie’s burger and fries, which he handed to him along with a milkshake.

“What was today’s word?” Daniel asked after he snagged a spare chair and sat down.

May had started doing words of the day, apparently. Daniel was fairly certain she was making up half of them.

“Zeugma,” Eddie answered. He popped the lid off his vanilla milkshake and dipped a fry into it. “A figure of speech where a word has double meaning in the sentence.”

“Worth a few points in Scrabble.”

Eddie absently nodded as he chewed on his food. Daniel stared at him and sighed.

“Austin said Hen’s still tormenting her floaters,” he said, and smirked when Eddie perked up at the bit of gossip. “Today’s was annoyed that she’s calling him Monday.”

“When are they going to settle on someone?”

“Probably when they finally find a replacement for you and Chimney.”

Eddie flinched.

“A replacement,” he echoed.

“You quit the job, man, and Chimney outran his leave. They’re gonna have to find someone to fill the gaps.”

“Right, yeah.” Eddie morosely bit into a milkshake dipped fry. “Of course.”

If Daniel rolled his eyes any harder, they’d probably roll right out of their sockets.

“Are you coming over for dinner tonight?” Eddie asked.

“Sorry, man, Tabitha got there first.” It hadn’t even been a question or an invite. Tabitha had simply told him he was coming for dinner with Austin. “Any fun school gossip?”

“Oh, um. There was something about one of the teachers…”

“I can’t believe we’re living through this movie,” Ravi grumbled.

They were on the ladder, rushing through the streets. There was something about a family in a truck being held hostage with a bomb. Buck didn’t know all the details, just that the bomb was supposed to go off if the truck dropped below a certain speed.

“What movie?” Buck asked as he tightened the straps on his helmet.

“What movie!? What do you mean, what movie? You’ve never watched Speed?”

“We’re lucky he’s seen the original Star Wars,” Hen told the probie.

“There they are,” Bobby suddenly announced.

Buck craned his neck to peer out the front. He could see the truck with the family, and spotted Athena’s cruiser keeping pace with it. There was another company’s engine, the 147, that pulled up along the 118’s ladder.

“Here’s the plan, so we’re all on the same page,” Bobby said over the headsets. “We’re gonna ride alongside that pickup truck and match its speed. We have to move fast, so the One Forty-seven is here to assist. Buck, once we’re in position, I want you to jump into that truck bed. Ravi is here to give you a hand. Somebody from the One Forty-Seven will be doing the same.”

“Alright.” Athena’s voice came through the radio. “We’re falling back.”

Her cruiser lost speed, Javier moving their ladder to the side a bit to avoid her while the 147’s engine did the same. With her out of the way, they picked up speed to pull alongside the pickup truck. Buck undid his seat belt, slid a halligan into his harness, and prepared to leap across empty air and pavement.

“Nobody tell Danny about this,” he said, before he opened the door and hauled himself out.

Climbing up onto the roof of the ladder truck was a bit daunting at these speeds. Buck clung to the ladder itself as he moved down the length until he was even with the truck bed. Ravi was right behind him and someone from the 147 was climbing out of their engine across from him.

“Okay.” Buck did his best to hype himself up as he eyed the pavement flying beneath them. “I’m just working out how to do this.’

If he died doing this, Danny was gonna kill him.

Across the way, the 147 firefighter leaped off their engine and across the empty air. They landed in the truck bed, crouching as they landed. Buck and Ravi stared for a moment.

“Just like that, I’m guessing,” the probie said.

Alright, fine. There was nothing left to it but to man up and do it. Buck took a breath, bent his legs, and leaped. After a moment of terror where his heart was in his chest, Buck landed n the truck bed with barely a stumble. The 147’s firefighter was waiting for him.

“Evan Buckley,” he introduced himself as they clipped their safety lines to a railing along the back of the cab. “Nice jump.”

“Lucy Donato,” she replied. “I give yours a seven.”

Everyone was a critic.

The back window was popped out with the halligan and a bit of force. The ladder truck moved behind them, Ravi extending the ladder. Normally, Javier would’ve been at the controls for it. But he was a bit busy driving.

“How’s everyone doing in here?” Buck poked his head into the cab to ask. The small family of four didn’t answer, not that he was expecting one. “Kids, you’re gonna be first. Okay? Grab onto us and hang on tight.”

The kids came out with the mother twisted around in her seat and looking on anxiously. Buck and Lucy got them hooked up to safety harnesses, and then hooked up to the ladder’s anchor line. One kid then the other was lifted up to Ravi’s waiting arms and then passed farther down the ladder to Duncan, on loan from C-shift.

“Guys, it’s Eddie.” His boyfriend’s voice over the radio had Buck perking up. “Do not move the driver. A bomb like that is a sophisticated device. There could be a pressure switch somewhere that senses the driver’s movements. Let me talk to the driver.”

Buck and Lucy listened as Eddie talked to the father of the line. There was, indeed, something beneath the gas pedal. Which made things even more complicated. Worse, however, was the electronic road sign up ahead declaring the road was ending in two miles.

“Everything’s gonna be fine,” Eddie assured the family.

“Problem, Eds,” Buck radioed back. “We’re running out of freeway.”

The plan that was decided on was to get the wife out and leave the husband in the driver’s seat. Inevitably, the wife and the husband wasted time arguing over who would actually be left behind and who would be rescued. Athena cut in and managed to talk the wife into letting them pull her out to safety.

“Buckley, Donato, you need to evacuate the pickup now,” Bobby radioed. “We need to fall back and let the bomb squad get to work.”

“I was just gonna stay here and die,” Buck mumbled. He and Lucy helped the wife walk the length of the bed to where Ravi was waiting. “Ravi! Hey!”

“Be right back!” Lucy suddenly said before heading back to the cab.

Buck was too busy getting the harness around the wife to pay attention to what she was doing. At least until Ravi was pulling the wife up and Lucy was suddenly radioing,

“Heads up, Dad’s coming your way.”

Wait, what?

Buck turned to look and hurried over to help the husband out. Through the window, or where the window used to be, he could see Lucy sitting in the driver’s seat with the halligan. What the hell was she doing?

A question he didn’t have time to wonder about. He got the harness around the dad and then got him hooked to the line. A small boost and he was soon climbing onto the ladder with Ravi’s assistance, leaving Buck and Lucy as the only ones on the truck. He helped her get onto the ladder, and then stumbled back a bit.

The jump he did to cross the growing gulf between the trucks was not a graceful one. He only barely managed to catch himself, the wind getting driven out of him from the way he slammed into the end of the ladder. But bruises were better than the alternative.

With no one left on the pickup truck, both the 118 and the 147 fell back. Buck clambered up with Lucy’s assistance, and saw the pickup slam into a barrier. It was a gnarly crash, but not the Michael Bay explosive he was promised. Which made him question whether there was ever a bomb at all.

Of course, as soon as he voiced his doubts, the pickup exploded.

“At least Danny doesn’t know I did any of that,” Buck said.

“Uh, Buck?” Ravi was pointing up, at the-

“Aw, fuck.”

Buck glared at the Channel 8 news helicopter hovering above them. He glared even harder when his phone started ringing from his pocket.

“He’s on the TV again,” Austin said, pointing behind Daniel.

Daniel turned enough to see the news once again covering that bomber call his brother had been on. A clip of the trucks racing down the freeway, one of someone leaping between them, and another of the pickup burning. He turned back with a groan and let his head fall onto the tabletop.

The 118 had gone out to drink after shift, along with some of the 147 who’d been on the call as well. Buck had texted him about it, in between begging Daniel not to be mad at him. Which Daniel wasn’t, but it was fun to wind his brother up about it. But Daniel had come out as well, after checking in with Austin. Now the pair had a little table of their own, not far from where Buck was holding court with Hen and Nash and some chick from the 147.

A hand patting him on the shoulder had Daniel lifting his head. He glowered at Austin’s smug little face.

“Shut up,” he said. “I live a much happier life when I have no idea he’s pulling that shit.”

“I’d ask if you’d prefer for the family to blow up and die,” Austin said. “But we both know the answer to that.”

“I don’t know those people.” Daniel drained the last of his fruity little drink and debated ordering another. “They aren’t real to me.”

“Filed under statements your therapist would have a field day with.”

Daniel flicked him off.

“That’s Monday, by the way.” Austin pointed the guy out, sitting at the bar. “Or Tuesday, now, I guess. Greenway.”

“He looks like off-brand Captain America.” Something about him was off putting, in a way that set off Daniel’s instincts without any obvious reason. Daniel wondered if he should ask Austin to keep an eye on him around Buck like he did Nash. “I’m ordering another round.”

It took far too long to get through the line to order, and longer still to get their drinks. By the time Daniel was carrying them back to the table, Nash had left – no one was missing him – and Buck was harassing Austin.

“What d’ya want, squirt?” Daniel asked, setting Austin’s lame lager in front of him.

“We’re gonna play some pool!” Buck was grinning, that bright and infectious one that made it difficult to say no to him. “C’mon!”

He groaned, but he allowed himself to be tugged over to one of the pool tables. Austin picked up his lager and followed them, silently laughing as they went. The chick from the 147 – Lucy, according to Buck – was already there with a pool stick.

Daniel did enjoy a game of pool, so he didn’t actually mind being dragged into it by his brother. Any excuse to spend time with him and all that. That Buck was a yapper and could therefore keep the conversation going on his own was a bonus. It meant Daniel mostly just stood back with Austin when it wasn’t his turn, downing fruity cocktails and irritating the man.

(They were definitely gonna take an Uber home.)

“So, uh.” Buck lined up and took his shot. “You’re an LA native? That’s kinda rare.”

“Is it though?” Daniel asked. “Is it really?”

“He grew up in Glendale,” Buck said, pointing at Austin with his pool cue. “Does that really count?”

Lucy shook her head with a laugh.

“I’m very rare, actually,” she said as she took her turn. “The one firefighter in a family full of cops. Is there a problem?”

Daniel had not been subtle with the noise of disgust he’d made. He couldn’t even blame the alcohol, because they all knew he’d have been the same if he were completely sober.

“Danny doesn’t like cops,” Buck told her.

“Useless pigs, the lot of ‘em.”

“They do an important job,” Lucy argued.

Daniel opened his mouth, only to glare at Austin when the man slapped a hand over it. Austin, for his part, was doing a good job pretending like nothing out of the usual was occurring and just sipped at his stupid boring drink while staring down Lucy.

“Well,” she finally said after a moment, going back to the game. “I like to keep ‘em on their toes.”

He rolled his eyes before tuning out the conversation. Austin was very warm where Daniel was leaning against him, and it was taking all he had not to sink into it more. Austin wouldn’t mind, Daniel knew that, but… There was something, a reason why it was important that Daniel didn’t do it, but it was lost to him right now.

“Why isn’t Eddie here?” Austin asked.

“I don’t know.” Daniel pulled his phone out to pull up his texts. “Buck invited him at the same time he did me.”

Yeah, there it was, in the little group chat Buck had made for the three of them, right above the one that had the three of them plus Austin, and right below the Jones family one he’d somehow gotten added to forever ago. Ethan and Emmett were complaining about something, Daniel wasn’t sure what. He’d had it muted for a while now.

“Anything from Maddie recently?” Austin suddenly asked. “Han hasn’t ambushed her, has he?”

“Not as far as I know.” Daniel tucked his phone away. “I talked to the people at the clinic again, just to make sure. They said he hasn’t been hanging around that bench any more.”

“I’m surprised no one complained about him sooner. Women’s clinic and all, you’d think somebody would be concerned about a guy watching the entrance.”

“Don’t think anyone was really paying attention.” Daniel checked on his brother and rolled his eyes when he saw the pair of them had margaritas now. “I wanna warn her, but I’m terrified what she’ll do if she knows he’s there.”

“Maybe she’d run back here.”

“Doubtful,” he scoffed. “She’d be too worried about him coming back for that.”

“I’ve got a bag of sugar waiting for him.” Austin was smirking. “His gas tank doesn’t lock.”

Daniel burst out laughing and let his head fall onto Austin’s shoulder.

“God,” he gasped out. “I love you.”

The shoulder beneath his forehead went stiff suddenly. Daniel frowned and lifted his head. Austin’s face was a picture of shock, with hints of fear and even guilt. His every muscle was tensed, like he was a moment away from sprinting for cover.

“What-?” And then Daniel realized what he’d said. “Oh, God. Austin, I- I didn’t-”

He needed to get out of here before he made things any worse. He didn’t know how he could make things worse, considering he’d just ruined everything with the three little words he’d spent years choking back, but he knew that he could. He was talented that way.

Thank God for his baby brother. He was suddenly throwing himself back from Lucy, wiping at his mouth and stammering out,

“I- I didn’t want that.”

“Sorry, I thought you did.” Lucy was frowning, a furrow between her brows. “It was just a kiss.”

“I didn’t…”

“Okay, squirt, I think it’s time to go.” Daniel didn’t look at Austin, too afraid of what else he’d see on his face now that the shock of his stupidity had time to settle. He just rounded the pool table, took both his and Buck’s pool sticks and set them aside, and then bustled him off to the entrance. “I’ll order an Uber.”

He felt Austin’s gaze on him the entire way out the door.

“What do I keep doing?” Buck blurted out in the Uber. “It’s- It’s something I’m doing, right? Lucy, and- and her, and- What do I keep doing, Danny?”

“Did you say you wanted to kiss her?”

“No!”

“Then I don’t think it’s anything you’re doing.”

Buck shook his head at him and then buried his face in his hands.

“Eddie’s going to be so angry,” he said, the words muffled a bit.

Daniel sighed and patted him on the back.

“He’s not going to be angry at you because someone kissed you.”

He hoped so anyway.

The Uber took them to Eddie’s, partially because Daniel didn’t want to try and get his drunk brother up to their apartment and partially because he wanted to cut this off before it actually became something. Eddie opened the door before they got to it, and Buck was throwing himself into him while repeating,

“I’m sorry, I’m sorry, I’m sorry.”

“Uh.” Eddie floundered for a moment. “What?”

“I’ll explain in a bit,” Daniel told him. “Let’s just get him to bed.”

It took some work to get Buck into bed, especially with the way he insisted on clinging to Eddie and apologizing. Daniel and Eddie managed it, though, leaving him tucked into bed with a glass of water and some Tylenol on the bedside table. It wasn’t even that Buck was that drunk, he was just that upset.

“I really wish you’d come out with us,” Daniel told Eddie in the kitchen as he poured himself a glass of water. “Maybe that wouldn’t have happened.”

“And what happened?”

“Some chick from the 147 kissed him. He thinks it’s his fault, somehow.”

Eddie scrunched up his face.

“He reassured me one day that he wanted to have sex with me every time we had sex,” he said. “I didn’t even know that was something I had to be worried about before then.”

Daniel eyed him, and judged that Eddie didn’t need murdering right then.

“I once asked him if he’d wanted to have sex with someone.” Daniel really wished this water was something alcoholic, but he’d ruined enough things tonight because of drinking. “He asked why it mattered. I blame Mom for that.”

He threw himself into a seat at the tiny kitchen table with a groan.

“Tonight sucked,” he said. “Not for you, though. Good job on catching that… Whatever.”

“Pressure switch. Got yelled at for it, too.”

“What? Why?”

“Because I didn’t go to Josh first.”

“What the hell does he know about explosives?”

Eddie shrugged.

“He reminded me that I’m a guest in his house. Not my job to save the day anymore.” The words were very bitter. “I’m gonna go to bed. You need help setting up the couch?”

“Nah.” Daniel shook his head. “I’ve got it.”

Not that he thought he was going to sleep well. God, how could he be so damn stupid?

“I’m sorry,” Buck said for what felt like the hundredth time since he woke up.

He’d only been awake for less than an hour.

“And I’ve told you, it’s… Not fine,” Eddie said as he pulled on his boots. “But I’m not upset with you, Buck.”

“But I-”

“Danny explained what happened. I’m not upset with you.”

Buck sighed and draped himself across his back.

“I just wish I knew what I kept doing that makes people think…”

“I think I’m gonna ask Bobby if I can transfer back.”

“He shouldn’t have let you transfer at all,” Buck complained.

He’d argued with Bobby about it, about the way he’d let Eddie make a huge decision like that without pulling the brakes. Bobby claimed he tried to talk Eddie out of it, but he clearly didn’t try very hard or for very long. Buck didn’t think he’d just let one of his firefighters follow through a rash over-night decision like that, not without time to actually think it through, but he wasn’t the captain, so what did he know?

“What led to the change in heart?” Buck asked.

“I made a mistake. Shut up.” Eddie stood up and glared at him. Buck mimed zipping his lips. “Chris said he never wanted me to stop being a firefighter. No, really, shut up.”

“No.” Buck shook his head. “I told you before, Chris was nothing but an excuse-”

“Buck.”

“No! Chris was an excuse to do what you wanted before, and you’re using him as an excuse again. He was afraid for you, so you quit. He didn’t want you to stop, so now you want to come back.”

“I’m not using my son as an excuse.”

“Yes, you are,” Buck insisted. “You’re hiding behind him. And I’m worried about you, Eds. Have you seen yourself in the mirror? Have you seen how awful you look?”

“I’m fine.”

“No, you’re not.”

“You’re one to talk,” Eddie spat. “You’ve been apologizing since last night, because you think I’m gonna be angry at you for what someone else did! Because you think you did something to lead her on or something.”

Buck cringed back a bit. He chewed on his lip in thought, and then said,

“I’ll go back to therapy if you do.”

“What?”

“I’ll go back to therapy,” he repeated. “But you have to, too.”

“I don’t need a therapist.”

“Cool, then I don’t, either.”

Buck grinned at his boyfriend’s glare.

“I’ll think about it,” Eddie ground out.

“Awesome!” Buck tugged him down to kiss him, ignoring the man’s complaints about morning breath. “Love you.”

“Love you, too,” Eddie mumbled as he walked out of the bedroom.

Buck watched him go and then flopped back onto the bed, blindly groping around for his phone. He might as well make that appointment with Dr. Copeland while he was thinking about it. Before he forgot to do it for another six months.

Daniel stepped out of the elevator and onto the dispatch floor with a smile. The kind of smile that had a dispatcher veering out of his way entirely. He looked around, gave May and Linda absentminded greetings, and smiled wider when he spotted Josh pouring himself coffee in the break room.

“Oh, Dan.” Josh was obviously taken aback by his presence. “Eddie’s in his office.”

“I figured.” Daniel shrugged. “He pulled a nice save yesterday. Don’t you think?”

Josh’s lips drew into a line.

“Yeah,” was said a bit reluctantly.

“He kept Buck from being turned into chunks,” Daniel continued. “I can imagine what would’ve happened if Eddie hadn’t warned them about it when he had.”

The dispatcher was looking distinctly uncomfortable now.

“I like Eddie far more than I like you.” He didn’t care about burning any bridges here, he’d already burnt more important bridges with much less care. “If you ever talk to him like that again, especially after he just kept my baby brother from exploding, our next conversation is gonna be far less friendly.”

Daniel clapped Josh on the shoulder, smiled at the way he flinched, and then left the break room. He whistled all the way to Eddie’s little office.

It wasn't often that Austin appeared as anything other than stoic and put together. But when Buck stepped out of the locker room with Hen, after getting changed into his uniform, he found the engineer an anxious mess. For him, anyway, which meant he merely appeared vaguely worried by normal people standards.

“Hey, everything okay?” Buck asked.

“Yeah,” was Austin’s unconvincing answer. “Have you… You’ve talked to Dan, right?”

“Um, yeah.” Buck shared a glance with Hen. “Why?”

“He didn’t say anything…?”

“About what?”

Austin silently stared at him, then he shook his head and walked away. Buck watched him go, before turning to Hen.

“That was weird, right?” he asked.

“It was weird,” she agreed. “Are they fighting?”

“If they were fighting, we’d know about it,” he pointed out. “Danny doesn’t do subtle like that.”

“Everybody upstairs!” Bobby called out as he walked past.

He didn’t look at Buck, and Buck knew the reason for that, at least. The talk between Bobby and Eddie had not gone well, though Eddie hadn’t given him much in the way of details. He just said Bobby wasn’t going to let him back, and Buck didn’t know how to help him there. Not when Eddie had asked to be transferred out.

“Rundown in five,” the captain continued. “And I’ve got some announcements to make.”

“Oh, that doesn’t fill me with good feelings,” Buck told Hen, and Ravi, when he joined them. “I wonder if any of them’s related to Eddie.”

“What about him?” Ravi asked.

“He wants to come back, Bobby’s not letting him.”

They joined the rest of the shift in the station’s loft. Hen’s mood plummeted almost as soon as they crested the stairs and they could see the paramedic floater, Jonah Greenway, standing beside Bobby.

“Why do I get the feeling that man is one of the announcements?” she quietly said.

“Morning everyone,” Bobby greeted the gathered firefighters. “As you all know, we’ve been down two people these last few months. But Jonah, here, has been with us for a few shifts. He’d done an excellent job, and he’ll be our new paramedic.”

Hen cursed under her breath.

“Thanks, Captain,” Jonah said. “I’m happy to be here and be a part of the team.”

“He doesn’t seem so bad,” Buck whispered to Hen. “He’ll probably be a good partner.”

Hen glared at him.

“What are you doing right now?”

“Looking on the bright side?”

“Well, stop it,” she told him.

“Who’s gonna be the other one?” Ravi asked Bobby.

“Someone else you know, just not as well,” Bobby answered, and then gestured to the back of the crowd.

Buck turned and saw Lucy climbing the stairs. He thought his reaction – silently turning back around and ignoring her existence – was perfectly reasonable, all things considered.

Chapter 23

Maddie was coming home.

In the couple of weeks since the kiss, Buck had been doing his best to interact with Lucy as little as possible on shift. Clearly something he did made her think she should kiss him, and the best way of avoiding a repeat of that would be to just avoid her entirely. He mostly stuck to Hen and Ravi whenever he could get away with it, and to Austin whenever the man wasn’t in hiding. Which was more often than not, and Buck was starting to get the feeling that Austin was trying to avoid him for some reason.

Danny wasn’t being very helpful there. Whenever Buck asked if there was something going on, his brother would either change the subject, give him some awful lie, or straight up ignore the question. It was enough to make it clear that something had happened between them, not that Buck could figure out what that could be. He could account for Danny’s whereabouts from the outing at the bar to the first shift that Austin started being weird, and there wasn’t a moment there that Buck could remember them getting into a fight or anything like that.

Not even Austin’s siblings had any idea. Buck had gotten their numbers forever ago, at the Christmas party at the station, but it was the first time he’d reached out to them. Asking them if they knew what was going on got him not results, though, and so Buck was left to stew in curiosity and worry.

At least Eddie was… Okay, he wasn’t necessarily doing better, but he was going to therapy now. He was seeing Frank, the same one-legged therapist Maddie went to. Buck had no idea how that was going – there’d been a grand total of one session so far – but he doubted Eddie spent it as sheepishly as Buck had, sitting at the kitchen table at the apartment and explaining to Copeland through a screen how he kept forgetting to reschedule after the shooting.

(It earned him a strong recommendation to make an appointment with someone named Dr. Weaver that would probably keep getting put off.)

Anyways, none of that mattered right now. Because Maddie was coming home. She’d called Danny to tell them, explaining how Chimney had somehow found her in Boston – and it was here that Buck avoided looking at Danny, who was staring at him far too knowingly – and they’d talked and now she was coming home. Buck got the feeling that their sister skimmed over a lot of the salient points, but he was content to let it go.

Because Maddie was coming home!

“Once again leaning into the Golden Retriever comparison,” Danny remarked.

Danny was perched on the edge of the counter, sipping at a bottle of beer. He had on one of those old, worn looking sweaters that appeared in his wardrobe from Buck didn’t know where. His phone was sitting on the counter beside him, and despite his teasing, he had been regularly checking it as they waited.

“Were you expecting me to sit while I waited?” Buck asked, half-turned where he stood.

He’d been pacing all evening, only stopping on occasion to stare at the door like it would magically make Maddie get there faster. The last update said she was in California, but that was hours ago. Hours that seemed to have dragged on far worse than the hours before.

“No.” Danny shrugged while he sipped at his drink. “Just felt like teasing you for it.”

“You’re such a-”

Knocking at the door cut him off. Buck felt the grin stretching his face as he bounded for the door and flung it open. Maddie was on the other side, and she laughed when he hauled her in for a hug.

“You’re home!” he nearly shouted.

“I am,” she said. “Finally.”

“Where’s, uh, where’s Chim?” Buck let her go to peer into the hall. “And Jee?”

“They’re at his apartment.”

Buck closed the door, frowning at the phrasing. Danny sat down his drink and crossed the space to give Maddie his own hug.

“We, uh.” Maddie shrugged once she stepped back from Danny. “We broke up.”

“Oh no, that’s awful.” Danny’s attempt at sounding sympathetic fell completely flat. Buck and Maddie both stared at him for it. “I tried! Dr. White says that’s what matters.”

Maddie rolled her eyes and then took a seat at the table.

“What happened?” Buck asked.

“Somewhere around St. Louis, we realized it was over,” Maddie said, and then thanked Danny when he handed her a beer from the fridge. “I mean, we didn’t talk to each other for six months. We grew apart. Or we grew while we were apart, I guess.”

“He’s not punishing you for being sick, is he?”

“No.” Maddie shook her head, strands of hair flying side-to-side. “That isn’t what’s going on. We made a mutual decision together that this is what’s best for Jee. No one is punishing anyone, so don’t be mad at Chim.”

Danny’s scoff had Buck turning in his seat in time to see his brother rubbing at his cheek.

“Dan.” Maddie’s tone was a bit stern.

“I make no promises,” Danny said. “Where are you staying at right now? Unless you’re planning on living with your ex-boyfriend-slash-baby daddy.”

“I don’t know.” Maddie shrugged. “I don’t have a job. I used up most of my savings when I left so I can’t afford to find a new apartment right now. Chimney used most of his chasing me, so he can’t afford to find a new one.”

“The answer was here,” their brother told her. “It was a rhetorical question, Mads, you’re staying here. Obviously.”

“Obviously,” she mocked. “In Buck’s bed or on the couch?”

“Uh.” Buck shrugged and rubbed at his neck. “I don’t know?”

“Let’s go with the couch,” Danny said, already heading off to get spare blankets and pillows.

“Things between you and Eddie still rough?” Maddie asked Buck.

“They’re better. He’s seeing a therapist now. The same one you did before, Frank.” Buck leaned to the side to make sure Daniel wasn’t close enough to hear. “I think something happened between Danny and Austin.”

Maddie stiffened a bit and then sat her drink down on the table

“Oh? Like what?”

“I have no idea. They were fine, and then they weren’t. I don’t even know when it could’ve happened.”

“I told him…” Maddie shook her head. “He hasn’t told you anything?”

“No.” Buck eyed her, wondered just what she’d told Danny. That, along with a couple comments Danny had said… “You don’t like Austin, do you?”

“I didn’t like Dan dating-” She put air quotes around the word. “-Austin. It just seemed to me like he was hurting himself.”

“Okay!” Danny came out of his room with blankets and pillows gripped in his arms, which he dumped at the end of the couch. “You haven’t had dinner yet, have you?”

“We got snacks in Primm.” Primm, formerly State Line, was a town right on the Nevada-California border that started out in the Fifties as a motel and coffee shop owned by a Mr. Primm, before expanding into casinos and hotels. One of the hotels had the car that Bonnie and Clyde were killed in on display. “Some actual food would be nice.”

“I’ll order something,” Danny said, already pulling out his phone.

“I need to get my car, too.”

“Oh, um.” Danny glanced at her, and then almost immediately dropped his gaze back to his phone. “Squirt, can you take her to Cynthia’s?”

“Sure, yeah. I can do that.”

Buck met Maddie’s eyes and threw out a hand, silently asking, you see what I mean?

His sister just sighed and grabbed her drink back up.

Frank’s office was minimalist. A sofa, a potted plant in the corner, a desk, a table off to the side. Blinds on the windows, gray painted walls with framed prints and certificates. There was a lot of space between everything, allowing Frank to navigate the room easier with his wheelchair.

Therapy was more frustrating than Buck and Danny made it seem. That was Eddie’s impression so far, even though this was only his second session. He couldn’t see how simply talking about things like this made anything better. It wasn’t like Frank had said anything so far that Eddie didn’t already know.

“What are you afraid of?”

And then there were those questions. Seemingly random and total non sequiturs. Eddie was too damn tired for this.

“That’s pretty open-ended,” he pointed out. “I mean, I don’t love going to the dentist.”

“I doubt the fear of having a cavity filled is why you blew up at Captain Nash the way you did.”

He bristled at the reminder. It wasn’t his best moment, he knew that. He knew it the moment after, sitting in his truck and fuming. But he’d gone to Bobby, ready to come back to the station, ready to be a firefighter again. Only for Bobby to block his return, just like he’d done Buck.

Eddie’s reaction had not been the best.

(“You’re gonna stand there with a hundred-something bodies on you and tell me I’m not fit for duty!?”)

“I apologized for that,” he said. “I was upset, I shouldn’t have said what I said to him.”

“So why did you?”

Because Eddie was more like Danny than he’d ever thought. Not that he was going to tell Frank that. In fact, he didn’t say anything at all in answer to the question.

Frank let out a sigh.

“You have a son, right?

“Christopher.”

“How is he doing?”

Eddie shrugged.

“There was a rough patch over the holidays,” he said. “He’s doing better now.”

“I’m glad.” Frank glanced down at his notes. “You said you transferred jobs because you were worried about him? His well being?”

That wasn’t why, said a voice in his head that sounded remarkably similar to Buck.

“Is that a bad thing now?” he asked instead.

“No, not at all.” Frank shook his head. “I just wonder if you ever worry about your own well-being, is all. You seem to be a man who focuses on other people’s pain, but don’t spend a lot of time facing your own.”

“There was this drill sergeant during boot camp. He used to say that pain was just weakness leaving the body.”

“You think pain is weakness?”

That wasn’t what Eddie said, but-

“It can be.”

“If you give in to it,” Frank countered. Eddie scoffed. “You can’t put all your feelings in a box. At some point it’s gonna blow open.”

“And take me with it.”

“You and anyone else around you.”

(“I don’t need to talk to you about doing what’s best for my son!”)

Eddie cringed a little as the memory came to him unbidden. That wasn’t even the box blowing open, he knew. That was just Buck poking at the box enough that the lid came loose.

“So what do I do?” Eddie asked the therapist. “Sit here and just unload on you about every bad thing that’s happened to me? ‘Cause that could take a while.”

“Let’s take a shortcut,” Frank suggested. “Maybe you should talk about your pain with someone who shares it. Think about that first trauma and then talk to someone who can understand exactly what you’ve been to.”

His first trauma? Eddie wasn’t certain what his “first” trauma would even be.

The last couple of weeks had sucked, in Austin’s opinion. For someone perfectly content with only himself for company, he’d gotten distressingly reliant on Daniel’s presence. Or maybe reliant wasn’t the right word, maybe it was better to say that he’d grown around Daniel’s presence like a tree growing around a bicycle – edaphoecotropism, according to the youngest Buckley during one of his info dump rambles – and now that it was gone, Austin wasn’t sure what to do with himself.

Twice, now, he’d made dinner for two because he was expecting Daniel to just walk through his door. There was a sweater currently draped across the back of his couch and had been for days. There was a sharing size bag of Skittles in Austin’s pantry, not yet opened.

The thing was, this was inevitable. The thing was, Austin had known how this was going to go the moment he realized how Daniel felt about him. The thing was, Austin should have cut the cord ages ago and saved them all the pain.

He’d ignored it, instead. He got the impression Daniel had been ignoring it, too, the pair of them pretending that the elephant in the room didn’t exist as long as they didn’t draw attention to it. But they were never going to be able to ignore it forever. It was going to come out eventually. It was always going to end like this eventually.

Austin couldn’t give Daniel what he wanted, what he needed, not all of it. Some of it, maybe, sure, but that was simply biology. That wasn’t all that different from masturbation, just with another person and far more hassle. But the other parts, whatever Cynthia got from Emmett, whatever Ethan got from Irene, whatever their parents got from each other, Austin couldn’t give to Daniel. Daniel knew that. And so, the moment attention was brought to the elephant, everything fell apart.

Quest failed, party broken. Print off a new sheet, reroll your character, and try again.

The last couple of weeks had sucked. Today was shaping up to suck even more. The other day, they’d had a call out to a fish market involving a diver suffering from decompression sickness bad enough to take out a lung. Somehow that meant they ended up with a ton of fish, and the captain decided to make sushi for lunch.

Austin, wanting no part of that, got a burger through UberEats. It was while he was getting his order, standing in the open door to the truck bay, that his day got significantly worse. Because Chimney Han walked in like he hadn’t done anything wrong in his life. Or, on second thought, maybe Austin’s day got significantly better.

He’d known Maddie was back in the city, because Cynthia said she and Buck picked up her car. It was a conversation that Austin had cut short, much like all his conversations with his siblings since that night at the bar, but he got enough details to know she’d broken up with Chimney and was staying with Daniel.

Chimney was here, now, and Austin’s burger could wait. He slipped past the man, who was being warmly greeted by Ravi, and to the locker room. The burger got tucked away in his locker and then he went in search of the tools he needed. No one questioned it, because he was the engineer and it was normal for him to be messing with the tools.

The paramedic’s car was sitting a bit back from the station, the parking lot filled as it was by those belonging to the people on duty. Which was convenient, because it made it less likely that someone would look outside and notice what he was doing. He still did it as quickly as he could, and was soon putting everything he’d grabbed back in their proper place before retrieving his food.

(When Austin was a kid, he’d hover around his father and grandfather whenever they did anything with their cars, watching and then helping. He knew his way around a car engine, and had done his best to keep Daniel’s shitty used one on the road. He knew how to fix them, and he knew how to break them.)

The paramedic was sitting at the dining table upstairs by this point, eating the godawful sushi with the others that made up the captain’s little group of favorites. There was tension between Chimney and Buck, Austin could see that well enough, more than had regrown between Buck and the captain. It would probably be worse if Buck knew what Chimney had done before running off. Austin didn’t claim a seat there, instead opting for one of the smaller tables off to the side.

The fresh meat, Lucy and Jonah, were at the kitchen island when Austin went to get ketchup from the fridge. He maybe took a bit of time grabbing it so he could listen in for gossip. Not that there was anything interesting to hear, beyond Lucy being underwhelmed by Chimney and Jonah realizing that his time at the 118 was going to be shorter than promised. Because the captain was going to let Chimney back, even though he’d been gone for months without leave, and they all knew it.

He wanted to tell Daniel, and he choked the urge down with ketchup-smothered fries.

“You’ve gotten so big.” Daniel took Jee-Yun out of Maddie’s arms almost as soon as Maddie got through the door. “There’re presents for you to unwrap.”

Chimney had dropped her off. The handover happened downstairs in the parking lot, which was much preferable to Chimney stepping foot in Daniel’s apartment again. If he did, Daniel couldn’t promise to be a polite host.

“Presents?” Maddie asked as she sat down on the couch.

Her bedding was neatly folded up and siting at the end. It was joined now by a couple bags of her clothes, while her toiletries were now populating the bathroom. Daniel didn’t mind, and was in fact wondering whether or not it was sensible to get an entire crib for Jee-Yun or just use a playpen for any overnights. If he had it his way, Buck and Maddie and Jee-Yun would all be living with him full time. Eddie and Chris, too, obviously.

(If he had it his way, Austin would be there, too.)

“Yeah!” Daniel went to the kitchen to dig out something for his niece to snack on. Buck bought a variety while they were out getting Maddie’s car. “We missed Christmas and her birthday, Mads. We had to make up for it.”

“Do I want to know how many there are?”

“Not that many.” He shrugged. “Okay, what d’ya want, baby girl? We’ve got peaches and we’ve got mangos.”

She made grabby hands at the small cups of diced peaches sitting on the shelf of the fridge, so Daniel took one out and ripped off the foil covering. He held it for her to take what she wanted while he talked to his sister.

“Anything you want to do today?” he asked Maddie. “Take her to the park? Zoo trip?”

“What’s going on with you and Austin?”

Yeah, no, screw that question. And screw whoever, probably Buck, told Maddie whatever had her asking.

“Trip to the park it is,” Daniel said, pretending like his sister hadn’t said anything. “There’s one not too far away, we could walk there if you wanna brave downtown.”

“Dan.”

“Maddie.”

His sister huffed and rolled her eyes.

“In December, it was nothing but, ‘look at this jacket Austin bought me, isn’t it so cool?’” Maddie dropped her voice to properly mock him. “Now you’re refusing to even go to his sister’s.”

“She lives in suburban hell, it gives me hives,” he argued. “And she scares me a little.”

“Most of LA county is suburban hell,” she pointed out. “Stop trying to avoid the subject. What happened?”

Daniel sighed, then hooked a chair with his foot to pull it out enough to sit down in. He sat the cup of diced peaches on the table for Jee-Yun to get at.

“I told him I loved him.”

There was silence from his sister. Then she crossed the room to yank out a chair and sat down as well, letting out a little,

“Oh.”

“Yeah.”

“So did he… Did he react badly?”

“Dunno.” He shrugged at her. “I left before he could.”

“You left.”

“I ran away, like a proper Buckley,” he said. “I was drunk, I just kinda… Blurted it out. Didn’t even realize I’d said it for a moment.”

“And you just left, and haven’t spoken to him since.”

“Why would I? I already know what he’s going to say,” Daniel pointed out. “Sorry Dan, I don’t feel that way for you and knowing how you feel is making me very uncomfortable. Let’s not be friends, since you couldn’t even do that right.”

“You don’t know-”

“Oh, cut the crap, Maddie. You’re happy I’m not gonna be fake-dating him anymore, I’m happy you and Chimney are broken up.”

“I’m not… I wouldn’t say I was happy. I didn’t want you getting hurt.”

“I was always going to get hurt.” Daniel shrugged. “C’mon, I’m not that stupid. I knew how this was gonna go, that’s why I didn’t say anything to him.”

Maddie was looking sad, now. Maddie was looking sad at him. It was making Daniel bristle.

“Seriously, c’mon, let’s go to the park,” he said. “We can do that and then, I don’t know. Load Jee up with sugar before handing her back to Chimney.”

His sister sighed, and then stood.

“I’ll grab her diaper bag.”

Chapter 24

On most calls, Austin found himself without much to do. Unless it was something big, he was mostly just stuck twiddling his thumbs. A lot of times, the captain seemed to forget he existed entirely and would send someone out to grab a tool instead of radioing for him to bring it to them.

It left him feeling like a glorified chauffeur sometimes. This captain was worse about it than some of the others he’d served under in the two decades he’d been a firefighter. The last one spent his tenure acting like he’d already hit retirement, and he would hang back at the station more often than not. Which meant that Austin, since an engineer was a higher rank than the run-of-the-mill firefighters and paramedics, found himself in charge of a few scenes.

Technically speaking, he and Javier should’ve been the first ones in line to be the interim captain. Neither of them had complained when Chimney was chosen for the role in a feat of nepotism, and then they both proceeded to ignore him when it went to the man’s head.

Austin had started to bring a book with him to most calls. He knew Buck thought he took naps, and sometimes he did, but he spent most of the time while the other were busy being firefighters just reading. This time it was the Hobbit, the copy he’d gotten for Christmas. He’d read it once before, and had decided to read it again. It was a decision that, if anyone asked, was completely unrelated to any recent events in his life.

This call, he especially didn’t mind not being involved in. Some idiot with too much money apparently had a collection of tarantulas, and didn’t do a good job of keeping them contained. They’d been called after cops did a wellness check after the owner went a couple days without hearing from the house sitter. Details were sparse, but both cops were acting like they’d seen Shelob in person.

The captain came hurrying out with much the same expression.

“Jones!” he called out. “They need the gurney.”

Closing his book with a sigh, Austin got out and went to the ambulance. The captain didn’t even help get the gurney out, too busy talking to dispatch over the radio. It was lucky Austin could manage it on his own.

The patient was inside a giant webbed cocoon that someone had cut open. That was the first thing Austin saw when he got the gurney to the paramedics. That and the growing welts that the patient had from spider bites. There were a few spiders, far too large, unmoving on the bed. Too few to have built the cocoon.

He and Jonah transferred the patient to the gurney using a sheet. Then he stepped back, allowing the paramedics to get him set up with IV, a lifepak, and a steroid injection. They also snipped the guy’s clothes off him, because modesty wasn’t a priority when there was the possibility of tarantulas hitching a ride.

Lucy and Jonah wheeled the guy out of the house to the ambulance, so Austin was free to stand around and do nothing. There really wasn’t much for him to do, there wasn’t even anything entertaining to listen in on except for Hen taunting the captain about some arachnophobia. It was something Austin found himself filing away to tell Daniel about later, and his mood plummeted when he realized it.

Thankfully, there was a distraction from that rabbit hole – God, he just couldn’t help it, could he? – in the form of Jonah announcing that the patient was coding.

“What’s happening?” Hen asked as she rushed to the patient’s side. “He was stable.”

“Pressure’s bottoming out,” Jonah said. “Might be a reaction to the steroids.”

“Wouldn’t that increase the pressure?”

“Unless he was on some sort of prescription meds.”

And that was why they asked about medical history.

They got the guy stable again and then loaded him up. The Hobbit had to wait until they were back at the station and Austin made sure the CO2 on the engine was restocked, after Lucy used it to subdue some of the eight-legged miscreants. The chapter Austin was on, “Flies and Spiders,” was far too on the nose.

“I told him I had to see an apartment,” Maddie groaned as she slid farther into the cushions of the couch.

“Do you?” Buck asked, setting out food on the dining table.

They’d ordered take out again, this time it was Chinese. He promised himself that next time, he would actually cook dinner for them. Or maybe when they weren’t all suffering from relationship troubles.

Seriously, all three of them? At the same time?

“No.” Maddie covered her face with her hands, muffling her words a little. “I just couldn’t stick around.”

“Why?” Danny asked. “Gimme a fortune cookie, squirt.”

“You don’t even eat them,” Buck complained, but he still tossed one to his brother.

“I’ve got you as a trash disposal.” Danny cracked open the cookie after he tore off the plastic wrapping, dropping the two halves onto the table top. His face twisted into a sneer as he read it aloud. “Your love life will be happy and harmonious.”

Maddie dropped her hands with a huff of laughter.

“The closest to that right now is Buck,” she said. “And I haven’t laid eyes on Eddie since I got back, so that’s not saying much.”

“Yeah, yeah, we’re gonna die spinsters,” Danny said. “Back to you running away from Chimney.”

Their sister let out a sigh.

“He asked if I’d given Jee a bath.”

Buck paused in the middle of digging out forks for the three of them and met his brother’s eye across the table as they both cringed.

The last time Maddie gave Jee-Yun a bath, she’d fallen asleep for a moment and Jee-Yun slipped beneath the water. Not for very long, but it was enough to send Maddie spiraling. It was enough to send Maddie running for Boston, convinced that was better and safer for her daughter.

“When is he gonna drop Jee off again?” Buck was desperate to steer the topic to something safer. “I don’t think I’ve spent any time with her since the three of you got back.”

“Oh, I’m gonna be picking her up, I think.” Maddie got up to join them at the table. “Please tell me- Yay.”

She took the thing of hot mustard that Danny held out to her, smiling in thanks.

“Why are you picking her up?” Buck asked as he sat down. “Is Chimney afraid of running into Danny or something?”

“No, he’s having car issues.”

“Car issues? He didn’t mention anything when he visited the station.”

Their brother’s head popped up from where he’d been stuffing his mouth full of noodles. His cheeks bulged a little before he swallowed it down in a way that had Buck worrying he’d be earning his own tracheotomy scar.

“Yeah,” Maddie said. “Something must’ve punctured his radiator hose, ‘cause there was a leak and everything overheated. There was other stuff, too, but that was the biggest.”

Danny stared at them for a bit, head tilted and mouth slightly open. Then he threw back his head and laughed. It was loud and filled the apartment. His body shook with it, shoulders heaving.

Maddie’s lips thinned in obvious disapproval.

“I know you don’t like him,” she said, once the laughter petered out a bit. “But the repairs are going to be expensive, if he doesn’t end up just buying a new car. That’s pretty much the rest of whatever he still has of his savings.”

“Yeah, I’m- I’m sure." Danny laughed a bit more. “Uh. Maybe Austin can take a look at it, he’s good with cars.”

It was Buck’s turn to stare. Did his brother just imply…?

“Anyway,” Danny said. “Did you see Albert?”

“I saw Albert’s mustache,” Maddie said, her lip curling a little.

“Yeah, the bullying isn’t working. Flowers was talking about pinning him down and shaving it off ourselves.”

“It would be for his own good,” she agreed. “Like how we would shove Buck into the bath as a kid when he started looking like Pig-Pen.”

Buck blew a raspberry at both of them, but didn’t let his suspicions go. Unfortunately, he couldn’t ask Danny, not now, not with Maddie staying with them. There wasn’t a good opportunity to catch him alone, either.

So, on his next shift, he made a point of hunting down Austin. It was a difficult endeavor, because the man seemed to just meld into the station walls when no one was looking right at him. Buck looked for him from top to bottom, and when he finally did find the man, it wasn’t even in the station proper. It was in the parking lot. Not the station’s, but the parking lot belonging to the taco place next door, with its back side marked out by a graffiti covered wall separating it from the adjoining property. There were a line of trees along the wall, each in little square plots of soil, that might’ve been flourishing entirely out of spite.

Austin was in the back corner where the wall met the station. There were concrete bollards, and he’d clearly used one of them so he could perch on the lowest branch. He was reading a book, the cover green with gold letters that reflected the sunlight.

For a moment, Buck found himself incapable of anything but staring at the man.

“Is this where you go all the time!?”

Austin startled where he sat. His head snapped up, and he seemed utterly confused by Buck’s presence.

“Not all the time. Sometimes it’s raining,” he answered after a moment. “It’s no farther from the trucks than the loft.”

He was probably right. Buck stared at the station wall – someone had spray painted a bright blue butterfly over the red brick, right next to a shadow-like silhouette of a figure in a wide brimmed hat; it gave him the creeps – and tried to guess where the loft was in relation to them. If he judged it right, they were actually closer right now.

“I can’t believe everyone wonders where in the station you disappear to, and the answer is that you don’t disappear in the station at all.”

“You completed the quest, congratulations.” Austin’s tone didn’t change at all. The sarcasm was still painfully clear. “What did you need?”

“Did you mess with Chimney’s car?”

“Why would I do that, Buck?”

“That’s what I’d like to know!”

The engineer shrugged and then went back to his book. Buck was tempted to throw something at him, or maybe push him out of the tree. He didn’t, though. Instead, he threw up his arms and stomped back into the station.

Daniel was slouched on the couch in the station’s den area, a bowl of chips balanced precariously on his stomach. The TV was set to Channel 8 news – if someone told his teenage self that he would be willingly watching the news as often as he did now, he’d ask what drugs they were on and where they got ‘em – covering the jumper on the recycling center that the 118 rescued. Normally, Austin would’ve told him the juiciest of the details, but his only texts had been from Buck. And Buck, beyond the excitement that always accompanied saving someone, did not share the juicy details.

When Albert collapsed beside him, it took a single glance for Daniel to realize the probie’s mood wasn’t much better than his own. He silently held the bowl out and Albert, after a second, grabbed a handful.

“Thanks.”

“De nada,” Daniel mumbled. “What’s got you so down?”

Albert shrugged as he munched on the chips.

“I don’t know,” he said. “It’s nothing.”

Daniel sighed. Then he reached over and flicked Albert in the head.

“Ow,” the kid whined, rubbing at the spot.

“I’ll do it again if you don’t give me a better answer.”

“Is this how you treat Buck?” Albert asked, and then cursed as Daniel did as promised. “Seriously, that hurts. What are your fingernails made of?”

“Keratin.” Daniel grinned at the probie’s unamused glower. “Seriously, what’s up?”

“It’s stu- Stop!” Albert leaned away to avoid the flick this time. “Fine, jeeze, it’s just… It’s the job.”

“The job.”

“Yeah. I guess I had this idea of what it would be like, you know?” Albert said. “How rewarding it would be to help people for a living. Like you and Howie do.”

Daniel tossed a chip into his mouth and chewed as he debated how to answer. He was reminded of Buck sitting across from him at their kitchen table, after the guy on the roller coaster who chose to let go and fall. He didn’t have a better answer at the time than the one he gave his brother, when he was struggling with the same thing, and didn’t really have a better one now.

“I do it for the paycheck,” he said. “You’re not finding the job rewarding, or whatever?”

“I feel like I spend all day trapped inside of people’s worst nightmares,” Albert told him, vaguely gesturing with his hands. “We catch them at the height of their fear and pain and then, what? Just go home? Pack it away?”

“That or kill yourself.”

Albert stared at him, horrorstruck. Daniel just shrugged and ate another chip.

“Things like that are why people call you a psychopath.”

“It’s antisocial personality disorder, thank you,” Daniel primly corrected. “But it’s true. If you’re gonna do this job, you either learn how to pack it away or you burn out. You help them, then you move on with your life.”

“That’s- That’s-” Albert struggled to come up with the word. “Callous. That’s so callous sounding.”

“Yeah, but I don’t need liquor and pills to sleep.” He thought he was coming out ahead there. “My last captain, in El Paso, he kept a bottle of mezcal in his desk. Some calls, we’d come back and he’d down a shot or two in his office. Some calls, we’d come back and the lieutenant would need to finish out the shift for him.”

“You’re really bad at this, Danny.”

Daniel sighed and set the bowl of chips aside so he could sit up straight.

“Look, what I’m saying is, if you don’t manage to not carry every person we meet on a call like baggage, you’re gonna collapse under the weight of ‘em,” he said. “If you don’t think you can do that, then you can just, y’know. Quit.”

“Quit,” Albert echoed, sounding dubious.

“It’s a job, Al, and it’s not for everyone. Like I said, I do it for the paycheck, not because I find it personally rewarding or whatever.” Not to say he didn’t find it rewarding, sometimes. Especially on calls he could put his paramedic training to use. “If it ain’t working out, move on. Find something else. Buck went through, like, a hundred different jobs before this one.”

Behind them, there was suddenly buzzing. It was vaguely electronic sounding. Daniel and Albert both turned to see what it was.

Flowers held a hair clipper in his hand, the source of the buzzing, like a weapon. Mendoza was right beside him. The pair loomed over the couch, while Nola stood a few paces away with her phone out.

“This is for your own good, man,” Flowers said. “That mustache has got to go. Jon, Dan, hold him down.”

Albert tried to run. He didn’t make it far.

Danny was trying to get out of talking about it.

Danny was not going to get out of talking about it.

“He sabotaged Chimney’s car!” Buck exclaimed, waving his arms around.

“You don’t know that,” his brother argued, waving it away.

Literally. He waved his hand in the air like he was trying to get rid of an annoying fly.

“He practically admitted to it.”

Danny frowned.

“Did he say it?” he asked.

“Well, no. But when I asked him if he did, he asked why he would, and then stopped talking,” Buck said. “I’ve spent enough time with him, Danny. That’s almost a confession.”

“You just told me he didn’t say it.” His brother rolled his eyes. “Silence isn’t a confession. We have the right to it, remember?”

“You think he did."

Buck got silence in return. Because he was right, Danny did think Austin was the one responsible for Chimney’s car troubles. That was the reason Buck was so certain it was true. There was obviously a reason Austin would mess with Chimney’s car, and Danny knew what the reason was.

“Why, though?” Buck wasn’t really expecting an answer, he was just talking out loud while he paced. “I mean, he doesn’t do this kind of thing for no reason. He doesn’t do this kind of thing at all, normally it’s small shit. He somehow managed to get itching powder in my uniform, and Ravi’s, for saying the q-word. He tormented Eddie for his last couple of shifts at the station, like ketchup packets in his boots and vinegar in his coffee. That was because he-”

Austin had tormented Eddie because he’d hurt Buck. He’d escalated from his normal, the itching powder, because of it. If he’d escalated all the way to vandalism…

“Chimney did something to you,” Buck said, confident that was it. “That’s why Austin went so far.”

“Chimney just got back and I haven’t even laid eyes on the man.” Danny was leaning against the counter, looking casual. Too casual. The kind of casual he pulled when he was lying about something or doing something that was lying adjacent. “When would he have had the time?”

That was a good point, and it seemed like Austin had started things the moment he saw Chimney. But it didn’t cancel out Buck’s point, either. It just meant whatever Chimney did, it must’ve been before he left to chase after Maddie. So what could-

Buck froze in place as his mind immediately went to the obvious answer.

“He punched you!?”

“Christ,” Danny sighed, and then pushed himself off the counter to head for the fridge. “I shoulda stuck you in a worse school, maybe make you watch Fox News growing up.”

“No.” Buck stomped over and slammed the fridge closed. “We’re not joking about this. Chimney punched you. And then you lied to me about it.”

“It was just a punch, squirt. I’ve been punched before. Harder, even.”

“That’s not-”

Buck’s phone interrupted him, the default marimba ringtone cutting through the air. He’d kept meaning to change it out, maybe give everyone their own so he knew who he could ignore and who he needed to sprint to his phone for. This one would’ve been the latter.

“Hey, Chris. What’s-”

“Dad!” Chris was shouting on the other end. “Dad! Dad!”

“Chris?”

The change in his tone had Danny straightening up in concern.

“Something’s wrong with Dad!”

“Okay, I’m on my way. I’ll be right there, I promise.”

“I’ll drive,” Danny said, already hopping into his shoes as he scooped up his keys.

Buck didn’t argue.

Chapter 25

The last time Daniel drove this fast, in anything but a fire engine, was when he was racing to Zuma Beach after the black out. He didn’t know what was going on, but he knew something was wrong with Eddie. He knew Chris was upset. He knew Buck was worried, even as he tried not to let Chris hear it over the phone.

When they got to Eddie’s, the car was still moving when Buck tore off the seat belt and went sprinting inside. Daniel parked and caught up with him to find him kneeling in front of a very distraught Chris in the hallway bend. They could see Eddie’s door from where they were and, if Eddie was in there, the silence on the other side was concerning.

“I keep calling him,” Chris was saying to Buck. “But he won’t answer.”

“Y- You just wait here,” Buck told him. “I’m gonna go get him, okay?”

“Okay.”

Daniel followed Buck as he crept down the hall to Eddie’s door. Buck took the knob gingerly, like he was scared of what he would find. It didn’t turn under his hand.

“Eddie? Eddie, it’s me,” Buck hesitantly called through the door. “Can I come in?”

There was nothing but silence to answer Buck’s question. The fear was plain to see on his face, but he managed to mask most of it when he looked back to check on Chris. Daniel was horrifyingly reminded of when he went to check on Maddie during the blackout, those long moments between him knocking on the door and her answering. The knowledge of what she’d been planning to do the entire time he talked to her that night.

“Alright, Eddie. I- I’m gonna come in, okay?” Buck said, voice raised. “Stay away from the door.”

The lock on the door was flimsy. With a bit of force, the door went flying open. It showed them the mess that was Eddie’s bedroom.

It was, in a word, destroyed. Holes in the wall, the curtains half yanked down, picture frames shattered. But there was no sign of Eddie himself, until they heard the sobbing breaths. He was on the other side of the bed, huddled against it with the bat that Daniel got him for Christmas, one year.

Daniel hesitated, but he let Buck handle him. Instead, he slowly crept out and went to Chris. The kid was standing where they’d left him, staring at what he could see with wide-eyed concern until Daniel blocked his view.

He refused, at first, when Daniel tried to coax him to his room. It wasn’t until he was assured that Daniel and Buck were going to take care of his dad that he finally went. Thankfully, Buck got Eddie out of his room after the door to Chris’ was closed. Daniel didn’t think Chris should see his father in the state he was in now, for both their sakes.

Buck got Eddie moved to the dining room table, the bat conspicuously absent from Eddie’s hands, and then went to check on Christopher for himself. Daniel took the time to take in how bad Eddie looked. The color of his shirt was soaked, either from sweat or tears, His face was red and puffy. And he was still drawn up around himself, arms wrapped around his middle like he was protecting the soft and vulnerable parts. His knuckles were scraped and bleeding, which meant the bat hadn’t made all the holes in his bedroom wall.

That Eddie’s mental health had not been the best since the shooting was not a secret. Ever since that first panic attack, Buck made his worry known. Something pushed Eddie to and then over that cliff’s edge, the only question was what.

“Is Chris okay?” Eddie asked, because of course that his was first question.

“He’s worried,” Daniel answered, from where he was leaning against the doorway to the wall. “Maybe a bit scared.”

“He’s not the only one,” Buck commented as he came back, taking the seat across from his boyfriend.

“I’m sorry,” Eddie sobbed. “I didn’t mean to, I just… Lost it.”

Daniel figured that was a bit of an understatement.

“Why?” Buck asked. “What happened?”

“Frank told me to talk to someone who could understand exactly what I’ve been through.” Eddie managed to muster up a mocking tone to the words, despite how broken he was sounding in the moment. Daniel was a little bit proud of him. “So I thought, I’d reach out to some of the people I served with. The, uh. The other ones in the helicopter.”

Eddie had a Silver Star, hidden away somewhere in his closet. He’d earned after a helicopter he was in was shot down and he managed to save (almost) everyone. He didn’t like talking about it much.

“I didn’t know you still kept in touch with them,” Buck said.

“I haven’t talked to any of them since I moved out here.” Eddie shook his head. “I pulled four people out of that chopper alive. They’re all dead now. Chief didn’t even make it stateside. Car crash, OD. Mills shot herself last August…”

Daniel grimaced, and then slipped away to get the couch set up for Eddie. He fetched the spare pillow and blanket that were basically his, tossing them on the couch. Eddie didn’t fight it much when Buck guided him to the couch, and didn’t argue at all when Buck shucked off the jeans he was wearing to squeeze into the small space with him.

It did not take very long for Eddie to fall asleep.

The same could not be said for Buck and Daniel.

The next morning, Daniel took responsibility of getting Christopher up, fed, and out the door. By the time he got back, Buck had bullied Eddie into proper clothing and they shuttled him off to Pepa to be properly looked after. Abuela would have been their first choice, but she’d moved to El Paso in the months since Christmas. Buck was supposed to have a shift, but he called in the night before. Instead, he and Daniel tackled the bedroom.

It didn’t take much, righting the curtains and the lamps, shaking out the glass from the bed sheets, throwing away what couldn’t be salvaged. They worked silently, which told Daniel how worried Buck was about everything. Because Buck did not do silent.

“I think that’s everything we can do now,” Daniel declared, nudging a trash bag with his foot. “The door and the walls are gonna require a trip to the store.”

“There goes the deposit,” Buck remarked. Then, “Chimney punched you.”

“Aw, Christ.” Daniel rubbed at his eyes. “It was just a punch, squirt. It’s not that serious.”

“Yes, it is!” Buck snapped. “He hurt you! He- If I had known, I wouldn’t have-”

He cut himself off, but Daniel already knew what the rest of that sentence was.

“You wouldn’t have told him that Maddie was in Boston?” He shrugged at Buck’s wide-eyed, startled expression. “I ran into him while we were there.”

His brother lunged forward, hands already reaching out to take stock of any injuries, before remembering that the Boston trip was months ago.

“I did the punching that time,” he said.

“Good.” Buck sounded a bit vicious as he spat out the word. His next words, however, carried uncertainty. “So, you knew, and you- You’re not… You weren’t angry?”

Daniel sighed, threw an arm around Buck’s shoulders, and then used the element of surprise to put him in a headlock.

“No.” He knuckled Buck’s head before letting him go. “You’ve got too much heart, you were always gonna break.”

“Thanks?” Buck screwed up his face as he rubbed his head. “I think?”

“But I thought you woulda last ‘til at least New Years. Kinda disappointed in ya.”

Buck huffed and shoved at him, but the punch seemed to have been forgotten.

The shift that Buck had missed was Chimney’s first shift back at the station. Considering Buck was very certain of what he would do when he saw the man next, it was definitely a good thing it didn’t happen at work. It did mean he missed a woman literally fall into Lucy’s arms, however, which everyone was raving about. The video clip on the news showed Lucy hanging half out a window when she caught the woman.

It was exceedingly lucky.

Also exceedingly lucky was the bounty of excuses Buck had available to him so he could go out without raising any suspicions. They’d swung by the apartment the day before, after cleaning up Eddie’s bedroom, so Buck had his jeep. He took Chris to school while Danny stayed back with Eddie, and then made a detour afterwards.

The blazing fury from before, from when he first realized what Chimney had done, flared up again. It surged through his veins, boiling his blood, as he stormed up the flight of stairs and then to Chimney’s door. He pounded on it with his fist, and then shoved past Albert when he answered the door.

Chimney was frozen mid-gesture, staring at him with his mouth agape. Maddie – and Buck had forgotten that Maddie would be here still, having spent the night with Jee at Chimney’s instead of theirs – looked just as shocked, a maybe a little bit frightened, as she unconsciously moved to put the kitchen island between herself and Buck. Not that Buck really noticed, focused on Chimney as he was.

“Buck,” Chimney started. “What the-”

Buck interrupted him, viciously and violently. He slammed Chimney into the fridge with enough force that it rocked slightly with the impact, before pressing an arm against his throat. Not enough to choke him out completely, but enough that the threat was there.

“I don’t care who you are,” Buck snarled in the man’s face, getting close and personal. “I don’t care why. If you ever, ever, lay a hand on my brother again, I will personally make sure it is the last thing you do.”

Suitably certain that the message was received and understood, Buck stepped back and let the man go. Chimney slid a bit against the fridge, shaken and breathing heavily. With a final glare at the paramedic, and a glance at both Albert and Maddie, Buck exited the apartment in much the same way he entered. The stunned silence he left behind was broken by Maddie’s hoarfrost covered words.

“Howard. What did he mean, lay a hand on Daniel again?”

Buck didn’t hear whatever answer Chimney gave her before the door closed.

It seemed to Maddie that the past year – hell, the past two years – had been exceptionally difficult. She’d been so excited when she found out she was pregnant, only for that excitement to be eclipsed by society shutting down for long months. Afterwards was a mess of worry and fear. There was Daniel and what had been done to him, there were her parents and her forced realization that Daniel had not been exaggerating this entire time, there was the utter darkness that followed Jee-Yun’s birth.

And now her daughter was in the ER.

Maybe, she thought as she paced the waiting room, it was all a sign that she should not have been a mother. From the moment she brought Jee-Yun into this world, it felt like Maddie had simply made things worse whenever she so much as touched her daughter. But she had thrived in Maddie’s absence.

Maddie shook her head, trying to dislodge those thoughts. She was past that. She’d spent months with professionals to help her get past it. Maybe she should take a page from her brothers’ book and pick out a regular therapist.

Thinking about her brothers had her pulling out her phone. They were both on shift right now, but she knew-

“What’s wrong?” was Daniel’s immediate question, voice strained with immediate worry.

“Jee’s sick,” she sighed into her phone. “She was tired, didn’t eat much, had a fever. I managed to get a late appointment with a doctor.”

“What did they say?”

“He thinks it’s intussusception.” Part of the intestine sliding into an adjacent section. Like a telescope. “They’re running tests right now.”

“Christ,” her brother swore.

“I just keep running through all the possible causes in my head,” she told him. “And I keep coming back to the same one. A tumor.”

“Maddie.”

“This was selfish of me, wasn’t it? Having a baby with our family history.”

“Do you remember, years ago, when I was freaking out because I thought it was cancer?”

Maddie sighed.

“Yes.”

“And what did it turn out to be?”

“Dan…”

“What did it turn out to be?” he asked again.

“Mono,” she sighed.

“That’s right, it was just mono. She probably just has mono-”

“She’s a year old, Dan,” Maddie said. “She does not have the kissing disease.”

“The mono’s a metaphor, Mads. Keep up.”

She rolled her eyes.

“Where’s Chimney? Has he not been talking you down?”

“Howard,” she said the name with acid dripping from her lips, “is at work.”

“Jesus, Maddie.”

“He punched you! I’m not in any hurry to be around him right now.”

Her baby brother, that Golden Retriever of a man, storming into Chimney’s apartment to assault him had been terrifying. But all that terror boiled away when it became clear it was because Chimney had hurt Daniel. Chimney had reluctantly, and shakily, given the story, including his encounter with Daniel in Boston. All of it had served to incense Maddie further.

“Yeah, he punched me. It’s fine, I’m over it. We’ve moved on.”

“It is not fine, Daniel. He punched you because of me-”

“He punched me ‘cause he’s a bitch that couldn’t handle the truth, not because of anything you did,” Daniel said. “You need to tell him his kid’s sick. I’d want someone to tell me if Buck was. Christ, was that empathy? Gross.”

“Fine, you’re right,” she admitted. “I’ll call him.”

Which she did after assuring Daniel that she didn’t need him or Buck there. Having either of them around Chimney right now would just be asking for trouble, anyway.

“Any word from Chimney or your sister?”

Lucy’s question had Buck looking up from where he’d been typing away at his phone. Things were still awkward between them, and Buck was not in any hurry to cross that divide. He didn’t know what it was that he did to have Lucy kissing him, but he must’ve done something. Right? So he’d kept a respectful distance from her to prevent him from making the mistake again.

(Copeland was trying to convince him that it wasn’t anything he was doing, necessarily. She wasn’t being very successful so far.)
