She received a warmer reception from him than Chimney had all day, though. Buck managed to freeze the paramedic out the entire shift, interacting with him only when the job demanded it. Everyone had noticed, he knew. It was near impossible not to when they were crammed into the engine and Buck simply ignored the man entirely. Bobby was sending the pair his patented Frown of Concern whenever they weren’t busy.

There’d been some relief mixed with Chimney’s anxious concern when he ran out of the station after Maddie’s call. He was no doubt just eager to get away from Buck.

“No. Still waiting on updates.” Buck hit send on his text to Danny. Lucy, he saw, was sat on the couch, tablet in hand. She was watching the clip of herself catching the woman. “That was an awesome catch. But, and I say this with experience, maybe don’t let it go to your head.”

“It’s not.” Lucy scoffed. “She should have died. Nine times out of ten, I don’t catch her. Or I don’t see her, or I’m not fast enough, or I fall out with her.”

Buck stared at her.

“So you’re watching yourself save someone,” he said. “Because you’re obsessing over how you might not have saved her?”

“I’m trying to figure out how I should have saved her in a way that doesn’t involve luck. So I’m ready for the next time.”

“How long have you been doing this job?”

“About ten years.”

“And you’re still…?” Buck shook his head. “She didn’t die. Take the win.”

“Seriously?” was Lucy’s incredulous response. “That’s the best you can come up with? Accept it was a lucky break and move on?”

“Yeah.” He shrugged at her. “’Cause that’s what it was.”

“Helpful,” she groused. “So what’s going on with you and Chimney? Whatever it is, it’s gotta be awkward. He’s your coworker and your sister’s boyfriend.”

“And his brother works with my brother,” Buck said, then frowned. “And with Jonah now.”

Bobby, to make up for giving him a permanent spot only to immediately yank it away from him, got Jonah a position at the 133. He was replacing Ryerson, who’d switched shifts. Buck was never all that close to him, or at least not as close as he was to Jon and Nola.

“This place needs a flowchart,” Lucy complained.

Buck grinned.

“My boyfriend works with Cap’s step-daughter,” he said.

He laughed when she groaned, and began to walk away. Her voice stopped him at the stairs, though.

“Hey, uh.” Lucy seemed uncomfortable. “I’m sorry. About the kiss.”

“It happens.” Buck shrugged when her face scrunched up at that. “Thanks, though.”

Eddie felt like he was dragging himself through the front door. Therapy before had felt like he was intentionally scraping at his insides with sandpaper. This time, after his moment, he walked out of Frank’s office feeling like he’d been eviscerated.

Buck and Danny were in his dining room, the former clearing the table of art supplies, the latter carelessly tilting his chair back. They both greeted him as he collapsed into a chair of his own.

“How’d it go?” Buck asked.

“The usual,” Eddie answered. “Crack myself open for fifty minutes, then walk out feeling like I was hit by a bus.”

“I’m familiar with the feeling. It’s why I go to Austin’s afterwards,” Danny said. Then he frowned. “Went. Went to Austin’s.”

“Pretty sure that means it’s working,” Buck said as he eyed his brother in obvious concern.

“Wish I could hurry up and get to the part where I feel better. Where’s Chris?”

“His room, finishing homework.”

Eddie reached out and grabbed a stack of drawings from the table. The top one had a very cartoonish looking heart with a smiling face. The other two were far more anatomically correct. One had the parts labeled in a neat scrawl. The other was a bit more gruesome, what with the geysers of blood spurting out of the aorta and carotid arteries. It also had a smiley face on it, making it seem far too gleeful.

“Did Chris draw this?”

“Uh, that one.” Buck pointed at the one with the labels, then the cartoonish one. “That one’s mine. I misunderstood the assignment.”

Which meant…

Eddie lifted up the drawing of the bloody heart and stared at Danny. Danny just shrugged.

“Do you ever think it’s worth it?” Eddie tossed the drawings back onto the table. “Not the therapy, but the job that makes us need the therapy. What I put myself through, my kid through. Do you ever wonder what’s the point of it all?”

Danny glanced at Buck, rolled his eyes, and then let his chair fall forward again.

“Pretty sure the point is saving people,” he said as he stood up.

“For the moment,” Eddie scoffed.

“For the moment,” Danny agreed.

His and Buck’s phone chimed at the same time, two separate tones. They pulled them out and let out identical sounding sighs of relief.

“Jee’s fine,” Buck told Eddie as he typed. Eddie assumed it was a reply. “She’ll get to go home after a few hours observation.”

When Buck sent the text, but Danny’s phone didn’t go off again, it probably should have stuck out to Eddie. But Eddie, honestly, just wanted to curl up somewhere and nap. Which was what he bullied Buck into doing with him.

A couple days later, Buck surprised Eddie and Chris with a trip to a place that offered horse therapy, with the director showing Chris around. Eddie assumed that was the surprise until Buck pointed out Charlie, the Munchausen by proxy kid, happy and healthy on a horse of his own. As messages went, it was not very subtle.

He appreciated it all the same.

Chapter 26

Chapter Notes

If you read the ficlets I post on my tumblr, part of this chapter will be very familiar to you.

Then

Buck wasn’t certain which was worse, the way his throat hurt or the utter boredom that came with being stuck in the hospital. Or, no, maybe it was the pureed mush the doctors were feeding him. Because, apparently, getting his throat cut open to keep from choking to death – on bread, of all things – came with the risk of… Choking to death on food later.

It’s not that he’d want to eat solid foods right now. Or, he did, but just drinking water felt like swallowing glass, so he was reluctant to find out what a burger would be like. It was that the mush might have been some of the worst stuff he’d eaten, and he’d eaten some godawful things in his time.

No. No, it was the boredom. The boredom had him here comparing the mush to everything else he could remember eating. Because there was nothing else to do! He had his phone, but he didn’t have his charger for it, so it was of little help.

He didn’t like feeling cooped up somewhere. It was why every road trip he’d taken with Danny involved numerous stops and side trips. He was an awful patient because of it, and he knew he was getting on the nurses’ nerves.

Thankfully, he had visitors. Not just Danny, either. Bobby came by to check on him, and to tell him he was on medical leave. Chimney swung by to tease him about almost dying on bread, and Hen was interested in the tracheotomy itself. She brought a card, too, that some of the others had signed, though they didn’t come see him in person.

Neither had Abby, but Buck understood that. She had her mom to worry about, and her shifts at dispatch. Buck wasn’t going to be in here long enough for her to stress herself out by trying to fit in a visit.

He did have an unexpected visitor, though. An extremely unexpected visitor.

Jones was… Forgettable, was a good word. He was there, but he might as well not have been. Buck didn’t think he’d ever seen the man interacting with anyone at the station, outside of what needed to be done on the job. Hell, honestly, Buck barely saw him at the station between calls.

He knew Jones was there, because he’d do maintenance on the engine and would show up when the bells went off. But most of the rest of the time, it was like he just melted into the woodwork or something.

So it was very much a surprise when, on his second full day since being admitted, Jones appeared in the doorway to his room. The man hung there awkwardly for a moment, as if he wasn’t quite sure what to do. Then he stepped fully into the room, though he still stuck close to the exit.

“Uh, h- hey.” Buck winced, hand going up to the hole in his throat. “Hey.”

“Hi.” Jones shifted his weight between his feet. “How are you-? I mean…”

His mouth pulled into a flat line.

“I’m, uh. Hanging in there.”

Buck raised his fist in a lame emphasis, and then let it drop to his lap again.

The fell into stilted silence. Buck didn’t know what to say, not really knowing the man and not being up for talking, for once in his life. Jones didn’t seem to be faring much better, standing uncomfortable by the door. His face wasn’t giving much of anything away, though, so Buck couldn’t really say what he was thinking.

“Right,” Jones finally said. “Get better soon, man.”

“Um. Thanks.”

Jones nodded and then left as awkwardly as he arrived, at a speed that might’ve constituted as fleeing.

Buck wished someone, he wished Danny, had been there to witness the entire exchange. Because he wasn’t quite sure he hadn’t hallucinated that entire exchange.

Now

Eddie didn’t think he would ever quite understand the people of Los Angeles. There was a level of weirdness to them, a brand of insanity, that was wildly different from the people of El Paso. The entire LA population seemed high strung, and prone to ending up in situations that no reasonable person would ever find themselves in.

Take, for instance, the call they just handled. A luxury influencer – someone whose job was apparently just to be rich, and Eddie still couldn’t believe that was an actual thing – was showing off her new house in Brentwood, and ended up getting injured in the sauna. Except that literally everything about the girl’s life was a lie. From her name to her money, not one thing she put online was true.

The scars to her face were definitely going to be real, thoughtful.

“She’s on her way to the burn unit now,” May updated him in the break room after.

“High price to pay for living a fake life,” Eddie commented as he shot Buck a text. It was the 118 who rescued her. “Was her regular life that bad?”

“Maybe she felt like she was missing out on a life she could have had,” May suggest. “If she’d been born someone else, or made different choices. Don’t you wonder about stuff like that?”

Eddie stared at her as he thought about the day he sent Buck fleeing from his life. As he thought about every moment missed with him over the years, as he thought about every moment missed with Chris. As he thought about how ruined his relationship with Shannon was by the end, the smoldering wreckage of a friendship with someone who would have been his best friend, if he’d never known Buck.

And then he let out a laugh loud enough to startle the both of them.

“No,” Eddie said, still laughing a little. “Not at all.”

“Well.” May was eyeing him warily, or maybe in concern. “Most people worry they’re not getting the full experience. You know, FOMO.”

“What in the world is fomo?”

“The fear of missing out.”

“You’re making it sound like a sickness,” he remarked.

She shrugged.

“I guess it kind of is.”

Maddie was having a crisis, the kind Buck really wished Danny was here for. She’d come back from dropping Jee-Yun off with Chimney, upset over Jee-Yun’s baby box. Or, no, upset over the evidence of everything she’d missed over the months she spent in Boston. Chimney apparently kept a photographic record of everything to show her later, which-

“It was so sweet,” Maddie said. “But I was looking at the pictures thinking I should’ve been there.”

Buck sighed and set down the fork he’d been using to eat his late lunch with. He’d decided, at the start of this conversation, not to comment on his sister being on speaking terms with Chimney again. Even if he was annoyed at how quickly she went from angry on Danny’s behalf to calling Chimney sweet, or at least something he’d done. Unlike him, she couldn’t get away with ignoring the man’s entire existence, what with having a daughter with the man and all.

“Look,” Buck said. “Even if you were here, living your normal life, chances are you probably would’ve missed some of those moments anyway. Right? You can’t be with her twenty-four/seven.”

“A few moments, sure, but every early milestone?” Maddie shook her head. “Only a bad mother misses every one.”

“You’re not a bad mother. You were in a bad place, but you’re better now. And Jee won’t ever remember you were gone.”

His sister scoffed, an unkind sound.

“How many things of yours did Dan miss?”

“Most of the first year of my life,” Buck reminded her. “Remember?”

Danny had mentioned it once, offhandedly. It was the first time they’d gotten to see Jee-Yun in person, with Danny holding her in his arms like she was a fragile doll wrapped in a blanket burrito. He’d said that he only got to see Buck once when he was that age, through a window, and that was only after begging.

Buck wasn’t certain how much time there had been between when he was born and when Danny got to go home, but he knew it was measured in months. No one had really talked about those details growing up, and they still didn’t. Hell, Buck wasn’t even certain if he’d donated stem cells from his umbilical cord or if they’d taken bone marrow. He’d tried to look for scars once and he thought he might’ve found a couple, but they were so faint and tiny it was hard to tell if they were really there. And they could have just as easily come from one of the hundreds of stupid stunts he’d done as a kid.

“And it’s not like he was there all the time in El Paso either,” Buck pointed out. “I spent a lot of nights alone at the apartment, or at Alma’s, or at Eddie’s.”

“I’m sure that last one was such a hardship,” Maddie drawled. He grinned at her. “How many of your football games did he miss?”

None of them. But that wasn’t a helpful answer for here and now.

“Jee isn’t playing football,” Buck teased. “Seriously, stop kicking yourself for being gone. You left for a reason. What would have happened if you’d stayed?”

“I would have seen Jee growing up.”

Buck figured the way his eyebrow popped up expressed his doubt. But just in case it didn’t…

“You were sick, Maddie.” He reached across the table to cover her hand in his where it was laying flat against the wood. “It’s lucky it wasn’t- I mean, I just can’t help but think it could have been worse, you know? What might have happened if you’d stayed and tried sticking it out instead of getting help. I was so relieved, afterwards, that you reached out to Danny before anything happened.”

His sister looked guilty, which was ridiculous. She didn’t have anything to feel guilty for. He watched as her eyes flickered away from him, then to the hand covering hers. Then she opened her mouth.

Rapping at the door startled them both. It was sharp, quick. The kind of rapping that didn’t request your attention, but demanded it. Buck and Maddie shared a glance, and then he got up to go answer it.

Cynthia Sheppard-Jones was on the other side. Her hair was done up in a neat bun and she was wearing a pale colored pants suit. A purse hung from her shoulder.

“Oh.” Buck blinked down at her; she half a foot shorter than him, at least. “Um. Hi? I wasn’t… I didn’t know you knew where we lived.”

“Why wouldn’t I?” Cynthia walked in like she owned the place, giving everything a quick once over. “It doesn’t look anything like I imagined it would.”

“And how would that be?”

“Something less… Domesticated, I think.” Cynthia ignored him as he mouth ‘domesticated?’ to himself. “Hello again, Maddie.”

Maddie awkwardly waved from where she was sitting.

“So, uh.” Buck adjusted his weight where he stood, debating what to say or ask. “Why are you here? Not- Not that it’s not nice to see you, it’s just… You live in Pasadena.”

“I work downtown, I’m on my lunch break,” she said. “I’m here because I’m tired of our brothers being idiots and I want to make them stop being idiots.”

Maddie let out a snort.

“Good luck with that,” she said.

“God, I know. But it’s that or kill ‘em. Where’s Dan? I can’t smack sense into him if he isn’t here.”

“He picked up an extra shift,” Buck said. “But I’m in. Danny’s all mopey, and he won’t even tell me what happened. They were fine one moment, then they weren’t.”

“Austin wouldn’t tell me, either. Or Ethan.” Cynthia huffed. “Getting anything out of him is impossible, unless he’s talking about someone behind their back.”

There was a beat of silence and then, as one, she and Buck turned to Maddie. The oldest Buckley sibling sighed.

“Dan told Austin he loved him,” she said.

Buck winced at the words. So did Cynthia. Which was odd. Yeah, wincing was a perfectly reasonable reaction, if you knew. But the Joneses all thought Danny and Austin were an actual couple, complete with kissing and everything else that being a couple implied. Why would she wince at hearing Austin’s boyfriend said he loved him? After six years, it would be expected for someone to say it in an average relationship.

“That’ll do it.” Cynthia shook her head. “Idiots.”

“Hold on. Wait.” Buck narrowed his eyes at her as a suspicion formed. “Do you… Do you know? About Austin?”

“That he’s ace? I’m his big sister, of course I know. I’m the reason he knows.” She pursed her lips in thought. “Ethan might know, but he hasn’t said.”

“You all knew? They’ve been faking a relationship for six years and you all knew?”

“I’m a mother and I work full time, I need to get my kicks from somewhere. It was fun to torment Dan.” Suddenly, the resemblance between her and her brother was impossible to ignore. “Mom and Dad don’t really get it. They have this idea of what happiness looks like, and it looks pretty much like what they have. And they want Austin to be happy. So we need to fix whatever the idiots broke, before Mom realizes what happened. She’ll give him time, I think, but she’s always been of the ‘try, try, try again’ mindset.”

“Maybe we shouldn’t,” Maddie suddenly said. “Maybe this is a good thing.”

Her words had Cynthia’s head twisting around to scowl at her.

“A good thing?”

“I’ve had to watch my brother hurt himself for two years, faking a relationship with someone he loves who doesn’t love him back.” Maddie stood as she spoke. “That isn’t healthy, that isn’t…”

Cynthia opened her, and then she shut it with a sigh.

“I don’t think they were faking it,” Buck quietly said. “C’mon, Mads. Danny was… He was happy. He was- He was relaxed. And you saw how Austin was with him, back with… You know, last year. When Mom and Dad visited.”

He glanced at Cynthia, unwilling to air his brother’s history any more than it already had been by that point. But he remembered how Austin had been that entire time, silent and supportive. The way Danny had gone to Austin when he ran and hid. The way he, by his own admission, would go to Austin’s when he was feeling raw from therapy.

And for some reason, Buck didn't believe for a moment that Austin had said a word to his sister about it.

“Look, I don’t know Austin’s feelings,” Cynthia said, and Buck was fairly certain she was lying a little there. “But I know he’s miserable, and Buck said that Dan’s moping. Neither of them are happy right now.”

Maddie reached up to rub at her eyes.

“Alright,” she said. “But what was your plan? To come in here and yell at him?”

“No.” Cynthia drew the word out. “It was to come in here and tell him he was an idiot.”

“It’s a good thing he isn’t here, then. Dan gets… Mean, when he’s pushed. Or stressed.” Something flashed across Maddie’s face, and Buck wondered if she knew that from personal experience. “Austin was the only person he didn’t send running when…"

She gestured vaguely to Buck’s leg.

“So if my plan was bad,” Cynthia said. “Do you have a better one?”

Maddie frowned and shook her head.

“Um.” Buck ducked his head a little when he found himself the center of attention again. “I think I do?”

Then

The thing was, it wasn't like Austin was trying to go unnoticed. To be fair, he didn't usually put in the effort for it. It was simply a fact that, if you're quiet enough and unassuming enough, people tend to start seeing you as part of the furniture. It became even easier ever since Daniel's baby brother, Buck, stepped into the station with all his noise and the way he seemed to fill the space wherever he went.

At the moment, Austin was in one of the comfy chairs in front of the television. There was something awful being watched, but Austin himself was mostly engrossed in his most recent book purchase, and honestly didn't care enough about whatever was on TV to really track it. And none of his coworkers who were watching it were saying anything interesting enough to eavesdrop on.

He'd been keeping track of Daniel's brother, though. Buck had come in with containers of the pozole he made - "It's so damn good," Daniel told him. "It's enough for rent on its own." – for Hen and Captain Nash, as well as one for himself. The kid was hovering around the former as she prepared to eat it, a puppy waiting to be told it had done good. Was, past-tense – at some point he'd decided to go bother the captain.

The captain apparently did not appreciate being bothered.

The grunt Buck let out was what drew Austin's attention, along with everyone else's. The sight of the captain pinning the kid to the wall was what kept their attention. They all fell silent as they stared, either too unwilling to step in or too surprised to see the normally level-headed Midwesterner lose it.

"Don't ever do that again," they heard him snarl, before he let Buck go and stomped off.

Buck stayed where he was, back pressed to the wall, making an obvious effort to calm his breathing. When he realized they were all staring, his face went red.

Austin knew, even as he typed out the text, that Daniel was probably gonna kill the captain. It didn't stop him from hitting send just as the alarm rang out.

(If, after that shift, he started paying extra attention whenever Buck and the captain were in proximity to one another, he could just say it was to make sure he didn't miss anything worth gossiping over.)

Chapter 27

Albert was well aware, by this point, that his coworkers all had a certain view of Danny. His first day at the station, he’d been taken aside more than once and given a warning about the man. Which, Albert could understand it. Danny could be very intense. Learning that he made the last probie cry had not been all that surprising, in hindsight.

They’d just gotten back from a call. Most of them, anyway – the paramedics were all still out. Something had happened on that last call, a traffic accident, though Albert wasn’t entirely sure what it was. He’d been too busy doing the grunt work he’d been assigned to really catch all of it. But he knew one of the victims they were extracting crashed at the scene.

Whatever happened, Danny was upset about it. Or maybe that was the wrong word, but he was definitely not being quiet about his concerns.

“I’m telling you, Cap.” Danny was hounding Mehta’s heels across the truck bay. Everyone else was giving the two a wide berth, or more specifically, they were giving Daniel a wide berth. “There was no reason for his vitals to have plummeted like that.”

“That you know of,” Mehta countered, stopping to turn and face Danny. “Maybe they missed something.”

“And it just so happened to rear its head right after Greenway saw to him?”

Mehta’s face turned grim.

“What are you saying, Dan?”

“That I think he fucked up,” Daniel said. “That he gave the patient the wrong thing, or maybe too much of the right thing.”

“If you’re right, then he made a mistake. We’ve all made them,” the captain said. "If you’re right. It could have just as easily been something outside his control. Either way, nobody died.”

Mehta turned and continued on his way across the bay, leaving Daniel standing in place. From where Albert was standing by his gear locker, he could see Daniel’s expression turn steadily stormier. Two of the others, Abdul and Petra, clocked his mood and took a sharp 90° turn to avoid him.

Albert was suddenly shoved from behind, sending him stumbling a few steps in Danny’s direction. When he looked to see who it was that did it, he only saw Flowers’ quickly retreating back. The message was received, though.

“Um,” he said, and then cringed a little.

Danny twisted his head around to look at him. His brows pulled close in a scowl and his mouth was twisted in something not quite a snarl. But then he sighed and shook his head.

“He’s gonna get someone killed,” Danny said. Albert got the impression it was not said to him. “C’mon, probie. I need to do the maintenance check on the engine. You get to help.”

Albert thought he heard an “oh thank God” from somewhere behind the ladder truck, but he couldn’t say for sure.

“Buck,” Bobby said. “Can I talk to you?”

Buck sighed and reluctantly followed Bobby into the captains’ office. He knew what this was about, and he knew how this conversation was most likely to go. It had been several shifts since he learned about Chimney punching Danny, and it was only a matter of time before the captain called him out on how he was acting around the paramedic.

“So,” Bobby started the moment the door to the office was closed. “You and Chimney.”

“What about him?” At Bobby’s look, Buck sighed. “He’s not my favorite person right now. We’ve been fine so far on calls, haven’t we?”

“You have,” Bobby agreed. “So far. What even happened?”

“He punched Danny.”

Buck watched the captain’s face, the way it twitched, but stayed resoundingly neutral. Tellingly neutral. He scowled at him.

“I know Danny’s not your favorite person-”

Bobby cut him off with a raised hand.

“As long as this enmity doesn’t effect your behavior in the station or on calls, it’s not my place,” he said. “Lord knows we’ve all had to work with people we didn’t like. Just tone down the overt hostility while we’re all stuck in the engine with the pair of you. And stop putting salt in his coffee.”

The snort of laughter was out before Buck could catch it.

“That’s- That’s not me, Cap.”

The captain stared at him, quizzically, and then sighed.

“Please ask Jones to stop doing it, then.”

“I make-” Buck was interrupted by the station alarms. He dashed for the trucks, shouting over his shoulder, “I make no promises!”

They piled onto the trucks, Buck climbing in and falling into the jump seat beside Lucy. Unfortunately, Chimney ended up taking the other seat on his side. The paramedic did it awkwardly, carefully keeping his legs in his own space.

The call they were responding to was about a woman who’d been out on a hike with her daughters, only to suddenly fall through the ground. The place Austin drove them to looked like an amphitheater, though Buck knew it wasn’t.

“This whole area was a missile base right up through the Cold War,” Bobby explained to Buck as they jogged down the steps. “My guess? It’s been years since they capped these silos and they’re not holding anymore.”

Considering they were currently standing on the cap, that was not precisely comforting to hear. The pair of them peered down the hole the mother fell through, seeing nothing but darkness. Her daughters – the oldest looking to be around Chris’ age – were sitting in a huddle on the bottom step, at the edge of the overgrown cap.

“Alright, Buck, you’ve got this,” Bobby said, and then left to deal with the girls.

Chimney knelt down on the other side of the hole. Buck barely glanced at him, seeing him pull a flashlight from his kit. He turned it on and shone it down the hole, the light barely cutting through the darkness.

“Might be as much as eighty feet,” Chimney commented.

Buck suppressed a wince at that. Eighty feet was a long way for someone to fall.

“There’s some kind of gate over there.” Lucy pointed and Buck’s gaze followed, spotting it on the other side of the amphitheater-like structure. “Maybe it’s a tunnel? We could get to her that way.”

“Rappelling down is gonna be the quickest way to get to her,” Hen remarked. “But it might be safer to bring her out that way.”

The gate was chained up, though, so they’d need to break in.

“Lucy, grab the bolt cutters,” Buck said. “Javier! We need harnesses”

Bobby had the girls sitting on the edge of the fire engine, the trio of them watching with interest as Javier manipulated the ladder. The others helped Chimney and Hen get set up with the harnesses, hooking them up to the rig. Buck and Lucy, meanwhile, got what they might need when they got down there, including the backboard.

Hen and Chimney were lowering down as Lucy snapped the chain that was keeping the gate closed. As they descended, Buck listened to Hen over the radio as she announced they had touched down and were with the mother. When they reached the bottom, Hen stopped him.

“I thought we were moving her?” Buck asked.

“She wants to talk to her kids first,” Hen said.

Buck looked past her and saw the woman, bloody and broken on the concrete floor. Even though he’d been expecting it, understanding was still harsh.

“She doesn’t think she’s making it out of here,” Lucy said, voicing their thoughts.

“She doesn’t want their last memory of her to be screaming,” Hen said.

And she would be screaming, Buck was sure. He looked up, at the circle of sunlight far above them. That she was conscious at all right now was… Not a miracle. Definitely not a miracle.

“Alright, Cap,” Chimney radioed, kneeling down beside the woman.

“Copy that, Chim.”

Chimney held his radio up to the mother’s face. A moment later, a girl’s voice – the youngest of her daughters, Buck thought – was able to be heard.

“Mom? Mom, are you there?”

“I’m here, sweetie,” the mom said. “Are you girls okay?”

“We’re scared,” another of her daughters said. “But we’re okay.”

“I know. I’m sorry that I scared you.”

“No, I’m sorry.” That was the oldest daughter, now. She sounded on the verge of crying. “If we hadn’t been playing, you wouldn’t have fell.”

“Not your fault, it was just an accident. I know it’s hard when we’re apart, when it seems like I’m not there. But the secret is, I’m always with you. Even when you can’t see me. You girls are the best part of me, so whenever you feel sad or alone, just reach for each other and that’s when you’ll feel me..” Her voice was growing weaker, the words taking longer to come out. “With you. And remember… that I love you.”

“But we’ll see you soon, right?”

“Yeah, baby.” Buck saw her eyes slip shut as she said it. “Soon…”

She exhaled a final time as her head tipped to the side. Chimney took his thumb off the radio and felt at her neck.

“Cap,” he radioed up to Bobby. “She’s ready to move.”

“What are we gonna say to her kids?” Buck asked.

“Nothing,” Chimney said. “They don’t hear this news from strangers. We transport her and them to the hospital. They’re gonna need their dad.”

Annoyance surged through Buck. Because Chimney might’ve been a dad, but he was a dad to a toddler, just barely more than a year old. It was easy to not talk about it to a toddler. But at least one of those girls up there, the one that had been on the verge of crying, she understood exactly what was going on.

“Chim, the oldest girl is Chris’ age and you just let her listen to her mom’s dying breath over the radio. She knows.” Buck snapped. The paramedic flinched at the words. “So what do we say to her?”

“I don’t know.” Chimney’s response felt like it should’ve had more heat behind it, but it was lacking. “Is there anything to say to her? What would you want someone to say?”

“Okay,” Lucy cut in. “What are we doing?”

Buck rubbed at his face, hating this part of the job.

“Get her on the bus, hit the lights and sirens? Like normal?” he suggested. “That way the other two just think…”

He trailed off.

“Yeah,” Chimney quietly said. “Sounds like a plan. I’ll call the dad on the way.”

“You guys go ahead.” Hen took the backboard from Buck. “We’ve got it.”

“C’mon,” Buck said to Lucy. “Let’s go distract the girls.”

The trip back up seemed simultaneously longer than the trip down and far too short. When they got topside, Bobby was putting on a positive face that dropped slightly when he made eye contact with Buck. The two of them, plus Lucy, got the girls into the engine. Just in time, too, for Hen and Chimney to bring the mom up.

Once the mom was loaded up onto the ambulance, it turned on its lights and sirens and pulled out. The engine followed it, Austin following its example after a quick glance Bobby and Buck’s way. The oldest girl spent the ride quiet and subdued, compared to the excitement of her sisters over being in a fire engine. They were all grins and laughter, but she just kept looking out the window.

When they got back to the station, after the girls were left with their father – the oldest girl had run crying to him – Buck slipped up the stairs to the roof. He had a plan to put into motion and sudden motivation to put it into motion sooner, rather than later. Once the door was closed behind him, he pulled out his phone.

Danny answered after only a couple rings.

“What’s up?”

Buck leaned back against the wall lining the roof’s edge.

“You wanna go to the aquarium with us this weekend?” he asked. “Chris has been asking...”

Daniel trailed along behind his brother and his brother’s boyfriend. It was, honestly, his first time at the Aquarium of the Pacific. Buck and Chris tended to go for the zoo, to the point that Buck splurged on the Family Deluxe membership last summer and it had paid for itself very quickly. But apparently Chris decided he wanted to see fish, so here they were.

“Dad’s finally retiring.” Eddie rolled his eyes. Not that Daniel could see his eyes, Eddie was facing away from him, but Diazes tended to put their whole heads into rolling their eyes. “Mom wants us to come down for the party.”

“And who all does ‘us’ cover?” Daniel asked.

“Him, Chris, and Pepa.” Buck looked up from his phone, which he’d been focused on far too much this trip, and grinned at him. “They definitely weren’t counting me, but I’m going anyway, I’ve already got the days off. Let’s take these elevators up.”

They were barely halfway through the first floor. Daniel and Eddie both shot him a confused glance and he waved his little map at them, mouthing ‘gift shop.’ Which didn’t really answer anything, until Daniel snatched the map from his hands and saw that taking the elevators now would have them avoiding the gift shop.

It still had them missing a lot of the exhibits.

Buck seemed to have a destination in mind. As soon as they stepped out of the elevator, he was directing them to the right. He led them right into the Northern Pacific Gallery and then right on through without so much as a moment’s hesitation, not even stopping at the spider crabs they passed or the exhibit on whale calls.

It was finally at the sea jellies and pacific nettles that he stopped. A family with a troupe of kids were already there, the boys smashing their faces against the glass while their mother-

Wait.

While Cynthia fussed at her sons to get off. Ethan was just laughing at her, his two kids bouncing in place, but far more well behaved. And then, beside them, was the reason Daniel was contemplating running away.

Because beside them was Austin.

And Austin was staring right at him, expressionless.

“What did you do?” Daniel hissed at his brother.

“Oh, boy, what a coincidence,” said Buck, the utter brat. “Chris, let’s go see the otters.”

“Okay,” the smaller brat agreed.

“Cynthia! How about you? You wanna see the otters?”

“Sure.” She laughed as Austin’s face broke into an expression of betrayal. “C’mon, boys.”

She herded her kids away. Ethan followed with his kids, after clapping his brother on the back. His oldest, Rosemary, broke away to hug Daniel before dashing off.

“Good luck,” Buck told Daniel, then he shoved him forward and ran away with the Diazes like the traitorous coward he was.

It was just Daniel and Austin, and the sea jellies. There wasn’t much light, aside from the soft light along the floor and the light that filtered through the water behind the glass. It played across Austin’s face, softening the edges while making his expression even harder to figure out.

Daniel could run. He could run right now, just turn and walk away. But there’d been a conspiracy to get him and Austin together, and he knew he’d just end up right back here – not here here, but… Whatever – if he tried to avoid this. He really wanted to avoid this, though.

He dragged himself forward to the glass, past Austin. The jellies were floating along, careless and carefree. To his left, a sea nettle trailed long limbs behind it on its aimless path through the water. Austin joining him was felt more than seen, the other man close enough to reach out and touch.

Daniel sighed and rested his forehead against the glass.

“I’m sorry,” he said.

There was a moment of silence and then a very confused,

“Why?”

“Because I ruined it,” Daniel said. “When we started this, there were- Not rules, but… It was a fake relationship, because you didn’t want a real one. It was just supposed to get your parents off your back. Then I had to go and grow feelings, and I couldn’t even keep them to myself.”

“Are you wearing my sweater?”

The question had nothing to do with what Daniel just said, which threw him entirely for a loop. He straightened up and glanced down at himself, even though he didn’t have to. Because underneath his leather jacket, the one he got for Christmas, was one of the sweaters he’d stolen from Austin on accident that had never made it back to him.

“Do you want it back?” Daniel asked, all while hoping the answer was no. “I could send them with Buck. If you want.”

“Them,” Austin repeated, and Daniel felt himself flush. “I… I do love you. I think.”

Daniel twisted his head around to gawk at him. But Austin wasn’t looking at him, he was staring at the jellies. His face was a mask of concentration, as if the words were an engine he was trying to tune up.

“Not like I love Cynthia and Ethan,” he continued. “But not like that either. Not in the way you want me to love you.”

“I know.” Daniel shrugged and smiled, though it felt very small. “I’ve known since the start. You made it very fucking clear you didn’t want any of that. It’s why I didn’t say anything. I didn’t want to make you, I don’t know. Uncomfortable.”

“It doesn’t make me feel uncomfortable.”

Daniel figured his blatant disbelief was visible in his phantom reflection in the glass, because Austin followed it with,

“It doesn’t make me uncomfortable coming from you. I just feel guilty.”

“Why?”

“Because if I was normal-”

“Shut up.” Daniel rolled his eyes and stuffed his hands into his jacket pockets. “You’re one of the most normal people I know. Your entire family is disgustingly average.”

“Dan, whatever you want from me, I can’t give it to you,” Austin said. “Not all of it. Whatever Buck and Eddie get from each other, I can’t give you that.”

“I just want you,” Daniel said, and this time it was his turn to pretend like the jellies were the most interesting thing ever while Austin gawked at him. “I was happy with what we had, so I kept my mouth shut and swallowed down my feelings so they didn’t ruin it. Then they ruined it anyway. But… You. I just want you. Whatever form that takes, whatever you’ll give me. Just you.”

Austin fell silent, slowly turning his head to watch the jellies again. The sounds of his brother’s booming laughter, accompanied by the lighter laughter of children, echoed from around the corner. Someone else was enjoying the whale calls they’d hurried past.

“Do you want to move in together?”

Once again, Austin’s question was very random and left Daniel feeling very off-balanced for a moment.

“What?”

“My lease runs out in a few months,” Austin explained. “I was thinking about finding a different place. Do you want to move in together?”

“Um.” Daniel blinked at him, then blinked again. “Sure?”

“Cool.”

“Cool,” Daniel echoed, shaking his head a little. “So, uh. Chimney’s car?”

Austin shrugged.

“He punched you,” he said, as simple as that.

Daniel burst out laughing, letting his head fall forward to land on Austin’s shoulder. He smelled like chocolate again, like summer days at Hershey Park.

“Hey, Danny!” Buck was peeking around the corner, looking far too pleased with himself. “Uh, we kinda skipped pretty much the entire aquarium to get here, and Chris-”

“I want to see the sharks!” the aforementioned kid shouted from somewhere beyond Buck.

“Yeah, that.”

“Go on ahead, squirt,” Daniel said, waving him off. “We’ll catch up.”

Buck grinned and did that little happy skip thing as he disappeared.

“Do you want to see the sharks?” he asked Austin.

“Saw them already.” Austin shrugged. “But sure.”

“C’mon, then,” Daniel said as he reluctantly pulled away. “Chris is pretty quick for a kid that can’t run.”

Chapter 28

When Daniel moved them to El Paso, he hadn’t actually gone apartment hunting. He’d found an apartment that had two bedrooms and rent that he thought he could afford, and that was the extent of his efforts. He certainly ended up regretting not doing more, because that apartment building had sucked. Then again, he wouldn’t have gotten Alma as a neighbor if they lived somewhere else, somewhere better, so things kinda evened out.

When he told Buck they were moving to Los Angeles, he put a little bit more effort into picking out the new place. The apartment itself was bigger and the building it was in was nicer, with its own gym on the ground floor and parking spaces for the both of them. It had been a bit of a splurge, near the high end of what he was willing to pay for rent at the time, but he figured it was worth it.

Still, he’d mostly found a place online, made a call, and that was that. He didn’t care all that much, not really. Anything was good, s’long as there was room for Buck. That was, more or less, his lone requirement. Well, that and no homeowner associations.

Austin seemed to be of a similar mind to Daniel, but in a different way. They were on Austin’s couch, Daniel having happily drifted to his apartment after the aquarium. Daniel was mostly focused on the TV, with some channel playing the old Beauty and the Beast. Beside him, Austin had his laptop out with a real estate website pulled up. When Daniel glanced over, he saw Austin was scrolling down a listing of places.

Daniel propped his chin on Austin’s shoulder, distracted from the kids’ movie for the moment. Each place Austin was looking at had three bedrooms, something he’d done without Daniel having to say a word. Things like that were why Daniel fell for him so hard.

God, the prices on some of these were outrageous. The housing market in this city was godawful. How could anyone be a homeowner here?

“How could Ethan afford their home in Los Feliz?” he asked. “I’d probably have to go back to hookering.”

“You could be a stripper,” Austin suggested without taking his eyes away from the screen.

“I’d break something,” Daniel said. “And you didn’t answer the question.”

“He used his trust fund for the down payment. Cynthia did the same.”

Daniel blinked, then lifted his head to stare at the other man. For a moment, he was rendered speechless as he tried to make sense of what Austin had just said.

“You’re a fucking trust fund baby!?” he about screeched.

“Yeah,” Austin confirmed, tone dismissive. “Dad’s mom did it. They were released when we turned thirty.”

“When you-” That would have been the year before Daniel even met him. “You’re almost forty. We have been… Whatever this is for seven years! And you didn’t think to mention that you had a trust fund?”

“I forgot,” Austin said with a shrug, and then turned his head to look at Daniel. “Am I more or less attractive to you now?”

Daniel groaned and let his head fall forward to land on Austin’s shoulder again. Austin gave a small huff of amusement, his breath tickling Daniel’s hair, and refocused on the laptop again.

“I want a tub,” Daniel grumbled. “One I can soak in.”

“Okay.”

Austin sounded like he was laughing, so Daniel – lightly – punched him in the side. He rolled his head so his cheek was resting against Austin and he could see what Austin was doing. And what Austin was doing was bringing up a house to look at. A house with a lawn.

“I thought you said you’d hate having to mow.”

“I figured we’d make Eddie or Buck do it,” Austin said, a statement that had Daniel once again lifting his head to stare at him.

“Uh,” he said. “What?”

“Well, I don’t want to mow, and I figure you don’t, either,” the other man said, like that was what was tripping Daniel. “So we’d make them do it.”

“Why would either of them mow a lawn for a house they don’t live in?”

Because, let’s be real. Buck’s room at the new place would probably just have a bed, because he was practically moved in with Eddie already.

“Oh.” Austin’s expression turned sheepish. Or as sheepish as it ever appeared, anyway. “I guess I forgot to ask them.”

“If they’d mow your lawn?”

“What? No. What they thought about moving.”

Daniel watched Austin pull up another house to look at. Not a rental, he noticed, but a house for sell. A house that Austin was picking out not just for the two of them, but for Buck and Eddie and Chris, too.

The next day, Sunday, he dropped in on Buck and Eddie. The former was being horribly smug, and Daniel couldn’t even be angry at him for it. He was, after all, the reason he got to spend last night curled up with Austin again. It dropped a little, however, when he saw Daniel’s face.

“What’s wrong?” Buck was quick to ask. “It’s not- You and Austin are okay, right?”

“We’re fine.” Daniel dropped into a chair at the dining table, where Eddie was eating lunch. “We’re more than fine, actually.”

Eddie’s face scrunched up.

“What does that mean?” he asked around a mouthful of sandwich.

“Austin wants to buy us a house.”

“Oh.” Buck took the seat across from Daniel. “That… That makes sense. You two have been together, or whatever, for years now. Of course you two would be moving in together. I didn’t think he was a house guy, though.”

“No, squirt, you don’t understand,” Daniel told him. “He wants to buy us a house.”

He gestured around the table. Buck’s look of confusion melted away into realization, and then more confusion. Eddie dropped his sandwich onto his plate.

“He wants- He wants to buy us a house?” he repeated. “As in, the five of us. Living together?”

“Hold on,” Buck said. “Can he even afford that?”

“Trust fund,” Daniel grumbled. “The five of us, yes. Maybe Maddie, too, I don’t know. I tapped out of the conversation when he dropped that bomb on me.”

A month ago, he was pretty sure the second best thing to ever happen to him had fallen apart. Now, here he was, being offered that ideal world he once dreamed about. Dr. White was going to be such a smug bitch when she found out about this.

Assuming, of course, Eddie went for it. He might not. Why would he? Why would he want to willingly share a house with his boyfriend’s psychopathic older brother and said older brother’s not-boyfriend?

“There couldn’t be any stairs,” Eddie said.

“What?” Daniel asked, a bit thrown by the statement.

“The house,” he clarified. “I don’t want to live somewhere that Chris can’t get places easily.”

“I want a pool,” Buck said and then, in response to the look Eddie shot him, “You have your priorities, I have mine.”

“I will pass your requests on,” Daniel said, leaning back in his chair.

Whether or not Austin would take those requests into consideration, though, was not guaranteed.

It felt like Buck’s shift had barely begun before his phone was ringing. Finding it a good excuse to put off his assigned chores for the day, he pulled it out to find it was Eddie. Answering it had Eddie’s face filling the screen, his tiny little office visible in the background from where he had his phone propped up on his desk.

“Where’s Austin?”

“Hi, Buck. I love you, Buck. It’s been less than an hour since I last saw you, Buck, but my life is just so empty without-”

His boyfriend – quite rudely – cut him off with a roll of his eyes and a,

“Yeah, yeah, yeah. Where’s Austin?”

Buck sighed and moved to the railing of the loft. A glance down confirmed what he already knew, although the other man did tend to disappear at a moment’s notice.

“Downstairs,” he said. “He’s about to do the checks on the engine.”

“Get me down to him.”

“Oh my God, did you call me just to talk to him?”

“I don’t have his number,” was Eddie’s shameless answer. “¡Ándale!”

Buck huffed in annoyance, but he headed for the stairs all the same. His boyfriend waited impatiently on his end, his fingers drumming against his desk just off screen. Personally, Buck couldn’t understand how his boyfriend was surviving being the department’s liaison there. He definitely wouldn’t. Then again, Eddie kept coming home in a thick cloud of misery, so maybe he wasn’t surviving it, either.

(The argument about him running scared from firefighting had been indefinitely tabled after Eddie’s little moment in his bedroom. With the walls still scarred by the holes Eddie had left in them, Buck didn’t see the worth in it.)

“What can I do for you, baby Buckley?” Austin asked when Buck got to his side.

The engineer was flipping through the pages on the maintenance checklist clipboard. Whatever he was seeing had him frowning.

“May and Claudette got into a shouting match.”

Austin tossed the clipboard onto the nearby rolling toolbox and gave Buck’s phone – and Eddie on the other end of the call – his full attention.

“Talk,” he ordered. “Who’s Claudette?”

“Hold on, wait.” Buck glowered at his boyfriend. “Did you call me to gossip with my brother’s zucchini?”

“Your brother’s what?”

“Yes,” was Eddie’s shameless answer. Buck rolled his eyes, but angled his phone so Austin could see the screen. “Claudette’s another dispatcher. Older, I think she transferred from Valley to fill the gap Maddie left. She’s been a bitch to May since she got here. Or so I’ve heard.”

“Oof.” Buck winced. “I’m kinda surprised Athena hasn’t stormed the place. What are you doing?”

“Telling Dan,” Austin answered as his thumbs tapped away at his own phone. “Keep talking, Diaz.”

“May sent an RA to her caller, only for Claudette to redirect it to another scene. So they got into it, right there on the call center floor. Sue broke it up and put them in timeout.”

“Timeout?” Buck chuckled as he asked.

“Yeah, they’ve got a room here for dispatchers to decompress. Pretty sure that’s where Sue took them.”

“We need that room here,” Austin muttered.

As Buck watched, he rolled his eyes at whatever Danny texted back and then tucked his phone away again.

“What the hell?”

On Eddie’s end of the call, the lights suddenly flickered. His brow was furrowed as he tapped away at his computer’s keyboard. It apparently wasn’t doing what he wanted it to, because he huffed in annoyance and bent down to pull out a laptop. Knocking from off-screen had him looking up briefly, a flicker of a scowl crossing his face.

“Did you get it, too?” Josh’s voice asked.

“Yeah, power surge.”

“No, this.”

Josh must’ve been holding something out, because Eddie lifted his head again and looked off-screen.

“Fire suppression system,” he read off. Buck and Austin frowned at each other. “Is that the sprinklers or the new CAS? It’s that waterless system.”

“Yeah, sucks all the oxygen out of a room. I met the guy who was installing it.”

“If you met him, you should go talk to him.”

Buck muffled his laughter. His boyfriend could be such a bitch, sometimes. He loved him so.

“Oh, uh, I- I can’t,” Josh stammered. “I’ve got the floor, and Sue is dealing with, um…”

“Yeah, I heard it.”

“Everyone heard.”

“Alright,” Eddie sighed, pushing his chair back. “I’m on it.”

He turned and grabbed a radio charging behind him. Then he stood and picked up his phone, the video blurring a little with the motion.

“He’s got the entire place a mess because he wanted to go paperless,” Eddie groused, and then sighed. “I guess I’ll go take care of… Whatever this is.”

“Have fun,” Buck said.

His boyfriend rolled his eyes and ended the call.

“So,” he said to Austin as he slipped his phone back into his pants. “You want us to buy a house together?”

“Sure,” the other man said.

“Okay, but why?” Buck asked. “Danny’s always said you like your space.”

“I do.” Austin picked the maintenance clipboard back up off the tool box. “I can have space in a house.”

“But why?”

Austin shrugged.

“Dan’ll be happier,” was his answer.

Buck stared at him, somewhat slack-jawed. The temptation to hug the man and never let go was a very strong one, but he didn’t think Austin would appreciate it. He liked that one game, maybe Buck could get him something for it. Many somethings.

“Zucchini?” Austin suddenly asked.

“Danny never really said what you two prefer to be called,” Buck said. “Besides not-boyfriends. That is what people call each other in a QPR, right? Hen explained it to me forever ago, and then I looked it up.”

The other man opened his mouth, like he was about to say something, and then closed it. He was facing the engine, so Buck could only see his face in profile and in the reflection in the red metal. Not that Buck would be able to tell what the man was thinking even if they were facing each other.

“There are others,” Buck offered. He pulled his phone back out and went into his notes app. “Danny gets annoyed when I call you his zucchini, so that’s why I use that one. But there’s, uh. There’s partner, if you wanna be boring, or marshmallow?”

“Marshmallow?”

“I didn’t come up with this stuff.

“Did you make a list?” Austin asked, apparently just now noticing that Buck was reading off his phone. He didn’t wait for an answer, though. “I’ll think about it. QPR?”

“Yeah…?”

That was what Danny and Austin were, right? Hen had seemed pretty certain that’s what they were when she explained what a queerplatonic relationship was to Buck. And it definitely seemed to fit whatever his brother had going on with Austin.

He was beginning to realize, however, that neither man had ever actually said what they were. Not to him, anyways. It was always what they weren’t, instead, what they didn’t do. They were not-boyfriends, they didn’t do the whole kissing thing, they weren’t dating.

So lost in thought about this realization that Buck missed Austin moving on. He was starting his inspection of the engine, jotting down notes and checking things off as he went. Buck started to follow after him, intent on asking what they considered themselves to be, but he was interrupted by Eddie’s voice coming from the speakers above.

“Mayday, mayday, mayday,” Eddie said, the words bringing the entire station to a halt. “This is Firefighter Eddie Diaz on a department-wide channel. We have a fire at Metro Dispatch downtown. Point of origin appears to be on the second floor records room. All non-essential personnel begin evacuation procedures.”

Buck was already heading for his turnouts before Eddie was even halfway done. Their station was one of the closest to Metro Dispatch, he knew, along with the 133. Behind him, he heard Austin throw the clipboard onto the toolbox and then send it rolling towards the wall with a kick.

“Repeat. This is Firefighter Eddie Diaz. Metro Dispatch is on fire.”

Eddie wasn't certain if he should be thankful for or annoyed at Josh foisting this task onto him. On the one hand, it certainly beat being bored in his little cave. On the other, he’d been perfectly fine talking to – gossiping with – Buck and Austin. This was very much cutting into that time, and it was only because Josh was quite obviously avoiding talking to the installation guy.

He was probably a dick to the guy, too, and was now embarrassed to go back and face him again.

All thoughts about Josh fled Eddie’s mind, however, the moment he stepped out of the elevator. The building’s server room had glass walls, much like the 118’s locker room. Inside, the clean agent system had gone off, leaving it full of fog. Terry, dispatch’s IT guy, lay unmoving on the ground.

He rushed for the door, slamming his ID badge to the scanner. Instead of the happy chirp and the green light, instead of unlocking and letting him in, he got an angry beeping while the light stayed red. The door did not budge, no matter how much he tried to force it.

Eddie was a firefighter. He was a firefighter. And he knew that there was not a locked room anywhere that he couldn’t get into with the right tools and enough time. He just needed-

There, on the wall, a fire extinguisher. He grabbed it and went back to the server room. Turning his face away, he slammed the butt of it into the glass wall. They were tempered, though, so there was only a spider’s web of cracks where he struck. Another hit only furthered the cracks.

So Eddie stepped back a bit, eyeballed the damage like he would the mitt of the umpire in high school, and hurled the extinguisher. It crashed through the glass, and though it didn’t shattered into pieces, he was able to use his radio to knock the broken shards out of the frame so he could step into the room.

Terry was still, too still. Eddie rolled him over onto his back, checked his pulse and his breathing, and rubbed at his sternum with his knuckles until the man opened his eyes.

“System shorted out,” Terry mumbled.

Yeah, Eddie had figured that out. What was more of a concern, however, was the thin cloud of what looked smoke coming from the other hallway. Eddie hefted Terry up over his shoulder and hauled him out, leaving him to sit on a stack of boxes as he went to go investigate.

The smoke was billowing out from beneath the door to the records room. A quick check with the back of his hand told him it was warm, but not scorching. He already knew what he would find when he opened the door, but that didn’t make the sight of fire devouring the boxes of records any better.

Right, then. Get Terry out, make sure everyone else got out, have Josh or Sue call for fire and EMS. Except, as he pulled the fire alarm on his way past, the plan hit a snag. Because the alarm didn’t go off. He pulled it again, just to be sure, and got the same (lack of) result.

The shortage must’ve knocked it out, too.

“Mayday, mayday, mayday,” Eddie spoke into his radio. He could hear an echo of himself coming from the building’s PA system. “This is Firefighter Eddie Diaz on a department-wide channel. We have a fire at Metro Dispatch downtown. Point of origin appears to be on the second floor records room. All non-essential personnel begin evacuation procedures.”

And to think, he mocked to himself, he’d told everyone he switched to dispatch because it was safer.

“Repeat. This is Firefighter Eddie Diaz. Metro Dispatch is on fire.”

Chapter 29

Chapter Notes

There is a lot of Daniel in this chapter, because he's involving himself in the parts of the episode I haven't covered in other fics. I figure you guys don't mind, though.

When Eddie stumbled out of the building, Terry hanging from his shoulder and both of them coughing from the smoke that had quickly filled up the halls and stairwells, he found both the 118 and the 133 had already arrived. Bobby and Captain Mehta were both there to greet him, while Buck and the others were jogging over. He spotted Danny strolling at a more casual pace, and Austin at the 118’s engine behind him getting the hoses hooked up.

“Records room on the second room is fully involved. Fire could spread to the main floor, the third floor,” he told the captains as he handed Terry off to Hen. “Terry here got knocked out by the clean agent system. He took in a lot of gas, but there could still be a lingering narcotic effect.”

“I got him," Hen assured him.

“Hen,” Bobby said, getting her attention. “I want you to set up a triage area in the parking garage next door.”

“Copy that,” she replied. “I’m gonna need a hand, though.”

“It is Monday,” a guy from the back of the group said.

Eddie was briefly confused, wondering what the day of the week had anything to do with setting up triage. Then he noticed Hen’s slight grimace and realized that the guy must’ve been the floater Danny told him about forever ago, the one she was calling Monday. Buck said his name was Jonah something-or-other.

“Fine.” Hen’s tone was grudging. “You’ll do.”

“Mendoza! Dorsey!” Mehta called out. “Go with them.”

“Hey.” Danny was suddenly in Eddie’s space, smacking Eddie’s arm with the back of his hand for attention. “You said that guy inhaled that CAS shit. What about you?”

“I’m fine.” Eddie waved the concern away, as nice as it was. The older man scowled when the statement was accompanied by a cough. “I am! This is mostly from the smoke.”

“Yeah, Eds, that’s…” Buck, standing behind Danny, shook his head. “That’s not really helping your case.”

“Chimney!” Oh, right, Bobby was still giving out orders. “Put together a team from the One Thirty-three, raise the ladders and start hitting the corner window.”

Eddie watched Chimney nod and march off to do just that, grabbing Albert as he went. Albert voiced excitement about working with his brother, only for his brother to shoot that down quite thoroughly. Eddie winced at it.

“Most of the One Thirty-three don’t really like him right now,” Buck remarked. “So that’ll be fun for him.”

“You told them about the punch?” Eddie guessed, and got a nod in return,

“Seriously?” Danny scoffed and rolled his eyes. “It was not that big of a deal.”

“Uh, yeah it was,” his brother disagreed.

“Wait. They’re upset at him about that?”

Buck scowled at his brother’s incredulous sounding words and opened his mouth, but whatever he was going to say got cut off by Bobby calling for him and Lucy. The woman who’d kissed his boyfriend at the bar, so long ago. It was the first time Eddie had gotten a good look at her; he’d only gotten a view of her back when Eddie saw everyone enjoying themselves without him that night and decided to go back home, instead. As Buck gave the captain his attention and walked over to him, Eddie noticed he kept space between himself and Lucy.

“You’re going inside,” Bobby told them. “You’re gonna hit the fire at the source.”

“Got it, Cap,” Buck agreed, and then jogged off with Lucy.

Danny left as well, mumbling something about helping out with the hoses. Which he was supposed to have been doing already, like Austin was, since he was his station’s engineer. Eddie wondered why he put it off.

There had been a steady stream of coughing maroon or blue clad dispatchers flowing out of the building. Sue only made an appearance towards the rear, clipboard in hand and heading straight for the captains.

“My people are initializing our disaster protocols,” she told them, then explained, “Which means they’re gonna need help turning a sizable part of that parking garage into a mobile dispatch center so we can keep taking calls.”

“What’s the call volume like?” Captain Mehta asked her.

“High,” Sue answered. “There’s no way that Valley Dispatch could handle all the traffic.”

“Grab Buckley, tell him I said to help you.”

“Buckley the older or Buckley the younger?”

“The older. And tell him to grab whoever he needs to help,” Mehta said. When he caught the expression Bobby was giving him, one that was very loudly questioning his decision, he just said, “Dan is very good at motivating people.”

“That’s a term for it,” Sue dryly remarked, before visibly getting herself back on track. “I have five of my senior dispatchers still on the floor, taking calls. Unless the fire’s encroaching, they won’t stand down until we’ve gotten the trunk line connected.”

“You said senior dispatchers,” Bobby broke in. “So May’s been evacuated?”

Horror crept across Sue’s face at the question. Eddie immediately knew what it meant.

“She’s still up there!?” he blurted out.

He hadn’t even thought to check in the rush of everything. He’d simply assumed that May had either heard him over the PA or that someone else had grabbed her.

“I put her in the quiet room with Claudette,” Sue told Bobby, hapless and apologetic. “I’m so sorry. With all this craziness, I- I forgot.”

“Where’s this room located?” Bobby asked.

“Third floor,” she said. “Just behind main dispatch.”

“I think that’s just above where the fire started,” Eddie said, trying to visualize the layout of the building.

“Mehta, I’m passing the baton. You’re IC now,” Bobby said, already marching for the 118’s engine. “Eddie, get geared up.”

“Got it, Cap.”

They kept a set or two of spare turnouts on the trucks, a just-in-case measure. When Eddie got to the engine to grab them, he slotted right beside Buck and Lucy, both of whom were still getting their oxygen tanks on. Buck looked over at Bobby, also gearing up, and then back to Eddie.

“Why is Cap going in?” he asked.

“May’s still up there,” Eddie answered as he got everything on.

Buck’s face took on an expression of horror similar to Sue’s.

“It feels like I’m missing something,” Lucy said, a clear attempt at fishing for information.

“Family history,” Eddie answered, because that’s all she needed to know. “C’mon, let’s go.”

Geared up, he slammed the compartment door closed and hurried to keep up with Bobby with Buck at his shoulder.

“Donato, you’re with me,” Bobby said. “Eddie, you’re with Buck.”

Eddie felt Buck slap his arm, and when he looked, his boyfriend was grinning and wagging his eyebrows. He scoffed and shook his head, but he didn’t bother hiding his own smile.

The parking garage was abuzz with people. Half of the ground was set up as the temporary call center, the other half triage. The cars that had been there were now pushed off to the side, out of the way.

Daniel got dragged away from the hoses before he even got started, Sue telling him that he’d been volunteered by Mehta to help set up something temporary for the dispatchers to use. Emergencies didn’t stop just because it wasn’t a convenient time for them. However much they might wish.

He grabbed a couple of the guys to help haul things around, leaving the technical stuff to the technology guy. Terry or something, the one Eddie half-carried out the building. It didn’t take very long, or at least Daniel’s part didn’t. He ended up stripping off his coat and leaving it in a pile on the ground, out of the way, along with his helmet. It wasn’t like he needed it for what they were doing.

Nola, he noticed, kept side-eyeing Greenway as she looked people over, very clearly annoyed about something. So was Mendoza, actually. Daniel didn’t even pretend that he went over for anything but gossip, once Terry and Sue didn’t need him any longer.

“So.” Daniel handed her the gauze she was reaching for, for appearance’s sake. “What did Greenway do?”

Nola’s scoff was derisive enough to peel paint. She didn’t answer at first as she finished wrapping up a burn. When she was done, though, she stood from where she’d been kneeling and stepped away from the various patients. Daniel followed.

“Give me plenty of room to work,” she said under her break. Her tone was mocking and her head wagged as she spoke. “I need my space.”

“Oh, are we complaining?” Mendoza joined them, helmet tucked under his arm. “I love complaining. Especially about this guy.”

The 133’s paramedics, the ones that were there before Greenway, had complained about him a lot since he transferred over. Daniel wondered if he’d been the same at the 118, if he’d rubbed Hen the wrong way like he did them. But Austin had said they were getting along for the couple of weeks they worked together, so Daniel figured he either toned himself down over there or working so long with both Nash and Chimney had simply thickened Hen’s skin.

“Egotistical little shit head,” Nola grumbled. “He talked to us like we were just a pair of fresh probies.”

“There’s going to be smoke inhalation, so stack up the O2 bottles.” Mendoza’s words were just as mocking as Nola’s had been, so Daniel assumed he was quoting Greenway. “We would have been so lost without him telling us what to do.”

As fun as it was to bitch together, they still had a job to do, so they broke up soon after that. Daniel stuck around to help go through everyone that was evacuated from the building, both in case Sue needed him again and so he could keep an eye on Greenway. He still remembered what happened the last time Greenway was left alone with a patient, after all. The guy clearly couldn’t be trusted not to fuck up, and Daniel didn’t particularly want to be dragged into any inquest that would come from him doing something they couldn’t fix.

“You’re keeping a close eye on Jonah, there.”

Daniel looked up to see Hen just a couple steps away, pulling off her medical gloves and balling them up. Much like himself, her turnout coat was discarded somewhere, so she was left in just the bunker pants and long sleeved uniform tee.

He didn’t answer her right away, surrounded as they were by dispatchers breathing in bottled oxygen. The one he was treating, Jamal or something, was not doing a good job hiding his interest in their conversation. So instead, Daniel stood from where he’d been kneeling and carried an oxygen tank over to the designated pile of empties. Hen followed him, tossing the gloves into a trash bag they passed.

“He messed up the other day,” Daniel told her. After making sure no prying ears – such as Taylor Kelly, who’d been sent to cover the fire by Channel 8 – were nearby. “Mehta’s all about giving him the benefit of the doubt, but I know he did.”

Mainly because Daniel had been one of the ones that checked that particular patient over originally.

“Messed up?” Hen repeated, clearly curious.

“We left him alone with a guy for five seconds and suddenly the guy’s crashing,” he explained. “He was just supposed to be giving the guy something for the pain. I’m pretty sure he gave too much. Or maybe the wrong thing, I don’t know. He fucked up.”

Sue and Terry were nearby, he noticed. Sue had been going going around checking people off on her clipboard, a mother goose doing a headcount of all her ducklings. That’s what the two of them were talking about, how almost everyone had been accounted for. All except for May and Claudette, the woman who’d apparently spent the last few months being a bitch to May and was in need of an informative talk.

Well. Those two, and-

“This is the only other name,” Sue was saying. She tapped on a name with her pen. “He signed in this morning.”

“Carson, yeah.” Terry nodded. “He was installing the clean agent system.”

“When was the last time you saw him?”

Terry was silent as he thought, and then shrugged apologetically.

“I don’t remember,” he admitted.

“Where was the last place you saw him?” Daniel broke in to ask, already grabbing his radio.

“Last place I saw him was in the server room,” the tech guy said. “He wasn’t there when the CAS went off, but it’s on the second floor.”

The floor the fire started on. Also the floor that Nash sent Buck and that Lucy chick.

“Buckley One Thirty-three to Buckley One Eighteen,” he radioed. He got into Sue’s space enough to spot the guy’s name on the list she had. “We’ve got a person unaccounted for, a worker named Carson Hayes. Last known location was on the second floor.”

“This is Diaz,” Eddie replied. Daniel blinked and looked around, only just realizing his brother’s boyfriend wasn’t in the garage. “We swept the whole floor.”

Apparently Eddie decided he wanted to be a firefighter again.

“This is dispatcher Russo, I think I know where he is.”

“Don’t keep us hanging, Josh.”

“He told me he was going to the basement to connect power to the system,” Josh said. “It’s probably the electrical vault. I can meet you and take you down there.”

“That’s a negative,” Eddie told him. “We’ve got this.”

“But- But I’m right by the fire stairs. I could be down there right away.”

“Josh, you’re a dispatcher, not a firefighter. Today, you’re a guest in this house.”

Daniel felt a bolt of pride go through him. Sue eyed him and his radio, lips pursed, and shook her head. She didn’t say anything, though.

More things needed doing, apparently. Daniel found himself helping Taylor set up a place to edit and upload footage, somewhere out of the way of the dispatchers and paramedics. Every once in a while, he would sweep the triage area until he spotted Greenway. The reporter caught him doing it, but he just gave her his most charming smile and shrugged.

“And what is your opinion of this fire, firefighter Buckley?” she asked.

“Engineer Buckley,” he idly corrected. He set down the whatever-the-hell he’d carried over for her. It was awkward to carry, was all he knew. “Is this on the record or somethin’?

She shrugged.

“Maybe.”

“Well, my opinion is that this is gonna be a fuckin’ pain in the ass for the next week.”

Try as they might, there was simply no way they could avoid disruptions. This garage was not meant to be used as a call center floor, but finding a suitable location for them to temporarily move into wasn’t going to happen over night. Then they’d have to get that place set up, and then transfer operations over.

Plus, it didn’t exactly look like the computers they were currently using were the newest and the shiniest. He didn’t know where they came from, but they’d been in storage somewhere for more than a bit of time. The dispatchers didn’t have access to all the bells and whistles they were used to, and that would have an effect on how they responded to calls.

“Yeah, I don’t think I can use that,” Taylor said, but she sounded more amused than annoyed. “Think I could get Sue to agree to an interview? I did help catch the guy that ran her over, after all.”

“Maybe.” Daniel glanced over his shoulder to where Sue was conversing with Terry. The tech guy was messing with a bunch of multicolored wires as he spoke. “Might want to wait until she has a moment to breathe, though. Anything else I need to haul over here?”

She stood there, trying very hard to come up with something. He’d caught her watching him with interest as he lifted and carried things. It was flattering, as that sort of attention was (almost) always flattering. But like he’d told Maddie, the reporter was closer to Buck’s age than his own and he’d been with too many creeps in El Paso that wanted him because he was young to be comfortable with that. Even if the age difference between the two of them wasn’t actually that bad.

(Plus, he didn’t know if Austin would even be okay with him going to a different watering hole to drink, so to speak. He’d been fine with it before, with Attica and all of Daniel’s other failed romantic ventures. But that was before, back when they weren’t… Whatever they were now. Whatever it was they’d become over the past handful of years.)

There wasn’t anything else that needed carrying over, however disappointed Taylor was about that. Much like with the dispatch stuff, she and her camera guy could deal with hooking everything up themselves. Daniel went to where he’d left his coat and helmet, scooping both up and shrugging the former on.

Sue had said everyone was accounted for, with the three exceptions. No one here was suffering from anything worse than a minor burn or a little smoke inhalation. Surely, surely, Greenway couldn’t find a way to screw something up again.

He probably would, though.

Deciding that he’d just take the opportunity to gloat to Mehta when it happened, Daniel went to see if there was any actual firefighting that needed doing. Most of the fire was inside the building, and Daniel hadn’t been able to park their engine in a place that made for convenient hose placement, so everything was currently dependent on the building’s emergency water supply and the 118’s hoses. The latter of which were a mess that was snaking through the building’s front doors, which someone had propped open.

Austin was tracking the gauges on his engine. He didn’t look up when Daniel join him, but he still knew he was there.

“Someone from your house said Buck went to the basement with Eddie,” Austin reported.

“They like the brat more than me, I’m pretty sure,” Daniel said without an ounce of heat. Because of course they liked Buck more than Daniel. “He told them about Chimney punching me.”

The other engineer froze for a moment, frowned, looked up.

“Oh,” he said. “That’s what I forgot.”

“What did you forget?”

“Chimney said Buck threatened to kill him.”

“Buck did what!?”

“Captain Mehta!” That was Sue, making her way to where Mehta was overseeing everything. Daniel was surprised one of the chiefs hadn’t shown up to take over. “Good news, mobile dispatch is almost ready to go live.”

“Gonna have to pop that bubble,” Mehta told her. “Fire’s broke through to the third floor. That roof might be compromised, and that tower.”

He pointed at the radio tower sprouting from the top of the building.

“That’s our only connection to every truck, ladder, and ambulance in the city.” Sue’s voice was fraught with worry. “Not just downtown, either.”

“It’ll effect Valley, too?”

“All of it, the entire network. Without it, it won’t matter how many calls we take. We wouldn’t be able to send anyone to answer.”

“Alright,” Mehta breathed out. He turned his head to the 118’s ladder, where Chimney and Albert were coming down. “Hans! We need a team up on the roof to vent and to secure that radio tower.”

“Copy that,” Chimney said, then put his hand to his mouth and called out, “Ortiz!”

“Dan, you’re not doing anything.” The captain had focused on him. “Go up there with them.”

Both Hans froze, Chimney slowly turning his head to look at Daniel with wide eyes and Albert taking on an expression of pure awkwardness. The probie scurried off after a moment, muttering something about pikes.

Daniel voiced an affirmative, and went to grab a pike pole.

“Try not to Mufasa my coworker in front of witnesses,” Austin told him as he left.

“Long live the king!”

Venting was integral to fighting a structure fire. It involved making holes in the roof to let the heat and smoke escape. This, in turn, made it easier for those inside to fight the fire, and it lowered the risk of a flashover.

Honestly, Daniel was surprised there wasn’t a team already assigned to roof operations. Then again, if the fire just reached the third floor, maybe Mehta hadn’t seen a need yet. Still, it was risky to put it off, because a single busted window could give the fire a large enough influx of oxygen to cause a flashover. Many, many firefighters had died that way over the years.

“Alright, Albert.” Chimney was in the lead, poking at the roof with his pike as he went. “Once I clear this area, you’re good to go.”

It didn’t take long for Chimney to check the stability of the roof by the ladder that led to the radio tower. Once he had, Albert clambered up. The rest of the small team started putting holes into the structure.

Daniel wasn’t paying much attention to what Albert was doing, the tower wasn’t his job. But someone should’ve checked the stability of the railing surrounding it, because part of it gave and sent Albert tumbling down a couple feet. Daniel jogged over to check on him as Chimney rushed and pulled him to his feet.

“Are you okay?” Chimney frantically asked. “Are you hurt?”

“I’m fine,” Albert said.

“Are you sure? You’re not dizzy? Can you-?”

“Got all your fingers and toes?” Daniel broke in.

“Uh, yeah?” Albert blinked at him, and then started nodding. “I mean, yeah.”

“Good.” Daniel clapped him on the shoulder and then gave him a light shove forward. “Walk it off, you’ve got a job to do.”

The probie rolled his eyes in a manner far too similar to Buck at his brattiest, but the backtalk was kept to a grumble that he could pretend not to hear. Chimney watched him climb back up to the ladder in worry, and then glared up at Daniel. Daniel gave him a smile that was, perhaps, too toothy to be friendly, and went back to his task.
