The fire was right beneath their feet, its tongues licking at the edges of the holes they cut away. It was an ominous orange-white glow, and it roared. There wasn’t going to be anything left of this building, Daniel didn’t think. Maybe the outer frame, but the inside was going to be gutted.

The radio tower was anchored with straps to the outer walls. Daniel wasn’t sure what that would do if everything went, but it would at least keep it from tipping over. Hopefully.

It was put to the test just as Chimney declared they were done, the last of the straps ratcheted as tight as they could get and the roof doing a very good Swiss cheese impression, if that Swiss cheese was on fire. It started as a rumble, as a shaking beneath their feet. Then the roof started to crack and crumble between the holes they’d cut into it. The middle opened into a giant maw, sparks flying into the air.

It opened right beneath Albert’s feet. There was nothing anyone could do to catch him, all of them far enough away to put him just out of reach. His face morphed into a mask of terror as he fell, and he managed to grab hold of the edge of the hole, hands scrambling for purchase.

Chimney was there first, and he grabbed an arm to help pull up his brother. Daniel was right behind him and he gripped the probie beneath his arm pits. Together, they managed to haul Albert to safety and clear of the death trap.

Albert was shaking beneath his turnouts.

“Let’s go, let’s go, let’s go,” Daniel repeated as he dragged Albert to the ladder they used to climb up.

He’d seen the aftermath of someone falling into a fire like Albert nearly had. It was his first year and the funeral had not been open casket. He didn’t particularly want to go to another one, and he definitely didn’t want to die that way.

“The building is unstable!” Mehta shouted over the radio. “Everyone, evacuate and report in!”

“Buckley and Han, One Thirty-three, coming down,” Daniel radioed, letting Chimney report in for himself and the two from the 118 that had come with them.

It was a chorus of names and positions as they went down. He heard Buck’s voice, reporting that he and Eddie were outside, and let out a breath he wasn’t aware he was holding. When he got to the bottom, he spotted the pair of them lounging on the side of the engine with Austin not too far away.

“Pretty sure Albert needs new pants,” was how Daniel greeted the three of them. “Chimney, too, come to think of it.”

“What happened?” Eddie asked.

“The fire tried to eat him.”

He saw Buck and Eddie’s eyes go wide, and then his brother opened his mouth. No doubt to ask if Albert was okay, as if Daniel wouldn’t have led with that if he wasn’t. Probably. Before Buck could get a word out, though, their radios broke in with Lucy’s frantic voice.

“Mayday, mayday, mayday! Captain Nash is down! I need firefighters and equipment to the third floor!”

Buck and Eddie were off like a rocket, the former heading for the entrance and the throwing open a compartment to pull out a pair of halligans. Then he was sprinting off as well. Daniel was left there with a hand pointlessly held out from where he’d tried and failed to catch Buck.

He let his arm drop with a sigh.

“You’re not going in?” Austin asked.

“For Nash?” Daniel scoffed, and then scoffed louder when he saw both Hen and Chimney go in, as well. “Fuck no.”

“Your brother just went in.”

“Exactly. If I went in, I’d only start an argument with him about getting the hell out. Also, fuck Nash.”

“Careful, you almost showed growth.”

Daniel gave Austin the finger and went to see what Mehta wanted them to do now, smiling when he heard Austin’s laughter.

Waiting rooms, Buck found, were always the worst. He’d been stuck in one with Eddie for the past however long, waiting for word about Bobby. They’d managed to dig him and May out of the debris they’d been trapped in, and while she was fine – if a bit singed – he had taken a roof to his back. Considering a back injury was what caused all of his problems in Saint Paul, this was cause for concern.

Hopefully Hen would have some answers.

“So he’s good, right?” Buck asked as she joined them.

She didn’t seem worried or upset, so he assumed it was good news. Hoped, at least.

“He had some leg pain on the way in,” she said. “Might be a pinched nerve.”

“With all that he’s been through, he’s lucky that’s all it is,” Eddie said.

“I think a lot of people got lucky today,” Buck added.

“Coming through!”

That was Jonah. He appeared a moment later through the ER doors, on a gurney being pushed by Aiden and Theo, two of the 133 that Buck had not gotten particularly close to. Jonah was crouched over an unmoving woman that Buck recognized from triage, but she’d been awake and talking then. Now, Jonah was doing chest compressions as they went.

Buck, curious, found himself drawn in their wake with the others. The doctor had called it by the time he got there. He passed Jonah, who was muttering to himself,

“I should have been able to save her. I usually save them.”

That was a very weird way to phrase that.

Chapter 30

Chapter Notes

Sorry about the long wait.

See the end of the chapter for more notes

Daniel Buckley was not the shining example of a “good” person. He knew this, he accepted this. It wasn’t something that bothered him. It wasn’t something that had ever bothered him.

Dr. White liked to blame some of it on trauma. The cancer, the prostitution, Officer Drake. Complex post-traumatic stress disorder was one of the big, fancy phrases she stamped him with. Right along with antisocial personality disorder and borderline personality disorder, a veritable ABC of ways he was damaged.

He remembered getting real comfortable lying straight to adults’ faces as a kid. He just turned on the charm and smiled his way out of trouble. Hell, they loved him. It helped to play up the littlest cancer patient routine or, when that well dried up, lean into the image of a responsible young adult picking up Mom and Dad’s slack when it came to parenting their youngest.

Half the reason he managed to make it to Evan’s football games in El Paso was because he was not shy about playing the single parent card. The fact he was perfectly willing to ply his captain with mezcal didn’t hurt, either. Or, it did hurt, it just didn’t hurt him.

The shits that bullied his baby brother learned to fear him, because he didn’t feel an ounce of guilt when it came to teaching them just why his brother was off limits. Even before he started putting on muscle, he was still taller and bigger than most of them, and he used that. He didn’t shy away from violence, and he used that, too, with the older ones. The ones that were his age and made themselves feel big by tormenting kids smaller than themselves.

(Daniel took one of those online screenings for psychopathy, in the long months of the pandemic when everything was shut down. The results had not been a surprise. He didn’t mention them to anyone, not even his therapist. Not even Austin.)

Dr. White liked to blame some of how he was on the trauma, especially El Paso, but even he knew that would be a cop out. Really, all that changed after that was that, maybe, his temper was a bit shorter, a bit hotter. He didn’t put as much effort into being friendly and charming as he used to. Didn’t see the point of it, really, when there wasn’t really a need to get people to like him.

All of this to say: Good people did not take a woman’s death as an opportunity to gloat. Daniel was not a good person.

Captain Mehta already seemed done with Daniel’s shit for the day, and the shift had just started. Hell, he adopted the expression of tired long-suffering before Daniel even opened his mouth. Which told Daniel that Mehta knew exactly why he barged into the captain’s office. It didn’t stop Daniel, though.

“He fucked up,” Daniel told Mehta. “Again. And this time someone died because of it.”

“We don’t know if it’s anything he did,” the captain said.

He sounded tired.

“What the hell, Cap!?” Daniel just about shouted. “She was fine at triage. She’s dead after spending an ambulance ride with Greenway.”

“And there are plenty of reasons why that could happen.” Mehta rubbed at his face. “Accusing your coworkers of… What are you accusing him of?”

“Of being a fuck up.”

“Incompetence.” Mehta rolled his eyes a little as he translated. “That’s not a light accusation, Dan. Especially when he’s doing fine all the rest of the time.”

“He thinks he’s hot shit. That’s probably why he keeps fucking-”

“Really?”

This time it was Daniel’s turn to roll his eyes.

“Screwing up,” he said, instead.

“I’ll put him with Mendoza and Dorsey for the shift.” Oh, they were gonna kill Daniel. “Have them keep an eye on him. Huff can make do with an EMT as a partner.”

“Well I hope they can keep his incompetence from killing someone else.”

Daniel wasn’t surprised that Aiden Huff wasn’t catching Greenway’s screw ups. He’d never had a high opinion of the man. He was better than Greenway, at least, low bar that it was.

He was about to leave the office when Mehta stopped him. The captain looked hesitant when Daniel turned back around. After a moment, spent in silence with Daniel wondering if he should just leave or check the man for a complex partial seizure, Mehta finally spoke.

“Why do you care so much about this?”

Daniel scoffed.

“Because he fu- screwed up, and I told you he screwed up.”

The captain’s expression went flat.

“Right,” he said, an odd tone to his voice.

The tone was similar enough to the one Dr. White used, sometimes, that Daniel realized quickly enough what had just happened. Mehta gave him a pop quiz on empathy and he gave the wrong answer. Whatever. It wasn’t worth the effort to put on the act that everyone wanted, and they all knew what he was about, anyways.

On top of not being a good person, Daniel could be an asshole. Sometimes it was even intentional. Like now, when he opted to be the one to tell Nola and Mendoza that they were getting saddled with Greenway for the day.

“You dick.” Nola shoved at his shoulder, hard enough to rock him back on his heels. “This is your fault.”

“How?” he asked, laughing as he did.

“I don’t know, but it is.”

“We saw you go into Cap’s office,” Mendoza pointed out. “We’re not dumb.”

“I’m innocent,” Daniel lied.

The pair scoffed.

The engine needed its weekly maintenance check, so Daniel went to hunt down Albert to bully him into helping. The kid was tucked away in the corner of the rec room, fidgeting with his phone and chewing on his lip. His anxiety was palpable.

Daniel stared at him for a moment.

“Do you need a firefighter or a brother?” he asked.

Albert startled, dropping his phone in his lap.

“What?”

“Do you need a firefighter or a brother?” Daniel repeated.

“I, um. I don’t know?” Albert eyes darted away. “I’ve never quit a job before.”

At least they couldn’t blame Daniel for them losing this probie. Wait. He’d told Albert he could quit a few weeks ago, hadn’t he? Damn.

“You go to Mehta and say you quit,” he said. “Easy as that.”

“That’s it?”

“He’ll probably ask why. But if you don’t want to do the job any more, then there isn’t much he can do about it.”

“I just don’t think I’m cut out for it,” Albert told him. “And, on that roof…”

“Hey, you don’t need to justify it to me.” Daniel rubbed his knuckle into the top of the kid’s head, who yelped and ducked and slapped him away. “C’mon, might as well get it over with.”

“Yeah, okay.”

Albert pushed himself to his feet, shoving his phone into his pocket as he did. As Daniel led the way to Mehta’s office, he remembered something he needed to ask. He caught Albert at the door.

“Did Buck really threaten to kill your brother?”

“Oh, uh. Y- Yeah.”

“Huh. I should get him ice cream or something.” Daniel opened the door. “Hey, Cap, we’re losing another one. And it’s not my fault this time!”

Mehta did not look as if he believed him.

Both times they moved, Danny had been the one to find and pick out the place. Buck had never actually been involved in picking out any of the places he lived at, because he was always moving in with someone. Which sounded kinda bad, now that he thought about it like that.

Anyways, the point was that he had no idea what to look for in a place, beyond making sure Chris could get around it easily enough. And fire hazards. He could spot those easily enough after years of firefighting. Hell, he could do it by the time he graduated high school, thanks to Danny.

(It was a miracle that old apartment building didn’t burn before it could be torn down.)

A couple days after the fire at dispatch, Danny and Austin had gone looking at apartments and houses together. Buck made sure to tease his brother about the domesticity of it all, and Danny was both smug and embarrassed about it at the same time. He was neither of those things by the time they were done, though. Instead, Austin managed to somehow drive him to levels of infuriated that Buck hadn’t reached even at his most annoying.

“I didn’t know I’d be the reasonable one between us!” Danny complained, waving his arms around in his frustration. “He took us to look at a penthouse, Buck. A penthouse! In downtown!”

“Do we want to know how much that cost?” Eddie asked, sounding very much like he did want to know. He popped the cap off a bottle and set it down on the table in front of Danny.

“Ten-thousand a month.”

Buck, who’d been in the middle of drinking, choked. He was left coughing and hacking, and the front of his shirt was wet from where he’d spewed a bit.

“Ten-thousand?” Buck set his bottle down on the table and accepted the paper towels Eddie gave him to clean up. “What the fuck?”

“That’s what I said! And that was the first place he took us to.”

“How big of a trust fund does he have?” Eddie asked. His face was scrunched up and incredulous.

That had been a surprise, to learn that the Joneses were trust fund babies. Buck had wondered how Ethan and Irene managed to own a house in Los Feliz, because Irene was a librarian and while electricians could bring in a lot – he looked it up, just out of curiosity – he didn’t think Ethan was anywhere near the far end of that bell curve. The average electrician’s salary in Los Angeles was right around how much a firefighter made and Buck certainly couldn’t afford a house there. Not without signing for a really big loan.

“Enough for a really good down payment, I don’t know,” Danny said. “God, I hate him.”

His voice went all gooey as he said it.

“So much hate,” Buck teased, and grinned when his brother flipped him off. “Wait, you said that was the first place he took you to?”

“Yeah! Then he took me to a place in Little Tokyo with a fuckin’ HOA-”

“Gross,” Eddie commented.

“I dragged his ass out without even looking at the apartment. And then! The next place had a damn putting green. Why does it need its own putting green!?”

“To play golf.”

“I will punch you.”

“So you didn’t like any of the places he took you to?” Buck asked.

“No. I don’t think he was even being serious about any of them,” Danny said. “He was fucking with me, I know he was. The beautiful bastard.”

“You think he hauled you around downtown all day, looking at apartments, just to fuck with you?” Buck knew his face was expressing all the doubt he was feeling. “Would he do that?”

“Yes!”

“That seems like a waste of time,” Eddie pointed out. “Are you sure he wasn’t actually interested in any of those places?”

“Ten-thousand dollar rent,” Danny reminded them. “And it wasn’t even the most expensive apartment we looked at.”

“If he takes you to, I don’t know, some cul-de-sac in Santa Clarita, then we’ll know he’s fucking with you,” Buck said.

He watched his brother shudder like someone had just threatened him with a visitation from three ghosts. Buck honestly wanted to see what would happen if Austin tried it. Would Danny suffer through it? Would he run away? Maybe he’d spontaneously combust just from exposure to suburbia.

“He won’t get a chance to try,” Danny declared. “He wants to look at some more tomorrow, but I’m covering for someone on C-shift for half the day.”

“Did you agree to that before or after you found out about Austin’s plans?” Buck asked.

His brother just took a long draw from his drink and didn’t answer.

Austin did not have any intention to make a trip to Santa Clarita, or at least not on that day. Since they had the day off, Chris was in school, and they had nothing to do, Buck and Eddie ended up going with Austin to look at places. He carried an air of disappointment as he climbed into Buck’s jeep, having picked him up at his apartment building, and Buck tried not to be insulted by it. He’d be disappointed, too, if he was looking forward to a day spent with Eddie and got his sisters, instead.

The first house they went to was in West Adams. It was definitely a good thing that Danny wasn’t with them, because he might’ve vetoed the house the moment Buck commented about it being near to Bobby and Athena. He figured Austin was thinking the same thing, with the little laugh he let out afterwards.

There was a privacy fence around the house, a combination brick and wrought iron, along with an (open) iron gate in front of the driveway. There was plant life, too, some fruit trues he couldn’t name right away that were large enough and placed in such a way to give the impression of even more privacy. Even if they’d probably be a pain to maintain later. The house itself looked box-ish from the street. A large window was placed in the middle of the wall and its stucco siding. The front door and the porch faced the driveway, with archway entrances. There was a garage at the end of the driveway, in a similar design to the house, and a car sat in front of it.

There was a short woman standing on the porch, wearing a blue blazer with an ugly blue brooch stuck on it. She plastered on a smile when they got out, eyes darting between the three of them. The smile wobbled for a moment as her brows furrowed in confusion.

“And, uh. Which one of you is Mr. Jones?” she asked.

Buck and Eddie pointed at Austin. The lady’s smile stabilized.

She introduced herself – something that started with an M – and started her spiel about the house, but Buck ignored her in favor of looking around. It was a cute house, with a cute yard. One of the trees had a sturdy branch that almost reached an attic vent, and he wondered if it would need to be trimmed back.

She kept talking as she led them inside and gave them a short tour of the place. It felt homey. Or, it felt like it could become homey. The utility room was in an odd spot, stuck between two of the bedrooms at the end of the solitary hall, after the turn.

“I like it,” Buck said. They’d returned to the first room, the presumable living room, once Marcy had slithered off after she’d shown them everything. “It’s got a nice sized dining room.”

“Four bedrooms,” Eddie added.

“We only really need three,” Austin commented.

He was eyeing the built-in shelves flanking the fireplace.

“Three?” Buck repeated. “Wait, are you and Danny planning on sharing a room?”

“He’s warmer than my pillows,” Austin said.

“Does Danny know you’re planning on sharing a room with him?” Eddie asked.

Austin frowned, and then shrugged.

“Wow,” Buck intoned. “I’m really seeing how that mess happened, now.”

“It’s not very big,” Austin complained, changing the subject. “I can afford a bigger place.”

“Austin.” Eddie said his name slowly. “She said the homeowners were trying to sell for over a million. How much was your trust fund?”

Austin glanced at them both, and then turned away from them.

“About that much,” he finally said after a moment.

Buck choked on his own spit.

“How much money do you have?” Eddie demanded, nearly shouting.

The other man sighed. He pulled out his phone, did something with it, and then held it out for Buck to look at. The first thing he saw was that Austin had pulled up a banking app. Then he saw how much was in the account.

He screamed.

Daniel burst into the apartment, letting the door slam shut behind him. Austin, lounging on the couch as he watched television, gave him a small wave.

“You fucker!” Daniel shouted. “You- You fucker!”

“Love you, too.”

Shit, all that anger threatened to burn out at those words. He still wasn’t used to hearing it from Austin, and it turned him into sentimental goo every time. But he had to be strong this time, he had to be.

“A year! It has been a year, and you never once mentioned it!” Daniel stomped to stand between Austin and the TV. “No, worse, you lied to me about it.”

“I never said I didn’t find the treasure.”

Daniel let out a sound akin to a tea kettle.

“You asked if the chest was empty! You asked why someone would go to the trouble of burying an-” He cut himself off as his curiosity took over. “Why did you rebury an empty chest?”

His not-boyfriend shrugged.

“I thought it would be funny,” Austin said. “And it was.”

Daniel wanted to strangle the man. And also kiss him. He wanted to strangle him while kissing him. He grabbed that black-gray-white-purple throw pillow and started hitting Austin with it, instead.

“You bought me a sweater for Christmas!” Daniel said as he smacked him on the head. “A sweater!”

“That was before-” Austin scowled, grabbed the pillow, and yanked it out of Daniel’s hands. “That was before the treasure hunt.”

“But after your million dollar trust fund.” Daniel huffed and threw himself onto the couch beside Austin. “Why did that asshole’s assistant run away to the Maldives, then?”

“He had unlimited access to the estate of a guy with a lot of money and no heirs.”

Okay, yeah, that made a bit more sense.

“You bought me a sweater,” Daniel repeated.

“And now I’m buying you a house.”

That was not an argument Daniel could beat.

“How did you even find it?” he asked. “The treasure.”

“How did I find the treasure buried in the Glendale Narrows after growing up in Glendale?”

“You do not get to be sarcastic right now.” Daniel poked him with every word. “And that explains nothing.”

Austin sighed, and then he answered.

Then

The poem was awful. Austin had never read any of Hollis Harcourt’s books, and if the poem was any indicator of the quality of his prose, he never would. The poem was awful, and this treasure hunt was stupid.

Daniel and Buck and Eddie were all working together, along with that reporter lady, to find the buried chest. Austin had told Daniel that he didn’t want anything to do with it, that it wasn’t worth it, and every person they had to rescue just proved his point. But…

But.

It was kinda like a quest, wasn’t it? It was kinda like something he would throw at Daniel and the brats while DMing, complete with a puzzle to work through. Except he’d put a magic sword or helm at the end, not a chest filled with money. Magic swords were cool, and they lost their panache when you just bought them.

The poem was awful, and the clues clunky. But parts of it made Austin’s ears perk up every time he heard or read it. The river’s edge, the narrow place, a gray bridge.

Austin used to bike as a kid, way back when they had to find their own entertainment outside instead of on a screen. He had friends, but he didn’t really have friends. Not the kind that he could just randomly hang out with. Not a friend like Daniel. So he would bike up and down Glendale, until the streetlights started coming on and he needed to get home.

One of his usual routes back in the day was along the Glendale Narrows. A part of the Los Angeles River – “river” – along Griffith Park. A bit farther away than his parents were comfortable with him going, but it wasn’t like they tracked him to know where he went. There were bridges that crossed it, all of them concrete gray.

The poem was awful, and the clues clunky, but Austin didn’t think it was that easy. Surely, if it was, someone would’ve figured it out before him and found it already. Maybe they had and simply hadn’t told anyone, which would be smart. He certainly wouldn’t want that kind of publicity, or all the people that would start bothering him if they knew about it.

It was simple curiosity that had Austin getting into his car one evening, while Daniel was with his brother, and driving to the Narrows. He stopped at the end of one of the bridges, grabbed a flashlight, and got out. A gate with herons on it blocked his way.

Austin stared at it, then at the sorry excuses of islands in the river. There were trees on them, and he thought they might’ve been willows. But it could not be that easy.

Except it was that easy. He made a quick trip to grab a shovel and a bag, and made his way to one of the islands. He’d spotted disturbed dirt from the bridge, obvious for anyone looking.

What was he going to do with $5,000,000?

Chapter End Notes

Teehee

Chapter 31

Evan Buckley was not a fan of flying. It wasn’t a fear, he was always quick to clarify that, but he did not like it. It was a ride on a crowded tube that flies thanks to a loophole in physics, book ended with what could conceivably be the actual First Circle of Hell. There was nothing enjoyable about it. He much preferred going on road trips, even if he did have a habit of making random stops and going off on tangents because he saw a billboard advertising a neat looking roadside attraction.

But it was more than twelve hours from Los Angeles to El Paso, and not everyone was up for that kind of drive. Especially since they needed to be in El Paso tonight, and they’d had Claudette’s funeral that morning. That was not a forgiving window of time.

Eddie wasn’t looking forward to this any more than Buck was. He was packing for Chris with the air of a soldier given an hour’s warning that he was being shipped off, grabbing and throwing things into a bag without much attention to what they were. Chris was nominally helping, but-

“Chris! Hurry up!” Eddie bellowed towards the bathroom, sticking his head out into the hallway. “We still got to pick up Tia Pepa!”

He pulled back into the room and shook his head in exasperation.

“It should not take that long to pack a toothbrush,” Eddie said to Buck.

“We could just get a later flight,” Buck told him. He was already packed and ready, and was entertaining himself with a tiny dinosaur figurine that usually sat on Chris’ desk. “You were at a funeral, your parents will understand.”

Eddie yanked open the doors to Chris’ wardrobe, and then simply gave Buck a look. He didn’t say anything, but Buck nodded in understanding all the same.

“Right, my bad,” he said. “Forgot who we were talking about.”

“What’s the dress code at a retirement party, anyway? A tie is probably too much, but it is my parents we’re talking about. Screw it, I’ll just grab it all.”

Eddie reached into the wardrobe and pulled out a bunch of button down shirts that had been sitting, folded, on the top shelf.

“We don’t have to go, Eds,” Buck pointed out.

“You don’t,” Eddie countered. “Mom’s been making this huge deal about the party. He worked for that company for forty years, and now he’s retiring.”

He gathered up the clothes he’d been pulling out and tossing onto the bed, transferring them into the unzipped suitcase with a bit more care.

“And Chris wants to see everyone,” he added. “He hasn’t seen them since…”

Since Eddie’s family had essentially disowned him for being with Buck. Buck didn’t get it, his boyfriend being so ready and willing to go back to them, to celebrate something with them, after such a long silence. He knew it had worn away at Eddie, but he also knew the phone calls with them these past months hadn’t been much better.

“Dad won’t know what to do with himself, now that he’ll be home for longer than a day.”

Buck cringed.

“Listen,” he said. “As someone who also comes from a screwed-up family-”

“My family isn’t screwed up,” Eddie immediately argued. Buck just looked at him. “It’s just my dad-”

“Bullshit. I’ve sat through dinners with your mom.”

Eddie glowered at him, but he didn’t try to keep arguing.

“The plan is to get in, get out, no drama. Don’t.” He fixed Buck with a finger, cutting off what he’d been about to say. “We’re just gonna say congratulations, have some cake, talk to Abuela, and call it a day.”

“And when your parents start to get in there and push your buttons? Because they will.”

“There’s a hotel not that far away. Three-and-a-half stars, and it even has a pool.”

“Why aren’t we staying there?”

“Because staying at the house saves money and Mom’s already told me we’re staying there,” Eddie said. Again, Buck gave him a look. “Okay, fine, yes, she said me and Chris. But you’re included in the package, whether they like it or not.”

“It’ll be just like old times,” Buck said. “Except now we won’t have to pretend I’m sleeping on the floor.”

“I think they’ve got my old sleeping bag, you can still sleep on the floor.” Eddie gave him a beaming smile as he zipped up the suitcase and hefted it up. “Stop playing with toys and go see what’s taking Chris so long?”

Buck groaned, but he set the dinosaur down and went to check all the same.

There were many things about Station 118 that Austin Jones disliked. The glass walls for the locker room, for one, and the warehouse feel of the building for another. But chief among them all was the bay door. The bay door.

Sure, there was a side entrance, but it was so awkwardly placed that there was no way to maneuver the trucks through it. The ambulances might’ve been capable of it, but there was no way that Austin could manage it with the engine. Which left him awkwardly backing through the lone door in the front, doing his best to park in a way that the ladder had room to back in, as well.

It was a pain in the fucking ass.

The floater captain that was filling in, since everyone was at that dispatcher’s funeral, was also ass at directing. Austin mostly eyeballed it, managing to avoid hitting the gear cages or any columns. When the ladder pulled in beside him, he caught Javier rolling his eyes at the mirrors when he thought no one was looking.

He’d just turned off the engine when, for some reason, Hen and Chimney came out of the captain’s office. They strolled past like it was business as usual, Hen clutching folders to her chest. Ravi called out to them as he got out, and Austin watched the pair freeze in place before turning to face Ravi and Lucy.

Austin set his elbow on his door and watched their reflections in the side view mirror.

“Thought you guys were off today,” Ravi stated.

“Yeah,” Chimney said as the paramedics turned around. “We are.”

“Then what are you doing here?” Lucy laughed a little as she said it. “And what’s with all the 902Ms?”

Those were the paramedic incident reports. They needed to be filled out after every call. Austin sat through Daniel complaining about them more than once, even though he rarely had to be responsible for them.

“Oh.” Hen glanced down at the folders in her arms and then back up to the firefighters, like she hadn’t realized they were clutched to her chest. “We were just, uh, working on…”

“A special project,” Chimney jumped in to say. “For the mayor.”

That man desperately needed to put more points into his deception skill.

“Seriously?” Austin couldn’t tell by his tone whether Ravi believed him or not. For his sake, Austin hoped the kid was smarter than that. “What kind of project?”

Okay, yeah, the kid was smarter than that.

“Beta testing a new piece of equipment for us to use in the field,” Hen explained, every word complete bullshit. Austin caught Lucy and Ravi exchanging glances as she spoke. “We just need to, uh, go over some of the data from out transfer forms.”

“Just need data,” Chimney echoed with a nod.

Points for the improv. Still didn’t quite pass that ability check.

“Data.” Lucy’s shoulders wiggled a bit. “Sounds official.”

“Well, we better get going,” Hen hurriedly said. “The mayor’s office is gonna want that report ASAP. Have a great shift.”

“See ya!” Chimney added.

The pair skittered away, heading for that side entrance that was utterly unusable for the trucks. Austin popped open the door finally and climbed out of the truck. He saw Lucy turn to Ravi and heard her ask,

“What do you think they’re hiding?”

“’Round here, I find it best not to know,” Ravi told her.

A pathetic attitude to have, in Austin’s opinion. The drama the people in this station got up to was enough to entertain someone for entire shifts at a time. Whatever it was, though, it was probably extremely stupid.

Austin was fairly certain they weren’t supposed to just take 902Ms out of the office. He was also fairly certain that, on the very small chance that their story about the secret project for the mayor’s office was true, it would be someone from the mayor’s office or someone from the administrative side of the department that would be collecting those files. And he figured it was an easy bet that that the captain would cover for them if he made any anonymous complaints.

He should tell Dan about it, he’d probably find it just as stupid.

It was a minor call in the grand scheme of things. The most interesting part of it, in Daniel’s opinion, was the odd Rube Goldberg-like series of events that ended with them arriving at the quaint little house. Dispatch, in the middle of transferring to their temporary location, hadn’t been very clear about the details. They’d only been able to tell him that there was something about marbles, a rolling pin, and a cat. A very angry cat that didn’t even belong to the family living in the house.

Mehta had stayed behind at the station for this call, using the excuse of wanting to catch up on the backlog of reports from that morning. That meant Daniel was the person in charge for the scene, because the Los Angeles Fire Department did not have lieutenants, leaving the engineer and apparatus operators as the next in line in rank. Daniel should’ve insisted on the ladder coming along, even if it would have served no purpose. At least then he’d be able to shove the responsibility of command onto Flowers. Not that there was much of anything to really shove onto anyone else at the moment.

They were waiting on animal control to arrive right now. The house had been surrendered to the cat, which was hissing and spitting and clawing at anything that could bleed. Daniel called for a tactical retreat after the damn thing tried to go for Corine’s face, and none of them were brave enough to attempt a counterattack to regain lost ground.

The only person severely injured was a lady who’d dislocated her shoulder when she slipped on the marbles. She was currently laying on the gurney as she waited to be loaded up, arm in a sling and ice pack to her shoulder. The other injuries mainly consisted of scratches from the psycho-kitty, and Aiden was patching those up.

Mainly, Daniel’s attention was focused on Jonah. Once again partnered with Aiden, he was getting the lady’s information down. Was, past tense, because suddenly he was tucking the clipboard he was using under his arm and saying,

“Why don’t I give you something for the pain?”

He reached into his pocket and- What the fuck? Why was he carrying around a syringe and vial in his pocket? Daniel found himself striding across the space separating them, reaching the pair just as the lady asked him,

“You sure that’s okay?”

“Trust me,” Jonah told her, smarmy smile in place.

“Does she need that?” Daniel got the pleasure of seeing the paramedic leap out of his skin. “How bad’s your pain?”

“Uh.” The lady winced. “Better than it was, but it’s still sore.”

“Yeah, it will be for a while. If the hospital prescribes you anything for it, it’ll just be some heavy duty ibuprofen at most.” Daniel gave her his brightest, most charming smile. The one that had everyone back in Hershey believing he was an innocent angel, and which reeled people in when they still lived in El Paso. “The good stuff comes with some drawbacks, so we try to save it for when people really need it. Paramedic Greenway was just being a bit too enthusiastic about patient comfort.”

His smile didn’t falter at all as he turned it onto Jonah, but he saw the paramedic’s start to crack and strain. He stuck around chatting with the lady until Aiden was done and she was ready to be loaded up, then managed to talk Aiden into being the one to ride in the back with her. Jonah seemed particularly put out to be stuck driving, and Daniel caught the glares the paramedic was sending his way when he thought Daniel wasn’t paying attention.

Daniel wondered about the vial that Jonah was evidently walking around with in his pants pocket. It was probably ketorolac, because ketorolac was an anti-inflammatory and that was the common sense drug to use in that situation. If you were an idiot that would waste ketorolac to treat an achy shoulder, but what kind of paramedic would do that?

Jonah would, apparently. He really was as incompetent as Daniel thought he was. But assuming that it was ketorolac and not some other analgesic, why would Jonah be carrying a vial of it around in his pants pocket? And where did he get it from?

Feeling like a dog with a bone, Daniel inventoried the meds they had on hand when they returned to the station. Mendoza and Nola both looked at him funny when they found him doing it, since it was not only not his job, but had already been done that day. They helped him without much question, all the same.

They started with the ketorolac, only to find that all of it was accounted for. So Daniel checked their morphine, their fentanyl, their dilaudid and their ketamine. All of it, every vial, was accounted for. The same was true of the drugs on Aiden’s ambulance, when Daniel inventoried them on their return, and the medkits that Aiden and Jonah used.

So Jonah was carrying around a vial of some kind of analgesic, presumably, but not a vial that came from their stock. Which raised some concerning questions. But when Daniel brought those questions to Mehta, and Mehta brought those questions to Jonah, the paramedic didn’t have anything except his phone in his pockets. There wasn’t even anything suspicious in the guy’s locker, when Daniel snooped through it that night.

Which was simply annoying.

The sun was well below the horizon, just barely turning the sky oranges and pinks, by the time Eddie brought the car to a stop in front of his childhood home. Abuela came bursting out of the front door almost the moment he did so. Tia Pepa, sitting in the front passenger sit of the rental, let out a sigh.

“Something’s wrong,” she announced.

“I haven’t even shut off the engine,” Eddie complained.

They climbed out, Buck unfolding himself from the backseat with a groan. Abuela greeted them with hugs and kisses. She even had one of each for Buck, which had him bashful.

“¿Que paso?” Pepa asked.

“Nada,” Abuela replied, waving it away. She looked past Eddie to the car. “Where’s Christopher?”

“Uh, asleep.” Buck plucked at the fabric of his shirt, wet patch on the sleeve. “He didn’t even wake up when I moved.”

“Ay, pobrecito mi angelito.”

“It’s good to see you again, Abuelita,” Eddie told her. “LA feels a little less like home witthout you.”

“Now what’s wrong?” Pepa asked. “You came stomping out like you couldn’t be away from them fast enough.”

“It’s Helena.” Abuela didn’t quite roll her eyes, but Eddie could tell she wanted to. “She’s in there doing all the cooking.”

“Hold on,” Buck said. “All the cooking? For the entire party?”

“I thought my fancy brother hired caterers for his big parranda,” Pepa said.

“They’re only allowed to warm and serve the food,” Abuela said. “Helena insisted on making everything herself.”

“Everything,” Buck repeated, disappointment tinting his words.

He had not been quiet about how much he was looking forward to Abuela’s cooking again.

“You didn’t help out at all?” Eddie asked, because he knew his abuela.

Her lips curved into a smile.

“I was allowed to make the tamales,” she said.

Pepa’s “ay, gracias,” was drowned out by Buck’s, “oh, thank God.” Eddie was still laughing as he got Chris out of the car, the kid grumbling and rubbing the sleep out of his eyes all the way up to the door. Buck carried their bags, all of them, despite Eddie offering to at least take his own.

Dad was around here somewhere, but Eddie didn’t see him right off. He was hesitant to leave Buck alone in the house when he wasn’t sure where his father was, but he knew both Pepa and Abuela would keep his father from saying anything impolite. Or at least, he could trust them to jump down his throat if – when – he did.

The kitchen wasn’t quite a mess, but it was filled with food. It covered the kitchen table and the counters, taking up every inch of available space. His mother was at the stove, still, something sizzling quietly in a skillet.

“Come on, Mom,” Eddie said as he entered fully into the room, pausing at the fridge to get a cold drink. He twisted the lid off, tossing it into the trash can that hadn’t moved from the corner in twenty years. “Take a break.”

“Leave me alone. I’m almost done.”

“Everything looks perfect. And I’m sure it tastes great, too.”

Eddie reached for one of the foods sitting out, only for her to smack his hand away like he was five again. He smiled at her and she shook her head, grabbing a nearby towel to wipe off her hands.

“It’s good to have my boys back home,” she said.

“It’s been too long.” There Dad was, joining them in the kitchen. “You should come visit more often.”

There were moments in Eddie’s life where he would stop and ask himself, what would Danny do? It rarely steered him wrong. So he asked himself, now, what Danny would do if he were here.

The answer was not a diplomatic one.

“You both spent two years pretending I didn’t exist,” Eddie reminded them, tone purposefully casual. He saw his mother tense up as she turned back to the stove. His father’s face twisted into a scowl. “I didn’t have much incentive to come visit.”

“Well.” His mother awkwardly stirred the contents of a pot. “Your father’s retired now, we’ll have time to visit more.”

“Yeah. Can’t believe it’s actually happening.”

He figured his father would keel over before he accepted retirement.

“Is…” Dad looked uncomfortable. “Is he here?”

“You mean Evan? My boyfriend?” Eddie took a swig of his drink as he stared his father down. “Of course he is. I’m here, aren’t I?”

“Ramon,” his mother suddenly said. Her voice was strained. “Show him the watch.”

“The watch?” his dad said. “It’s nothing.”

“No, no, show him. It was such a nice gesture.”

Eddie watched his father’s shoulder slump with a quiet groan. He turned to the counter behind him, picking up a black cube box that he passed to Eddie. Eddie opened it, finding a very fancy watch inside, along with a card with the silhouette of a pumpjack.

He opened the card and read aloud.

“In recognition of your years of service helping us reach new horizons for our company, and for all of our stakeholders, the board thanks you. From our family to yours.”

“It’s not quite a Silver Star,” his father said. “But it’s- it’s something, I guess.”

“You didn’t have to get shot or have any friends die to get it.” Eddie tucked the card back into the box, and then held the box out for his father to take. “Yeah, it’s something, I guess. Congratulations, Dad.”

His father took the box back, clutching it between his hands.

“How is…” His father cleared his throat. “How’s life as an operator?”

Eddie took another, longer draw of his drink.

Chapter 32

Chapter Notes

If I don't keep a tight enough leash on Daniel and Austin, they're liable to take over the story entirely.

Note: The beginning of this chapter overlaps with the last chapter a bit, timeline wise.

“Tell me about Jonah Greenway.”

Daniel was not expecting for that to be how Henrietta Wilson greeted him when he answered the call. More to the point, he hadn’t expected Hen to call him at all. He’d answered, despite it not being a number he recognized, because he’d been a firefighter for too long to risk it. It was an LA area code, so it could’ve been someone calling to tell him that something had happened to Maddie or Austin.

But no, it was just Hen. This part of the station was close to abandoned after dinner, but Daniel still looked around to make sure Jonah wasn’t nearby. He even went and peeked around doorways, because he’d been friends with Austin for too long. He stuck the phone between his ear and his shoulder, freeing up his hands so he could continue rinsing dishes and stacking them in the dishwasher, and asked Hen,

“Why are you asking about him?”

“Uh. I’m just curious.”

“Uh-huh. And how is this related to you walking out with some 902Ms today?”

“Of course Jones told you about that,” she said, and then in reply to something said in the background that Daniel couldn’t quite make out, “He backed the engine into the station, obviously he was there.”

“You guys really need to work on your lying,” Daniel advised. He tried to find a spot for a pot in the dishwasher, finally just sticking it on top of everything else. Let God decide. “Austin’s sister works at the mayor’s office.”

“Well, we weren’t lying to him, were we? But come on, Jonah Greenway.”

“He’s crap,” was Daniel’s blunt answer. “I said he was gonna get someone killed.”

There was silence on the other end, heavy and charged. And then Hen asked,

“What do you mean?”

“I mean that he’s fuckin’…” Daniel was going to say ‘incompetent,’ but then he remembered the incident with the vial earlier that day. That didn’t speak to incompetence, that… That spoke to something else. “He got left alone with a patient, and that patient crashed for no reason. I thought he just gave the guy the wrong thing, or too much of the right thing.”

“You thought,” she repeated.

Daniel made another look around for eavesdroppers before answering.

“He’s carrying vials around in his pocket.”

“Vials? Vials of what?”

“Hell if I know.” Dishwashing liquid went into the dispenser, and then the dishwasher door was closed. It rumbled q-wordly when Daniel started it. “I thought it was ketorolac. The lady had dislocated her shoulder and he was offering to help with the pain.”

“You thought,” Hen said again. “Was it not ketorolac?”

“I don’t know what it was. It wasn’t anything from our stock, I checked, and he didn’t have it on him when Mehta made him turn out his pockets.”

Jonah had been side-eyeing Daniel ever since. Not that Daniel cared. What was the guy gonna do, kidnap him and kill him?

“That’s… That’s creepy, Dan.”

“He’s creepy. Didn’t you notice when he was at the One Eighteen?”

“Notice what?”

“The guy’s smile,” Daniel said. “The only time I’ve seen him give a real smile was when he was getting praise for saving someone or when he got to be important for a hot second at the dispatch fire. All the rest of the time, it’s… The guy’s figured out the eyes, but his smiles are still pretty empty. Flat.”

“I could ask,” Hen said. “But I know better than that. Thanks for the info, Dan.”

She hung up, and pretty hurriedly.

Daniel’s shift didn’t end until the next morning. He texted Austin and Buck, made a fruitless search of Jonah’s locker, and caught a solid six hours of sleep between calls. He went home to the empty apartment, expecting to suffer through a lonely morning until he finally broke and went to Austin’s.

He was not expecting to find Austin waiting outside his apartment when he stepped out of the elevator.

“Oh,” was all Daniel could say at first, before he could manage a, “I thought you’d…”

“You said you needed to do laundry.”

Daniel laughed a little as he dug out his keys to unlock the door.

“So, what? Did you come here to watch?”

The other man shrugged.

The apartment was empty. Maddie wasn’t in, but Daniel wasn’t expecting her to be. She’d shot him a text that morning, saying she was going to have Jee-Yun for the day. In turn, he requested there be some quality uncle time at some point in the day. Her reply was a question of whether or not that included Austin, and Daniel sort of short circuited every time he read it.

“Do you have anything to make for breakfast?” Austin asked, taking off his shoes and making a line for the fridge.

“Uh, we should have eggs and bacon, and some frozen waffles.” Daniel hung up his jacket – everyone was regularly making fun of him, now, for how often he wore it – and then found himself needing to shuffle his sister’s laundry over to the dryer before he could dump anything into the washer. “Maddie mentioned we were out of bread and cereal.”

“I’ve seen the cereal you eat,” Austin remarked as he pulled out everything he needed to make breakfast. “You might as well just pour yourself a bowl of sugar. Or Skittles.”

“I’d eat Skittles cereal.”

“I know.”

Austin’s voice was filled with fondness, and it had Daniel fleeing into his bedroom with the excuse of gathering up his laundry. Silence filled the apartment as they worked, only occasionally broken by the sound of something popping on the stove and change falling out of pants pockets when Daniel shook them. It was comfortable, and so close to that ideal world he thought he’d never have, that ideal world he was going to have.

Honestly, sometimes he was surprised that Austin wasn’t spending his millions of dollars on a cabin in the woods. The guy liked his space, and had mentioned more than once that he would sometimes get irrationally irritated with people in his proximity. They didn’t even have to be doing anything particularly annoying, but simply talking too much or too loudly, or just constantly moving.

Daniel understood, without it being said, that Buck probably sparked it more often than not. But for once, he didn’t get as upset by the implication as he might have if it was coming from someone else. Probably because Austin never actually complained about Buck. He never tried to make Buck smaller or lesser for his own convenience, like so many people had done when Buck was growing up. As far as Daniel was aware, whenever it happened, Austin either made himself scarce if he could, or simply toughed it out if he couldn’t.

(He remembered when Buck was a kid, how he would sometimes just erupt. They weren’t tantrums, they weren’t because Buck wasn’t getting his way. It just seemed like sometimes everything got to be too much and it would come bursting out, like a shaken can of soda. They would pass, and Buck would be embarrassed about it. Something not helped by how Mom and Dad always reacted when they happened, whether they were there for the eruption or had heard about it secondhand from another adult.)

“Are you sure about us sharing a room?” Daniel blurted out, once the washer was going and Austin was plating up the food. Daniel had changed into a pair of running shorts and one of Austin’s sweaters, the civvies he wore home getting tossed into the wash as well. “I can sleep on my own, I’m not that clingy.”

Back in the day, he preferred sleeping on his own and being able to spread out across the bed. That was before he’d learn how nice it was to have someone wrap around him like a particularly handsy octopus. He didn’t know why some guys balked at being the little spoon, ‘cause it was really nice.

But it wasn’t like Daniel was going to die because the other side of the bed was empty and cold. He managed it on the nights he didn’t spend at Austin’s, after all. Honestly, just having everyone under one roof was enough for him to be content.

“Do you want your own room?” Austin asked.

“That’s not what I said,” Daniel quickly corrected. Because if the option to share a bed with Austin every night was being offered to him, then he was taking it. He was psychotic, not stupid. “But I know how you are about needing your space to, like. Decompress or whatever.”

“Oh.” Austin shrugged and sat down at the kitchen table. “You’re fine, you don’t count.”

Daniel swallowed the forkful of egg he’d just shoveled into his mouth.

“I don’t count?”

“That’s what I said. What do you call me?”

The question came so far out of left field that it didn’t even register with Daniel at first. It took a moment for his brain to process it, though understanding eluded him.

“What do I call you…?”

“When talking to other people.”

“Uh, your name?” Daniel picked up a slice of bacon to nibble at. “What else would I call you…?”

“Buck mentioned-”

“Oh, God,” Daniel sighed. “It’s the vegetable thing.”

“I think zucchini’s a fruit.”

“Whatever. I don’t call you a fruit.” Daniel paused and grinned. It got wider when Austin preemptively rolled his eyes. “I mean, sometimes I call you a fruit.”

“That would be you,” Austin countered without missing a beat. “But Buck mentioned something, the morning before the fire at dispatch. I know what we’re not, but what are we?”

“I- I don’t...”

Daniel didn’t know how to answer that, because he didn’t have an answer. He knew what they weren’t – God, did he know – but he had no idea how to define what they were. Every time he tried to come up with something suitable, he immediately dismissed it as not being the right fit. It left him feeling like Goldilocks.

“Do… Do we need to have a label?” Immediately after the words left his mouth, he groaned and covered his face with his hands. “I did not just say that. When did I become that asshole?”

“Apparently other people have given us a label,” Austin said. He did not seem very bothered by this, but he rarely seemed bothered by anything. “Or at least Hen and your brother have.”

“I’m gonna regret asking this,” Daniel said. He dropped his hands and picked up his fork again. “But what label did they give us?”

Austin opened his mouth, but his answer was preempted by knocking at the door. Daniel was very tempted to ignore it, because it only took a moment to think of and then cross off anyone important that it could be. But whoever was knocking was not showing any intention of stopping any time soon. So, with a sigh, he went to the door and peeked through the peephole.

He immediately grabbed his bat and threw the door open.

“What?” Daniel snapped out. Chimney was forced back by jabs to the chest with the bat. Hen was frozen in shock, fist still raised to knock. “What the hell do you want?”

“I told you this was a bad idea,” Chimney whined to his friend.

“I- I know it’s early,” Hen started.

Daniel cut her off, asking,

“Why is he here?”

“We’re- We’re here because, um.” Hen kept looking at her friend, clearly no longer as sure about what they were doing as when she was trying to batter down the door. “Because we want to watch the news footage from the call canter.”

“...and?”

“And you’re friends with Taylor Kelly. We want to see what happened before Claudette coded.”

Daniel stared at her, making no effort to hide his incredulity. A glance back to Austin showed the man had moved from his seat to just a couple feet away. Close enough to intervene if something happened.

“Should make that one stay in the hall,” Daniel muttered, but he opened the door wider and let them both in.

Chimney entered with far less confidence than Hen, creeping over the threshold and then freezing when he realized he had both Daniel’s and Austin’s gazes locked on him.

“H- Hi, Jones.” The paramedic’s smile was too big, too bright, too cheerful. “Fancy seeing you here.”

Austin replied with silence.

“I have to grab my phone,” Daniel said.

He passed Austin the bat as he went by, the man taking it without comment. Chimney bumped into the table by the door in his effort to put more space between himself and the two people in the apartment who did not particularly like him in that moment.

Daniel’s phone was sitting on his bed. He plugged it up to charge as he was gathering his laundry and left it there. Now he picked it up and scrolled through his contacts until he found Taylor, swiped his thumb over her name, and then waited for her to answer.

“This is far too early for a booty call.”

“Nah, I’d wine and dine ya first. Or at least buy you a Lunchable,” he told her. “A couple of Buck’s coworkers want to see what happened before that dispatcher died.”

“Claudette Collins?” Fabric moved against fabric on the other end of the line; she must have been still in bed when he called. “Why? Are they trying to get ahead of an inquiry?”

“Come over and find out.” He grinned when she groaned. “Hey, if there’s something worth making a story out of, you’ll be the first to get the scoop or whatever. And I’ll make you breakfast. We’ve got frozen waffles.”

He said the last bit in a sing-song voice. She sighed in his ear, and then she agreed.

The energy in the living room was awkward when he walked out of his bedroom. The three of them were standing and staring at each other, with the only one not showing any unease being Austin. He was still staring down Chimney, bat gripped in both hands, while Chimney was still hovering by the door. Daniel was willing to bet Austin hadn’t taken his eyes off the man the entire time it took him to call Taylor, and probably hadn’t said a single word, either.

“She’s on her way,” he declared. Then, because everyone there knew and he simply couldn’t help himself, he said, “But I promised her sex, so I hope one of you brought condoms. I’m all out.”

Hen and Chimney’s reactions were exactly what he was hoping for: horrified sputtering with stammered attempts to assure him that that wasn’t needed, that they could get the footage some other way or even make do without it. Austin’s reaction was just to give him the flattest of looks and say,

“There is no way she’s that desperate to get laid.”

“Are you saying I’m not good looking enough to be extorted for sex?” Daniel asked, playing up the hurt.

The exchange seemed to only further Hen and Chimney’s horror.

“Yes,” was Austin’s blunt response.

“Rude and hateful.” Daniel crossed the room, passing by Austin close enough to brush against him. “I did promise her breakfast, though. Hen, do you want anything? We have…”

He trailed off and looked to Austin.

“I used the last of the bacon and eggs.”

“We have frozen waffles,” Daniel finished.

“No thanks,” Hen weakly said. “I’m good.”

Beside her, Chimney appeared conflicted. Daniel was willing to bet he was torn between offense at the very obvious slight of not being included in the offer, and keeping his mouth shut to avoid drawing anyone’s ire. It was very different from the last time Chimney was in the apartment, when Daniel was alone and the bat was out of reach, and Chimney felt no fear about letting Daniel bear the brunt of his anger.

Daniel didn’t know where Taylor lived. He thought it might’ve been somewhere downtown, so not far from the apartment, but he wasn’t sure. What he did know was that heating up the waffles now would be dumb when they might be cold by the time she arrived. So he grabbed his forgotten plate of breakfast and leaned back against the kitchen counter, where he could keep an eye on his guests while he ate and there was a table between himself and Chimney.

Austin silently stared Chimney down the entire time they spent waiting for Taylor, to the man’s increasingly apparent distress. When she arrived, she announced herself with more restrained knocking than Hen had. Chimney still leaped out of his skin, actually lifting an inch or two off the ground. He fumbled for the door handle to let Taylor in, a task made more difficult by the way he was reluctant to take his eyes off the bat in Austin’s hands.

The reporter came in with a laptop bag hanging from her shoulder and a to-go cup of coffee in her hand, all made up and put together to face the day. She glanced about, from Chimney, to Hen, and finally to Austin. She pointed at the latter with a finger that ended in a manicured nail.

“Let me guess,” she said. “The mysterious totally-not-a-boyfriend that keeps being mentioned.”

Austin finally took his eyes of Chimney, turning his head to Daniel with a frown.

“We didn’t decide what we were settling on,” he said.

“Seriously, does it matter?” Daniel asked.

He ignored the way the other three were playing audience to the exchange..

“I kinda liked zucchini.”

“Of course you do. Christ.”

Daniel set his now empty plate into the sink next to Austin’s, then pulled a pair of frozen waffles out of the freezer and jammed them into the toaster. He’d promised Taylor breakfast, after all. He didn’t promise her a continental breakfast, however.

“So, what am I doing here?” Taylor had moved to the coffee table and was setting up. She stopped short of the TV, a cord in her hand that led to the laptop. “No, more pressing question: Dan, how old is this thing?”

“Hey.” Daniel opened the fridge and scowled at her over the door. “It works.”

“So does the Electoral College. Allegedly.” She knelt by the TV and started feeling around, letting out a sound of triumph when she found whatever she was looking for and jabbed the cord into it. “That doesn’t mean it shouldn’t have been replaced a century ago.”

“I am making you breakfast.”

“You’re heating up frozen waffles.” She raked her gaze over his bare legs. “I like the shorts.”

He glanced down at his shorts, which stopped long before they reached his knees, and gave her his brightest smile.

“Thank you.”

“Why do you need the footage from the fire?” Taylor asked Hen and Chimney. “Dan said it had something to do with the dispatcher who died.”

“We’re just…” Hen said, then exchanged glances with Chimney before finishing. “Testing a theory.”

Taylor’s “right” was full of unspoken suspicion.

It didn’t take the reporter long to get the footage pulled up onto the TV. By that point, the waffles were done. Daniel, because he was a good host and a gentleman, cut them up and drizzled them in syrup before taking the plate and a fork to Taylor on the couch.

Maddie, if she were here, would fuss about people eating on the couch and not the table, something she got from Mom. Daniel didn’t really care. Hell, the couch came pre-stained; he’d managed to get most of them out.

On the screen was close-up footage of Jonah Greenway. He was chatting with Claudette, heavy with the superficial charm. Claudette was laying on one of the cots in the triage that had been set up in the parking garage and she had an oxygen mask pressed to her face.

Daniel saw something on the screen that caught his attention.

“Pause it,” he told Taylor.

The reporter sent him a questioning frown, but did so all the same. The frame it was stopped on showed Jonah injecting Claudette’s IV with a syringe of something.

“There’s no reason for him to be doing that,” he said, more for Taylor and Austin’s benefit. Or, he hoped it was only for their benefit. If this was new information for Chimney and Hen, after being paramedics far longer than him, there was no helping them. “She’s awake, she’s talking, she’s not showing any discomfort.”

“Except for the coughing,” Austin amended. He was standing beside Daniel

“Yeah, yeah, except for that.” Daniel was resting his weight against the back of the couch, so he bumped the other man with his hip. “But you just give ‘em O2 for that.”

And O2 wasn’t injected intravenously unless you were a really bad medic.

“What do you think that is?” Chimney asked. He was huddled on the far end of the couch, but he leaned forward for a closer look. The footage didn’t show the syringe’s label well enough to read. “Potassium?”

“I’m not sure,” Hen said.

“What is this about?” Austin asked. “I know Dan thinks the guy’s bad at his job, but this seems a bit more serious.”

“We’re not sure,” Chimney repeated, with a meaningful nudge of his head in Taylor’s direction.

Daniel rolled his eyes. He didn’t know either, but he could make a guess.

“It’s like I said,” he told Austin and Taylor. “She was fine, except for the smoke. There wasn’t a need to push drugs of any kind.”

“Wait.” Taylor’s head swiveled as she looked between all of them. “Her death wasn’t related to her injuries?

“Taylor,” Hen started.

The reporter cut her off with an aggravated sigh,

“Off the record,” she said.

Daniel wasn’t certain how that worked. Was it like doctor/patient confidentiality? Attorney privilege? How binding of a phrase was it? For a trustworthy reporter with strong morals, it was probably akin to a paladin’s oath. But he didn’t think Taylor Kelly fit that description.

He kept his mouth shut; Hen and Chimney could figure that out on their own.

“Four times, counting what Dan told me last night, a stable patient crashed after he was left alone with them. Those are the times we know about,” Hen said, taking that metaphorical leap. “Two of them have died.”

“Whatever he gave Claudette,” Chimney said. “It caused her heart attack.”

“So maybe ‘bad at his job’ is an understatement,” Daniel said. “Maybe ‘very bad at his job’ would be better.”

“Or.” Taylor’s voice trembled with excitement. “Maybe he’s a serial killer.”

Daniel didn’t think she was gonna honor that promise of keeping this off the record.

Chapter 33

Chapter Notes

When ADHD meds are working, they can make you hypfocused. But!☝ You don't get to choose what you're hyperfocused on.

cw: Daniel makes references to his time as a sex-worker.

The Diazes’ yard was decorated for a party. There was a huge awning, with balloons wrapped around the supports. A large handmade sign, reading, “Happy Retirement, Ramon” hung from it. In the shade of the awning were tables laden down with food, including a large cake with an oil pump on top.

Buck had not expected to receive a warm reception from Eddie’s family on this trip. Isabel not withstanding, his expectations had been met the first night. Helena and Ramon did their best to pretend that he didn’t exist, the pair of them barely acknowledging him unless someone made them acknowledge him. They definitely weren’t in the accepting camp, just frightened by last year’s reminder that their son was mortal.

He didn’t think the rest of the Diazes would be any better, barring any of Pepa’s that might show up, and was fully prepared for a day spent awkwardly hanging around with Isabel and Pepa. Except, when people started showing up for Ramon’s, the ones that recognized him were happy to see him, while the one’s didn’t and only knew him as Eddie’s plus-one all greeted with polite cheer. Even Sophia and Adriana, despite contact between them and Eddie having been patchy, so to speak, seemed happy to see him.

So, instead of being the awkward wallflower that he was expecting, Buck found himself being drifting from one conversation to the next. He ended up losing track of Eddie at one point, somewhere in the middle of catching up with one of the cousins their age and being accosted by a troupe of children. It was so similar to the last parranda held here, their combined birthday, that Buck decided to find wherever his boyfriend had ended up, even though he knew a repeat of back then was impossible.

The search did not take long, with Buck spotting Eddie with Pepa and Isabel on the party’s edges. Because Eddie would always end up stuck in his abuela’s orbit if they were in proximity. But what Buck wasn’t expecting to find was-

“Alma?” he blurted out when he joined them.

His old neighbor broke off her conversation with Isabel and Pepa to give him a bright smile.

“There you are,” she said. “We were wondering if you’d ever show up. Eddie’s been pouting.”

“I have not,” Eddie grumbled.

He moved to stand shoulder-to-shoulder with Buck, and passed him a beer to drink. An open one was already in Eddie’s hand, but Buck could tell he hadn’t been doing much more than sipping at it.

“I’ve been catching up with people,” Buck said, still feeling very wrong footed. “Okay, uh. What?”

“When I told her I’d moved back down, we met up,” Isabel said.

Because she and Alma had been talking to each other since Alma came to Los Angeles after the ladder truck. It was a fact that Buck did his best to forget, an effort hampered by how Alma would occasionally call just to tease him over something Eddie told Isabel.

“I haven’t had an abuela since before the girls were born,” Alma said. “So I decided to steal Eddie’s.”

“And…” Buck turned to Eddie for help. “She came to your dad’s retirement party?”

“Free food, cabrón.” Alma held up the paper plate she had in her hands, well on its way to being pecked cleaned. “’Sides, with Delia busy with her girlfriend- Has she told you about Estella?”

“Celia did,” Buck confirmed. “She sounded nice.”

He twisted the cap off his bottle and, for lack of any other options right then, pocketed it. He didn’t think anyone would appreciate it if he just tossed it to the ground.

“She is. But Delia’s busy with her, and Celia barely comes down to visit these days.” Alma’s mouth screwed up as she shook her head. “There’s a medical school right here in town, I don’t know why she she had to go to Pittsburgh.”

“To get away from you,” Eddie cheerfully told her, and then ducked away when she took a swat at him.

His phone was in his hands. Buck hadn’t noticed before, distracted by Alma as he was. As he watched, Eddie’s thumbs tapped away at the screen before he tucked it into his pocket. When Buck’s phone didn’t go off, his curiosity was piqued even further.

“Who are you texting?”

“Austin. Taylor thinks one of the floaters that covered for Chimney is a serial killer.”

“Taylor… No, wait, hold on.” Buck closed his eyes and shook his head, like that would get things back in order. “Did you get Austin’s number so the two of you could share gossip?”

“...no,” Eddie lied, so bald faced that it had his aunt and grandmother scoffing. “Austin wants to know if you got any of the clouds to tell you what love smells like.”

Daniel Buckley was not the shining example of a “good” person. He knew this, he accepted this. It wasn’t something that bothered him. It wasn’t something that had ever bothered him.

So, of course, this meant he was not above taking the opportunity to gloat.

But being captain came with responsibilities, and Mehta wasn’t the type to ignore them or shove them onto other people. So he pulled on his big boy breeches and came out to give the morning brief.
