“And they teach classes for it?” Daniel leaned forward a little as he asked. “Like- Like- Whipping 101?”

“Probably. But the classes I took were just on monitoring. For safety and whatever.” Austin shrugged. “The ad said it was a dungeon master class. The description was vague.”

Neither of them looked over at the wall, but they didn’t need to. On the wall was shelves, each with little figurines of fantasy monsters or warriors. Austin dusted them on the regular. He was quite proud of his collection, even if he never said it.

“Then which was the lie?” Daniel demanded to know while he refilled his shot glass. “The kiss or being a scout?”

“My first kiss was with a girl named Tiffany in eighth grade,” Austin answered. “Justin was the guy I lost my virginity to my senior year. And the only person I’ve had sex with.”

“His name rhymes with yours,” Daniel teased. “You were a boy scout?”

“Troop One Eighteen. I was an Eagle Scout.”

“I don’t even know what that is,” Daniel grumbled. “One Eighteen? Seriously? Is that where the fake anniversary date comes from? Not your station?”

Austin simply shrugged once more.

“Your turn,” he prompted.

“Fine, fine. Um.” Daniel thought about it for a moment. “I didn’t lose my virginity until after high school, my baby brother was made for me, and I can recognize GHB by taste.”

The other man stared at him.

“Why?” he intoned.

Daniel burst out laughing, throwing his head back with it.

“C’mon,” he urged with a grin. “Make a guess.”

“Please tell me you don’t know how GHB tastes.”

“It’s kinda salty.”

A look of horror crossed over Austin’s face. He took the glass when Daniel pushed it towards him, downing it and then grimacing.

“Ketamine’s bitter,” Daniel said, perhaps a bit too cheerfully. “If you were curious.”

“I wasn’t,” Austin bit out. “So which one was the lie?”

“I had cancer as a kid. Leukemia.” He shrugged as he said it. “I needed a transplant, so Mom and Dad had Evan to give me bone marrow. He was about three months old when they did it.”

Austin stared at him again.

“The virginity one, then,” he said. “Please don’t tell me your first time was when you started getting paid for it.”

“Nope.” Daniel shook his head. “I mean, it was the first time I’d done it with a guy. Got paid half a grand to learn a few things about myself.”

His pretend boyfriend was the perfect image of suffering.

“So you lost it in high school, too.”

“Sure,” Daniel said, easy as that.

Austin’s eyebrow inched upwards ever so slightly. Daniel sighed.

“It was before high school,” he said. “I was friends with this kid, Peter. Kinda. I went to his place one weekend and ended up staying over. His sister, Becky, was home from college. She thought I was cute.”

“Dan.” Austin was putting effort into keeping his voice even. It still trembled ever-so-slightly. Daniel was getting good at picking those things up with Austin. “That’s...”

Daniel simply shrugged, because he knew how fucked up it was. It hadn’t seemed that way to him at the time, not that it would’ve mattered. He would’ve gone along with it anyways.

He grabbed the tequila and poured it into Austin’s shot glass. Some of it missed and pooled on the table top.

“Your turn, again,” he told Austin.

“I don’t think I like this game,” Austin complained, but he kept going along with it. “I don’t like heights, I don’t have any cousins, and I know more than twenty different knots.”

“You have cousins.” Daniel rolled his eyes. “I got stuck listening to Alan complain about your cousin Shanna’s husband. Something about the guy being a bigoted shit-for-brains of a dick.”

“Edward is a bigoted shit-for-brains of a dick.”

“Eddie. Of course he’s a dick.” Daniel ignored Austin’s clear curiosity over the statement. “I bet those knots come in handy with the, uh. Monitoring.”

“The scouts teach you valuable life skills.”

Daniel burst out laughing.

“Alright, alright,” he said, chuckling still. “My turn again.”

Austin grimaced, and then downed his shot.

“I haven’t even- Seriously?”

“Fortitude,” was Austin explanation as he refilled the glass.

“My last turn was not that bad.”

“Yes.” Austin set the tequila down firmly on his side of the table. “It was.”

Daniel rolled his eyes.

“Alright, I’ll stick to kiddy shit this time.” He tried to think of ones that wouldn’t upset Austin’s delicate sensibilities. “Uh. I have a sister, I was raised Catholic, and I worked a summer at Hershey Park...”

Now

The engine wasn’t always needed on a call. Sometimes, it was just the ladder that went while Austin got to stay back and either catch up on his assigned chores or, more likely, laze the time away. Unfortunately, that day, Austin’s lazing got interrupted by Buck calling to ask for a ride back to the station. Because the ladder had taken a tumble off a cliff.

Javier was more than a little embarrassed by what happened. The aerial ladders could extend nearly a hundred feet, so the truck hadn’t needed to be as close to the edge as it was. It could’ve been farther away, where the ground proved to be more solid. But he’d listened to the captain, and parked where he was told. Dan would say that was his first and biggest mistake.

Worse for everyone involved, someone had tipped off the media and so there was a news chopper circling the place by the time Austin got there with the engine. It was Channel 8, so he was betting Dan decided to embarrass his brother and/or the captain by proxy of Taylor Kelly. It was now on every channel, it seemed, much to the annoyance of the people who’d been there. Austin thought it would’ve been a welcome break from the constant coverage on Jonah Greenway.

Austin was sure the whole thing with Jonah was stressful. Not for him, but for other people. The department brass held a hearing about the guy, dragging in people from the stations he’d worked. It was mostly the paramedics and captains, but a couple of the others also won that lotto. Daniel and Buck, for example.

The fire chief dragged the pair of them – separately – into his office to interrogate them about Taylor Kelly. First responders were a gossipy bunch, Austin himself was a prime example, so it wasn’t surprising that word of the pair being friends with the reporter went up the grapevine and reached the brass. According to Dan, the fire chief was spitting mad and nearly pulled out the thumbscrews.

Buck, of course, took it personally. As apparent by his current argument with the captain. Austin, by habit built over five years, started watching them the moment he saw Dan’s brother and the captain were in proximity to each other.

“It wasn’t a punishment,” the captain said as he crested the top of the stairs.

“Come on, Cap.” Buck was right on the captain’s tail. “I know everyone’s mad about Taylor’s story.”

At the call, the one where they lost their third ladder truck under the captain’s tenure, the captain had opted to go down and rescue the patient himself. Buck, as mentioned, was taking it personally the way he always did these things. The kid was far too eager to please, and far too anxious at the idea of someone being displeased with him. So, of course, he thought the captain not letting him go down meant the captain no longer trusted him or whatever.

Personally, Austin thought it was the same stupidity that flared up just before Buck transferred to the 133. The time the captain did his best to hold Buck back because he cared too much and was afraid of Buck getting hurt again. More specifically, he was afraid of how he’d feel if Buck got injured again, because the man clearly didn’t mind hurting the kid.

This was that, only not focused on Buck. After Chimney and Hen’s brush with a serial killer, the captain was just letting his fears get the better of him. Again. So, instead of letting the others do their jobs and thereby take on the scant risk involved in abseiling down the cliff, he opted to keep everyone back and go down himself. Well, himself and Lucy Donato.

It really made one question how the captain really felt about Lucy, if he didn’t mind the idea of her being at risk.

“No one is mad,” the captain tried to assure Buck.

It didn’t work.

“Chief Alonzo seemed pretty mad when he was interrogating me.”

“That was a hearing,” the captain said. “Not an interrogation.”

“No, the interrogation was in his office afterwards,” Buck bit out. “He kept asking me about Taylor, where she got her information, who her sources were. If I was one of them. Why the hell would I tell her anything? I know better than that.”

Tweedle Dee and Tweedle Dum – Hen and Chimney – were sat at the couches and chairs in front of the now turned off television. Neither of them looked up, but they both wore a very prominent air of sheepishness. The captain, too, seemed uncomfortable for a moment.

Austin did wonder, just a little, if they’d been honest about where the footage came from.

“The brass is just embarrassed,” the captain said. “It would have been better for the department if they could have released the story themselves, controlled the narrative a little bit. A heads-up would have been appreciated, that’s all.”

“I don’t know how they think I had anything to do with it. I was in Texas!”

“Of course, this wasn’t your fault,” the captain said. “I can’t even blame Taylor, she’s a reporter. She’s just reporting the news. She wasn’t the one running around playing God right under my nose.”

“Cap, you couldn’t have known.”

“I should have!” the captain snapped.

Buck wasn’t the only one to react with surprise at the outburst. All the others within earshot stopped pretending that they weren’t eavesdropping as well, heads twisting around to stare at the captain.

“It’s my job,” the captain said at a more even level. “Sorry, kid.”

Guilt, Austin thought as the captain walked off. That was the other thing driving the captain's actions today. Guilt, and the fear of the pain he felt over someone he cared about getting hurt.

Austin could already hear Dan calling the man an idiot.

“The guy’s an idiot,” Daniel scoffed.

“Danny,” Buck sighed.

They were at Eddie’s to finally get around to patching the holes left in Eddie’s bedroom wall. While they did, Buck filled Eddie and Daniel in on the talk with Bobby from the day before, since he hadn’t gotten the chance to yet. Well, Buck filled in Eddie. Daniel didn’t need filling in, as Austin already told him all about it.

“Jonah was at that station for, what?” Daniel asked. “A couple of weeks?”

“About a month,” Eddie said, then looked to Buck. “Wasn’t it?”

“Yeah. His first shift as a floater was the Monday before that call, the one from the movie-”

“Speed.”

“Right. And he transferred to the One Thirty-three around the time Jee-Yun got sick.”

“Whatever.” Daniel dismissed it with a wave as he slathered spackle over the mesh patch that covered a hole. “Still not a lot of time, and it wasn’t like Nash was going to be up the guy’s ass for all of it. If anyone should be kicking themselves for it, it’s Hen. Or Aiden.”

“Who’s Aiden?” a confused Eddie asked.

“A paramedic at Danny’s station,” Buck answered. “I wasn’t ever close to him.”

He stuck his tongue out slightly as he smoothed a mesh patch onto the wall. Between the three of them, the holes were nearly all covered. They might even manage to save Eddie’s security deposit, though it was doubtful.

“Jonah usually rode with him,” Daniel explained. “Really not surprised Aiden didn’t know shit.”

“Danny doesn’t like him,” Buck stage whispered to Eddie.

“You don’t say,” Eddie replied, also in a stage whisper.

Then Buck was having to dodge as Daniel tried to cover his hair in plaster with his putty knife. He nearly tripped over his own feet in his hurry to get away, glowering at Daniel the entire time.

“Jerk,” Buck sniffed.

He ran his fingers through his hair, probably checking to make sure it was plaster free. His curls were loose and wild today. When Daniel asked about it, he’d mentioned something about being in a rush to respond to a call this morning and then get home.

Daniel thought it made his brother look younger, but he was careful not to voice it. Mom taught Buck to hate his curls growing up, always getting on him about the curls looking messy and childish. She always made sure his hair never got long enough to curl, and if it did, she pinned Buck down and straightened them out.

He tried to argue with her about it, but by the time he did, it was already routine. Her words were dripped poison into Buck’s ears, teaching him to hate his own hair. It was simply cemented after a throw away comment on how much Buck, with his hair straightened, looked like his big brother.

Personally, Daniel liked the curls. But he knew if he said anything about them, positive or negative, his brother would get self-conscious and they’d never see the sun for another decade or two.

“My point,” he said, getting back on topic. “Is that Nash ain’t God, even if he likes to pretend he is. I mean, I thought Jonah was just a fuck up.”

“Bobby takes responsibility for everything,” Buck said. “It’s his way.”

“Even the shit that isn’t his fault.” Daniel scoffed. “Y’know what happens to guys who do that? They burn out and drown in a bottle.”

“I didn’t say I agreed with it,” his brother huffed.

“Good.”

“It’s a bad road to go down.” Eddie was running a sponge over his patch job. “You lose sight of things, of who you are.”

“I just- I just wish I could…” Buck trailed off.

“Fix it?” Eddie finished the thought for him.

Daniel swayed a bit to bump his shoulder into his brother’s.

“Too much heart,” he said.

“Yeah, yeah,” Buck grumbled. “I’m the guy that always wants to fix everything. Practically born for it.”

More born to be useful talk. It had been so long, Daniel was starting to think Buck’s therapist managed to crack it apart. Apparently not.

(His brother was still seeing that therapist of his, right?)

“It comes in handy when you’ve got a bunch of holes in your wall,” Eddie said, though he looked slightly unsettled. He always did whenever Buck started talking like that. “Speaking of fixing, I, uh… I’m gonna see about transferring back. Assuming Bobby let’s me back this time.”

“I don’t know why he wouldn’t. I don’t know why he didn’t agree to it in March,” Buck said. “You never told me. You didn’t really tell me much about whatever the two of you said to each other.”

“Oh, uh.” Eddie’s discomfort was palpable. “He was worried about me, my mental health- Shut up.”

“I didn’t say anything,” Buck denied, and then blindly smacked at Daniel when Daniel scoffed. “Wait, he’s why you started seeing a therapist? After I spent months bugging you about it?”

“Um,” was all Eddie said, and then, “Anyway-”

“Bitch.”

“-I kind of lost my temper and, um…”

“And, um?” Daniel prodded, curiosity provoked now.

“Pulled a you.”

“What the hell does that mean?”

“I threw the fire in his face.”

Buck groaned and covered his face. Daniel, meanwhile, grinned and held out his hand for a high-five.

Eddie did not give him a high-five.

Daniel was making a sandwich when his sister arrived from her lunch with Chimney. Maddie was less anxious than she’d been when she left, but she came back a bit dazed looking. She came over without a word and sat down at the kitchen table, dropping her purse on the floor by her feet.

“Uh.” Daniel delicately placed a handful of bbq chips on top of the bologna, cheese, and mayo. “Is everything okay?”

“Yeah,” Maddie said, and then shook her head. “No. I don’t know.”

“Such clarity,” he teased. “Want a sandwich?”

“I just had lunch, Dan.”

“That wasn’t a no,” he remarked. He pulled out more bread and more bologna. “What’s up? What happened?”

“He thought I wanted to talk about dating other people.”

“Huh.”

Maddie had gone to talk about her going back to work at Metro Dispatch, since it would impact childcare. She’d been eager, but also nervous about her baby daddy’s reaction. According to her, she was looking forward to having the money to get her own place again, somewhere the toilet seat wasn’t always left up.

Personally, as much as he loved his sister, Daniel would not miss all the damn hair she left in the shower.

“Do you want to?” Daniel asked. “Date other people, I mean.”

“No.”

She sounded offended by the prospect, leaning back in her seat.

“You’re allowed, you two are broken up. You can date whoever the hell you want,” he reminded her. “But maybe you shouldn’t. Maybe it would be good for you to be single for a while.”

“What do you mean?”

“You want my honest answer on that?”

His sister opened her mouth, her answer already half way out. But then she paused and caught herself. He could see her giving the question actual thought, brows furrowed and lips pursed. Because they both knew that if he was going to be honest, then he was going to be honest. Not nice, not polite, just honest.

He put together her sandwich while he waited. It wasn’t the same as his, she didn’t like the crunch of the chips and preferred mustard instead of mayo. She was a weirdo, but he loved her in spite of her odd taste in foods.

“Yes,” Maddie said after a minute. She nodded, firm and decisive. “Yes, I do want you to be honest.”

“Alright.” Daniel placed the sandwich, and the plate it was on, in front of her. Then he took the seat across from her. “This is the longest you’ve been single since you met Doug your sophomore year. You were with him for two decades, and then the moment you finally left him, you moved onto guy number two.”

“Not the moment.”

“Whatever. You met Chimney and almost immediately started dating him, no matter how much you both tried to lie about it. You’ve been attached to one guy or another for more than half your life, Mads. Your entire adulthood. Do you know who you are outside of that? When it’s just you, when you can’t define yourself as someone’s wife or girlfriend. Who are you?”

“Of course I do,” she snapped, though uncertainty bubbled beneath the surface. She covered it up with anger. “Do you? Do you know who you are outside of Evan?”

Dr. White once asked him that very same question. He got the impression she didn’t appreciate the answers he brought her the following session. He didn’t think Maddie was going to appreciate them, either.

“I’m an asshole, and a dick,” he stated plainly. “I’m selfish. I’m manipulative. I’m violent. I’m a liar. I’m terrified of being alone. My coworkers think I’m a murderer-”

“Wait, what?”

“I don’t care about people, and I’m really good at sex,” he continued. His sister made a face, everything scrunching up in disgust. “But the point is-”

“Can we go back to the murderer thing?”

“I caught them gossiping, it’s whatever.” He waved it away. It didn’t matter. “The point is, can you do that? Can you tell me, right now, who you are? When you’re not Doug’s wife, or Chimney’s girlfriend, or Jee’s mom, or even our sister. Who are you?”

Maddie blinked at him, and then averted her gaze. Daniel ate his sandwich as he waited and as the silence dragged on. It had taken him two whole weeks to answer the question, he’d allow Maddie at least a couple minutes.

“I’m a helper,” she finally answered, the words come out hesitant and hushed. “I’m a survivor. I’m a nag-”

“You said it, not me.”

“Jerk,” she laughed. “You really think I should be single awhile? It’s not just that you hate Chimney?”

“I hate Nash,” Daniel corrected her. “My feelings for your baby daddy are not that strong. Pretty sure Buck and Austin have stronger feelings for him.”

“Yeah.” By the grim set to her mouth, Daniel figured his sister was remembering Buck threatening Chimney’s life. “He was worried about what you might’ve said to the chief. About Taylor’s sources.”

“I didn’t say anything to the chief, not really.” He shrugged at Maddie’s blatant expression of doubt. “Alonzo’s a dickwad. Seriously, dragging me into his office like it’s high school all over? Fuck that shit, I wasn’t gonna give him anything.”

“So you decided not to rat out Chimney and Hen out of rebelliousness?”

“No gods, no masters,” he said, raising a fist in the air. She rolled her eyes. “And I like Hen. Didn’t feel like throwing her under the bus, too.”

“So if it was just Chimney...?”

“In a heartbeat,” Daniel answered. “The man attacked me in my own home.”

His sister looked uncomfortable at the reminder. He sighed and leaned forward, resting his elbows on the table top.

“Look, Mads,” he said. “Whatever you do, he’s Jee-Yun’s dad, and as far as I can tell, he hasn’t been a bad one. Not a great one, but not a bad one, either.”

“Such praise,” Maddie drawled.

Daniel shrugged.

“He’s Jee’s dad. Better, he’s a dad who’s there, which is more than what Eddie grew up with,” he told her. “He hasn’t done anything to deserve being cut out of his kid’s life. And frankly, you don’t have the right.”

“Sure didn’t stop you from cutting Mom and Dad out of Buck’s life,” she pointed out.

“Because Buck’s my kid. ‘Sides, they made me his legal guardian before we left Hershey. There was paperwork filed and everything,” Daniel said. “But all I’m saying is that you shouldn’t feel guilty for spending time with Chimney. It’s kinda inevitable if you’re gonna be raising a kid together, even if you’re separated.”

“Birthdays and holidays are gonna be so fun,” Maddie joked.

“Hey, as long as he doesn’t say shit, we’re good. Hell, if Mom and Dad manage to not be fucking awful to Buck, I wouldn’t mind them coming down, either.”

Once, not even that long, Maddie would have jumped to their defense. They weren’t bad people, she would say, just bad parents. But then those dinners with their parents last year happened. It might have been overshadowed by everyone learning about his self-employment in El Paso, but Maddie had gotten to see and to hear how their parents treated their baby brother. With a child of her own on the way, and with years between herself and their childhood at home. Years between herself and the conditioning, the grooming, from their parents.

Maddie hadn’t defended their parents since.

“You’d be fine with them coming down again?” she dubiously asked.

“I’d beat them to death with my bat if they said the wrong thing to my kid,” he told her. “But yeah, if they watch their mouths. We talk just fine on the phone. Mostly because they never ask about Buck.”

It used to annoy him more, back in the day. The way Mom could call him and spend half an hour chattering into his ear and not mention her other son once. These days, he accepted it as the minor blessing that it was.

It still annoyed him a little bit, though.

“Anyways, dispatch. Are we excited? Are we nervous? Do we even know where the hell dispatch is right now?”

Maddie didn’t look at him as she said it.

Daniel groaned, but he minded talking about Austin as much as he minded talking about Buck. That is to say, not at all.

Probably because it was so similar to how it was so long ago, with Buck in the back seat as he talked about the newest cool thing that happened or that he learned. Not that he’d told Austin that, but Austin guessed it anyway.

The house, when they reached it, was tucked away from the road a bit. A driveway led to a garage, large enough for three cars to park inside, which stood separate from the house. The house was L-shaped, with a wing projected forward. Both buildings had terracotta roof tiles and solar panels.

It took all of an instant for Daniel, and Buck, to spot the problem with this place. The house had a rectangular second floor over half the building, at the corner where the two legs of the L came together, with a wrap-around balcony and a sliding door on the left side. She looked at Daniel in question.

“That’s Dan,” Austin told her with a lazy jerk of his thumb. “My courtesan.”

(“Stop calling yourself that!” Maddie snapped.

“Would you prefer prostitute? Hooker? Escort?”

“Courtesan,” Austin broke his silence to suggest.

Daniel tilted his head back to smile at him.

“That makes me sound fancier than I am.”

“I was,” Daniel agreed.)

The smile Austin wore was very smug, very self-satisfied.

It just made Daniel laugh even more.

“But.” Buck gestured helplessly. “Stairs.”

Marcy ignored him as she led the way inside. Daniel, still catching his breath, trailed after her and Austin, with Buck right behind. The realtor was talking, stuff about the house, but Daniel didn’t really pay attention as he opted to peer around the place.

The first room was a foyer. Small, with wooden accents and a built in bench along one wall that double as a chest. A second door allowed them into the house proper. The place was open plan, the living room blending into the dining room blending into the kitchen. Hardwood floors throughout, it seemed, except for where it switched to tile in the kitchen. It was a nice kitchen, judging from Buck’s sudden excitement and the way he started on about chefs.

Weren’t all kitchens a chef’s kitchen? Whatever.

Most of the back wall was nothing but floor-to-ceiling windows, allowing them to see into the backyard, with a set of sliding doors directly across from the front entrance. The kitchen had a counter along the back wall, and a large window that was folded open. From where Daniel stood, another counter could be seen on the other side.

There was an open doorway to the right of the front entrance that led to the projecting wing. Another doorway on the left led into the other wing. A pair of doors along the right wall were presumably closets or the like.

Through the wall of windows in the back, Daniel could see a large built-in grill on the back patio beneath a wooden awning. He could also see a fire pit sunk into the ground in the middle of the yard, and a good sized pool. A tree in the back – a coast live oak, according to Austin – towered over everything, its branches stretching out until they just barely missed the pool.

“This is the common area,” Marcy declared, words firmer than before. She was back in her element. “To the left, your left, through there, is what I’ve been referring to as the utility wing. Through there on the right, your right, is the residential wing.”

“The house has wings,” he whispered.

“Must’ve drank some Redbull,” Daniel whispered back.
