A closet was right beside it, and then a bathroom after that.

It was the bathroom that gave Daniel pause. It was a nice bathroom, he guessed, with the floor and walls tiled in a tan-ish colored stone. The right was the toilet, with a half wall separating it, and the sink across from it, from the rest of the room. On the left was the shower, completely open.

But it was the other door that had Daniel asking,

“Where does that lead?”

“The backyard,” was Marcy’s answer. “You can come in directly from the pool and shower it off.”

“There’s a pool?” Buck excitedly asked. Then he followed it with a sheepish, “I wasn’t really paying attention to the backyard earlier.”

Marcy’s eyes flickered to Daniel. The lines around her mouth went tense.

“Yes,” she said after a moment. Her tone was careful. “A saltwater pool. It’s heated. I’ll give you a tour of the backyard after we’re done inside.”

After the bathroom was a sauna, which just seemed indecently fancy to Daniel, and then-

“Oh, shit,” Daniel found himself blurting out as he came to a stop.

The last room of the utility room took up the entire end of it. It was a home gym, with two of the walls covered in mirrors. There was already a bunch of equipment, better than what they had at the station. It reminded Daniel a bit of a scaled down version of the gym at their apartment building.

He really hoped the equipment wasn’t just for show.

The other wing, once Marcy led them back through the common area, was the bedrooms. Four in total, each with an en suite. They had large walk-in showers, large enough to stick a chair in if they wanted, with no ledges to step over. It sounded ideal for Chris. Or for them after a shift that was particularly pain in the ass.

That concerning second floor was next, through one of the doors in the common area, with Marcy saying something as they climbed the stairs. Daniel didn’t register a word of it, because he was behind and below Austin, and so was somewhat distracted until they got to the top landing. Another door opened up into-

“The apartment,” Marcy declared. “The original owners built it to be soundproof from the floor below. The bedrooms on the first floor are all soundproofed, too.”

“Oh, thank God,” Daniel heard Buck mutter to himself.

A hallway, with windows along their left that let them look down into the front yard, led to a large room. The sliding door he saw from outside was on the far end, and there were windows along that wall and the two perpendicular to it. To their right was the bedroom and its adjoining bathroom.

“There’s a nicely sized soaking tub,” Marcy said, snagging Daniel’s attention completely and utterly. “With a separate shower, just as you were looking for.”

She and Austin went to check out the balcony. Daniel started to follow, only to stop when he realized Buck was standing in the middle of the room. He was slowly turning in place, a quizzical frown on his face and his head tilted like a puppy.

“What’s up, squirt?”

“This would be Austin’s space, wouldn’t it? Where he can go to shut us all out?” His brother didn’t sound like he was actually asking. “I bet everything downstairs was just a bonus. This is why he has us looking at this house.”

“Probably,” Daniel agreed with a shrug. “I’ve told you, he likes his space. I’m surprised he hasn’t taken his millions and bought a small forest or something to hide in.”

“Eddie didn’t want a house with places Chris couldn’t easily get to. But I guess there wouldn’t be any reason for Chris to come up here.”

“I’m somehow the courtesan to a multimillionaire, and I don’t think this house is gonna cost all of it,” Daniel said. “We could probably get him to install a stair lift. Maybe. He’s immune to my best manipulation tactics, so we might need you and Chris to look real cute while you ask.”

“I always look real cute,” Buck retorted. “How much is this house gonna cost, anyway? I mean, it’s Los Feliz.”

“I don’t want to know. If they mention a number around me, I’m gonna get blitzed on enough alcohol that I forget how to do math.”

Buck rolled his eyes. He unfortunately wasn’t close enough that Daniel could just reach out and put him into a headlock. The little shit would run if Daniel tried, and Daniel did not feel like chasing him around this house yet. Not until they’d decided they were buying it and it didn’t matter if they broke any windows.

“So. Courtesan?”

Daniel shrugged.

“It’s better than zucchini. Or- What were the others? Squish? Marshmallow?”

“I kinda like squish.”

“You can use that for yourself and Eddie then.” Daniel rolled his eyes as he went to join Austin and Marcy. “I’mma stick to being called a fancy whore, thanks.”

“Do you have to make it terrible every time?”

“Yes.”

Unfortunately, Daniel opened the sliding door in the middle of Austin discussing making an offer on the house. He closed it as quickly as he could, cutting off the absurd number being said, and left in the opposite direction. Buck was laughing, so Daniel didn’t feel (much) guilt for pushing him to the floor.

Chapter End Notes

Sometime ago, on tumblr, I brought up the problem of tagging for Daniel and Austin, because they've become - they've been - a prominent relationship in this series. But Daniel/OMC doesn't quite fit, as Austin neither loves Daniel romantically nor finds him sexually attractive. Daniel&OMC would be platonic, which might fit for a QPR, but that tag always reads to me as platonic in the sense of simple friends or something familial. Except Daniel and Austin are far more than simple friends atp, and they don't feel anything familial for eachother, either.

I would like to tag for them, though. So. Suggestions? Ideas? Do I just throw both the / and & version up there and call it a day?

Edit: The consensus seems to be in favor of /, so that is what I am going with.
Edit the 2nd: That awkward moment when you use one word (deck) for a feature of a house, when you really meant another (balcony)

Also, have my thoughts about Maddie and how her parents raised her for abuse.

Chapter 36

Chapter Notes

What's this? A new chapter two days after the last one? Witchcraft.

Also! As you might have noticed, or might not have, I decided to go with Daniel/OMC for the relationship tag, because that was the consensus in the comments last chapter. I went back and tagged the previous story, Forgive Us Our Trespasses, as well as Against the Current, with the same. I also tagged Against the Current with Daniel&OMC, along with Spare Parts and Yesterday's Ghosts. That should hopefully cover the tagging bases, and keep me from feeling like I'm blindsiding any reader that comes for Buddie only to get chapter upon chapter of Austiel.

Then

“Well,” Cynthia said. She stood in the middle of it and turned in place as she took it in. “It sure is an apartment.”

Austin could hear the judgment in her words. He couldn’t even blame here, because this apartment did leave much to be desired. It wasn’t quite a single room, there was a clear delineation between the kitchen and the living room, which was also the bedroom. It was longer than it was wide, the back wall taken up by a large window that let it in the sunlight during the day. His twin bed was pushed up against it. It looked small in the mostly empty space of the studio, and he would definitely be replacing it with a bigger one. He'd be buying a lot of new stuff, the things he brought from home not doing much to fill the place up.

He hadn’t rented it quite the moment his probational year was up, but it was a close thing. Instead, he waited a couple months’ worth of paychecks. Long enough to make sure that, if he ate through what he saved from the year before, he wouldn’t end up moving back in with his parents. The original plan was to wait a bit longer, enjoy the free room and board that came with living with his parents, but…

“It’s better than staying at home,” Austin said. “The new neighbors have a daughter that’s our age.”

He grimaced as he spoke. His version of a grimace, which admittedly was never much of a grimace. A slight crease of the brow, a slight scrunch of the nose. It used to bother him, how naturally inexpressive he was. Or rather, it used to bother him how much it bothered other people, and how they were never all that shy with letting him know. They’d complain, or passive-aggressively observe, that his face was too hard to read and they never knew what he was thinking.

It was news to him. He always thought he was expressing the same as everybody else.

“Is that why you moved out?” Cynthia asked, brows furrowing in concern. No one ever complained about not being able to read her. “You told me you weren’t in a hurry, before.”

“Yeah.” Austin shrugged. “Didn’t want to come home from a shift and find Mom’s newest attempt at setting me up sitting on the couch. Again.”

His sister winced.

“The engagement hasn’t helped, has it?” she asked.

Emmett popped the question a few months ago. Not quite as soon as they got their college diplomas, but after he got his first couple of paychecks from that museum he worked at. The ring was pretty, it glinted in the light even now when Cynthia moved her hand just right, but it was also plain and cheap. Cynthia said it was perfect. Knowing the way those two were, the way those two had been since she met him, she would probably declare a Ring Pop was perfect if it was Emmett proposing with it.

They were putting off the wedding. Maybe a few years down the line, maybe, when things weren’t so tight. Mom was already bombarding them both with wedding ideas, but she was unfortunately quite capable of multitasking.

“Not really,” he admitted to her. “Seeing Ethan and Irene together doesn’t help, either.”

“We’ll be lucky if those two don’t try and elope before they graduate high school.” She rolled her eyes, but she did it fondly. “Have you tried talking to her and Dad? Telling them?”

“I have told them. So many times. They just don’t get it. Mom keeps saying that I just haven’t met the right one yet,” he said. “What if there isn’t a right one? What if I don’t want there to be a right one? But every time…”

“You’ll change your mind when you meet them.” Cynthia sounded far too much like their mother as she said it. It was unsettling. “At least we know she’s open minded? That’s a plus, right?”

“That just means she has more people to try with.” Austin huffed. “I know she loves me, I know she wants me to be happy.”

“But you’re happiest when you’re alone,” his sister said.

“That’s not- I’m happy when it’s the two of us, or if Ethan’s there. I’m happy right now.”

“Don’t lie and say you’re not going to be more relaxed the moment that door shuts behind me.” She poked him in the chest, smiling as she did. “Spending all day at that station has to be hell for you.”

“It’s okay,” he said with a shrug.

The truth of it was that it was hell, sometimes. The times everyone around him got to be too much. Too much noise, too much presence. The majority of his probie year, when he wasn’t being saddled with the bulk of the menial chores, was spent finding places to hide and get away from it all. Secluded corners, rooms that were never used, shaded spots outside. He tried the roof, once, but the parapet was rather short and so it didn’t help at all.

“Sorry I didn’t help with the move,” Cynthia said.

“No you’re not.”

“Nope,” she laughed. “How bad were Mom and Dad? Did he cry?”

“Little bit,” he said, which made her laugh more.

“Because you’re his favorite.”

“Dad doesn’t have a favorite.”

“Because he loves us all the same?” Cynthia scoffed. “He only tells us that to hide the fact you’re his favorite. Ethan and I weren’t spending our weekends tinkering in the garage with him.”

Austin shrugged, unashamed of those weekends. Cars and their engines, he found, consistently made more sense than people did. It was nice, too, to take stuff apart with his hands and put them back together again.

“You’re sure you’re going to be okay?” Cynthia asked, head tilted a bit and brows raised a little. Caring and concern. “You like being alone, but there’s a difference between being alone and- Well, between being alone and being alone.”

“I’ll be fine,” he assured her. Then pointed at a book on the kitchen counter. “Mom bought me a cookbook.”

“You’re going to be cooking. Unsupervised.” His sister’s tone was utterly flat. “Now I’m even more worried.”

“If I start a fire, I’ll just call myself and have myself put it out.”

From Cynthia’s slight glare, Austin didn’t think the humor helped at all.

Now

It felt odd to be wearing the maroon dispatcher uniform again. Not a bad sort of odd, Maddie didn’t think. Just odd. She wasn’t sure if it didn’t fit right anymore, or if the discomfort was all in her mind. A lot of problems were all in her mind, for as real as they were. But it was still odd, and she did feel anxious and uncomfortable, and so she opted to wear her leather jacket. It made her think of Daniel, which was half the reason she bought it, with that would-be punk jacket he had when they were kids and the new one that Austin bought him.

(The other half was how good she looked in it.)

She’d barely seen Daniel that morning. He went into the station earlier than she needed to be anywhere. It was a whirlwind of a hug, a “good luck,” and then the door was slamming closed behind him. All of it while she was still blinking sleep from her eyes.

She hadn’t seen Buck at all, since he was more-or-less living with Eddie, but he sent her a text that was more emoji than English.

The temporary building was different. Josh told her the city was renting out office space, so she pictured something straight out of a corporate nightmare. A sea of sectioned off desks, each dispatcher an isolated island. Turns out, she pictured it entirely wrong.

“This is… Interesting,” she told Josh.

“Everybody hates it,” he replied.

It was not that sea of sectioned off desks. Instead, from above, it appeared to be someone’s failed attempt at an atrium. She’d say everything was open, but most of it was beneath the floor she was on. Or so she hoped, because otherwise, they’d seriously downgraded size-wise.

Josh led them downstairs. The stairs were covered in the same dark blue carpet as all the floors.

“It was supposed to be some new communal workspace,” Josh explained. “Opened right before the pandemic, and closed two months later.”

“And there’s buzzword salad on the walls.”

The accent wall was painted blue. On it were fun corporate words like “work” and “grow” and “hustle.” Each word was white, but not a single one was written in the same font.

“It’s just temporary until the new space is ready for us.”

“And that’ll be…?”

“Mid-September. Ish.”

Josh gave her an apologetic shrug, and then stepped out of the way for Jamal. He had a frosted doughnut in one hand and a to-go coffee in the other.

“Welcome back,” Jamal said, smiling warmly, as he walked past. “There’s doughnuts in the break room.”

“I’m good,” Maddie replied. “But thanks.”

She and Josh passed May at her station. Her caller was complaining about his car being towed, apparently. If that was the worst call she took that day, they’d all be thankful.

“We’re sharing the break room with the accounting firm next door,” Josh told her. “The good news is, they always have doughnuts. The bad news is...”

“They always have doughnuts,” she finished with a laugh.

“Maddie!” Linda looked up from her station. Her face broke out in a broad grin. “Welcome back.”

Maddie returned the smile. A little smaller, maybe, but she hoped it was just as friendly.

“It is good to be back,” she said, and hoped she wasn’t lying.

“Sir, I could just send the police.” May seemed to have hit the brick wall of her patience with her caller. “That’s what I thought. Have a great day.”

Josh chuckled, shook his head, and then led Maddie to the empty station beside Linda.”

“You’ve got this,” he assured her.

“Do I?” Maddie asked, all of the doubt rushing to the fore now that she was here. It wasn’t just a thought, a hypothetical. It was real, it was solid. “Because I thought I was coming back to work, but now it feels like I’m starting over.”

“The space may have changed,” Josh said. “But It’s still the same job. It’s still the same calls. It’s still the same people asking for help. And you’ve always been great at answering them.”

He gave her a final smile, one she thought was intended to show his confidence in her, and then left.

Maddie watched him go, wishing for a moment that he’d stayed. But she knew, from experience, that using him as a crutch like that would neither help nor work. There was nothing to do but to do, and so she did.

She slipped off her jacket and hung it on the back of the chair. Then she slid into the seat, put on the headset, and answered the first call for help.

“Nine-One-One,” she recited, the words coming out rote, despite how long ago she last said them. “What’s your emergency?”

Ever since Buck figured out that Chimney punched his brother, the engine usually had a slight tension in the air on the way to and from calls. It was an enclosed space, with not much room. Especially if you were someone like Buck, with awkwardly long legs. It made keeping to their corners difficult.

But Buck made an effort to be, at worst, neutral with Chimney. He could work with the guy despite everything. Hell, considering how Buck was as a person, they’d probably grow to be friendly again. Forced by constant proximity, if nothing else.

That wasn’t happening today, though. Today, Buck’s phone buzzed with a message in the sibling group chat. Chimney didn’t pay it any mind, until Buck said,

“Maddie had her first call. She says no one died.”

That had Chimney turning his head to him with a frown. That frown deepened after he pulled out his phone and thumbed on the screen.

“That’s great news,” Ravi said. He was sat next to Buck, with Lucy on the other side of him in the pull-down seat. “Both parts.”

He glanced between Buck and Chimney as he no doubt picked up on the increasing tension. Tension that just about popped like a balloon when Chimney’s phone chimed in his hand.

“Um.” Buck glanced down at his phone again, and felt compelled to say, “I text a lot, I was probably at the top.”

“Right,” Chimney said, but he didn’t quite sound like he believed it.

“Hey, Cap,” Lucy broke in, seemingly desperate to fix the awkwardness. “I heard that your daughter- May, right? I heard that May was leaving the call center?”

“Yeah,” Bobby confirmed.

He was twisted in his seat slightly and was peering at the group with a worried eye.

“You and your wife must be really happy about that,” Lucy said.

“Relieved more than anything,” Bobby told her. “I think it’ll be good for her to get away from the emergencies, the tragedies.”

“The troubles,” Chimney supplied with a grim tone.

His thumbs were tapping across his phone screen, probably texting Maddie back.

“What’s she doing over the summer?” Buck asked. “Any road trips planned?”

“Hopefully ones that aren’t neverending,” Hen said.

She shot a teasing smirk Buck’s way. Buck, as the incredibly mature adult that he was, stuck his tongue out at her.

“She and Harry are going to Miami,” Bobby said. He turned back around to stare out the windshield. “Then she’s planning on visiting her grandparents. They’re gonna have a few weeks together before school starts.”

“She’s gonna miss the wedding,” Hen complained with a frown. “Mom doesn’t have a lot of friends left in the area. I think Karen was hoping to draft some people to fill in that side of the aisle.”

Right. Because in the middle of all the drama, Hen’s mother Toni got engaged to the old flame she reunited with in the fall, Clive. She was wanting to have the wedding in the Wilsons’ backyard, but-

“How did Karen go from hard no to planning the wedding?” Lucy asked, voicing Buck’s own questions.

Because Karen started out firmly against Toni and Clive using their backyard as the venue. Toni was a no-show for Hen’s wedding, and Karen took offense on her wife’s behalf.

“Overcompensating for massive amounts of guilt,” was Hen’s answer.

“I think it’s sweet she was defending you,” Ravi said. “Isn’t that what we all want in a partner? Knowing that they have your back?”

“It’s one of the best parts,” Buck agreed.

“Until you drink a little too much tequila.” Chimney was looking at his phone as he spoke. It had chimed a couple times while they were talking. “And then kiss somebody else.”

Buck glared at Chimney, opening his mouth for a retort. Lucy was faster, though.

“He didn’t kiss me,” she said. “I kissed him.”

Bobby whirled around to stare at them, eyes wide and brows up to his hair.

“Then stop leading people on,” Chimney told Buck.

“Hold on,” Bobby said, then pointed between Buck and Lucy. “When did you two kiss?”

“Um,” Buck and Lucy said together, leaning forward to share a glance.

“About an hour or two after you left the bar,” Lucy answered. “That night with the Speed rip off.”

“That was two months ago,” Bobby said. Then his eyes, if possible, got wider. “That was the first night we met you.”

“What can I say?” Lucy shrugged. “I move fast.”

Bobby stared at them, mouth gaping a little. Then, shaking his head, he settled back into the captain’s seat.

He didn’t speak for the rest of the ride.

It hadn’t been planned, but the Buckley siblings managed to gather together at the apartment. Considering how much time Daniel and Buck were spending at other people’s places nowadays, the latter far more than the former, it was a bit unusual to have all three of them there. The fact that it happened without them coordinating it could’ve been declared a miracle.

Daniel figured Buck was there instead of Eddie’s for the same reason Daniel didn’t go to Austin’s.

“So,” Daniel said. He set the glass of wine in front of Maddie at the table, and open beers in front of Buck and his own seat. “How was your first day back?”

“It was fine,” she said. Then, at Buck’s loudly doubtful face, sighed and corrected herself. “It was wonderful, okay? I knew I missed it, but I didn’t know how much I missed it.”

“I get it.” Buck sat back in his seat with his drink. “That’s how I was when I went on my first call, after my leg.”

“I didn’t have any losses, but how was…?”

Maddie trailed off. She was asking after the couple that got T-boned in their own neighborhood. The wife was in labor and on her way to the hospital when it happened. Daniel knew about it because of Buck and Austin sharing the details in their shared chat with Eddie.

“Hen said the baby was fine. The mom was still in labor when they left, but there was nothing really wrong with either of them,” Buck said. “The dad, uh. Doesn’t remember that he is a dad. Or a husband.”

“Christ,” Daniel swore. “TBI?”

“Emphasis on the T.”

“How much time do you think he lost?” Maddie asked.

“Six or seven years, maybe?” Buck shrugged. “According to Hen, there’s a chance he’ll remember everything again.”

“Or,” Maddie said. She was gripping the glass of wine in both hands. “There’ll be a big black hole in the middle of his life.”

“Six years ago is when Austin and I started fake dating,” Daniel said.

“How does that work now? Are you guys using the same anniversary? A new one?”

“A new anniversary would mean explaining everything to his family. That sounded like too much effort to the both of us.”

“Only his parents,” Buck said. “Cynthia and Ethan know.”

“Cynthia and- How long!?”

Maddie smirked at him from over the rim of her glass. She was far too amused.

“Cynthia’s known the entire time,” she told him. “Your not-boyfriend knew it, too.”

“That mother-”

“You remember that old movie?” Buck cut Daniel off to ask. Rudely, in Daniel’s opinion. He’d taught the kid better manners than interrupting a good cussing. “That funny guy you don’t think is funny is in it. The woman wakes up every morning and her brain resets, so anything she does today, she won’t remember tomorrow.”

“First, Fifty First Dates isn’t old. It came out in-” Maddie cut herself off as her face crumpled in horror. “Oh, God, Dan was still in high school. It is an old movie.”

“It’s not an old movie,” Daniel argued. “If it’s old, then we’re old. You might be a hag, but I’m still young and beautiful.”

“You complain about your back every time you sleep on Eddie’s couch,” Buck reminded him.

“That means nothing.”

“Anyway.” Maddie rolled her eyes at them. She didn’t seem to appreciate the way they synchronized the raspberries they blew at her in response. “Second, Drew Barrymore had anterograde amnesia. She could remember everything before her accident, but she couldn’t form new long-term memories.”

“That sounds like hell,” Daniel said. “Weren’t they pretending it was her birthday every day? Eventually she’d notice they were a lot older than they were yesterday. Pretty sure they could rerelease it as a horror movie without much editing.”

“They switched to having her watch a video tape every morning. But I think it was supposed to be romantic. The idea that the guy would work hard every day so she’d fall in love with him again and again.“

“So every day is the best day ever,” Buck said.

He didn’t really sound as if he thought that, though.

“If the first day of your relationship is the best day of your relationship, then I don’t think it’s a good relationship,” Daniel said all the same. “And it definitely ain’t the best day every day.”

“Yeah,” Maddie agreed with a nod. “Sometimes you have a really shitty day and flee the state.”

“Or the country," Buck added.

“Running away from our problems is the Buckley way.”

“Speak for yourselves,” Daniel told them.

“You told Austin you loved him,” Buck said. “And then we had to trick the two of you into talking.”

“That was avoidance,” Daniel insisted with a sniff. “It’s different.”

“Pretty sure I remember some running happening on the way out the bar that night.”

“You were drunk, what do you know?”

“That you ran away from your problems.”

Buck grinned at him like he was just so cute and clever. Daniel scoffed and rolled his eyes and ignored his siblings’ laughter.

Chapter 37

Chapter Notes

And on Pride Month eve, your favorite author gave to you....
We are at the end! In less than nine months this time. If you're interested, here's far too much of an explanation about the title and a new fic in the fusion universe of this series and my other one, Darling, So It Goes.
CW for self harm, public unfriendly mental illness, Doug and associated Ick, and the author's blatant ACAB sensibilities

See the end of the chapter for more notes

The call was a minor one, in that it was one patient and didn’t involve any burning buildings or crashing cars. It did, however, involve a schizophrenic man going off his medication without telling anyone. As was so often the case, the only person who ended up getting hurt was him.

Okay, small caveat: the guy had a knife when the 133 got there. Nothing big, just a pocket knife. Barely enough to get through their turnouts, if they were wearing them. The cop that got there before them, however, was certainly of a different opinion.

The cop, some asshole with a buzz cut and pockmarks on his face, was standing in the front hall with his gun out when they entered the house, pointing it at their patient. Said patient was too busy cutting into himself to follow any commands. There was a woman who kept putting herself between the man and the cop, and who looked very relieved when they entered the house.

Daniel probably could’ve been more diplomatic in the way he handled it. But he also caught a glimpse of the kid, a terrified little girl in Disney princess pajamas, huddling just behind a doorway that led to the living room. There really wasn’t any hope of him keeping his temper at that point.

“What the fuck are you doing!?” was how Daniel opened things, followed by, “Put that down!”

“He has a knife!” the cop snapped, and then refocused on the man they were there for. “Sir, I told you, put the weapon down or I will-”

“Shoot him in front of his kid? Or maybe shoot his wife on accident, and then shoot him, too? Brilliant idea, shit for brains.”

He heard Mehta let out a groan, but he didn’t particularly care. Mainly because he pissed off the cop enough that the asshole focused on him and not on the LAPD’s quota for shooting innocent people. It allowed the woman to relax, and for Mendoza and Nola to make for the patient. Between the expletives he was exchanging with Officer Dick – it was actually Donato, according to his tag, which was familiar for whatever reason – he kept an ear on what actually mattered.

“Lizzie said he was acting weird and I knew what that meant,” the woman was telling Mehta and the paramedics. “I tried to get back as soon as I could. I would have had someone come by and check earlier, but I didn’t want…”

She didn’t want this precise scenario happening.

“Okay, sir? Can you hand me the knife?” Mehta asked. A quick glance over there and Daniel saw the captain had his hand out, even while keeping his distance. “Hand me the knife and my people here can help you.”

“I can’t!” the man gritted out. His left arm was a bloody mess. Daniel thought he caught a glimpse of muscle. “They’re everywhere, I have to get them out!”

“You know.” The new voice had both Daniel and Officer Dick, Donato, whichever, shutting up. “When Dispatch said there was an altercation between an officer and a firefighter, I just knew it would be you.”

Athena did not look very impressed with him. Nor did she look impressed with the cop. She was standing there in the doorway to the house, glaring at them both with her hands on her hips. In that moment, she was less of a police sergeant and more of a very disappointed mother.

“The idiot was about to shoot the guy he was sent here to help,” Daniel told her. “And his wife.”

“He has a knife!” the officer defended himself.

“Did you try talking him down?” Athena asked, tone weary.

“The psycho wouldn’t listen to me.”

Daniel rolled his eyes, because he doubted the asshole tried to talk the guy down at all. Orders and demands only, probably. Then got pissed off when his make-believe authority failed to live up to its hype. But that wasn’t Daniel’s problem anymore.

Mehta hadn’t gotten anywhere with the guy. The captain was trying, but he might as well have been talking to a wall. The guy – George? He thought that’s what the woman called him – was busy trying to dig out whatever the hell he was seeing. Nola and Mendoza were standing a bit behind Mehta, grimacing and hovering helplessly.

“Hey! It’s George, right?” Daniel walked right by Mehta and the wife to stand next to the guy. He ignored the captain cursing his name. “George, um, what exactly are you trying to…?”

“I- I don’t know!” The guy dug into another spot and Daniel winced. “But they’re everywhere! They’re crawling everywhere, but I can’t find them!”

“They could probably take them out at the hospital,” Daniel said.

George’s head snapped up at him.

“Really?”

“Yeah! They’ve got all those machines that let them see beneath your skin and shit. Bet they could find ‘em and dig ‘em out before they crawl away.”

“They’re everywhere,” George gritted out. “I can’t sleep, I can’t- I need to get them out.”

“That sounds awful, buddy,” Daniel told him. He pointed at Mendoza and Nola, and then tugged out a nitrite glove from his pocket to pull on. “If you let them, they’ll give you a ride to the hospital and they’ll use those machines, the ones I told you about. They’ll find all the nasty crawlies and get rid of ‘em. But, uh. Mendoza’s a bit of a pansy ass and you’re scaring him with that knife, so…”

Daniel held out his gloved hand, ignoring Mendoza’s grumbled “Pansy ass!?” as he did. After a moment, George very reluctantly gave Daniel the bloody weapon. Not that it stopped George from hurting himself any, because he simply started trying to claw his skin off. It did allow the two paramedics to get close and actually help the guy, though.

“I’ll just go put this somewhere,” Daniel announced to the room at large.

Athena eyed him and the bloody weapon with distaste when he passed her with it, while Donato glared at him. The fact that Daniel ignored the guy instead of doing anything to rile him up further should’ve earned Daniel some sort of reward. It wouldn’t, because the world was unfair, but it should have.

Once outside, Daniel realized he had no clue what to do with the knife. This wasn’t really a crime, so it wasn’t needed for evidence. Stick it in a sharps container, maybe? There was one on the ambulance, and they tossed used scalpels in there. That should work, right?

It did, indeed, work. The pocket knife went in easily, causing the scalpels and needles already inside to jangle a bit. The glove, with the transferred blood, went into the biowaste bag.

Donato and Athena were leaving the house when Daniel hopped out. The former was glaring daggers at him, an expression that twisted into uneasy disgust when Daniel batted his eyes and blew the cop a kiss. Just to be an ass. Athena, when she saw the exchange, snapped out a sharp,

“Behave.”

“Who’s misbehaving?” Daniel’s attention was caught by a glimpse of color on the sidewalk. “Not me, no ma’am.”

It was a rock. Reddish-pink, or maybe pink-ish red, it was an almost perfect oval. Smooth and small, it fit neatly in his palm. He was pretty sure it was one of those rocks people used in their gardens, but there weren’t any gardens nearby with colored rocks like this one.

“Daniel!”

Mehta was standing in the doorway, arms crossed and expression stern.

“Time to get yelled at,” Daniel quipped.

“Maybe if you didn’t do things to get yelled at for…” Athena remarked.

“Who, me? I’m a saint.” Daniel absentmindedly slipped the rock into his pocket. “I’ve never done anything wrong in my life.”

It was a lie so bold, he was surprised he didn’t combust with the words.

Everyone else hated the temporary location for Metro Dispatch. Eddie wasn’t the biggest fan of it, but he also wasn’t stuck in a cave all day, so he considered it an improvement. It hopefully wouldn’t matter for much longer, but in the meanwhile, it mattered.

He was still set off a bit away from everyone, at a desk with a computer and a phone. But the room had plants in it, a potted tree of some kind in the corner and another potted plant hanging in front of the window. An actual window, not some arrow slit of a thing. He could see sky and city and the homeless guy pushing his cart down the sidewalk.

No one claimed the views were picturesque, only that he had views now.

Still, he would leave his office for the call floor somewhat often. Mainly for human interaction that didn’t have him close to weeping at the state of the country’s education system. Many, many children were being left behind. Besides, he had a particular piece of gossip he needed to chase.

Intercepting May just as she left her station for her break was not coincidence. Besides the outside, he could also see onto the call floor if he left his door open. He’d been waiting for this moment, and so he did his best to hurry over without seeming like he was hurrying.

“You got a sec?” he asked her.

“Hey.” May slowed to a stop and faced him. “I heard you hit a million followers.”

“They’re excited about the ladder truck falling off a cliff. A couple days actually following should take care of it.”

There was footage of it. Someone turned it into one of those jiffy thingies and now it was everywhere. Austin found it hilarious when Eddie told him about it. Javier, the 118’s apparatus operator for their shift, was apparently not so amused when Austin shared it with him.

“Probably,” she agreed with a laugh. “What did you need?”

“How’s Bobby doing?”

May immediately went tense and guarded, eyeing him with caution.

“Why would you ask me that?”

“Same reason you’re looking at me like that.”

She let out a sigh.

“I went over the other night,” she said. “But it was weird. He’d just dropped a glass and cut his hand.”

“Okay.” Eddie drew the word out a bit. “Why was that weird?”

“Not that, but the way he acted. It felt like I was interrupting more than just a broken glass.”

That was concerning. Especially when taken in consideration with what Buck told him. Combine that with some good ol’ Catholic guilt, lapsed or not, and Eddie was trying to figure out how to talk to Bobby.

See, Eddie wasn’t exactly proud of what he’d said to Bobby that first time he tried transferring back. Sure, yes, he still disagreed immensely with the man for how he blocked Eddie from returning, and for why. After all, Eddie wasn’t the one missing shifts because he was too drunk to come in. But he digressed.

He loved Danny, he did. He was lucky enough to be allowed to see the Danny that Buck so adored. Since they reunited, however, he was also seeing what everyone else saw. The unfettered man who saw the problem, came up with a solution, and didn’t care an ounce about the people that were crushed on the path from point A to point B.

Usually, Danny being proud of him would make Eddie’s day. Hell, his week. But Danny being proud of him for being cruel? Purposefully so, at that? It made him a little queasy.

Honestly, Eddie started seeing Frank because of that argument. Not because Bobby was right, but because Eddie was seeing a pattern start to form. No, he’d seen a pattern that had already formed. The argument with Buck on Christmas, the way he took a verbal knife and stabbed his boyfriend with it, that should have been enough. But Diazes were stubborn, so of course it wasn’t.

Danny had the privilege of being cruel without feeling guilty about it later. Eddie was envious a little, because that was beyond him. Catholic, and all.

This conversation with Bobby, if what was going on was what Eddie thought it was, would be delicate. Also bound to be awkward, because it would be his first real conversation with the man since the argument. Especially since Eddie was right. Cruel or not, he would stand by the fact that he was right.

(“So is this what you do?” Eddie had a foot on the bottom step of the stairs. “People tell you they want to come home, and you slam the door in their face?”

“I’m not slamming the door in anyone’s face,” Bobby told him. The captain’s own was drawn. “I’m just concerned. For your sake, and for the sake of the team, I can’t-”

“Wow,” Eddie loudly interrupted. “Wasn’t that the bullshit you tried to feed Buck? You know, when he came to you asking to come home. What did you do then, Bobby? Huh?”

The captain drew in a breath, chest rising and falling with it. It was probably meant to be calming. It didn’t seem to be working.

“I can’t have you back the way you are right now,” he said. His tone was purposefully, forcefully even. “You need to go talk to someone, work out whatever’s going on.”

Those words reminded Eddie far too much of Buck’s nagging him about that very subject.

“That’s rich coming from you,” he spat at the captain. “You know what? I don’t need to ask you for anything. I can go to another house.”

“Then you better hope they don’t call me for a recommendation.”

“Why? Because you’ll lie to them about me? Just like you lied to everyone about Buck?” Eddie scoffed. “Trust is so damn important in our job. And you are doing everything you can to prove we can’t trust you. That we can’t depend on you.”

“I am your friend, Eddie. I’m simply trying to look out for you.”

“No, you’re standing there on a pile of bodies telling me I’m not fit for duty. You burnt a hundred-something people alive and then all you did was drink about it. Go fuck yourself.”

Eddie stormed up the stairs, Bobby’s silence following him the entire way.)

The surprise on Bobby’s face when he answered the door was plain to see. As was the apprehension.

“No transfer papers this time.” Eddie held out his hands. “Promise.”

Mainly because he went over the captain’s head on Danny’s advice. He was simply waiting for everything to go through now.

“Alright.” Bobby’s tone was still cautious. “Then you can come in.”

He stepped aside, allowing Eddie to enter. Eddie went down the short flight of stairs, doing his best at looking casual as he did.

“I can’t stay long,” he said as he turned to face Bobby. “Chris has some friends over, and it’s Danny and Buck in charge.”

“Then you definitely want to hurry back. Lord knows what Dan’s going to teach them.”

“Valuable life skills,” Eddie joked. Then he sighed. “Bobby, look, Jonah wasn’t your fault.”

The captain’s face fell.

“Eddie-”

“No, listen. You cannot be carrying someone else’s cross. It’s not your weight to bear, it’ll just drag you down.”

“I’m the captain,” Bobby insisted. “My responsibility-”

“Is to the team,” Eddie said. “Jonah was at the station for a month, Bobby. Just a month. You barely knew him. You’re here blaming yourself for missing something, but everyone else missed the same.”

“This happened on my watch.” Bobby’s voice cracked as he spoke. “He killed Claudette and who knows how many others.”

“He thought he was a god. He’s not. Neither are you,” Eddie said. “You’re not all-knowing. You’re just a man, imperfect and doing your best. Just like the rest of us. But if you keep up like this, shouldering responsibility that isn’t yours? You’re gonna burn out, Bobby. And then you’re gonna drown yourself in a bottle.”

The hit landed like Eddie knew it would. This time it wasn’t out of cruelty, but out of hope that it would have the man course correct before disaster struck.

Eddie didn’t linger long, now that his piece was said. He clapped the captain on the shoulder, squeezed it, then let it go. He got a weak smile in return.

Bobby’s silence followed him up the stairs and out the door.

“So why are we meeting here?”

Taylor’s voice had Daniel looking up from the menu. He gave her his brightest smile and pulled his hand out of his jacket pocket, where he’d been fiddling with that pinkish-reddish rock he found the other day. It got transferred from his uniform pants when he changed at the end of the shift, and then he simply hadn’t done anything with it since.

They were at some outdoor cafe, the same one Maddie said she’d met Chimney at for the job talk. Every table was wooden and small, fit for only two people, and had a bundle of sunflowers in a fat glass vase. There were large citronella torches dotted about. The menu was alright, but everything looked overly expensive.

Daniel really hoped Chimney had the decency to pay last time, since he’d had the chance to rebuild his savings

“That’s why,” he said.

He pointed behind her. The reporter turned and almost immediately spotted Maddie, sitting at another table not too far away.

“This is a bit Victorian, isn’t it?” Taylor remarked. She set her purse on the table and took the seat across from him. “Chaperoning your sister’s date, I mean.”

“She’s not dating anyone yet,” Daniel replied. “But that’s why we’re here. Now why are we here?”

Taylor called him the day before, wanting to get together for something. She hadn’t really said on the phone, much to his aggravation. To be fair, he was already aggravated by one of Chris’s shithead friends, but that was neither here nor there.

The point was, while Daniel came up with the location – technically Maddie did – it was Taylor who was the reason they were meeting up.

“I’m writing a book,” the reported said.

Daniel set down his menu.

“A book?”

“Yes. About the LAFD.”

Daniel raised an eyebrow.

“Why?” he asked.

“Because I am a damn good reporter, but I’ve only got name recognition in Los Angeles,” was Taylor’s answer. “I need that if I want to do more than just local news.”

“But why the LAFD? Why not something true crime? That’s all the rage, right?”

She twitched. Daniel kept his grin bright and friendly, and no wider than it was. He knew about her parents, because she was acting squirrely a few months back. Enough so that Buck got concerned and then managed to get the details out of her. It went unsaid, but Buck was probably supposed to keep it to himself. He still told Daniel, all the same.

Ever since, Daniel liked to make random oblique jabs while talking to Taylor. For shits and giggles. He made sure there was no sign that he knew, though.

“It is all the rage,” Taylor gritted out through a razor edged smile. “Everyone’s doing it. Hardly anyone’s doing firefighters, and the exploitation is more morally acceptable.”

“You care about morals?”

“My potential readers care about morals, and I care about them buying the book.”

That was fair enough.

“That doesn’t explain why you wanted to talk to me,” Daniel pointed out. “There’s Buck, there’s… You have other friends, right? You never really gave me an answer when I asked last time.”

“Most people can’t handle me,” Taylor said with a flick of her wrist. “Feel blessed.”

“Sure, right. Blessed. Uh-huh.”

She gave him the stink-eye.

“You don’t care,” she said. “That’s why you’re here. As long as I only say nice things about Buck and Austin, and maybe Eddie-”

“That one depends on my mood,” Daniel said. “So, what? I’m here because you think I’m an amoral psychopath?”

“There’s nothing stopping you from sharing the worst, most embarrassing, most interesting material,” Taylor said. He wasn’t certain if she was confirming what he said or simply ignoring the question all together. “The dirtiest laundry, the most vile gossip. Because you don’t care. Buck would care, any other firefighter would probably care. But I could put all the details about Captain Nash and the spiked brownies into the book, and you won’t have a moral quibble over it.”

“That’s not a fair example, that’s Nash. I wouldn’t care if you gave him spiked brownies today and filmed it.”

Behind Taylor, he watched as Chimney finally arrived. He sat down at Maddie’s table, excitedly greeting her. Maddie’s return greeting was not quite so upbeat.

“The department lawyers are still scarier than the station’s, they’d never allow the footage to air. Though last time I tried was before that suspension.” Taylor eyed him with consideration. “Do you know why he was suspended?”

“I’m sure lots of people do. Department’s a bunch of gossips,” was his answer. “If you made more friends, they’d probably be happy to tell you.”

He might not like Nash, and would probably laugh throughout the man’s funeral if he died today, but he wasn’t going to upset Buck by sharing that particular tidbit of lore. It was better that Taylor put in the effort herself. Plausible deniability and all that.

“What a perfect non-answer, you should be a politician,” she drawled. “Does that mean you won’t be giving me any material for the book?”

“Hell no,” Daniel scoffed. He kept an eye on Chimney and Maddie. “It means if I’m doing this, it needs to happen in a way that I don’t have Buck radiating disappointment at me.”

“That’s doable.” She dug into her purse, taking out a notepad and a golf pencil. Apparently they were starting right away. “It’s always best to verify and corroborate sources.”

“I’m bringing Austin with me next time.” He leaned forward a bit. “But there’s this guy at Harbor…”

It was Maddie’s idea to meet at the same little outdoor cafe as last time. She thought, briefly, about going to Andrea’s, which had better fish tacos, but she felt this conversation would taint wherever it was held. She didn’t want Chimney to never be able to think of Andrea’s without thinking of it.

It was Daniel’s idea for him to tag along. He was a few tables away, by himself. Or he was until Taylor Kelly popped up like an unfortunate pimple. But it was his idea, because,

“He lashed out because he was angry,” Daniel reminded her. “Maybe he’ll keep his temper better in public.”

She didn’t think Chimney would do that. Lash out at her. There was always that uncertainty, though, an invisible scar left over from Doug she wasn’t certain would ever entirely heal or disappear. A tendril of doubt that crept up and through, because once…

She didn’t think Chimney would lash out at her, but she could see the benefit of a just-in-case arrangement. At least neither of them were stuck in an apartment. Both could, if – when – this conversation got overly charged, leave entirely.

“Maddie!” Chimney was excited to see her. “If we do this a third time, we might make a tradition out of it.”

“Right, yeah.”

Her return smile was not quite so wide and cheerful as his.

“Have you ordered yet?” he asked after he sat down.

“No, uh, just a drink.” She took a sip of her water. “I’m not all that hungry, actually.”

Maddie would feel worse about taking up space, but there weren’t that many people here. The only reason Chimney didn’t spot Daniel was because he only saw Daniel’s back and then focused on Maddie. She didn’t think either of them would be sticking around very long after this, either.

“So what did you want to talk about?” Chimney asked.

He sounded just as excited as when he arrived. Hopeful, too, and…

Oh, Howie.

“It’s about something we- something you brought up the other day,” she said. His brows came together in confusion. “Dating.”

Chimney froze, and then visibly forced himself to relax.

“Dating,” he repeated.

“I wasn’t thinking about it before you brought it up,” she said. “But now…”

Maddie gave a small shrug.

“Dating each other?” there was that hopeful lilt to his voice again. It made her stomach squirm uneasily. “Or- Or other people?”

That was said with some reluctance.

“I don’t know,” Maddie admitted, and she tried not to watch his face crumple at the three simple words. Her napkin was much more interesting, as was Daniel’s conversation with Taylor behind Chimney. “I think it would be better for us- No.”

She cut herself off, and then shook her head.

“No,” she said. “I think it would be better for me if I was… Single? For a while? Playing the field? I don’t know.”

“Better for you.” Chimney scrunched up a napkin in his hand. She wasn’t sure he even noticed it. “How- What does that- Why, Maddie?”

“I met Doug on my first day of my sophomore year in high school. AP US History.” Maddie grimaced at the memory of her younger, more naive self. “He was handsome, and charming. One day, the teacher paired us up for a project, and...”

Now, as an adult, the age difference between them would have been negligible. Barely anything at all, really. But it was a gulf back then.

“I’ve been dating, or engaged, or married almost the entire time since,” she continued. “This is the longest I’ve been single since I was fifteen, Howie. Over twenty years.”

“Wow, I didn’t…” Chimney seemed a bit lost, and uncertain how to reply. “Fifteen?”

“Doug said I was mature for my age.” Maddie’s voice came out flat, she knew. “He graduated and went to Penn State in Harrisburg, we’d see each other on the weekends or whenever Mom and Dad weren’t home. Then I graduated and moved in with him, then I moved to Boston with him, then… I was with him from the time I was fifteen to when I finally left him and came here. And then- Buck and Dan were right, those early hang-outs were dates. I finally left him and then you and I got together just a month later.”

“So- So- What? Maddie, I’m not understanding.” There was a plaintiveness to his words, a pleading quality. “We’re here so you can tell me, what? That- That you want to- I don’t know, Maddie, enjoy the buffet? Eat Pray Love your way through LA?”

She let out a huff of laughter at the words.

“Maybe,” she admitted. “Maybe I’ll date a different guy every week, have flings that don’t matter beyond having fun. Or maybe I’ll do what Buck did, go out to bars and clubs, or download whatever apps are popular. Have bad, disappointing sex with strangers I’ll never see again.”

Both options had Chimney wincing.

“Or maybe I’ll spend the whole time by myself,” she said. “Just me. A chance to get to know me. Whoever I am when I’m not with someone. Whoever I am when I’m not someone’s girlfriend or wife. Whoever I am when I’m not attached to someone.”

She sighed.

“Maddie. I want to get to know Maddie,” she said. “I think she’s been a bit lost for a while.”

The temptation was there to reach across the table and soften the blows. She didn’t do that. She wasn’t certain he would welcome it in that moment.

“I love you, Howie,” she told him.

“I- I love you, too,” Chimney croaked. “Maddie…”

“In Boston, we made a promise. We wouldn’t pretend. We would be honest about how we feel, and what we want.”

“I remember.”

“The other day, Dan-” She saw brief flash of anger across his face, a scowl he quickly smoothed away. His hand curled into a fist on the table. “He asked me if I knew who I was. He had this whole laundry list, almost all of them negative, but an entire list of things that he was, ways to describe himself. I could barely come up with three things. Three things that weren’t related to my job, or to Jee-Yun.”

“You talked to Dan.”

It was a statement, not a question.

“I did. He told me whatever I decide, whatever I do, however I feel about you, you’re Jee’s dad. Not a bad dad, either.” She wasn’t certain if she was pleased or not by the surprise, verging on shock, that morphed Chimney’s face. “A dad who has been there for Jee-Yun, which is more than I can say for myself.”

“Maddie,” he said. “C’mon, that isn’t…”

“It’s still true, even if it was because I was sick. You were there for her while I was sick.”

“More than what my own father can say.”

“Whatever I decide to do, we’re Jee’s parents. Before anything else, we are both that,” Maddie said. “So this isn’t- This isn’t a goodbye. This isn’t even a break-up, because-”

“Because we did that in St. Louis,” Chimney said.

There was enough humor in the words that she didn’t feel quite as terrible about this. Quite being the operative word there.

“Co-parents,” he said. The humor leaked out, leaving his words a flat tire. “We’ll be exactly what we are now.”

“Yeah,” she needlessly confirmed. “I just- I didn’t want…”

She didn’t want him to be blindsided by anything.

She didn’t want to lead him on, because she knew…

(A part of her saw this as only a break. A small period of her life where she could figure herself out and then they’d find their way back together. Better, this time, with a stronger foundation. That outlook was not very fair to him, because it meant that same part of her was expecting him to wait. It was expecting him to be available the moment she decided she was ready.)

“Right, yeah, I understand.” The words left Chimney’s mouth whispery and empty. “I- I’m just…”

Chimney got up from the table and left without another word, shoulder’s slumped. He walked past Daniel without seeing him, which was undoubtedly a good thing. Her brother watched him go and then moved to her table. Taylor was left rolling her eyes, but she was already packing away a notepad covered in very elegant looking handwriting.

“So,” Daniel breathed out. “This place has a wine menu.”

Maddie had scarcely heard better sounding words.

“No, no,” Eddie said. “This isn’t going to be awkward at all.”

Buck could not help but agree. The moment the four of them – Buck and Eddie, Danny and Austin – arrived at the Wilsons’ backyard, things got… Well, very awkward. Chimney went stiff at the sight of them, took a very deep breath, and then stomped off.

They all took a moment to be thankful Maddie decided not to come.

“It’s not too late to transfer back to the One Thirty-Three, squirt. They miss you,” Danny said, then told Austin and Eddie, “I guess you two can come over, too.”

For once, Buck heard the words, heard that his presence was missed, that his absence was noted and not with relief, and did not doubt them. Part of it was because it was Danny, because he’d grown up treating his big brother’s words as gospel and that kid hadn’t gone away entirely. Even as he learned, in harsh ways and gentle, that Danny spoke out his ass so often his neck had to be sore.

But also, Buck did very much doubt the words, because-

“I talk to them all the time.” He held up his phone. "I’m talking to them right now.”

His brother frowned.

“Am I not…?” Danny pulled out his own phone and, after some thumbing, let out an, “Oh, I muted it. Why did I-? Right, Petra was being annoying.”

“I can’t transfer over to your station,” Austin pointed out. “Not unless you plan on moving to a full-time paramedic position.”

“That would have me partnering with Aiden most of the time.” Danny’s face made his thoughts on that quite clear. “I’d do it if I could steal Nola or Mendoza.”

“I’m gonna tell them you like ‘em,” Buck announced, already typing away.

“I didn’t say that!”

“Please.” Karen’s voice cut through them, interrupting Danny’s attempt to pull Buck into a headlock. “If you’re going to be like this, go away.”

“Sorry Karen,” Buck mumbled as he straightened the jacket of his suit.

Eddie snickered until Buck stomped on his foot.

“Austin drove,” Danny said. “I’m stuck here.”

“We’ll order you an Uber,” Karen retorted.

“What are you guys even doing here?” Hen asked, looking confusedly between them. “Dan, are you wearing a leather jacket to my mother’s wedding?”

“No,” Danny guilelessly replied.

Hen huffed. Her eyes landed on Buck’s hair – the curls were free again; a conscious decision this time – and he braced himself for a comment. His mom always had a few. But her eyes flicked past him and she ended up not saying anything at all.

When Buck glanced back, there was only Danny and Austin there. Danny was rocking on his heels a bit, his hands stuffed into his jacket pockets. One hand looked like it was fidgeting with something in the pocket- Wait.

“Wait.” Buck’s finger bounced between the Hen and Karen. “Your mother’s…?”

“I think you should talk to your mom,” Eddie suggested, fingers already gripping at Buck’s sleeve. “Come on, let’s go.”

See, here’s the thing: this was going to be Toni and Clive’s wedding. That was, indeed, the original plan with the details worked out by Karen’s guilt. Guilt for what, exactly, Buck never got a straight answer for. As far as he knew, Toni was the one who should feel guilty, considering she willingly chose to skip her only child’s wedding.

And Toni definitely felt guilty. Guilty enough she decided that, instead of her and Clive getting married, her daughter and daughter-in-law would be. Again. The message went out, and yet it seemed to have somehow missed the two people most important to the plan.

“It’s kinda an asshole move,” Danny said. They were lounging in the folding chairs, waiting for Hen and Karen to catch up with the rest of them. “Surprising your spouse-to-be with a wedding, I mean. I don’t know what it is when someone surprises both people, though.”

“I think they’re both romantic,” Buck argued.

“You would.”

Distance and barriers prevented another squabble from breaking out. Eddie and Austin were in the seats between them, no doubt a strategic decision. Buck and Danny would need to climb over them to get at each other.

They managed to behave until everything started, and then through the ceremony itself. Buck’s experience with weddings amounted to a grand total of one, and this one was leagues ahead of that one simply because Doug was rotting in a hole this time. Toni officiated, since they were already married and didn’t anyone to legally do anything.

Afterwards, they had food. Always the best part in the opinions of both brothers. There were round tables with flower arrangements as centerpieces. Tables big enough for the four of them to sit together, which meant Buck and Eddie could torture Danny by being overly romantic.

“Oh, yeah.” Danny pulled something out of his jacket pocket and held it out to Austin. A small rock, reddish-pinkish in color and oval shaped. “Here.”

Austin glanced at it with a frown, but took he took the rock. Danny just shrugged when his squash silently asked for an explanation.

“Hey.” Buck turned and poked at Eddie until his boyfriend paid attention to him. “There’s a penguin documentary coming out in a few weeks.”

“Nerd,” Danny jeered. “How’s the house?”

Austin sighed.

“Annoying,” he said. “You’d think it’d be quicker, since there won’t be a mortgage...”

Fin.

Chapter End Notes

For the sake of your emotional wellbeing, do not look up the statistics regarding LAPD and people with knives.
